# The optimal decentralized-web architecture

This report states one mechanism for each component of a decentralized internet. Each mechanism was
compared against every rejected candidate, and the comparison cites a paper read in full. Each
mechanism was then checked against every other selected mechanism for a precondition it removes. The
design target is a system whose identity, indexing, and storage no single company can control, and a
client that performs the functions of the most-visited websites. Privacy is a set of tiers the user
selects. Each tier states the adversary it defeats. Each tier states the latency and the bandwidth it
adds, both measured.

## Every figure comes from a full-text primary source

Every figure in this report comes from the full text of a primary source. A short label cites each
source: an uppercase author, venue, and year, such as `KOMLO-SAC-20`. The corpus holds 407 papers,
each read in full. A choice whose justification cites no source is labeled a guess where it is made.
One choice, capacity ordering, is labeled a pick rather than a selection for half of itself, because
the overlay it recommends has no full-text entry in the corpus. An audit checked 408 cited claims
against their source entries. The audit confirmed 396 claims and corrected 13 in place.

## Three kinds of statement go to three different places

A measured fact is a number an experiment produced, with its conditions stated. A measured fact goes
to the evidence corpus, cited to the paper. A structural consequence is a property that follows by
reasoning from a measured fact or a proof. A structural consequence goes to this report, with the
reasoning shown. A value judgment is a decision that a cost is or is not worth paying. A value
judgment is excluded, or attributed to whoever made it. A number enters the corpus only from a full
text. An abstract establishes that a paper exists. An abstract establishes no measurement.

---

## One mechanism is selected per component

**Identity and keys**

1. **Identity** — A Decentralized Identifier (DID) document holds two keys, one per purpose. The first
   key signs everyday actions. It is a threshold Schnorr key produced with FROST. Its shares are held
   on the participant's own devices, and are never combined into one key. The second key authorizes
   replacing that device set. It is a hash-based post-quantum key (SPHINCS+), split under Pedersen
   verifiable secret sharing, and combined only during a rotation.
2. **Forgery resistance** — No social-graph defense bounds the number of forged identities on a real
   network. Instead, each write costs a fixed amount of memory-hard computation. Every peer that
   relays or stores the write checks that cost itself. This bounds one entity's write rate to the
   computation it controls, whatever number of identities it holds. No identity system is consulted.
3. **Key transparency** — A key directory records the current public key of each identity. A committee
   of witnesses signs every update round of the directory (Parakeet). A verifier detects a substituted
   key as long as fewer than a threshold of the witnesses are dishonest. The rejected alternative needs
   an audit population to volunteer to exchange directory snapshots, and nothing compels them to.
4. **Key recovery** — User-chosen guardians hold shares of a standing (t,n) threshold BLS signing key.
   The guardians use the key only to jointly sign a fresh identity key for a user who lost their own.
   The guardians never reconstruct the lost key.

**Naming, location, and search**

5. **Naming** — Each human-readable label binds to a self-certifying public key through private,
   per-zone delegation (GNS). The rejected alternatives are a synchronized naming ledger and a Domain
   Name System link. Measured deployments of the ledger end in single-party control. Measured
   deployments of the link end in a market where names are scarce and priced.
6. **Content location** — S/Kademlia locates content. It keeps Kademlia's routing-table structure,
   which orders nodes by the bitwise-XOR distance between their identifiers. It adds two defenses: a
   lookup over several disjoint paths, and node identifiers derived from a computational puzzle. An
   unmodified Kademlia deployment on a live network suffered an eclipse attack, in which an attacker
   surrounds a target with nodes it controls. The two defenses stop that attack.
7. **Indexing and search** — A node keeps a local index of content it fetched, authored, or was shown.
   That index answers a query first, at no network cost. A query the local index cannot answer goes to
   the network. The network protocol samples random peers and returns a probably-approximately-correct
   result — correct with a stated probability, not with certainty.

**Storage**

8. **Storage encoding** — Plain Reed-Solomon (n,k) erasure coding stores each object. It splits an
   object into k data fragments and computes n−k parity fragments, so any k of the n fragments
   reconstruct the object. It is selected over whole-copy replication, over regenerating codes, and
   over locally repairable codes. High participant turnover decides the comparison, the regime this
   system runs in, not the low-turnover regime in which most repair-cost-reducing codes were measured.
9. **Repair** — Locally Repairable Codes repair a lost fragment (the Azure/Xorbas construction). A
   small local group of fragments reconstructs one lost fragment by itself, the default path. A full
   Reed-Solomon decode is the fallback, used when a local group loses more fragments than its local
   parity covers. Local-group repair moves one third to one half of the traffic a plain Reed-Solomon
   repair moves.
10. **Incentives** — Two mechanisms settle payment, because no single candidate settles both
    resources. A storage-retrievability proof settles payment for storage capacity: the storing peer
    proves it still holds the data. A per-connection reciprocation rule (PropShare) settles bandwidth:
    each peer serves a neighbor in proportion to what that neighbor served it. No credit ledger is used.

**Messaging and groups**

11. **Group encryption** — BeeKEM encrypts group messages. It is a decentralized ratchet tree: a tree
    of keys in which each membership change re-keys one path, at a cost proportional to log n for a
    group of n members. It runs over authenticated causal broadcast, so it needs no service that puts
    messages into one total order.

**Network transport**

12. **Transport** — Three transports carry connections, chosen by the two endpoints. WebRTC data
    channels carry every connection that reaches a browser tab. QUIC carries every connection between
    two processes that can open a socket. Onion-routed multi-hop encapsulation is a tier the user can
    add over either transport. It is not the default.
13. **NAT traversal** — A relay introduces two peers, each of which is behind a network address
    translator. Each peer learns its own externally observed address from the relay. Both peers then
    start an outbound connection at the same moment, timed by the relay (DCUtR/Circuit-v2). The relay
    stays as the session's fallback path if the direct attempt fails. This is the only such mechanism
    in the corpus measured at internet scale.
14. **Capacity ordering** — A minority of peers, selected by claimed capacity, forms a transit tier
    that carries all relay traffic. Every other peer connects through that tier and does no relay work,
    by its structural role, not by a throttle. No mechanism in the corpus verifies the capacity a peer
    claims to gain admission to the transit tier.

**Application layer**

15. **Application data model** — Each data shape gets its own mechanism. No one mechanism serves all
    data. Editable multi-writer state uses hash-identified operation-based CRDTs — Conflict-free
    Replicated Data Types, whose concurrent updates merge to one result without coordination. Feeds use
    single-writer signed logs. Media uses content-addressed immutable objects, each addressed by the
    hash of its own bytes. Operational Transformation is excluded in every topology this design permits.
16. **Ranking** — Independently operated feed generators compute every ranking. A client subscribes to
    the generator it chooses. One deployed measurement shows fewer than 3 in 100 users ever change the
    default generator. So the mechanism computing the default ranking, not the set of alternatives, is
    what nearly every user receives. That default mechanism is gossip-based collaborative filtering.
17. **Moderation** — Client-subscribed labeling services handle unwanted but lawful content. Each
    client subscribes to the labelers it chooses. The rejected mechanisms act at the granularity of a
    server or a shared list. Each of them removes content for every viewer of that server or list,
    regardless of one viewer's preference. Illegal content instead goes through a deny list of content
    identifiers, checked at the moment a peer serves the content.
18. **Reputation** — To assess a stranger, a participant computes a bounded network-flow value from
    itself, as the source, to that stranger (SumUp / Bazaar / Ostra). The value flows over a graph of
    trust links whose edges each cost something to create. A capacity bound on the edges limits how
    much flow a set of forged identities can supply.
19. **Privacy tiers** — Three ladders let the user trade privacy for cost, one ladder per action:
    fetch, query, and message. The rungs of each ladder are ordered by measured cost. Each rung states
    the adversary it defeats. Two candidate families are withheld from the ladders, because their
    measured cost is not reliable enough to offer a user as an informed choice.

---

# Part 2 — Each component's candidates, selection, cost, and failure condition

Each section below is the full record for one component. It gives the component's candidate table, the
selected mechanism, the comparison against every rejected candidate, the preconditions the selection
places on other components, its measured cost, its failure condition, and what the corpus does not
settle.



---

## Identity: A Decentralized Identifier holds two purpose-separated keys — a threshold Schnorr key spread across a participant's own devices for everyday signing, never reconstructed, and a secret-shared, hash-based post-quantum key exercised only to rotate that device set

A participant's cryptographic identity is the set of keys that authorize action under one persistent identifier, and the mechanism that lets a verifier keep resolving that same identifier correctly after the participant loses a device, adds a device, or must replace a compromised key. Every candidate below answers a different part of that problem: an identifier syntax and document format for stating which keys currently apply (`W3C-DIDCORE-22`), a way for an external party to attest additional claims about the identifier's holder (`W3C-VCDM-25`, `BORMANN-SSR-25`), or a way to hold or use a signing key across more than one device without any single device holding the whole key (`SHAMIR-CACM-79`, `PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`, `KOMLO-SAC-20`, `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`). A long-lived identifier also has to choose a signature algorithm that stays unforgeable if a large-scale quantum computer becomes available during the identifier's own lifetime (`BERNSTEIN-EUROCRYPT-15`, `HULSING-CCS-19`, `DUCAS-TCHES-18`).

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Self-certifying identifier + one unshared key (W3C DID Core baseline; deployed as `did:plc`) | No benchmark in the normative specification itself `W3C-DIDCORE-22`. Deployed at over 10 million registered users under `did:plc`, the one production measurement in this corpus, this design ended in signing keys held custodially by the hosting operator, not the user's own device, for ordinary users `KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`. | Possession of the one private key controlling the DID document's `authentication`/`capabilityInvocation` entries `W3C-DIDCORE-22`; for `did:plc`, each later document version is valid only if signed by the key authorized in the immediately preceding version `KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`. | "There are currently no common recovery mechanisms that apply to all DID methods" `W3C-DIDCORE-22`. The one deployment measured routed around this by handing key custody to the server operator, so a compromised or malicious operator "holds the keys needed to forge repository updates on that user's behalf" `KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`. | A DID method plus a verifiable data registry component to resolve the current document `W3C-DIDCORE-22`; for `did:plc`, an always-available directory server serving full version history, whose honesty is required only for availability and fork-choice, not basic integrity `KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`. |

Selective disclosure alone does not prevent a verifier from linking repeated presentations of the same credential to one individual `MAZZOCCA-COMST-25`. | An external issuer already trusted off-protocol `AMBATI-SACMAT-26`; a separately hosted status mechanism the verifier fetches `W3C-VCDM-25`; a DID resolver for the issuer and holder identifiers `W3C-VCDM-25`. |

Hybrid BBS-ECDSA device-binding proof, from an external prototype this paper reports rather than measures itself: ≈800 ms generation time, 150 KB proof size `BORMANN-SSR-25`. | A pairing-friendly elliptic curve deployed across every issuer, holder, and verifier, or acceptance of the added overhead of a batch-issued or server-aided substitute `BORMANN-SSR-25`. | Native device binding "does not work for immediate deployment," because commercial Secure Elements support only the P-256/ECDSA curve, not the pairing-curve groups G1/G2 `BORMANN-SSR-25`. None of the surveyed constructions is quantum-safe; a quantum-safe zero-knowledge proof system does not, by itself, make a credential built on a classical signature quantum-safe `BORMANN-SSR-25`. | Every issuer, holder, and verifier currently using ECDSA-based credentials to change software `BORMANN-SSR-25`; a cloud hardware security module where Secure Element hardware cannot perform the pairing-curve operation `BORMANN-SSR-25`. |

Shamir reconstruction via known O(n log² n) interpolation algorithms; a 16-bit field modulus suffices for schemes up to 64,000 shares `SHAMIR-CACM-79`. | Shamir alone gives information-theoretic secrecy against fewer than k shares `SHAMIR-CACM-79`. Pedersen adds computational (discrete-log-hardness) detection of a cheating dealer; the paper proves no non-interactive scheme can make both properties unconditional at once `PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`. | Reconstruction assembles the complete secret at one point in order to sign or reissue it; neither paper specifies a re-sharing protocol for who initiates or verifies a later rotation `SHAMIR-CACM-79`. Plain Shamir sharing implicitly trusts the dealer at the moment of the split `SHAMIR-CACM-79`. | A uniformly random coefficient source `SHAMIR-CACM-79`. For Pedersen's variant, an agreed group setup (primes p, q and generators g, h with unknown discrete logarithm log_g(h)) fixed by a trusted authority or a coin-flip protocol, plus a confidential per-participant channel separate from the public commitment broadcast `PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`. |
| Threshold signature, non-robust, Schnorr-based — FROST | One signing round after a one-time, non-interactive preprocessing stage, versus 4 rounds for the Stinson-Strobl robust baseline and 8 rounds for Gennaro-Goldfeder's one-round-with-identifiable-abort threshold ECDSA; round counts only — "the paper reports no runtime benchmark" `KOMLO-SAC-20`. | Discrete-log hardness in the signing group, benchmarked over Ristretto/curve25519; a per-participant zero-knowledge proof of knowledge of the constant term of that participant's key-generation polynomial, added specifically to block rogue-key attacks `KOMLO-SAC-20`. | Non-robust: "a single misbehaving or unresponsive participant causes the entire signing operation to abort," requiring exclusion and a rerun `KOMLO-SAC-20`. | An available, correctness-checked (not confidentiality-trusted) location for participants to publish preprocessing-stage nonce commitments `KOMLO-SAC-20`; an authenticated channel so a failure can be attributed to a specific participant; reliable message delivery, stated as an assumption the protocol does not itself tolerate violations of `KOMLO-SAC-20`. |
| Threshold signature, robust, BLS-pairing-based — TGS | None empirical; structurally, share length independent of n, key generation via Gennaro-Jarecki-Krawczyk-Rabin distributed key generation (DKG) with no trusted dealer `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`. | Tolerates any t < n/2 malicious parties, stated by the authors as an optimal fraction for a robust, non-interactive scheme of this kind; proved in the random oracle model because the base BLS signature scheme's own proof requires it `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`. | Robustness itself depends on every participant's public per-share commitment being "publicly broadcast commitments persistently available to the combiner role," not just visible during key generation `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`. Periodic re-randomization against rotating device compromise requires layering a separate proactive-secret-sharing protocol with synchronized refresh rounds among all n parties `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`. | A broadcast channel plus secure point-to-point channels among all n parties `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`; a pairing-friendly Gap Diffie-Hellman group; proof-of-possession enforced at public-key registration to block rogue-key attacks `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`. |
| Post-quantum stateless hash-based signature as the identity's root key — SPHINCS / SPHINCS+ | SPHINCS-256: 41,000-byte signature, 1,056-byte public key, 47,466,005 cycles single-core signing (≈13.6 ms), 1,369,060 cycles verification, on an Intel Xeon E3-1275 Haswell at 3.5 GHz `BERNSTEIN-EUROCRYPT-15`. SPHINCS+ (Haraka, robust, n=192): 30,696-byte signature, 48-byte public key, 29,015,002 cycles signing, 2,739,770 cycles verification on the same hardware family — 25% shorter signatures and 1.7× faster signing than SPHINCS-256 at the same 2^50-signature ceiling `HULSING-CCS-19`. | Unforgeability rests only on stated hash-function properties (one-wayness, second-preimage resistance, subset-resilience), not a number-theoretic problem a quantum computer breaks `BERNSTEIN-EUROCRYPT-15`. SPHINCS-256's original 128-bit claim was overstated: a cited external tightness-gap attack reduced it to under 190 bits classical and 95 bits post-quantum `HULSING-CCS-19`. | Signature and key sizes run roughly two orders of magnitude above an elliptic-curve signature `BERNSTEIN-EUROCRYPT-15`. No published (t, n)-threshold variant of SPHINCS or SPHINCS+ appears anywhere in this corpus. | Identical hash-function and tweakable-hash public parameters trusted by every verifier `HULSING-CCS-19`; a maximum-signature ceiling fixed in advance during parameter search `HULSING-CCS-19`. Requires "no persistent mutable state at the signer between signing operations," so the same key copies to a second device with no rotation protocol needed for the signing operation itself `BERNSTEIN-EUROCRYPT-15`. |
| Post-quantum lattice-based signature as the identity's root key — Dilithium | Recommended parameters, AVX2-optimized: 218,724 cycles key generation, 789,926 median (1,035,284 average) cycles signing, 209,292 cycles verification, on an Intel Core i7-4770K Haswell at 3.5 GHz; 1,472-byte public key, 2,701-byte signature `DUCAS-TCHES-18`. | Hardness of Module Learning With Errors (MLWE) and Module Short Integer Solution (MSIS) `DUCAS-TCHES-18`. | The classical forgery-security reduction "does not transfer over to the quantum setting"; the paper's own substitute quantum argument (SelfTargetMSIS) is itself non-tight in the classical random oracle model, defended only empirically `DUCAS-TCHES-18`. No published (t, n)-threshold variant appears anywhere in this corpus. | A constant-time implementation — a stated design goal, not claimed to be audited beyond timing `DUCAS-TCHES-18`; every verifier must retain the signer-supplied hint bytes alongside (z, c), or verification fails outright `DUCAS-TCHES-18`. |

### Selection

The identifier layer is the W3C Decentralized Identifier (DID) document (`W3C-DIDCORE-22`), which registers two purpose-separated keys rather than one. An `authentication` key is a t-of-n threshold Schnorr key produced and used with FROST (`KOMLO-SAC-20`), spread across the participant's own devices, never reconstructed at signing time. A `capabilityInvocation` key — the key that authorizes replacing which devices hold shares of the authentication key — is a single SPHINCS+ keypair (`HULSING-CCS-19`) whose seed is itself split under Pedersen verifiable secret sharing (`PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`) across a custodian set distinct from the everyday device set, reconstructed only at the moment of a deliberate rotation and immediately re-split afterward.

Against the bare single-unshared-key baseline: `W3C-DIDCORE-22` states plainly that no common recovery mechanism exists across DID methods, and the one production deployment measured in this corpus confirms the practical consequence — `did:plc`, at over 10 million registered users, ended up with signing keys "held custodially by the PDS operator for ordinary users" rather than solving device-loss recovery in the protocol (`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`). Splitting the key across the participant's own devices removes the single point of failure the baseline has no answer for, without reintroducing the custodian the deployed system was pushed into.

Against the issuer-attested verifiable credential as the identity's own root: rejected as a base representation, not as a mechanism. A credential's `credentialSubject` binds claims to a DID that must already resolve to keys behind it — the credential itself carries no answer to device loss for the holder's own proof-of-possession key, and establishing that a DID corresponds to a real-world entity is explicitly outside what the credential mechanism supplies (`AMBATI-SACMAT-26`: "the protocol itself supplies no mechanism to establish that correspondence"). A credential is a claims layer that presupposes a working identity root, not a substitute for one.

Against BBS-based anonymous credentials as the identity's own root: rejected for the same layering reason, and concretely because none of the surveyed constructions is quantum-safe (`BORMANN-SSR-25`) and native device binding "does not work for immediate deployment" given current Secure Element hardware (`BORMANN-SSR-25`). Confining BBS to an optional presentation layer, chosen per interaction rather than fixed at the identity's root, keeps that cost — and that quantum-safety gap — out of the mechanism every other action depends on.

Against reconstruct-to-sign secret sharing (plain Shamir, or Pedersen's verifiable variant) as the day-to-day signing mechanism: rejected, because reconstruction assembles the complete secret at one point in order to sign, which is exactly the exposure a threshold signature scheme is built to avoid — FROST's own combiner "does not perform a DKG during signing operations" and no participant's share is ever assembled into the whole key (`KOMLO-SAC-20`). Pedersen's mechanism is selected instead for the one role that matches its own description: backing up a key deliberately reconstructed once, rarely, immediately followed by generating a fresh key and re-splitting — the periodic re-share technique `SHAMIR-CACM-79` itself describes — which is exactly the root-rotation role assigned to it above, not the everyday signing role.

Against robust BLS threshold signing (TGS) as the day-to-day mechanism: `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`'s robustness — continuing past up to t misbehaving parties without aborting — is precisely the property FROST gives up (`KOMLO-SAC-20`: "a single misbehaving or unresponsive participant causes the entire signing operation to abort"). FROST is selected anyway for this role on the one dimension both papers report, round count: one round after one-time preprocessing, against TGS's requirement of both a broadcast channel and secure point-to-point channels among all n parties for its distributed key generation (`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`), plus a combiner that must keep every party's public commitment persistently reachable. TGS's robustness matters most when signers are a set of mutually distrusting servers that cannot be relied on to respond — the deployment model `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03` states explicitly — whereas a personal device set under the identity holder's own control can supply FROST's required authenticated exclusion-and-rerun path directly. Choosing FROST also avoids importing a second, pairing-based hardness assumption alongside the hash-based assumption the selected post-quantum root key already carries. This specific trade-off is not settled by a head-to-head measurement in either paper; it is stated here as a reasoned choice, not a citation-backed comparison, and is flagged again below.

Against a raw, unshared post-quantum key (SPHINCS+ or Dilithium) as the sole root, with no secret-sharing layer: rejected because neither has a published threshold variant in this corpus, so a single unshared copy reproduces the exact single point of failure `W3C-DIDCORE-22` already states is unsolved. Layering Pedersen verifiable secret sharing underneath removes that single point for the rare rotation role without requiring a threshold signature scheme to exist for either post-quantum algorithm, because the split key is reconstructed, used once, and immediately re-split rather than held open across live, repeated signing operations.

Between SPHINCS+ and Dilithium specifically, for the root key's algorithm: SPHINCS+ is selected. Dilithium's own paper states its classical forgery-security reduction "does not transfer over to the quantum setting," and states its substitute quantum-era argument (SelfTargetMSIS) is itself non-tight, defended only empirically, not by proof (`DUCAS-TCHES-18`). SPHINCS+'s security instead rests only on stated hash-function properties with no comparable stated proof gap (`BERNSTEIN-EUROCRYPT-15`, `HULSING-CCS-19`). SPHINCS+'s roughly two-orders-of-magnitude larger signature (30,696 bytes against Dilithium's 2,701 bytes, `HULSING-CCS-19`, `DUCAS-TCHES-18`) is exactly why this selection confines it to the rare rotation role rather than the frequent authentication role — where the size difference would recur on every signature — and not a reason to prefer Dilithium at the root, since the root key's own proof gap, not its size, is what this selection weighs against it.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- A DID method plus a verifiable data registry component (the naming and content-location components) available whenever any party resolves this identity's current DID document `W3C-DIDCORE-22`.
- A key transparency component (a separate synthesis component) observing every DID document update and producing non-equivocation evidence, since DID Core supplies no non-repudiation of updates on its own `W3C-DIDCORE-22`, and the one production deployment measured in this corpus left exactly this gap open, stating its own planned transparency log "does not exist yet" `KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`.
- An available, correctness-checked location for FROST's preprocessing-stage nonce commitments, reachable by the identity holder's own devices; this location need not be trusted for confidentiality, only to serve correct, unused values `KOMLO-SAC-20`.
- An authenticated channel among the holder's own device set so an unresponsive or misbehaving device can be attributed and excluded, since FROST is non-robust `KOMLO-SAC-20`, and reliable message delivery among that device set during a live signing operation, which FROST's authors state as an assumption the protocol does not itself tolerate violations of `KOMLO-SAC-20`. This is a property of the identity holder's own small device set, not of arbitrary network peers; the transport/reachability component supplies it only at that scope.
- Retry-and-exclusion logic external to FROST after an abort, since "FROST itself only detects and halts" `KOMLO-SAC-20`.
- A group setup (primes p, q and generators g, h with unknown discrete logarithm log_g(h)) agreed once, at enrollment, for the Pedersen split of the SPHINCS+ root seed, established by a trusted authority or a coin-flip protocol among the custodian set, plus a confidential per-custodian channel separate from the public commitment broadcast `PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`.
- A live coordination event of at least k custodians, simultaneously reachable, to execute any root rotation; fewer than k custodians together learn nothing about the seed `PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`, `SHAMIR-CACM-79`.
- Every verifier resolving this identity supports both signature algorithms (threshold Schnorr and SPHINCS+) and reads the DID document's `verificationMethod` type field to know which key authorizes which action `W3C-DIDCORE-22`.

### What it costs and where it fails

FROST signing is measured in this corpus only by round count, not wall-clock time: one round after a one-time, non-interactive preprocessing stage `KOMLO-SAC-20`. It fails — aborts, requiring a rerun with the offending device excluded — the instant any one of the t selected devices is unresponsive or returns an invalid share `KOMLO-SAC-20`.

SPHINCS+ root-key operations cost 29,015,002 cycles signing and 2,739,770 cycles verification on a 3.5 GHz Haswell core (≈8.3 ms and ≈0.78 ms respectively) at the Haraka-robust, n=192 parameter set, with a 30,696-byte signature `HULSING-CCS-19` — a cost this selection accepts because the key signs only rare rotation events, not because the size is small in absolute terms.

Pedersen verifiable secret sharing costs fewer than 2|q|k modular multiplications to distribute and (2k+1)|q|+(k-1) multiplications per custodian to verify `PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`, negligible against either signature scheme's own cost. It exposes the raw SPHINCS+ seed at the single moment of reconstruction; a set of k colluding or compromised custodians present at exactly that moment defeats it `PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`.

The compound failure condition for this selection is: the device set falling below FROST's threshold t at the same time the custodian set falls below Pedersen's threshold k. That double-threshold event is unrecoverable, the same "no common recovery mechanisms" residual `W3C-DIDCORE-22` states for DIDs generally, now bounded to a joint condition rather than a single lost key. No entry in this corpus measures the frequency of device replacement or custodian availability under a composition like this one; the one production system measured avoided the underlying problem by concentrating key custody at the server operator rather than solving it `KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`.

### What the corpus does not settle

1. No entry measures a published post-quantum (t, n)-threshold signature scheme with per-share confidentiality preserved through signing. FROST and Boldyreva's TGS both depend on discrete-log hardness, which Shor's algorithm breaks on a sufficiently large quantum computer; SPHINCS+ and Dilithium have no published threshold variant anywhere in this corpus. Combining device-loss survival and quantum-safety in a single, frequently used signing key is an open problem this selection routes around — by confining the quantum-safe key to the rare rotation role — rather than one it resolves. A solution would need a threshold signature construction built on hash-function or lattice hardness in which no subset of t or fewer participants, at any point including during signing, holds enough information to forge a signature or reconstruct the key.
2. No entry measures FROST's behavior when the device set's membership itself changes. A fresh distributed key generation round for the new device set is the implied mechanism, but no measurement of its cost, round count, or failure rate under real device churn exists in this corpus `KOMLO-SAC-20`.
3. No entry measures FROST signing wall-clock time on real hardware; `KOMLO-SAC-20` reports round counts and asymptotic security bounds only, stating explicitly that "the paper reports no runtime benchmark."
4. No entry evaluates the two-key decomposition proposed above (a frequent threshold key plus a rare, Pedersen-backed post-quantum root) as a composed system. Each measured figure cited above comes from a paper evaluating that one piece in isolation; no paper in this corpus evaluates the composition as a whole.
5. `AMBATI-SACMAT-26` states key recovery is "arguably the most critical unsolved problem" across the 47 papers it surveyed, and found zero of 12 real deployments achieving full self-sovereignty on any of five dimensions, including key control. This selection is a composition of individually measured pieces, not a deployment measurement in its own right, and no entry in this corpus measures a deployed multi-device-threshold DID at any population scale.
6. No entry recommends or measures values for (k, n) or (t, n). `SHAMIR-CACM-79` and `PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91` both leave the threshold and total-share count as designer-chosen with no recommended values; `KOMLO-SAC-20` leaves FROST's (t, n) similarly unassigned. Choosing them for a real population requires a measurement this corpus does not supply: how often a real participant loses or replaces a device, and how many custodians a real participant can keep simultaneously reachable.
7. No entry describes or measures a proactive-refresh (periodic re-randomization) technique compatible with FROST specifically. Boldyreva's TGS states compatibility with the general Herzberg-Jarecki-Krawczyk-Yung proactive-secret-sharing technique `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`; this compatibility claim is not established for FROST's Schnorr construction anywhere in this corpus, so whether the selected everyday key can be refreshed against a slowly rotating set of compromised devices, without a full re-share, is unmeasured.
8. No entry measures whether device pairing under this threshold construction is less error-prone in practice than the device pairing key-transparency systems report generally as "cumbersome and error-prone for users in practice" `MELARA-USENIXSEC-15`. The structural argument for this selection — no single device ever holds the complete private key `KOMLO-SAC-20` — is a property derivable from FROST's own design, not a usability measurement.


---

## Forgery resistance: No social-graph defense bounds Sybil identity count on a real network; a write priced in memory-hard computation bounds an entity's write rate to compute it controls, independent of identity count

Forgery resistance is the property that lets a component elsewhere in the system (search ranking,
replication, a per-viewer aggregate over signed claims) treat one entity's influence as bounded by
the real resources that entity controls, so that splitting into many identities does not multiply
what that entity can do. Four candidate mechanisms attempt this: ranking or partitioning a social
graph to detect fabricated identities, certifying that one credential corresponds to one human,
charging computation for each write, and charging a forfeitable staked deposit for each write. The
first two try to bound the count of identities directly; the corpus shows both fail to reach that
goal on a real network. The last two do not try to count identities at all — they price each
write in a resource, so an entity's total achievable write rate is bounded by (resource it
controls) / (price per write), regardless of how many identities issue those writes.

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Social-graph community detection (SybilGuard/SybilLimit/SybilInfer/ACL/SybilRank family) | Exact max-flow underlying the credit-network variant of this family: 0.352 s (Renren, 1.4M links) to 220 s (Orkut, 234M links) per query on a dual-12-core Xeon X5650 `VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`. SybilRank's power-iteration ranking processes a 160-million-node synthetic graph in under 33 hours on an 11-node Amazon EC2 cluster, and was deployed on the complete Tuenti graph (11,291,486 nodes, 1,421,367,504 edges) with roughly 100 nodes manually inspected to pick trust seeds `CAO-NSDI-12`. | The honest region of the graph forms one fast-mixing community reachable by a bounded number of attack edges from a trusted seed `YU-SIGCOMM-06`, `YU-SP-08`, `VISWANATH-SIGCOMM-10`. | On a real 21,297,772-node Twitter graph, the benign/Sybil partition has modularity 0.0042 — the paper's own cited threshold for detectable community structure is 0.3 — and restricting to the largest Sybil connected component raises modularity only to 0.0046 `GAO-CNS-18`. An adversary who places attack edges at the k nodes nearest the trusted seed, instead of uniformly at random, drives detection accuracy on a real Facebook graph first to random (0.5) and then below it, so Sybils outrank honest nodes `VISWANATH-SIGCOMM-10`. Under the isolated-Sybil attack pattern actually observed on RenRen, five tested schemes (SybilLimit, SybilGuard, Mislove's community detection, GateKeeper, ACL) score 0.34–0.49, all at or below random `ALVISI-SP-13`. Real graphs (correlation coefficient −0.81 between modularity and detection accuracy across 8 datasets) show weaker community structure than the synthetic graphs the schemes were validated on `VISWANATH-SIGCOMM-10`. | A designated trusted seed per verifying node, whose selection and refresh procedure neither `VISWANATH-SIGCOMM-10` nor `ALVISI-SP-13` specifies. A materialized or locally samplable social graph with mixing time varying by more than an order of magnitude across real networks (100–400 steps for Wiki-vote/Slashdot/DBLP/YouTube/Facebook versus 1,500–2,500 for LiveJournal, at ε=0.1) `MOHAISEN-IMC-10`, so the deploying system must measure its own graph's mixing time rather than assume it. Manual, out-of-band verification of trust-seed accounts — roughly 100 per deployment in `CAO-NSDI-12`'s own case. |
| Proof of personhood (biometric enrollment, government-document matching, or reverse-Turing-test / web-of-trust ceremonies certifying one credential per human) | The cryptographic wrapper alone, once a personhood proof is already available: 10.42 ms sign, 11.24 ms verify, threshold issuance 0.0651 s (t=5,n=10) to 72.6 s (t=150,n=300) `CRITES-CCS-25`. The personhood check itself is not measured: Idena's reverse-Turing test (FLIP) reaches 95% human accuracy against 60–76% for the best attacking AI teams, cited by the paper from a source it does not independently verify `SIDDARTH-FRONTIERS-20`. | An external, off-protocol channel correctly binds one credential to one human and refuses a second credential to the same human `CRITES-CCS-25`, `ADLER-ARXIV-24`. | `CRITES-CCS-25`'s own construction states plainly that it does not build this channel — it lists a signed government certificate, a biometric reading, or an OAuth token as possible instantiations and states security holds "only when at least t of the n issuers behave honestly," which is a claim about the cryptographic wrapper, not about whether the plugged-in personhood check itself resists forgery. Every web-of-trust instantiation (BrightID, Duniter, Kleros/Proof of Humanity) inherits the community-detection failure above: a person can obtain a second credential by enrolling through a second, non-intersecting vouching group, because vouching validates that a person exists without bounding how many distinct graphs will vouch for the same person `ADLER-ARXIV-24`, `SIDDARTH-FRONTIERS-20`. A reverse-Turing test alone "fails to address human-generated attacks, in which one individual passes the test multiple times and creates multiple different identities" `SIDDARTH-FRONTIERS-20`. Biometric enrollment depends on a TEE (trusted execution environment) deployment the corpus records as having already been discontinued after a disclosed DRAM-bus-interposition attack against the specific TEE product one such scheme names `LAS-EPRINT-26`. | An external personhood-verification channel this component cannot itself build or verify, per `CRITES-CCS-25`'s own stated scope. A functioning, sufficiently liquid financial-stake market and a dispute-resolution process for any vouching-with-stake variant `SIDDARTH-FRONTIERS-20`. |
| Computational work priced per write, verified locally by every relaying or storing peer | The base hash-based construction reports no usable minting figure — `BACK-HASHCASH-02` states only that it "describes initial experience from experiments" with no run count, hardware, or wall-clock time given. A memory-hard cost function does have measured figures, for the closely related password-hashing use: scrypt at N=2^20 (3.8 s per evaluation on a 2.5 GHz reference core) raises the estimated one-year brute-force hardware cost for an 8-character password to roughly $19 billion, against $18,000 for PBKDF2 at the same wall-clock target, both derived from 130 nm circuit die-area and manufacturing-cost data `PERCIVAL-BSDCAN-09`. Argon2d evaluates at 0.6 cycles/byte at 4 threads on a Haswell i7-4500U, filling 1 GB of memory in under a second `BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16`. Verification of a Hashcash-style token is one hash evaluation `BACK-HASHCASH-02`; verification of a memory-hard token is one full evaluation of that function. | An entity's achievable write rate is bounded by (compute it controls) / (difficulty per write), independent of how many identities it presents: a faulty entity commanding resources ρ times a minimally capable entity's resources can obtain g = ρ times that entity's write rate, no more `DOUCEUR-IPTPS-02` (Lemma 1). | Bounded on both sides by the same free parameter, the required difficulty: set too low, the throttle enforces nothing; set too high, honest participants on constrained hardware (mobile, embedded) cannot sustain any write rate at all. Plain hash-based work lets specialized hardware mint at a cost per hash orders of magnitude below a commodity CPU's, breaking the assumption that compute controlled tracks resources controlled evenly across participants; a memory-hard cost function narrows this but does not close it — `ALWEN-EUROSP-17` measured a 2x cost reduction against Argon2i-B at 1 GB memory and 6 passes, inside the parameter region the function's own designers' analysis (`BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16`) had called safe. A non-interactively minted token can be produced in advance from idle compute and spent in a burst later, unless bound to an unpredictable, frequently changing value — a mitigation `BACK-HASHCASH-02` proposes but does not implement or measure. | Local verification of the attached proof by every peer that relays or stores the write, at the cost of one hash or one memory-hard evaluation per write. A spent-token or nullifier store, retained at least as long as the token's validity window, at every verifying peer, plus loosely synchronized clocks across peers to bound that window without either rejecting valid recent tokens or letting the store grow unboundedly `BACK-HASHCASH-02`. A canonical, per-write target string bound into the proof (the write's own content address or destination), so a solved token cannot be replayed against a different write `BACK-HASHCASH-02`. |
| Resource-based admission: a forfeitable staked deposit priced per write, recovered by cryptographic slashing on detected double-use (Rate-Limiting Nullifier construction) | Membership-proof generation ≈0.5 s on an iPhone 8 at a group size of 2^32; proof verification ≈30 ms, constant regardless of group size; per-peer identity-commitment-tree storage 67 MB at depth 20 (up to ≈1,048,576 members); on-chain registration or deletion costs 40,000 gas, stated to exceed 20 USD "at the time of writing" `TAHERIBOSHROOYEH-ARXIV-22`. | A globally consistent, slashable ledger of deposits exists and is itself resistant to the same forgery this mechanism defends against, and at least one honest routing peer observes any double-signal within the retained epoch window `TAHERIBOSHROOYEH-ARXIV-22`. | The paper's own authors state the bound is not eliminated, only priced: an entity that registers k identities and pays k times the deposit obtains k writes per epoch rather than one, a limitation they say "can only be raised in cost, not eliminated, by increasing the membership fee." A spammer can withdraw its deposit before a routing peer catches and reports a double-signal, forfeiting only the registration cost rather than the full stake a slasher would otherwise claim `TAHERIBOSHROOYEH-ARXIV-22`. | A smart-contract-capable, globally ordered ledger the deployed instance builds on Ethereum specifically; every verifying peer independently reconstructing the identity-commitment tree from that ledger's event stream, since the contract itself stores only the ordered public-key list, not the tree; a zkSNARK verifier for the specific proof system used (Groth16), and, if a single-party trusted setup is unacceptable, a multi-party setup ceremony trusted by every verifier; loosely synchronized clocks to define the shared epoch boundary `TAHERIBOSHROOYEH-ARXIV-22`. |

### Selection

Computational work priced per write, verified locally by every peer that relays or stores the
write, with no identity system consulted at all. An entity's total achievable write rate across
every identity it holds is bounded by the compute it controls divided by the difficulty set per
write — Douceur's Lemma 1 states this bound formally for any resource-demanding challenge `DOUCEUR-IPTPS-02`
— and identity count plays no part in the bound, because minting a fresh identity costs nothing
while producing a fresh write still does.

Against social-graph community detection: rejected because the corpus establishes the mechanism
does not reach its stated goal on a real network, not merely that it costs more than an
alternative. `GAO-CNS-18` measures modularity 0.0042 on a 21,297,772-node Twitter graph, against
the 0.3 threshold the field itself uses for detectable community structure — the graph property
every scheme in this family assumes is, on this measured real network, absent by two orders of
magnitude. `ALVISI-SP-13` and `VISWANATH-SIGCOMM-10` are two independent analyses that reduce every
published social-graph scheme to the same underlying operation, local community detection around a
trusted seed, and both measure that an adversary who chooses which honest nodes to attach Sybils
to, rather than attaching uniformly at random, defeats it: accuracy at or below random guessing
(0.34–0.49 across five schemes in `ALVISI-SP-13`; below 0.5, meaning Sybils outrank honest nodes, in
`VISWANATH-SIGCOMM-10`'s Facebook experiment). A mechanism whose own literature shows it degrading to
worse than a coin flip under the attack it exists to resist is not a workable candidate, regardless
of what it costs to run.

Against proof of personhood: rejected because no entry in this corpus builds the mechanism the
approach depends on. `CRITES-CCS-25`'s own construction states it does not supply the personhood
relation — the check that a claimed identity string corresponds to one real, previously-unenrolled
human — and lists a government certificate, a biometric reading, or an OAuth token as external
instantiations whose own forgery resistance the paper does not evaluate. Every instantiation this
corpus does evaluate that avoids depending on an external, centrally-operated issuer instead
depends on a social graph of vouches, which `ADLER-ARXIV-24` and `SIDDARTH-FRONTIERS-20` both state
fails on exactly the axis social-graph community detection fails on: a person obtains a second
credential by enrolling through a second, non-intersecting vouching group, since vouching
establishes that a person exists without bounding how many distinct groups will vouch for the same
person. A reverse-Turing test (Idena's FLIP, 95% human accuracy against 60–76% for the best
attacking AI team, `SIDDARTH-FRONTIERS-20`) distinguishes a human from a machine, which is a
different claim from distinguishing one human from many humans, and does not bound identity count
by itself.

Against resource-based admission (staked deposit with slashing): not rejected as broken — measured,
deployed-scale figures exist for it (`TAHERIBOSHROOYEH-ARXIV-22`: 0.5 s proof generation, 30 ms
verification, 67 MB tree storage at 2^20 members) and its own authors state the same
resources-not-identity-count property this selection is chosen for: k identities cost k deposits
for k writes, a bound that is priced rather than eliminated, matching what per-write compute cost
also delivers. The two mechanisms are preferred on what each requires from the rest of the system,
not on what either costs to run. Staked-deposit admission requires a globally ordered,
smart-contract-capable ledger recording and slashing balances — in the measured deployment,
Ethereum specifically — plus every verifying peer holding a live view of that one ledger's state
`TAHERIBOSHROOYEH-ARXIV-22`. That ledger's own forgery resistance (why an attacker cannot mint
arbitrary balances to stake) is a second, unstated instance of the same problem this component
exists to solve, pushed onto whichever component secures the ledger rather than solved locally.
Per-write compute cost needs no ledger, no consensus over balances, and no external asset: any peer
independently checks one hash or one memory-hard function evaluation against the write's own
content. Where the architecture already includes a global consensus ledger for an unrelated reason,
staked-deposit admission becomes attractive on its measured constant-time, sub-second-latency
verification (`TAHERIBOSHROOYEH-ARXIV-22`); absent one, requiring it here to solve forgery resistance
adds exactly the centralized dependency the rest of the design exists to avoid.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- Every peer that relays or stores a write verifies the attached proof before consuming further
  bandwidth or storage on that write — one hash evaluation for a plain construction, one full
  memory-hard function evaluation for the hardened variant `BACK-HASHCASH-02`, `BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16`.
- The proof is bound into a canonical, per-write target string — the write's own content address or
  destination — derived by the canonical-encoding / content-addressing component, so a solved proof
  cannot be replayed against a different write; `BACK-HASHCASH-02` states a token not bound this way
  can be replayed against a resource other than the one it was minted for.
- Every verifying peer holds a spent-token or nullifier store, keyed by the proof's own value, and
  loosely synchronized clocks across peers to bound how long that store must retain an entry before
  the token's own validity window expires; too short a window and legitimate recent writes are
  rejected, too long a window and the store grows unboundedly `BACK-HASHCASH-02`.
- No identity, key-transparency, or admission component is consulted by this mechanism at all —
  this is the resources-not-identity-count property itself. Any per-identity quota or rate limit
  elsewhere in the architecture is a separate mechanism this component neither supplies nor
  interferes with; the composition between the two (whether a per-identity quota and a per-write
  price compound or substitute) is not addressed by anything in this corpus.
- The deploying system independently estimates or bounds ρ, the ratio between the most and least
  capable participant's compute it intends to admit, since `DOUCEUR-IPTPS-02`'s Lemma 1 ties the
  resulting write-rate advantage directly to that ratio and supplies no method for measuring or
  enforcing it.
- If tokens are minted non-interactively (no live challenge from the verifying peer), the
  per-write target string should incorporate a value that changes unpredictably and frequently — a
  public beacon — to prevent an entity from banking tokens from idle compute today and spending
  them in a burst later; `BACK-HASHCASH-02` proposes this without implementing or measuring it.

### What it costs and where it fails

No entry in this corpus measures a deployed wall-clock or dollar cost for a hash-based per-write
proof in this exact use — `BACK-HASHCASH-02` states explicitly that its own reported experience
carries no usable run count, hardware, or timing figure. The closest measured figures are for a
different use of a memory-hard cost function, password-hashing rather than a write-rate throttle:
`PERCIVAL-BSDCAN-09` derives that scrypt at a 3.8-second-per-evaluation setting (N=2^20) raises
estimated one-year brute-force attacker hardware cost to roughly $19 billion, against $18,000 for
PBKDF2 at the same wall-clock target, both figures derived from circa-2002, 130 nm circuit
die-area data the authors themselves call "very approximate." Argon2d evaluates at 0.6 cycles per
byte at four threads on a Haswell-generation commodity CPU, filling 1 GB of memory in under a
second `BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16` — the cost an honest participant pays per unit of difficulty, on
hardware from that period.

The mechanism fails on the same axis on both sides of one bounded range: below some difficulty
setting the throttle imposes no real cost, so an entity with even modest excess compute floods
writes unopposed; above some difficulty setting, a legitimate participant on constrained hardware
(a mobile device, an embedded node) cannot sustain any write rate the mechanism will accept. No
entry in this corpus derives or measures where that boundary falls for a decentralized write-rate
throttle specifically — every cost figure available is for a different application (password
hashing) at a different latency target (0.1–5 seconds per single evaluation, chosen for
interactive login or file-encryption use, not for a sustained per-write rate) `PERCIVAL-BSDCAN-09`,
`BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16`.

It also fails when the difficulty function's own hardness assumption is wrong: `ALWEN-EUROSP-17`
measured a concrete 2x cost reduction against Argon2i-B at 1 GB memory and 6 passes, a parameter
region its own designers' published security analysis (`BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16`) had concluded was
safe, using heuristics that analysis did not consider. A memory-hard cost function narrows the gap
between commodity and specialized attacker hardware relative to a plain hash function; it does not
close that gap to zero, and the corpus records at least one case of the designers' own safety
margin being wrong in practice.

### What the corpus does not settle

1. No entry measures a deployed wall-clock or dollar cost for computational work priced per write
   in a peer-to-peer network specifically — every cost figure available in this corpus for a
   compute-cost mechanism is either a symbolic bound with no numeric value (`DOUCEUR-IPTPS-02`) or a
   measurement for password-hashing at a different latency target (`PERCIVAL-BSDCAN-09`,
   `BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16`). A solution needs a measured figure for minting and verification cost at a
   difficulty calibrated specifically to a sustained per-write throttle, on hardware ranging from a
   commodity mobile device to purpose-built attacker hardware.
2. No entry measures what write rate a legitimate participant on the least capable hardware this
   architecture intends to admit can sustain at any given difficulty setting, so the corpus supplies
   no way to place the operating point within the bounded range stated above.
3. No entry measures the real-world ratio ρ between the most and least capable participant's
   compute in a deployed, heterogeneous peer-to-peer network — the one number `DOUCEUR-IPTPS-02`'s
   Lemma 1 needs to state what write-rate advantage the resulting bound actually permits.
4. No entry composes a per-write computational cost with a replication mechanism elsewhere in this
   architecture that itself depends on write or query rate (for instance, a search-feedback-driven
   replication scheme). Whether a per-write cost throttle changes the write pattern such a mechanism
   observes is not addressed by anything in this corpus.
5. No entry measures the effect of the public-beacon mitigation against a precomputation attack —
   `BACK-HASHCASH-02` proposes it without implementing or measuring it, so its actual cost and
   effectiveness against a well-resourced adversary banking tokens over time is unmeasured.
6. Whether a memory-hard cost function's hardware-heterogeneity advantage (commodity CPU against
   specialized attacker hardware) is adequately bounded for this specific threat model — an
   adversary that controls a botnet of ordinary consumer devices rather than purpose-built hardware
   — is not measured anywhere in this corpus; every measured hardware-cost figure available concerns
   dedicated attacker hardware built to crack passwords, not a botnet of commodity devices already
   under an adversary's control.


---

## Key transparency: a fixed witness committee that must sign every round beats an audit population that must volunteer to gossip

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (conditions) | Security assumption | Failure condition | Requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Chained signed Merkle-prefix-tree snapshots, client self-audits its own binding each epoch (CONIKS) | Server epoch computation 2.6 s average (Java prototype, 2.4 GHz Xeon E5620, 10M-user tree, 1,000 insertions/epoch); client verification of one authentication path 159 µs average (10M-user directory); monitoring one day ≈16.1–22.6 kB depending on signature scheme; auditing one provider one day 0.8–6.9 kB depending on signature scheme; 99.9% probability of equivocation detection after 5 checks each by two non-colluding users, dropping to 94% detection within 5 checks at up to 50% colluding auditors — `MELARA-USENIXSEC-15` | The provider is caught only if two parties who were shown different signed tree roots (STRs) for the same epoch compare notes, or an independent auditor tracks the STR chain and finds a fork — `MELARA-USENIXSEC-15` | The mechanism "provides no mechanism to force that gossip to happen, only the format of the evidence once it does" — `MELARA-USENIXSEC-15`. An entry a client never re-checks, or whose two divergent viewers never communicate, keeps a substituted key undetected indefinitely, with each party's own local check showing no anomaly | Persistent per-client local storage of the last verified STR across sessions; at least one client device the identity provider cannot fully block from the network; a verifiable unpredictable function keyed to the provider's private key for index privacy — `MELARA-USENIXSEC-15` |
| Nested chronological + prefix-tree snapshots, same self-audit model, owner-monitoring cost held constant in epoch count (Merkle²) | 8×10⁶ users per server machine at a 1-second epoch (single AWS r5.2xlarge, Go prototype), 100x CONIKS's user count at that epoch length; owner monitoring cost 2.33 ms / 22.9 kB regardless of epochs elapsed, versus CONIKS's 1.29–88.03 ms and 2.1 kB–2.1 MB growing linearly with epochs monitored (2^20-user system) — `HU-SP-21` | Same non-equivocation model as CONIKS: an auditor population must independently track and compare digests; Merkle² only bounds what that population's own re-check costs, it does not create the population or force it to compare | CONIKS outperforms Merkle² once the epoch interval reaches ≈1 hour, because append/lookup cost then dominates monitoring cost; the paper states no mechanism for extending log capacity past the pre-chosen chronological-tree-height cap without redeployment — `HU-SP-21` | Fixing a maximum chronological tree height before deployment (caps total capacity at 2^31 entries at the tested height of 31); server-side append serialization; Ed25519 signatures and SHA-3 hashing as the sole cryptographic primitives — `HU-SP-21` |
| Persistent append-only zero-knowledge set, chained via a Merkle tree of per-epoch commitments, VRF-hidden labels, any-party adjacent-epoch audit (SEEMless) | Server update 0.28 s average at 10M users (RAM-only, 125 GB Xeon VM); adjacent-epoch audit under 4.24 MB / under 0.3 s; CONIKS's own Java implementation exhausted heap at ≈4.5M users on the same harness — `CHASE-CCS-19` | "It is sufficient to have at least one honest auditor perform audits over each adjacent server epoch," and audits can be distributed across independent parties with no coordination — `CHASE-CCS-19` | The paper names no mechanism inside SEEMless that ensures that one honest auditor exists for a given adjacent-epoch pair; it explicitly defers cross-client consistency (stopping the current root from silently diverging across users) to an external blockchain post or gossip protocol not supplied by SEEMless itself — `CHASE-CCS-19` | An external, out-of-band root-commitment consistency mechanism (a blockchain post as in Catena, or gossip as in CONIKS); synchronized-enough clocks between server and clients; an out-of-band second factor already authenticating the user to the provider — `CHASE-CCS-19` |
| Compaction over a verifiable key directory plus threshold-witness quorum-certificate signing (Parakeet) | Verify notification 68.70 ms mean (single core, 4-witness committee, 1,024-update batch); create vote 0.02 ms; verify certificate 0.16 ms; end-to-end throughput ≈800 updates/s at latency under 3.5 s (WAN testbed, 5 AWS regions, 10 witnesses); throughput flat across 10/20/50 witnesses and unaffected by 1 or 3 crashed witnesses out of 10; storage ≈4.5 GB at 5M users, growing linearly in users, not in epochs elapsed — `MALVAI-NDSS-23` | A quorum of 2f+1 out of 3f+1 witnesses is honest each round; at least one of those honest witnesses actually checks the append-only audit proof before voting, and "the proof is void with zero honest auditors" — `MALVAI-NDSS-23` | Byzantine (as opposed to crash) witness behavior is stated by the authors as untested: "evaluating BFT protocols in the presence of Byzantine faults is still an open question," so no measured degradation curve exists for actively malicious witnesses. Separately, compaction's deletion-soundness depends on each user re-querying their own key history at least once between a tombstone epoch and the following deletion epoch — a user who never does so cannot detect a wrongful deletion of their own binding — `MALVAI-NDSS-23` | A witness committee reachable over an asynchronous network by both the identity provider and every client; persistent storage with fast random writes at the provider (RocksDB/MySQL scale in the benchmarked prototype); durable Ed25519 signing keys at the provider and each witness; bounded clock drift among witnesses sufficient for the paper's f-fault-tolerance argument, with no numeric bound stated — `MALVAI-NDSS-23` |
| Periodic root-digest posting to a public blockchain (EthIKS, Catena) | No full-text entry retrieved in this corpus for either paper — listed as having none. Secondhand characterizations only, each attributed to the citing paper, not independently checked against the source: `CHASE-CCS-19`'s own bibliography annotation states EthIKS "weakens CONIKS's privacy guarantee and makes per-operation blockchain invocation 'quickly infeasible' at scale"; `MALVAI-NDSS-23`'s own bibliography annotation states Parakeet's authors argue Catena requires flooding open ports at scale and characterize Catena's scalable alternative, a header-relay network, as "effectively centralized" | Whatever majority controls block production on the chosen chain does not rewrite or omit the posted digest | Neither underlying paper's own stated failure conditions are available in this corpus; only the citing papers' summary judgments are, and those are value judgments about cost, not measured figures | A single chain used network-wide for this purpose; every resolving client either running a full node or trusting a relay/SPV chain for chain state, which reintroduces the same everybody-must-independently-check gap as the CONIKS family unless every client is a full node |
| Full username-to-key binding stored directly in blockchain consensus state, no separate off-chain directory (Namecoin-style) | Registered .bit names ≈120,000 at time of measurement; ≥76% squatted (value recurs ≥10 times); Namecoin held ≈1/3 of Bitcoin's contemporaneous hash rate including merge-miners; expiration window 36,000 blocks (≈250 days) after a March 2012 hard fork — `KALODNER-WEIS-15` | The chain's mining majority does not rewrite committed history; a name owner's private key is not compromised | "A sufficiently large Bitcoin mining pool could still carry out" a 51% attack against the merge-mined chain — `KALODNER-WEIS-15`. Across the wider blockchain-anchored-identity literature, single-chain anchoring is a named, unresolved risk: 39 of 47 surveyed self-sovereign-identity systems anchor to one blockchain, and 0 of 12 real deployments achieve full self-sovereignty on all five surveyed dimensions — `AMBATI-SACMAT-26` | "A consuming application needs continuous access to a synchronized copy of the [chain] to resolve a name, because the mapping lives only in chain state, not on any server the application can query directly" — `KALODNER-WEIS-15`. A naming layer external to the chain must supply its own renewal reminders, since a name reverts to the available pool automatically after the expiration window with no protocol-level warning |

### Selection

Compaction over a verifiable key directory with threshold-witness quorum-certificate signing (Parakeet, `MALVAI-NDSS-23`) is selected.

Against the CONIKS-family chained-snapshot mechanisms audited by the key owner (CONIKS `MELARA-USENIXSEC-15`, Merkle² `HU-SP-21`, SEEMless `CHASE-CCS-19`): all three place the cross-party equivocation check on a population of auditors the mechanism does not itself create. `MELARA-USENIXSEC-15` states this directly: the scheme "provides no mechanism to force that gossip to happen, only the format of the evidence once it does." `CHASE-CCS-19` states the equivalent gap explicitly as an out-of-scope dependency: it "explicitly leaves that mechanism outside SEEMless itself," naming a blockchain post or a gossip protocol as external options neither built nor measured in that paper. `HU-SP-21` inherits the same gap; its contribution is bounding what an eventual auditor's own re-check costs, not supplying the auditor. An entry that nobody in this population happens to check is served with a fully valid-looking authentication path to every individual requester, each of whom sees no local anomaly, and the substitution stands until two specific viewers who were shown different values compare notes or a volunteer auditor finds the fork. Parakeet closes this by making the check itself a required step of every round: a quorum-certificate cannot exist for an epoch unless at least 2f+1 of 3f+1 witnesses verified an append-only audit proof against the previous digest and voted, at a measured 68.70 ms per verification and 0.02 ms per vote on ordinary server hardware (`MALVAI-NDSS-23`), cheap enough to run on every round rather than depend on whether some volunteer happened to check.

Against periodic root-digest posting to a public blockchain (EthIKS, Catena): neither paper has a full-text entry in this corpus, so no measured cost or proof for this specific variant can be cited as evidence rather than as the citing papers' own summary judgment. `CHASE-CCS-19`'s and `MALVAI-NDSS-23`'s bibliography annotations both characterize per-operation blockchain interaction as reaching an infeasible or centralization-reintroducing cost at scale, but these are secondhand characterizations attributed to the citing paper, not independently checked figures, and are recorded here as such rather than as a settled comparison.

Against a full on-chain binding with no separate directory (Namecoin-style, `KALODNER-WEIS-15`): this variant does supply universal, non-optional auditing — any full node validates every committed binding as a side effect of following consensus, which is a stronger property than any of the other candidates offer on paper. But the property it substitutes for auditing is trust in whoever currently holds a majority of block-production power, and `KALODNER-WEIS-15` measures that this specific deployment held roughly one-third of Bitcoin's hash rate, explicitly noting "a sufficiently large Bitcoin mining pool could still carry [a 51% attack] out." `AMBATI-SACMAT-26`'s survey of 47 blockchain-anchored identity systems finds this single-chain dependence unresolved across the field (0 of 12 real deployments achieve full self-sovereignty on all five surveyed dimensions), and states no cross-ledger interoperability standard exists to mitigate it. A design whose stated goal is that identity cannot be captured by any company should not make key-substitution detection depend on which entity currently controls the largest share of one chain's block production; Parakeet's witness quorum is a smaller, purpose-built, explicitly Byzantine-fault-tolerant committee (3f+1, tolerating f), not a general-purpose mining/staking majority, and its size and membership are a parameter the surrounding system controls rather than a majority contest against a much larger external chain's total hash power.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- A witness committee of size 3f+1, reachable over an asynchronous network by both the identity provider and every client, with the assumption that no more than f witnesses are simultaneously Byzantine in any given round; the composition/governance layer of the wider architecture must supply the process that selects and rotates this committee's membership without letting one operator control 2f+1 of it — `MALVAI-NDSS-23` does not itself specify this process.
- An identity or naming component that publishes username-to-key updates in discrete rounds (epochs) to a verifiable key directory instantiated as an ordered zero-knowledge set over a compressed Merkle Patricia Trie, so that a compact per-round digest exists for the witnesses to check — `MALVAI-NDSS-23`.
- Persistent storage at the identity provider with fast random writes at the scale of the benchmarked prototype (RocksDB or MySQL); the measured storage grows linearly with registered users (≈4.5 GB at 5 million users), not with epochs elapsed — `MALVAI-NDSS-23`.
- Durable private signing keys held by the identity provider and by each witness, with identity-provider key compromise explicitly out of scope: this mechanism detects equivocation given an honest witness majority, it does not prevent a compromised provider's data from looking internally consistent before compromise is discovered by some other means — `MALVAI-NDSS-23`.
- A residual per-user obligation the client software must schedule on its own: re-querying the user's own key history at least once between any tombstone epoch and the following deletion epoch, or a wrongful deletion of that specific user's binding goes undetected — `MALVAI-NDSS-23`.
- Bounded clock drift among witnesses sufficient for the paper's stated f-fault-tolerance argument; the paper references this assumption without a numeric bound, so the transport/time-synchronization component must supply a bound this corpus does not derive.
- A client-reachability path to a quorum of 2f+1 witnesses (not merely to the identity provider), so whatever transport and NAT-traversal mechanism the wider architecture selects must let ordinary clients reach that quorum, not only reach the provider.

### What it costs and where it fails

Verify notification 68.70 ms mean (1.50 ms std, single core, AWS t3.medium witness machine, 4-witness committee, notification batch of 1,024 updates at 64 bytes each, 10-run mean); create vote 0.02 ms mean; verify certificate 0.16 ms mean (certificate = 2f+1 = 3 votes for f=1 on a 4-witness committee); end-to-end maximum throughput ≈800 updates/s at latency under 3.5 s on a WAN testbed spanning 5 AWS regions with 10 honest witnesses and batch size 1,024; throughput is flat across 10, 20, and 50 witnesses and unaffected by 1 or 3 crashed witnesses out of 10 — all `MALVAI-NDSS-23`. VKD storage measured at ≈4.5 GB at 5 million users, growing linearly; a projection (not a direct measurement) of ≈850 GB at 1 billion users — `MALVAI-NDSS-23`.

It stops working, for the non-equivocation guarantee specifically, once fewer than 2f+1 of the 3f+1 witnesses are honest in a given round; the paper states the soundness proof is void with zero honest auditors and does not report a measured degradation curve between that point and full quorum, because the reported crash-fault benchmarks (0, 1, 3 crashes out of 10) all stayed within the tolerable range and no Byzantine-fault benchmark was run. It stops protecting a specific user's binding against wrongful deletion whenever that user does not re-query their own key history between a tombstone epoch and the following deletion epoch. It has no answer in the retrieved text for the "right to be forgotten" tension the paper itself names: the append-only base construction beneath compaction conflicts with a regulatory requirement for full account-data deletion.

### What the corpus does not settle

No numeric bound for the required clock-drift tolerance among witnesses; `MALVAI-NDSS-23` states the assumption exists without a stated value. No measured behavior under actively Byzantine (as opposed to crash-faulty) witnesses; the authors call this "still an open question" and report only crash-fault results. No description in this corpus of a witness-selection or witness-rotation process that itself resists capture by a single operator — this is exactly the composition question the architecture's "cannot be captured by any company" goal raises, and neither `MALVAI-NDSS-23` nor any other entry in this corpus answers it. The 850 GB storage figure at 1-billion-user scale is an extrapolation from a 5-million-user measurement, not a direct measurement. No entry in this corpus reports a direct, matched-configuration benchmark comparing Parakeet against Merkle²; `MALVAI-NDSS-23` names Merkle² only in its bibliography. Full-text evidence for the periodic blockchain-digest-posting family (EthIKS, Catena) was not retrieved anywhere in this corpus; every claim about that family's cost above is a secondhand characterization from a paper that cites it, not an independently checked figure, and a person choosing between that family and the selection here would need those two papers' own text before the comparison in this document could be treated as settled rather than provisional.


---

## Key recovery: A standing (t,n) threshold signing key held by user-chosen guardians, invoked only to co-sign a fresh identity key and never to reconstruct the lost one, is selected over raw secret reconstruction, unstructured guardian authorization, memorized-secret derivation, and hardware-held keys — none of which the corpus shows solving loss unassisted

Key recovery restores a person's ability to act under their existing network identity after the private key that identity depends on becomes unusable — lost, on a destroyed device, or forgotten — without any party capable of resetting it unilaterally, since no single party is allowed to control identity in this architecture. Two structurally different targets answer that need. A mechanism can regenerate the literal private key value that was lost, so the recovering device ends up holding the same bytes it held before — this is what Shamir reconstruction and password-derived keys both do. Or a mechanism can authorize a replacement key under the same identity without ever bringing the original value back into existence — this is what a threshold signature over a "rotate to this new key" statement does, and what an ordinary guardian vote does. The two targets carry different exposure properties, developed below; a person recovering access needs only the second, since what matters to every other party in the network is that the identity's current key changed with proper authorization, not that any particular bytes returned.

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Guardian quorum, unstructured: n guardians each hold their own independent signing key, registered in advance; t of them each sign an ordinary "approve rotation to key PK′" statement with their own key, and a verifier checks t valid signatures against the registered list | No evidence entry in this corpus measures this mechanism, quantitatively or at all — it composes from ordinary digital-signature verification, so any signature scheme's own cost applies, but no paper here benchmarks the guardian-authorization step itself | Unforgeability of whichever ordinary signature scheme the guardians use, plus t of the n registered guardians being honest at the moment of recovery | AMBATI-SACMAT-26 states key recovery is "arguably the most critical unsolved problem" of the field and states social-recovery quorums "introduce their own trust assumptions and coordination overhead" [`AMBATI-SACMAT-26`], without quantifying guardian availability or response time under any condition | A registry the verifier trusts for the current guardian list and threshold, since changing that list is itself an authorization event this mechanism does not itself define who may perform |
| Threshold secret sharing, raw reconstruction: Shamir (t,n) polynomial split of the private key itself; t shares combined by Lagrange interpolation recover the value | No implementation or benchmark; the paper is a construction-and-proof paper. Sizing guidance only: a 16-bit field modulus suffices for up to 64,000 shares [`SHAMIR-CACM-79`] | Information-theoretic: fewer than t shares "leaves D completely undetermined," no computational assumption at all [`SHAMIR-CACM-79`] | Whoever performs the t-share combination holds the original secret value in the clear at that moment — the paper's own words: "knowledge of any k or more Di pieces makes D easily computable" [`SHAMIR-CACM-79`] — and a dealer distributing shares at setup time is trusted with no way for recipients to check consistency, absent a verifiable-sharing extension [`SHAMIR-CACM-79`] | A confidential channel to deliver shares at setup and again at recovery, each carrying the share's index; a re-sharing protocol supplied externally if periodic rotation of shares is wanted, since the paper describes the technique but not who initiates or verifies it [`SHAMIR-CACM-79`] |
