| Threshold secret sharing, verifiable reconstruction: Pedersen VSS layers a discrete-log commitment onto Shamir sharing so each recipient checks its own share against a broadcast commitment without contacting anyone else | Fewer than 2·q modular multiplications per bit of the secret for distribution, about (2k+1)·q + (k−1) multiplications per participant for verification, all analytically derived from the protocol's operation count, not measured on hardware [`PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`] | Unconditional secrecy against fewer than t shares; only computational (discrete-log) security detects a cheating dealer — the paper proves no non-interactive scheme can make both properties unconditional at once [`PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`] | A dealer able to solve discrete log in the working group before the last share ships distributes inconsistent shares undetected — "the correctness of the shares depends on a computational assumption" [`PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`]; reconstruction still recombines to the raw secret via ordinary Lagrange interpolation, inheriting Shamir's exposure-at-combination property [`PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`] | An agreed group setup (primes p, q, generators g, h with unknown discrete-log relation) fixed before any share is distributed, by a trusted setup or a coin-flipping protocol among participants; a confidentiality-protected channel for private shares, separate from the public broadcast channel carrying commitments [`PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`] |
| Threshold secret sharing, signing without reconstruction — robust: threshold BLS (TGS), guardians' shares from a dealer-free Gennaro-Jarecki-Krawczyk-Rabin key generation each independently produce a signature share, combined by exponentiation, never recombined into the raw key | No wall-clock benchmark; every result is a formal proof or a structural round count. Signing needs no extra interaction beyond each guardian broadcasting its share: "does not require interaction or any zero-knowledge proofs" [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`] | Computational Diffie-Hellman hardness in a Gap Diffie-Hellman group, proof stated to hold "in the random oracle model only" [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`] | Tolerates up to t < n/2 actively malicious guardians and still produces a valid signature, stated as "an optimal result" for this class of scheme; at or beyond that fraction, both correctness and secrecy fail together [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`] | Every guardian's per-share public commitment (B_i = g^xi), generated once during key generation, kept persistently available to whichever party performs combination — not just to guardians at setup time [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`]; a broadcast channel plus secure point-to-point channels among all n guardians during key generation [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`] |
| Threshold secret sharing, signing without reconstruction — non-robust: FROST, guardians' shares from a modified Pedersen DKG each produce a Schnorr signature share in one round; a single bad or absent response aborts the whole operation | No wall-clock benchmark; the paper's own Rust implementation carries no timing figures in the text [`KOMLO-SAC-20`]. One signing round after a non-interactive preprocessing stage, versus a stated minimum of 4 rounds for the comparable robust Stinson-Strobl construction [`KOMLO-SAC-20`] | Discrete-log hardness plus the paper's own new one-more Gap Diffie-Hellman-family assumption for its interactive variant; three assumptions the paper states implementations must independently supply: message validation before signing, reliable message delivery, and authenticated attribution of a bad response [`KOMLO-SAC-20`] | "FROST trades off robustness for more efficient signing operations" [`KOMLO-SAC-20`] — a single misbehaving or unresponsive guardian aborts the operation, requiring an out-of-band retry that excludes that guardian rather than completing with the remainder | A commitment-publication point for preprocessed nonce pairs, trusted to serve correct unused values though not for confidentiality; an external authenticated channel for participant identification, since misbehavior detection depends on attributing a bad response to a specific guardian [`KOMLO-SAC-20`] |
| Deterministic derivation, unhardened: a raw hash of a memorized secret regenerates the key each time, no server, no stretching | An 8-character password from a 95-symbol alphabet costs under 1 USD in estimated 1-year cracking hardware under DES-crypt or plain MD5, at a 100 ms-per-guess interactive-login target — versus 18,000 USD for PBKDF2, 130,000 USD for bcrypt, 4.8 million USD for scrypt at the same target, all derived cost estimates from circa-2002, 130 nm circuit data, stated by the authors as "very approximate" [`PERCIVAL-BSDCAN-09`] | None beyond whatever entropy the memorized secret itself carries; average measured web-password entropy is cited elsewhere in this corpus's bibliography at roughly 42 bits [`PERCIVAL-BSDCAN-09`, citing Florencio and Herley] | An attacker with the public derivation function and unlimited offline time recovers any secret at cost bounded only by its entropy — no rate limit exists, since no online party is ever contacted | None — this is precisely its weakness: no other component observes or gates a guessing attempt |
| Deterministic derivation, memory-hard-hardened: scrypt or Argon2 stretches the memorized secret, forcing every guess to hold a large pseudorandom array in memory | scrypt raises the same 8-character-password estimated attack cost to 4.8 million USD (interactive, 64 ms) or 19 billion USD (file-encryption target, 3.8 s) — 35× and 4,000× the cost of bcrypt respectively at those two targets, same derived-cost caveat [`PERCIVAL-BSDCAN-09`]. Argon2 fills 1 GB of RAM in "a fraction of a second" and its best documented tradeoff attack (v1.3) reduces attacker time-area product by a factor of 3 for multi-pass Argon2i [`BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16`] | Sequential memory-hardness is a conjecture for scrypt, "appears very likely," not a proof [`PERCIVAL-BSDCAN-09`]; Argon2's block-distinctness proof depends on two unproven-in-this-document assumptions about its internal permutation [`BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16`] | A deployer following the IRTF's own selection procedure (maximize memory for a fixed latency budget) lands on Argon2i-B at one pass, exactly the parameter region an independent attack later showed reduces cost by 2× at 1 GB — more than 10 passes are needed for that specific attack to fail [`ALWEN-EUROSP-17`], a finding the Argon2 authors' own document had argued was impractical and which this later paper disputes directly [`BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16`, `ALWEN-EUROSP-17`] | Still requires no other component to gate an offline guess; hardening only raises the per-guess price, and provides no fallback if the one memorized secret is forgotten outright |
| Deterministic derivation, network-hardened: a partially oblivious pseudorandom function service (Pythia) evaluates the derivation on the client's behalf, so the server observes an account selector and can rate-limit, without learning the secret | 15 ms mean end-to-end latency in a LAN setting (cold connection, TLS included), 82–96 ms in a WAN setting (client at University of Wisconsin-Madison, server on AWS US-West, 82 ms ping RTT), 1,350 connections/second sustained throughput on one EC2 c4.xlarge instance, versus 2,200 cps for a static HTTPS page baseline [`EVERSPAUGH-USENIXSEC-15`] | Decisional bilinear Diffie-Hellman-family hardness in a pairing group, random-oracle-modeled hashes, and a new "one-more bilinear CDH" assumption the paper states is unproven in prior literature [`EVERSPAUGH-USENIXSEC-15`] | Depends entirely on the server learning the account selector (tweak) in the clear to rate-limit; any oblivious variant hiding it "does not allow granular rate limiting," so an attacker who can associate a tweak with a target user gains a lockout vector against that user [`EVERSPAUGH-USENIXSEC-15`] | A single service (or federation) that must stay reachable and honest at recovery time — a dependency this design otherwise structures its whole architecture to avoid concentrating in one operator |
| Deterministic derivation, threshold-server-hardened: password-protected secret sharing (PPSS) splits the secret across n servers; the user reconstructs it after one round trip to any t+1, using a verifiable oblivious PRF per server | No implementation or benchmark; every figure is a protocol-structure fact. Online-guessing advantage for an attacker making q total interactions against a dictionary of size \|D\|: q/\|D\| [`JARECKI-ASIACRYPT-14`] | The One-More Gap Diffie-Hellman assumption for the underlying oblivious PRF; correct reconstruction under up to t corrupted servers requires 2t+1 ≤ n, a bound the paper calls "an intrinsic limitation" not shared by its secrecy and soundness properties [`JARECKI-ASIACRYPT-14`] | Requires t+1 of n servers online and honest at recovery time — structurally the same guardian-availability dependency as a human quorum, with named server operators substituted for named people [`JARECKI-ASIACRYPT-14`] | Independent per-client keys at each server (the paper states adding client identity as an input to one shared key does not achieve the same guessing-count property); a common reference string agreed by all parties before any run [`JARECKI-ASIACRYPT-14`] |
| Hardware-held keys: key material generated and held inside a Secure Element, Trusted Platform Module, or hardware security module (HSM), never leaving the device in the clear | A BBS-ECDSA hybrid device-binding proof (a related but not identical operation — proof of possessing a device-bound key, not key recovery) measured at approximately 800 ms and 150 KB in one prototype, an external artifact this survey reports rather than measures itself [`BORMANN-SSR-25`] | Whatever tamper-resistance property the specific hardware element provides; current commercial phone Secure Elements support only the P-256 curve with ECDSA, not the pairing-friendly curves several credential schemes need [`BORMANN-SSR-25`] | Directly stated: hardware-backed key storage "protects keys at rest but does not solve backup if the device is lost or destroyed" [`AMBATI-SACMAT-26`] — the device itself is the single point of failure this mechanism is meant to protect, and its loss is exactly the event key recovery must survive | An external replacement channel supplied by one of the other candidates, since this mechanism supplies none of its own; where native pairing-curve support is unavailable, several national programs plan to route device-binding through an external cloud HSM instead, trading the loss risk for dependence on that external service's availability [`BORMANN-SSR-25`] |

### Selection

A standing (t,n) threshold BLS signing key (`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`'s TGS construction) is distributed once, at identity-creation time, among guardians the user chooses from their own existing contacts in the same network — not a dedicated service. Recovery consists of collecting t guardians' signature shares over one statement, "the current key for this identity is now PK′," and combining them into a single signature the rest of the network verifies against the one standing guardian-threshold public key. The original private key is never regenerated at any point in this flow.

Against unstructured guardian authorization, where each guardian instead signs the rotation statement with its own independent key: no evidence entry in this corpus supplies any formal security bound for that construction, since it is not a distinct cryptographic scheme but a policy layered over ordinary signature verification — whereas the threshold-signing selection carries a proven, cited bound (t < n/2, stated to be optimal for this class of scheme) [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`]. The unstructured form also requires every verifier in the network to track a guardian list that can itself change, and changing it is an authorization event the mechanism does not itself define who may perform; the threshold-signing selection instead presents one fixed public key to every verifier regardless of guardian-set changes behind it, since the underlying shares can be refreshed through the same proactive re-sharing technique both `SHAMIR-CACM-79` and `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03` state their constructions are compatible with. This preference for one stable externally-visible key is a structural consequence drawn from reading the two mechanisms' descriptions together, not a claim either paper makes about identity systems.

Against raw Shamir or Pedersen reconstruction of the original private key: `SHAMIR-CACM-79`'s own text states that whoever combines t shares has the secret "easily computable" at that moment — a single point where the full key exists again in the clear, safe only if the party performing combination is the legitimately recovering user's own device and nobody else. Threshold BLS signing avoids this exposure entirely: each guardian applies its share directly inside signature computation, and the combiner assembles only signature shares, values that reveal no more about the underlying key than any ordinary signature does [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`]. Pedersen VSS adds the ability for guardians to check their shares are consistent without contacting each other [`PEDERSEN-CRYPTO-91`], which the selection also needs during its own key-generation step, but Pedersen's reconstruction path still recombines to the raw secret through ordinary interpolation, inheriting the same combination-time exposure Shamir has.

Between the two signing-without-reconstruction instantiations, robust threshold BLS (`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`) is preferred over FROST (`KOMLO-SAC-20`) specifically for recovery, though FROST remains preferable for day-to-day signing where its single-round efficiency dominates and guardians are reliably online. Recovery is triggered by exactly the condition robustness defends against: some guardians unreachable or misbehaving. FROST's own paper states it "trades off robustness for more efficient signing operations" [`KOMLO-SAC-20`] and that a single bad or absent response aborts the entire operation, requiring an out-of-band retry that excludes that guardian — a cost better absorbed during routine signing, where a retry is cheap, than during a rare, high-stakes recovery event, where every guardian re-contacted is another chance for the recovery window itself to be observed or delayed. Threshold BLS instead detects and excludes a bad share within the same attempt, via the public per-share commitment check, and still produces a valid signature from the remaining t honest guardians [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`].

Against deterministic derivation from a memorized secret, in every hardness tier measured here: the unhardened form is defeated outright by realistic password entropy — an 8-character password costs under 1 USD in estimated attack hardware under a plain hash, at parameters PERCIVAL uses specifically to illustrate why stretching is needed [`PERCIVAL-BSDCAN-09`]. The memory-hard-hardened form raises that cost by orders of magnitude but remains an unlimited offline target with no rate limit, and the specific parameters a deployer would naturally choose following the Argon2 designers' own selection procedure sit inside the range an independent attack later showed halves the effective cost [`ALWEN-EUROSP-17`], disputing the designers' own prior claim that this attack class posed no practical threat [`BIRYUKOV-EUROSP-16`]. The network-hardened forms (Pythia, PPSS) close the rate-limiting gap with measured or proven bounds this corpus's guardian-signing papers do not supply — Pythia's 15 ms measured LAN latency [`EVERSPAUGH-USENIXSEC-15`] and PPSS's proven q/\|D\| guessing bound [`JARECKI-ASIACRYPT-14`] are stronger, better-quantified guarantees than anything cited for threshold BLS above — but both require t+1 (or one) always-online, independently operated servers reachable at the moment of recovery, structurally the same guardian-availability dependency the selection carries, with named service operators substituted for a person's own chosen contacts. Choosing guardians the user already has a relationship with over dedicated server operators is a value judgment made specifically to satisfy this architecture's stated requirement that no company be able to capture the network — a deployment without that requirement, or one that already depends on independently operated server infrastructure elsewhere, should weigh `JARECKI-ASIACRYPT-14` and `EVERSPAUGH-USENIXSEC-15`'s better-quantified bounds against this tradeoff instead. Finally, deterministic derivation in every variant shares one failure mode threshold sharing does not: if the one memorized secret is forgotten outright, no partial recovery exists, whereas losing contact with fewer than n − t guardians still permits recovery.

Against hardware-held keys: `AMBATI-SACMAT-26` states directly that hardware-backed storage "does not solve backup if the device is lost or destroyed," which is exactly the loss this component exists to survive — hardware-held keys are not a competing recovery mechanism at all, but a complementary protection for whichever key an operating device holds day to day. The selection's guardian shares protect the capacity to authorize a rotation, not the device's own operating key, so a Secure Element can and should still hold that operating key between recovery events [`BORMANN-SSR-25`].

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- The identity component supplies an onboarding flow, run while the user still controls their key, in which n guardians are chosen and a dealer-free Gennaro-Jarecki-Krawczyk-Rabin key generation runs among them over a channel providing both broadcast and secure point-to-point delivery [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`].
- The storage or content-location component hosts every guardian's per-share public commitment (B_i = g^xi) persistently and discoverably by identity, surviving the loss event itself, since these values cannot live only on the device that is later lost [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`].
- The key-transparency component accepts a valid t-of-n guardian-threshold signature over a "rotate to PK′" statement as authoritative proof of continuity for the identity, publishes the rotation, and treats it as equivalent to a signature from the identity's own prior key — a verification rule this component states the requirement for but does not itself implement or log.
- Guardian availability: at least t of the n guardians (or their refreshed shares, if proactive re-sharing per `SHAMIR-CACM-79` and `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`'s cited compatibility has run) reachable and willing to compute a signature share when recovery is needed — an unquantified dependency; see below.
- n and t are chosen jointly at setup with n > 2t, the bound `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03` states is optimal for a robust, non-interactive threshold scheme of this kind, trading the size of a corrupting-or-blocking minority against the size of a cooperating majority.
- A discrete-log or Gap-Diffie-Hellman hardness assumption in the chosen pairing group, and a random-oracle-modeled hash function, since `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`'s proof is stated to hold only under that model.
- No defense once t guardians actively cooperate against the user — an assumption inherent to every (t,n) threshold construction cited here, not specific to this instantiation.
- If proactive re-sharing is used to bound exposure from a guardian compromised long before recovery is ever needed, periodic synchronized share-refresh rounds among all n guardians must be scheduled and enforced by a component this construction describes but does not itself supply [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`, citing Herzberg-Jarecki-Krawczyk-Yung].

### What it costs and where it fails

No entry in this corpus measures wall-clock latency, bandwidth, or throughput for threshold BLS signing or key generation; both `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03` and `KOMLO-SAC-20` state they report proofs and round counts, not benchmarks. What is stated: signing itself needs no round beyond each guardian broadcasting one share, matching FROST's single-round efficiency while additionally detecting and excluding a bad share through the public commitment check [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`]. Robustness holds only while corrupted or unavailable guardians number fewer than n/2; at or beyond that fraction, correctness and secrecy fail together, a bound the paper states is the optimal achievable one for this class of scheme, so no construction cited anywhere in this corpus does better on this specific axis [`BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`]. The security proof holds only in the random-oracle model and rests on a computational hardness assumption, not an information-theoretic one — a strictly weaker guarantee than raw Shamir sharing's unconditional secrecy against fewer than t shares [`SHAMIR-CACM-79`], the price paid here for avoiding Shamir's combination-time exposure.

### What the corpus does not settle

1. No entry anywhere in this corpus measures real guardian availability or response time: how many of a person's chosen contacts remain reachable and willing to act within a bounded window (hours, days, weeks) after that person loses a device. `AMBATI-SACMAT-26` states this is the field's largest open problem in these words but supplies no figure. A builder needs an empirical study of this before n and t can be chosen with any evidence behind the choice, rather than as an unlabeled guess.
2. No wall-clock latency, bandwidth, or throughput benchmark exists for `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`'s TGS or `KOMLO-SAC-20`'s FROST specifically. A builder needs an actual implementation measurement, in the shape `EVERSPAUGH-USENIXSEC-15` supplies for Pythia, to size real recovery latency against a target user-tolerance window.
3. The Gennaro-Jarecki-Krawczyk-Rabin distributed key generation `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03`'s key generation reuses is cited, not independently retrieved and measured in this evidence file; its own round count and communication cost, needed to size the guardian-onboarding step, is not available from any entry here.
4. No entry evaluates how a relying party first learns and comes to trust the identity of a user's chosen guardians before any loss occurs — `AMBATI-SACMAT-26`'s identity-binding challenge covers the general problem but not this specific composition of it.
5. Neither `SHAMIR-CACM-79` nor `BOLDYREVA-PKC-03` states how long a proactive re-sharing period can run before the security margin it is meant to preserve is itself at risk; both state the technique exists without measuring or proving a safe interval.
6. No entry in this corpus directly compares guardian-threshold recovery against password-protected secret sharing on any shared metric — cost, success rate, or measured attacker advantage. The comparison drawn in this document's Selection section is reasoned from each mechanism's separately stated properties, not a head-to-head experimental result; a builder choosing between them on cost alone, rather than on the centralization tradeoff argued here, has no corpus entry to settle that choice.


---

## Naming: Private, per-zone delegation over self-certifying public keys is selected over synchronized naming ledgers and Domain Name System links, both of which measured deployments show collapsing into single-party control or into a name-scarcity market

Naming resolves a stable identifier to the specific key-holding party or content that identifier currently refers to, so a person or a piece of code can address a target across the target's own changes of location, hosting provider, or key rotation, using a string shorter and more durable than a raw network address. Every candidate below produces or resolves such an identifier by one of two structurally different means: binding the identifier directly to a cryptographic key, so identity and address are one fact verified without a third party, or binding a separately chosen readable string to that key through a third mechanism — a ledger, a per-user delegation graph, or the existing Domain Name System (DNS) — which then becomes the point at which the binding can be contested, captured, or fail.

### The three-property bound

Three separate papers in this corpus each independently state the same impossibility result in their own words, without citing a shared source paper the corpus holds as full text (Wilcox-O'Hearn's original statement of it exists only as an unretrieved archived page; see "What the corpus does not settle" below). The GNU Name System's (GNS) own design paper states it as the premise its design works around: "no name system can provide globally unique and memorable names and be secure" [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. The Ethereum Name Service (ENS) measurement paper states the same conclusion, applied to blockchain naming specifically, as its own finding: "DNS and the aforementioned solutions still cannot achieve human-readability, security and decentralization simultaneously" [`XIA-IMC-22`]. The W3C's Decentralized Identifiers (DID) specification states that mapping a human-friendly identifier to a DID "is outside the scope" of DID resolution and that "this trade-off follows Zooko's Triangle" [`W3C-DIDCORE-22`]. The Namecoin measurement paper states the claim a naming ledger makes against this bound: a blockchain-secured directory can supply "decentralization, security, and human-readable names at once (the three properties jointly conjectured impossible before Bitcoin, called Zooko's triangle)" [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`] — the one claim of the four that asserts an escape, and the one checked against its own paper's measured facts below.

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Self-certifying identifier: a public-key hash used directly as the name (SFS HostID; a DID's `did:plc` chain) | SFS reports no benchmark of its own; its client/server implementation is stated to "perform comparably to Sun's unencrypted NFS on standard application workloads," with no throughput or latency figure given [`MAZIERES-SIGOPSEW-98`]. `did:plc`'s identifier is a SHA-256 hash of the initial DID document, truncated to 120 bits [`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`]; no resolution-latency or throughput figure for `did:plc` itself is measured in this corpus. | Collision resistance of the chosen hash function alone; no external party mints or resolves the base identifier [`MAZIERES-SIGOPSEW-98`]. `did:plc`'s later versions are valid only if signed by the key authorized in the immediately preceding version [`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`]. | No in-protocol discovery mechanism exists for a name not already known: SFS states "no discovery mechanism for finding an unknown server's correct HostID other than symbolic links, digitally signed email, or other manual distribution" [`MAZIERES-SIGOPSEW-98`]. The `did:plc` directory server is "a single point that can omit valid DID document versions or fail to respond" [`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`]. | An out-of-band channel supplied by some other component for first contact [`MAZIERES-SIGOPSEW-98`]; a directory or transparency-log component for availability once other parties need to resolve a chain they were not present at the start of [`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`]. |
| Naming ledger — Namecoin (whole-namespace blockchain consensus) | At least 76% of ~120,000 registered `.bit` names classed squatted; 28 domains found with real, unique, non-error content [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`]. Registration availability by length: 1- and 2-character 100% taken, 3-character 58.61%, 4-character 1.00%, 5-character 0.02% [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`]. Requires a continuously synchronized chain copy to resolve any name; a name lapses after 36,000 blocks (~250 days) without renewal [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`]. | Consensus held by a majority of computational power, a conditional guarantee, not an unconditional cryptographic one; merge-mined hash rate approximately one-third of Bitcoin's at time of measurement [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`]. | An adversary "with more computational power than all other participants combined can construct an alternative valid timeline" [`WACHS-CANS-14`, characterizing Namecoin's consensus model]. | Continuous synchronized ledger access from every resolving party [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`]; an external renewal process, since the protocol enforces none [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`]. |
| Naming ledger — Handshake (root-zone-only auction-and-destroy chain, DNS-compatible) | Project-stated, not independently measured: 500-leaf Flat-File Merkle Tree (FFMT) insertion batches averaged 100–150 ms, a 44,000-leaf commit averaged 400–600 ms, on one Intel Core i7-7500U laptop [`HANDSHAKE-WP-18`]. | Proof-of-work majority honesty, plus continued absence of a proof-of-work hardware monopoly, which the whitepaper itself calls "an acceptable risk" with no quantified bound [`HANDSHAKE-WP-18`]. | The whitepaper's own Disclaimers section: "no guarantees are provided for functionality of the naming and auction system," including renewal, fees, or name value [`HANDSHAKE-WP-18`]. Every unclaimed legacy top-level domain (TLD) still resolves only via a hardcoded or live-queried ICANN root-zone source [`HANDSHAKE-WP-18`]. | A Simplified Payment Verification (SPV) resolver able to check FFMT inclusion/exclusion proofs; a Domain Name System Security Extensions (DNSSEC) proof chain rooted in IANA's root Key Signing Keys for legacy-TLD reclamation [`HANDSHAKE-WP-18`]. |
| Naming ledger — ENS (smart-contract registry on a general-purpose chain) | 465,827 registered names; 33,662 of them (7.2%) classed squatting by a two-heuristic count, held by 6,548 addresses, expanding to 279,193 further suspicious names under a guilt-by-association pass [`XIA-IMC-22`]. 16,017 expired names still carry resolvable records, because ENS does not clear records on expiry [`XIA-IMC-22`]. Top-10 addresses hold ~17% of all names ever registered [`XIA-IMC-22`]. | Correctness of Ethereum consensus plus correct operation of ENS's own registry, registrar, and resolver contracts; no identity vetting at registration [`XIA-IMC-22`]. | The paper's own conclusion: "DNS and the aforementioned solutions still cannot achieve human-readability, security and decentralization simultaneously" [`XIA-IMC-22`]. | An externally published plaintext-name dictionary, since ENS stores only a name's hash [`XIA-IMC-22`]; a client-side expiry check independent of the resolved record, since a lapsed name's records are not cleared [`XIA-IMC-22`]. |
| Private per-user name scopes — GNS (local zone delegation, petname-style) | No deployment measurement exists; the paper states usability experiments are future work [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. Stated design bounds, not measured under load: Distributed Hash Table (DHT) reply payload up to 63 kB; trusted computing base under about 125 entities per individual name, derived from DNS label-length limits carried into GNS names; revocation notice a few bytes [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. | Elliptic Curve Digital Signature Algorithm (ECDSA) over Curve25519 signature unforgeability per delegation link; each zone's own key, not any external party, authorizes changes to that zone's records [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. | No in-band mechanism exists for first-contact discovery of a zone one has not already been delegated into [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. No definite non-existence response is possible, so an absent record produces indefinite lookup latency instead of DNS's NXDOMAIN [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. A confirmation attack lets anyone who already holds a zone's public key and one candidate label confirm that record exists [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. | A DHT trusted for availability only, never integrity, since every record self-verifies [`WACHS-CANS-14`]; a peer-to-peer flooding substrate for revocation, gated by proof-of-work [`WACHS-CANS-14`]; a set-reconciliation mechanism to heal a partitioned peer's revocation-set difference in bandwidth proportional to that difference [`WACHS-CANS-14`, citing Eppstein's method; the general mechanism is separately evidenced by `EPPSTEIN-SIGCOMM-11` and its successor `MEYER-TR-24`]. |
| Domain Name System links — `did:web` / AT Protocol handle (DNS TXT or HTTPS well-known verification layered on a self-certifying DID) | Of 5,077,159 measured DID Documents, only 6 use `did:web`; the rest use `did:plc` [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. Of the small non-custodial minority, 98.7% verify via a DNS TXT record, 1.3% via an HTTPS well-known file [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. Of self-managed domains, 50% register through four registrars, one (Namecheap) holding 21% alone [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. Only 1.1% of all users manage their own key and domain; 98.9% of handles sit under the platform-operated custodial domain [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. | Trust in the domain's registrar, registry, DNS resolution path, and — for the HTTPS variant — the certificate authorities that authenticate its endpoint [`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`]. | Whoever controls DNS or the HTTPS endpoint for a domain controls that identity's human-readable binding; DNSSEC, the defense against DNS cache poisoning of this path, is stated as a "should," not implemented, in the one deployed system measured [`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`]. | Continuous availability of the ordinary DNS or CA infrastructure this architecture otherwise routes around; a verifier component to actively defend the binding against cache poisoning, which the measured deployment does not yet implement [`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`]. |

### Selection

Every human-readable label is bound to a self-certifying public key through private, per-zone delegation — GNS's mechanism [`WACHS-CANS-14`] — rather than through a synchronized naming ledger or a Domain Name System link. The raw self-certifying key remains the identifier that always resolves on its own; a label is an additional, non-authoritative convenience a zone owner layers on top through delegation, never a replacement for the key.

Against Namecoin: Namecoin's own founding claim, as this corpus's paper on it frames it, is an escape from the three-property bound through blockchain consensus [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`]. Checked against that same paper's own measured data, the claim survives only by redefining "secure" from an unconditional guarantee into one conditioned on holding a majority of a specific external resource — computational power, roughly one-third of Bitcoin's contemporaneous hash rate through merged mining [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`] — a condition absent from the original three-property statement. Even granting that redefinition, the same paper's data shows "human-readable" degenerating under the resulting competition for names: registration availability falls from 100% at one and two characters to 0.02% at five characters [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`], and at least 76% of registered names are held by squatters, leaving 28 domains, of roughly 120,000 registered, with real, distinct content [`KALODNER-WEIS-15`]. WACHS's own characterization of the same consensus mechanism, stated as fact rather than as a value judgment: an adversary "with more computational power than all other participants combined can construct an alternative valid timeline" [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. GNS's per-link security instead reduces to a single, fixed cryptographic hardness assumption (ECDSA over Curve25519) with no resource-majority condition attached [`WACHS-CANS-14`].

Against Handshake: Handshake secures only the root-zone (TLD) level and explicitly delegates everything below a TLD to conventional DNS operated by that TLD's owner [`HANDSHAKE-WP-18`], so even granting its auction-and-destroy scarcity mechanism, it hands the large majority of readable names — everything below a TLD — back to ordinary domain registration, the same single-registrar-per-name structure the selected mechanism avoids. Its performance figures are project-stated, not independently measured [`HANDSHAKE-WP-18`], so no independently verified cost comparison exists against GNS's stated design bounds. Its own text supplies no quantified bound on the proof-of-work hardware-monopoly risk it acknowledges [`HANDSHAKE-WP-18`], where the selected mechanism's per-link guarantee carries no comparable unquantified risk.

Against ENS: ENS's own measured deployment is the strongest evidence in this corpus that a naming ledger's readable-name property degrades in practice: 7.2% of registered names are squatting by the paper's own two-heuristic count (33,662 of 465,827), expanding to hundreds of thousands more under a guilt-by-association pass [`XIA-IMC-22`], and a name's records are not cleared on expiry, so a resolved binding can point at content its nominal owner no longer controls with no signal to the resolving client [`XIA-IMC-22`]. GNS's per-zone labels are not competed for in an open market and carry no possibility of expiry-linked record persistence, since each zone's owner alone controls that zone's records for as long as that owner's key is uncompromised [`WACHS-CANS-14`].

Against Domain Name System links: the one deployed measurement of this mechanism in the corpus shows it reproducing exactly the single-party control this architecture exists to avoid — 98.9% of measured handles sit under the platform operator's own custodial domain, and even among the 1.1% of users who manage their own domain, half register through four registrars [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. GNS's zone-delegation mechanism has no registrar or DNS-provider role at all: every zone owner alone signs their own records [`WACHS-CANS-14`], so it cannot reproduce this specific concentration mechanism, though (see below) whether it avoids concentration by a different mechanism — a small number of widely-delegated-into zones becoming de facto naming authorities as the delegation graph grows — is a question this corpus has no deployment data to answer.

Against bare self-certifying identifiers with no naming layer at all (SFS-style: the name is the raw hash, and any human-readable label stays purely local and unpublished): this achieves the same per-link cryptographic guarantee GNS achieves [`MAZIERES-SIGOPSEW-98`] but supplies no network-visible resolution of a human-chosen label — a readable identifier that is readable only to the one person who created the local symbolic link, and resolvable by no other party without a separate, manual, out-of-band exchange for every pairwise relationship [`MAZIERES-SIGOPSEW-98`]. This fails the design requirement that a client reproduce the functional patterns of the most-visited websites, where one user's chosen handle is visible to and resolvable by every other user without a private manual exchange per relationship. GNS is preferred specifically because it publishes labels onto a network-reachable DHT, encrypted per label so a party who does not already hold both the label and the zone's public key cannot enumerate it [`WACHS-CANS-14`], rather than leaving publication entirely outside the protocol as SFS does [`MAZIERES-SIGOPSEW-98`].

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- The identity or key-issuance component supplies one ECDSA-over-Curve25519 (or equivalent) key pair per zone a user or service operates; a zone's authority to publish or delegate records is entirely the possession of that zone's private key [`WACHS-CANS-14`].
- The content-location component's DHT accepts publication of encrypted, per-label record blocks and serves them on query, and every other component treats it as authoritative for availability only, never for integrity — each record self-verifies through its own signature and expiration field [`WACHS-CANS-14`].
- The transport or mesh layer supports flooding a small (few-byte) revocation notice to every neighbor, gated by a proof-of-work check the revoker performs once, not one every resolver repeats, to bound flooding abuse [`WACHS-CANS-14`].
- The synchronization and replicated-state component supplies a set-reconciliation mechanism bounding a rejoining or newly partitioned peer's bandwidth to the size of its revocation-set difference from its peers, not to the full revocation set; WACHS cites Eppstein's method for this, and this corpus separately evidences that mechanism family achieving bounded-round reconciliation [`EPPSTEIN-SIGCOMM-11`, `MEYER-TR-24`].
- Whatever component handles first contact between two identities that have never interacted — onboarding, search, or an explicit out-of-band introduction flow — supplies the first delegation for any zone a party has not yet been introduced to; the selected mechanism provides no in-band bootstrap of its own for an unfamiliar zone [`WACHS-CANS-14`], so without this supplied elsewhere, a name into that zone does not resolve at all.
- Any content-location or client-facing resolver consuming this mechanism tolerates indefinite latency, not a definite negative response, for a record that does not exist, because the query-privacy construction makes "not published yet" and "will never exist" indistinguishable to the querying client [`WACHS-CANS-14`]; a client-facing timeout policy must be supplied elsewhere, since the naming mechanism states none of its own.
- Any application layer resolving multi-label names budgets up to roughly 125 sequential zone lookups for the worst-case delegation chain the label-length bound permits, and up to 63 kB per reply payload [`WACHS-CANS-14`], though ordinary chains are shorter.

### What it costs and where it fails

No entry in this corpus measures latency, resolution success rate, or delegation-graph resilience for GNS, or for any private-per-user-scope naming mechanism, in actual deployment [`WACHS-CANS-14`] — a limitation the mechanism's own authors state directly, not one inferred here. The only quantified costs available are protocol-stated design bounds, not figures measured under load: DHT reply payload up to 63 kB, trusted computing base under about 125 entities per individual name (derived from DNS label-length limits carried into GNS names), and revocation notices a few bytes in size [`WACHS-CANS-14`].

It fails at three points its own authors state explicitly. First, first-contact resolution of a zone with no existing delegation is impossible in-band; a party must obtain an initial delegation through an out-of-band channel before any name into that zone resolves [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. Second, an absent record produces indefinite lookup latency rather than a definite negative answer, because the same encryption construction hiding which record is queried from an outside observer also prevents the client from distinguishing "not published yet" from "never will be" [`WACHS-CANS-14`]; the paper's own BOX record reduces, without eliminating, this cost for one specific case (grouping dependent records under a single lookup), not the general case [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. Third, a confirmation attack always succeeds: a party who already holds both a zone's public key and one candidate label can perform the identical query as any other resolver and confirm that record's existence [`WACHS-CANS-14`] — the mechanism defends against blind enumeration of a zone's labels, not against confirming an already-guessed pair.

Overall correctness of a multi-hop delegation chain depends on every zone along that chain having delegated correctly and honestly; compromise or social engineering of any single zone in a chain compromises resolution of every label reachable through it, and the paper's own authors state they cannot give a numeric estimate of this risk because "the system is not deployed yet," with no measurement of a real GNS delegation graph existing anywhere [`WACHS-CANS-14`].

### What the corpus does not settle

1. No entry measures GNS, or any private-per-user-scope naming mechanism, in actual deployment: latency, resolution success rate, and delegation-graph structure under real social-engineering pressure are all unmeasured, deferred by the paper's own authors to future work [`WACHS-CANS-14`]. The selection above rejects the ledger and DNS-link candidates on measured grounds, but does not itself rest on a positive deployment measurement of the selected mechanism; no entry elsewhere in this corpus fills that gap.
2. No entry measures how a new participant with zero existing delegations obtains a first one at social-network scale. The same cold-start problem appears identically for bare self-certifying naming [`MAZIERES-SIGOPSEW-98`] and for GNS [`WACHS-CANS-14`], and no paper in this corpus proposes or measures a solution.
3. Whether combining a naming ledger, used narrowly as an anti-squatting reservation mechanism for a small number of widely recognized root labels, with private per-user scopes for everything reachable through delegation from those roots, would recover a ledger's own measured squatting failure without inheriting its continuous-synchronization cost, is not evaluated by any paper in this corpus. This composition is suggested by reading the individual mechanism descriptions together; it is stated here as an open question, not a finding.
4. No entry evaluates GNS's DHT-key derivation (a scalar multiple of the zone's private key by a representation of the label, hashed to form the DHT lookup key) against an adaptive, query-choosing adversary. Whether an adversary who observes many queries into one zone gains any advantage over blind guessing at further labels in that zone is not measured or proved in the retrieved text.
5. No entry measures whether a delegation-graph structure resembling GNS's, layered under a client reproducing the functional patterns of the most-visited websites — where most users expect a discoverable, memorable handle from first use, not a private key they must distribute out of band — produces adoption or usability outcomes comparable to the default-custodial-domain pattern measured for the AT Protocol [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. This corpus contains deployment data for the DNS-link and ledger families and none for the selected family, so a like-for-like usability comparison cannot be made from the entries retrieved here.
6. Zooko Wilcox-O'Hearn's original statement of the three-property bound is cited by three papers in this corpus [`WACHS-CANS-14`, `KALODNER-WEIS-15`] but was never itself retrieved as full text: the only archived copy is served from an address this retrieval environment could not reach [retrieval-log.md, "Zooko's original page"]. Every formulation of the bound cited above is therefore a restatement by a citing paper's own authors, checked against that paper's own measured or stated facts, not a quotation of the original proof or its exact scope conditions.


---

## Content location: S/Kademlia's disjoint-path lookup and puzzle-derived identifiers close the eclipse an unmodified Kademlia deployment already suffered on a live network

A content-location mechanism lets a participant, holding only a key, determine which other participant currently holds the item that key names, using state and messages proportional to the size of the whole network, not the whole network itself. Every candidate below does this by giving each participant an identifier from the same space as the keys and forwarding a lookup, hop by hop, toward whichever known participant's identifier is closest to the target key under some distance function.

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kademlia, plain iterative XOR routing | Live IPFS deployment (256-bit SHA-256 keyspace, k=20, α=3): 99% of lookups under 18 hops, 1% tail to 100 hops [`CORTESGOICOECHEA-ARXIV-24`]; median record-to-peer lookup under 1 s, retrieval 50th/90th/95th-pct 2.90/4.34/4.74 s [`TRAUTWEIN-SIGCOMM-22`]; provider-record query averaging 6 s, maximum 1.5 h [`COSTA-DAIS-23`] | None — any node's self-reported identifier is accepted and inserted into a peer's routing table on an unsolicited "hello," with no cost to obtaining one [`STEINER-CCR-07`] | On the live, several-million-peer KAD network, as few as 8 attacker identities positioned around a keyword's hash made every search for that keyword terminate on an attacker node [`STEINER-CCR-07`]; a single node's provide() operation for 262,144 items took 10–14 minutes against a 12-second deadline because publication load concentrates on the ~250–300 longest-lived contacts in the seeder's own routing table [`CORTESGOICOECHEA-ARXIV-24`] | Needs an identifier-assignment source (the corpus's Kademlia entries assume a hash of a public key or IP address, not verified further); needs publication load spread across many originating nodes, since a single seeder's own routing table becomes the throughput bottleneck [`CORTESGOICOECHEA-ARXIV-24`] |
| S/Kademlia (Kademlia + disjoint-path routing-failure test + crypto-puzzle node-ID generation) | 99% lookup success at 20% adversarial nodes using disjoint paths; recommends d=4–8 disjoint paths, k=8–16 sibling/bucket size; adaptive k=2d performs worse than fixed k=16 because shorter routing tables lengthen paths (BRIEF §7, Baumgart & Mies, ICPADS 2007 — read in full but held in this corpus only as the compressed summary in BRIEF.md §7, not as a full evidence-file entry with stated experimental scale) | Obtaining a usable node identifier costs a static and a dynamic computational puzzle, so an attacker's Sybil-identity count is bounded by compute spent, not free as in plain Kademlia | No entry in this corpus measures S/Kademlia against a live deployment or against the specific 8-Sybil eclipse technique `STEINER-CCR-07` demonstrated against plain KAD; the 99%-success figure is the paper's own reported result, not independently reproduced here | Requires every node to solve and verify static and dynamic identifier puzzles before joining; requires d ≥ 2 independent lookup paths to be attempted per query, multiplying per-query bandwidth by d over single-path iterative lookup |
| R/Kademlia (recursive routing + Proximity Neighbor Selection/Proximity Routing) | Simulation (OverSim, 5,000 nodes, Weibull churn mean 10,000 s): recursive without topology adaptation ≈350 ms / 140 bytes/s per node vs. plain iterative-exhaustive Kademlia 2–5 s; recursive+PNS ≈225 ms but ≈4× the bandwidth of non-PNS variants [`HEEP-ATNAC-10`] | Same identity assumption as plain Kademlia — R/Kademlia adds no identity cost or adversary defense of its own | Recursive routing loses its latency advantage over iterative-with-PNS specifically in high-churn scenarios when active probing is disabled [`HEEP-ATNAC-10`]; Direct signaling mode risks NAT/PAT connection problems because the initiator and path nodes exchange messages without being established peers [`HEEP-ATNAC-10`] | Requires every node on a routing path to run R/Kademlia, not plain Kademlia, for its signaling modes to function; requires per-neighbor round-trip-time mean/variance tracking for hop-by-hop failure detection [`HEEP-ATNAC-10`] |
| Kadabra (bandit-based k-bucket peer selection) | Simulation only (2,048 nodes, synthetic and real-world city-latency topologies): 15–50% lower lookup latency than vanilla Kademlia, Proximity Neighbor Selection, and Proximity Routing baselines across every tested setting, including a 20%-adversarial-node scenario where Kadabra bypasses a concentrated delay region that Proximity Neighbor Selection does not [`ZHANG-FC-23`] | Depends entirely on an operator-set minimum-round-trip-time exploration threshold ρ; sampling replacement peers with no such floor converges toward pure proximity selection, which concentrates a bucket geographically and makes a Sybil-flooding attack near the victim cheap [`ZHANG-FC-23`] | The paper's own conclusion defers a "thorough analysis of Kadabra's robustness against attacks beyond Sybil, eclipse, and adversarial routing" to future work, and states no deployed-network test (IPFS, Swarm) has been run [`ZHANG-FC-23`] | Requires every node to record, per routed query, which peer it passed through and the response time, continuously; requires an externally supplied policy for setting ρ, which the paper does not derive [`ZHANG-FC-23`] |
| Chord (ring, fixed identifier-digit base b=1) | Deployed 10-site RON-testbed prototype: median lookup 180–285 ms at up to 180 node instances, matching a 5-message-exchange × 60 ms measured inter-site round-trip [`STOICA-SIGCOMM-01`]; under Bamboo's churn benchmark, MIT Chord used "slightly under 2,400 bytes/second/node" versus Bamboo's 750 [`RHEA-USENIXATC-04`] | None — node identifiers are unauthenticated SHA-1 hashes of IP address; the paper's own future-work section states checking this "makes [identifier-placement] attack harder" without eliminating it [`STOICA-SIGCOMM-01`] | Under the same routing-success model applied to Pastry and CAN, Chord's fixed b=1 base reaches only 42% success at 20% malicious nodes and N=100,000, the worst of the compared geometries [`CASTRO-OSDI-02`]; the paper states Chord "currently has no specific mechanism to heal partitioned rings" [`STOICA-SIGCOMM-01`] | Requires continuous background stabilization (30 s average interval in the deployed prototype) for successor-pointer correctness; requires the application layer to supply its own value replication, since Chord routes but does not store [`STOICA-SIGCOMM-01`] |
| Pastry (prefix routing + Proximity Neighbor Selection) | Emulated single-JVM network: average hops match the log_(2^b) N prediction from 1,000–100,000 nodes; routing throughput over 3,000 messages/s/node on a 4×500 MHz Alpha server; average repair cost 57 RPCs per failed node at 10% simultaneous failure [`ROWSTRON-MIDDLEWARE-01`] | Node identifiers assumed close to uniformly distributed (typically SHA-1 of IP or public key); the paper states extending join/failure repair to tolerate misbehaving nodes is "beyond the scope of this paper" [`ROWSTRON-MIDDLEWARE-01`] | Its own density-estimation heuristic for locating the nearest of k=5 replicas misses the true nearest node 24% of the time even under "perfect estimation" [`ROWSTRON-MIDDLEWARE-01`]; under the added certified-identifier + constrained-routing-table + redundant-routing defense, success only exceeds 99.9% for f ≤ 0.18–0.25, and this apparatus was "fully evaluated only in the context of Pastry" [`CASTRO-OSDI-02`] | The secure variant requires an external certification-authority infrastructure distributing public keys out of band, plus certificate reissuance whenever a node's IP address changes [`CASTRO-OSDI-02`]; the base protocol requires an application-supplied network-proximity metric [`ROWSTRON-MIDDLEWARE-01`] |
| Tapestry (Plaxton-mesh prefix routing) | 400-node PlanetLab deployment: median routing-to-node Relative Delay Penalty (overlay latency ÷ direct-ping latency) approaches 1 as network distance grows; median routing-to-object Relative Delay Penalty stays under 2 in the wide area [`ZHAO-JSAC-04`] | Sybil resistance for the node-identifier space is stated to require an external trusted public-key infrastructure; Tapestry's own routing and repair mechanisms provide no defense on their own [`ZHAO-JSAC-04`] | On the real, faulty PlanetLab deployment, roughly 5% of queries failed overall, against near-100% success in the controlled SOSS simulation under matching fault conditions — a live-vs-simulator gap the authors attribute to real background load and failures, not the protocol design [`ZHAO-JSAC-04`] | Requires periodic soft-state republishing of both routing-table entries and object-location pointers, so an object owner that stops republishing loses findability through no fault of the overlay; requires an out-of-band network-distance metric for its locality optimization [`ZHAO-JSAC-04`] |
| Bamboo (Pastry-geometry overlay isolating churn-handling design choices) | Emulated 1,000-node, 10,000-node-topology network: FreePastry fails a majority of lookups under heavy churn; MIT Chord completes almost all lookups but with mean latency growing sharply with churn; Bamboo with Proximity Neighbor Selection shows the smallest latency growth of the three, at "slightly under 750 bytes/second/node" [`RHEA-USENIXATC-04`] | None studied — the paper explicitly does not address security or anonymity, stating their relationship to churn-handling is "unclear" [`RHEA-USENIXATC-04`] | A fixed 5-second lookup timeout pulls mean latency to more than double every other tested timeout scheme, even under light churn; reactive failure-recovery alone (no periodic supplement) enters a positive-feedback congestion loop under high churn [`RHEA-USENIXATC-04`] | Global proximity-neighbor sampling requires that a routing-table slot be filled quickly with any node matching the required identifier prefix, deferring latency-based replacement to a lower-priority background step — a design that instead blocks table population on finding a low-latency neighbor forfeits the O(log N)-hop guarantee under churn [`RHEA-USENIXATC-04`] |
| CAN (coordinate-space routing) | Simulation up to 262,144 nodes: path length falls from 198.0 hops (2-dimensional, 4 neighbors/node) to 5.0 hops (10-dimensional, ~30 neighbors/node); at 10 dimensions with all optimizations, path latency drops from 23,008 ms to 135 ms against a ~116 ms direct-IP baseline [`RATNASAMY-SIGCOMM-01`] | None — no identifier-certification or adversary-resistant variant is described | The paper's own authors call designing a denial-of-service-resistant CAN "a particularly hard problem," stating "a malicious node can act, not only as a malicious client, but also as a malicious server or router" [`RATNASAMY-SIGCOMM-01`]; at equal connection count, CAN routes in more hops than Chord, Pastry, or Symphony at the same network size [`MANKU-USITS-03`] | Requires a bootstrap mechanism supplying an existing node's address; requires periodic soft-state neighbor updates as the sole failure-detection signal; multi-reality or multi-hash-function replication multiplies per-node storage and query traffic by the replication factor [`RATNASAMY-SIGCOMM-01`] |
| Koorde (constant-degree de Bruijn-graph routing) | No measurement of any kind — proven asymptotic bounds only, no implementation or simulation run reported [`KAASHOEK-IPTPS-03`] | None studied — the authors state they "mostly ignore" resilience against malicious, as opposed to independently failing, nodes | Proven, not merely observed: any network in which nodes fail independently with probability 1/2 requires some nodes to have degree Ω(log n) to stay connected with constant probability, so base (degree-2 or degree-k for fixed k) Koorde "cannot be fault tolerant against nodes failing with constant probability"; fault tolerance requires moving to a degree-O(log n) variant, which forfeits the constant-degree property [`KAASHOEK-IPTPS-03`] | The degree-O(log n) fault-tolerant variant requires every node to locally estimate total network size n; the paper states it does not know whether Koorde can self-stabilize the way Chord does [`KAASHOEK-IPTPS-03`] |
| Viceroy (constant-degree butterfly-network emulation) | No measurement of any kind — proven worst-case and expected-case bounds only [`MALKHI-PODC-02`] | None studied | The model explicitly excludes concurrent joins/leaves and any node failure: "we assume that multiple join and leave operations do not overlap, and servers never fail" [`MALKHI-PODC-02`]; the paper states it "does not address the issue of hotspots" | Requires non-overlapping join/leave events (an external serialization discipline) for its proved O(1)-expected-link-change bound to hold; requires a separate bootstrap mechanism, unspecified [`MALKHI-PODC-02`] |
| Symphony (small-world routing, tunable k long-distance links per node) | Simulation up to 100,000 nodes: k=4 bidirectional links with 1-Lookahead give 7.6 hops at 32,768 nodes; dynamic 100,000-node network (24-hour churn ramp) keeps average latency under 5 hops throughout [`MANKU-USITS-03`] | None — every experiment "assumes cooperative, non-adversarial nodes"; no mechanism limits identities per physical node or defends the long-link-selection procedure against a false network-size report [`MANKU-USITS-03`] | Removing short (ring) links isolates nodes and causes lookup failure, while removing long-distance links only slowly raises latency, so availability rests entirely on ring-successor integrity, not on the long links that give Symphony its speed [`MANKU-USITS-03`] | Requires an honest random-identifier assignment mechanism (none specified); requires each node to estimate current network size via an Estimation Protocol contacting s=3 existing nodes before drawing long-link targets [`MANKU-USITS-03`] |
| One-hop / two-hop full-membership routing (Gupta, Liskov, Rodrigues) | Analytic + p2psim simulation (2,000–20,000 nodes): one-hop steady-state failure rate ≈0.2%, 99.99% of queries satisfied within two or fewer hops; per-node upstream bandwidth 3.84–350 kbps depending on role, at n=100,000 [`GUPTA-NSDI-04`] | None — no identity cost, no adversary model; the scheme "requires significantly larger bandwidth than" the proven Liben-Nowell et al. maintenance-bandwidth lower bound, in exchange for latency, by the paper's own statement [`GUPTA-NSDI-04`] | After a mass-crash event (45% of 2,000 nodes), the system fails to stabilize below 2% query failure for the full 50,000 s simulation window when the background query rate is only 1 per 10 s, because stale routing entries are corrected only by queries that happen to hit them [`GUPTA-NSDI-04`] | Requires an explicit "supernode"-designation mechanism for slice/unit leaders, whose selection cost and accuracy the paper does not itself specify or measure; requires sustained background query volume to bound post-failure recovery time [`GUPTA-NSDI-04`] |
| Kelips (gossip-maintained affinity groups) | No evidence entry retrieved in this corpus. Referenced only as a competing system inside `GUPTA-NSDI-04`'s Related Work, which states Kelips' O(√n·log³n) convergence time exceeds an hour at 10⁵–10⁶ nodes, "causing a large fraction of routing entries to go stale" — an attributed characterization from a competing paper, not independently verified here | Not established here | Not established here (only the convergence-time critique above) | Not established here |
| Whanau (random-walk sampling over a social graph, layered identifiers) | Live social-graph datasets (Flickr 1.6M nodes/15.5M edges, LiveJournal, YouTube, DBLP): median LOOKUP 2 messages at 20,000 attack edges, 20 messages at 2,000,000 attack edges, at 10,000 table entries per social-graph edge; 4,000-virtual-node PlanetLab test under induced churn holds median lookup latency near one network round trip [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-NSDI-10`] | Lookup correctness depends only on the count of edges linking honest users to attacker-controlled identities, not on how many identities the attacker creates, provided the honest region of the graph is a fast-mixing expander [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-NSDI-10`] | Supplies availability, not integrity — an adversary can insert or overwrite records for a queried key and Whanau returns them alongside honest values [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-NSDI-10`]; can be broken by social engineering that persuades honest users to link to attacker-controlled identities, by the authors' own statement | Requires a pre-existing social graph of honest, mostly-symmetric trust edges with no internal sparse cut; requires a synchronized, network-wide periodic re-execution of the SETUP phase to track churn, not an incremental per-node update; requires a separate signature scheme elsewhere in the system for record integrity [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-NSDI-10`] |
| Sybil-proof one-hop DHT (earlier, single-author version of the same mechanism) | Orkut social graph, 7,335 nodes/56,211 edges, simulation of the simpler broadcast sub-protocol only: 15.8% lookup failure at 12,000 attack edges against 43,930 honest edges [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-SNS-08`] | Same fast-mixing-social-graph assumption as Whanau; the paper states this is "a simulation proof-of-concept," and the O(1)-message one-hop routing table itself was never simulated, only the slower broadcast variant [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-SNS-08`] | The strict guarantee holds only below g = o(n/log n) attack edges (≈750 for this graph); the paper states multi-hop routing is not solved, since after O(log n) hops "almost all routes become tainted" [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-SNS-08`] | Requires a social graph in which every honest node already knows its immediate friends' public keys through an out-of-band channel [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-SNS-08`] |
| Delegated lookup with client-side verification | No evidence entry exists in this corpus for any scheme under this description. No paper retrieved evaluates a design in which an untrusted third party performs the lookup and the requester verifies the result cryptographically, as a distinct content-location mechanism | Not established here | Not established here | Not established here |

### Selection

S/Kademlia — Kademlia's XOR-metric routing-table structure and bucket-based state, with disjoint-path lookup and crypto-puzzle-derived node identifiers added — is selected.

Against plain Kademlia: plain Kademlia has the strongest live-deployment evidence in the entire corpus for this component — hundreds of thousands of nodes, multi-year IPFS measurement showing sub-second median lookups and 99% of lookups completing under 18 hops [`CORTESGOICOECHEA-ARXIV-24`, `TRAUTWEIN-SIGCOMM-22`] — but that same live-deployment evidence, on a different Kademlia network (KAD), shows plain Kademlia's identity assumption fails outright: an attacker holding as few as 8 identities eclipsed a keyword completely on a several-million-peer live network, because any node's self-reported identifier is accepted without cost [`STEINER-CCR-07`]. S/Kademlia is preferred because it keeps Kademlia's proven-at-scale routing-table structure while adding exactly the missing cost (a computational puzzle per identifier) and exactly the missing redundancy (multiple disjoint lookup paths) that the demonstrated attack exploited.

Against Chord: at 20% malicious nodes under the same routing-success model, fixed-base Chord reaches only 42% success, the worst-performing geometry the model was applied to, because Chord's larger hop count under a small digit base gives an adversary more chances per lookup to sit on the path [`CASTRO-OSDI-02`]. Chord's own paper documents no defense beyond hashing identifiers from IP addresses, calling this only a hardening, not a fix [`STOICA-SIGCOMM-01`]. S/Kademlia's disjoint-path test and puzzle cost address the same identifier-forgery weakness structurally rather than incidentally.

Against Pastry with the Castro et al. secure-routing extension: the extension reaches over 99.9% routing success for f ≤ 0.18–0.25, better than S/Kademlia's stated 20%-adversary figure in raw success rate, but only by adding an external certification-authority infrastructure that issues and revokes per-node certificates bound to IP address — the paper's own words state this evaluation was performed "only in the context of Pastry," and the CA-based identity assumption reintroduces exactly the single-point-of-trust the rest of this architecture is designed to avoid [`CASTRO-OSDI-02`]. S/Kademlia's puzzle-based identity cost achieves comparable order-of-magnitude adversary resistance (99% at 20% adversarial, versus Pastry's 99.9% at 18–25%) without a certificate authority.

Against Tapestry: the same external-PKI identity dependency appears, stated directly by Tapestry's own authors as the mechanism's Sybil defense [`ZHAO-JSAC-04`]. Tapestry additionally shows the sharpest documented live-versus-simulator gap in this corpus — near-100% success in simulation against roughly 5% real-world query failure on PlanetLab under matching fault conditions [`ZHAO-JSAC-04`] — which argues for weighting live-network Kademlia figures (already available for the selected mechanism's base structure) over simulator-only figures wherever both exist.

Against CAN: CAN's own authors call adversary resistance "a particularly hard problem" and describe no defense at all, stating a malicious node "can act, not only as a malicious client, but also as a malicious server or router" [`RATNASAMY-SIGCOMM-01`]. At matched per-node connection counts, CAN also routes in more hops than Kademlia-family and ring/prefix designs [`MANKU-USITS-03`].

Against constant-degree designs (Koorde, Viceroy): Koorde's own proof rules the whole family out for this architecture, independent of any specific implementation — any network with maximum node degree below Ω(log n) cannot stay connected with constant probability once nodes fail independently at probability 1/2, so a true constant-degree design "cannot be fault tolerant against nodes failing with constant probability" [`KAASHOEK-IPTPS-03`]. Viceroy's own model excludes node failure and concurrent joins/leaves entirely by assumption [`MALKHI-PODC-02`]. Neither paper reports any implementation or simulation measurement, only proved bounds [`KAASHOEK-IPTPS-03`, `MALKHI-PODC-02`], so this exclusion rests on a structural proof, not a comparative cost figure.

Against Symphony: Symphony's own evaluation explicitly assumes cooperative, non-adversarial nodes throughout and supplies no identity-cost or adversary-resistance mechanism [`MANKU-USITS-03`], leaving the same identifier-forgery vulnerability S/Kademlia was built to close.

Against one-hop/two-hop full-membership routing: the Gupta-Liskov-Rodrigues design achieves lower hop count (99.99% of queries in ≤2 hops) [`GUPTA-NSDI-04`] but supplies no identity cost or adversary model anywhere in the paper, and its recovery from failure depends on background query volume happening to hit stale entries — a mechanism that stalls at low query rates for the full simulated window [`GUPTA-NSDI-04`]. It also concentrates load on an unspecified "supernode" selection mechanism, an availability and capture risk the paper does not analyze [`GUPTA-NSDI-04`]. Kelips, the gossip-affinity-group alternative in the same family, was never retrieved as a primary source in this corpus; the only figure available is a competing-paper's characterization of its convergence time exceeding an hour at 10⁵–10⁶ nodes [`GUPTA-NSDI-04`], not evidence this synthesis can rely on directly.

Against Whanau / the Sybil-proof one-hop DHT: this family has the strongest Sybil-resistance evidence in the corpus — correctness proved to depend only on attack-edge count, not identity count, validated against real multi-million-edge social graphs [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-NSDI-10`] — and is preferable to S/Kademlia wherever a fast-mixing honest social graph already exists as a byproduct of identity in the target deployment. It is not selected as the general-purpose mechanism because that graph is an external precondition this content-location layer cannot itself supply, SETUP is a synchronized, network-wide rebuild rather than an incremental per-node operation [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-NSDI-10`], and the mechanism supplies no result integrity of its own [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-NSDI-10`]. Where the rest of this architecture's identity layer does produce such a graph, Whanau is the better-supported choice for that deployment; this is a candidate for a follow-up composition check against whatever identity mechanism is selected elsewhere, not a rejection on cost grounds.

Against delegated lookup with client-side verification: no entry in this corpus evaluates this mechanism. Selecting it would not be a selection under this document's own rule — it is a guess, and this document does not make it. It is retained in the candidates table, marked as having no evidence, because the architecture may still need it as an open problem for a future retrieval pass, particularly for constrained clients unable to hold routing-table state at all.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- An identity or key-issuance mechanism elsewhere in the system must produce the public key each node's identifier is derived from; S/Kademlia's added cost is a computational puzzle solved once per identifier, not a substitute for that key's own provenance.
- Every lookup issues d ≥ 2 independent, disjoint paths simultaneously (S/Kademlia recommends d=4–8), so any bandwidth budgeting elsewhere in the system for the content-location layer must multiply single-path Kademlia's bandwidth by d, not assume single-path cost.
- Bulk content publication (a single node calling provide() for a large batch of items, as Ethereum's Data Availability Sampling design would) must be spread across many originating nodes or amortized over more than one round, because a single seeder's own routing-table contacts become the throughput bottleneck regardless of the disjoint-path defense, which addresses lookup security, not provide() throughput [`CORTESGOICOECHEA-ARXIV-24`].
- The storage or replication component must supply its own value durability and integrity; content location resolves "who holds this key" and returns whatever that holder currently serves, and provides no guarantee the value is authentic or current beyond what a self-certifying object identifier (a content hash or signature checked by the caller) supplies. Kademlia's own evidence entries in this corpus never claim otherwise, and Whanau's entry states this limitation explicitly for the same DHT family [`LESNIEWSKI-LAAS-NSDI-10`].
- A churn-recovery discipline must be selected alongside routing-table structure: the strongest churn evidence in the corpus (Bamboo versus FreePastry versus Chord) argues for reactive per-neighbor failure recovery combined with TCP-style or Vivaldi-coordinate round-trip-time timeout estimation, not a fixed timeout or periodic-only recovery [`RHEA-USENIXATC-04`] — measured on Pastry's geometry, not Kademlia's, so this is a transferred requirement, not a directly measured one for the selected mechanism.
- Any deployment expecting to publish or retrieve DankSharding-scale item counts (hundreds of thousands of keys per publishing round) within a fixed per-round deadline must either shard publication across many nodes or accept that single-node Kademlia-family provide() cannot meet a 12-second-class deadline at that scale [`CORTESGOICOECHEA-ARXIV-24`].

### What it costs and where it fails

Base routing cost, inherited from Kademlia and confirmed at IPFS's live scale: median lookup under 1 second, 99% of lookups under 18 hops, at hundreds of thousands of active nodes with a 256-bit keyspace and k=20 bucket size [`CORTESGOICOECHEA-ARXIV-24`, `TRAUTWEIN-SIGCOMM-22`]. S/Kademlia's disjoint-path addition multiplies per-lookup bandwidth by d (4–8 recommended), a cost this corpus does not hold measured together with IPFS-scale conditions — the 99%-at-20%-adversarial figure comes from Baumgart & Mies' own paper, held here only as BRIEF §7's compressed summary, not as a full evidence-file entry stating node count, topology, or churn model.

It fails, by the security model it targets, once the fraction of colluding malicious nodes exceeds what its recommended d and k parameters were tuned for; adaptive k=2d is documented as worse than a fixed k=16, because shorter routing tables lengthen the paths an adversary can intercept (BRIEF §7). It fails independently, by the corpus's live-network evidence, if node-identifier issuance is not actually gated by the puzzle — plain Kademlia's demonstrated 8-identity eclipse on KAD [`STEINER-CCR-07`] is precisely the failure mode the puzzle exists to close, so any implementation gap in enforcing puzzle verification reopens that attack. It fails on publication throughput, independent of the security mechanism entirely, once a single node must announce more items per round than its routing-table contacts can absorb within the round's deadline [`CORTESGOICOECHEA-ARXIV-24`].

### What the corpus does not settle

- No entry in this corpus reports S/Kademlia's node count, topology, churn model, or run count — the 99%-at-20%-adversarial figure and the d/k recommendations are held only as BRIEF.md §7's compressed summary of a paper marked "read in full" outside this evidence-collection pass. A builder needs the primary paper's actual experimental conditions, not this summary, before treating the 99% figure as load-bearing.
- No entry measures S/Kademlia, or any Kademlia secure variant, against a live deployment; every live-network figure in this document (`CORTESGOICOECHEA-ARXIV-24`, `TRAUTWEIN-SIGCOMM-22`, `COSTA-DAIS-23`, `STEINER-CCR-07`) describes plain, undefended Kademlia deployments (IPFS or KAD). Whether disjoint-path lookup and puzzle-gated identifiers preserve IPFS-scale latency at IPFS scale, rather than only at the smaller scales simulated in the secure-Kademlia literature, is unmeasured here.
- No entry in this corpus combines S/Kademlia's identity-cost and disjoint-path mechanism with R/Kademlia's recursive-routing mode or with Kadabra's adaptive bucket selection. Each is measured only against plain Kademlia or against each other's baselines [`HEEP-ATNAC-10`, `ZHANG-FC-23`]; whether Kadabra's exploration threshold ρ and S/Kademlia's disjoint-path count d can be tuned jointly without one undermining the other is not established anywhere in this corpus.
- No entry measures eclipse-attack cost against IPFS's actual live Kademlia deployment specifically — `STEINER-CCR-07`'s 8-identity figure is from KAD (eMule/aMule), a different deployment of the same protocol family with a different bucket size, key length, and transport. Whether IPFS's larger k=20 buckets and 256-bit keyspace raise or lower the attacker-identity count needed for the same eclipse is not measured in this corpus.
- No entry establishes a derivation for the value of d (disjoint-path count) or k (bucket size) as a function of an assumed adversary fraction for this architecture's own expected node population and churn rate; BRIEF §7's d=4–8, k=8–16 are the original paper's own recommendations, not values derived here from this system's parameters, and must be labeled a pick, not a derivation, if carried forward.
- No entry in this corpus evaluates delegated lookup with client-side verification as a distinct mechanism. This is a genuine gap, not merely an unretrieved paper the escalation ladder in BRIEF.md §3.1 would resolve — a further retrieval pass targeting light-client and SPV-style (simplified-payment-verification-style) lookup designs is needed before this candidate can be compared on evidence rather than left blank.


---

## Indexing and search: a client-local index answers first, and a probably-approximately-correct random-peer-sample query is the network fallback — the only fallback candidate with an analytically demonstrated path under the standing per-query bound, at web scale, and only under three preconditions this component does not itself supply

A search mechanism lets a person locate content by the words it contains, not by an identifier they must already hold. The standing per-query cost bound for this problem is Li, Loo, Hellerstein, Kaashoek, Karger, and Morris's 1-megabyte budget: 100-gigabit 1999 U.S. Internet backbone bisection bandwidth, 10% of it allocated to search, 1,000 queries per second (`LI-IPTPS-03`). Against that budget, `LI-IPTPS-03` measures naive document-partitioned query broadcast at 6 megabytes per query (6x over) and naive term-partitioned distributed-hash-table (DHT) posting-list intersection at 530 megabytes per query (530x over), both at a 3-billion-document target. No entry in this corpus reports a lower cost under matching conditions — a full-text, ranked, multi-term query, keyword-partitioned, at a multi-billion-document collection.
