Every lower figure in the corpus changes the document count, the network architecture, or the query semantics, usually more than one at once; this is stated directly, entry by entry, in `registry/conflicts/search.md` section 1 and `registry/open-problems/web-scale-search.md`, and nothing found while building this selection overturns it. The nearest paper is Asthana, Fu, and Cox (`ASTHANA-ICTIR-11`), which reports 1.0-3.6 megabytes per query at a larger, 10-billion-document collection — below Li's own bound — by replacing exact posting-list intersection with probabilistic random-peer sampling, an entirely different mechanism addressed as its own candidate below.

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Local index answers first, network query only on a miss | No entry in this corpus measures this pattern for keyword search. The nearest measured analog is content-by-hash retrieval: a requester checks its already-connected neighbors over Bitswap (a 1-second opportunistic timeout) before starting a two-hop Kademlia DHT walk (`TRAUTWEIN-SIGCOMM-22`). | None beyond trusting the node's own prior storage. | Any query for content the node has never fetched, authored, or been shown always misses locally, so this mechanism alone answers zero such queries; no figure in this corpus states what fraction of real queries a local index would answer. | A client-local record of everything the node has fetched, authored, or displayed, from the application-data-model component; a network fallback mechanism, since a local-only index cannot be the whole answer. |
| Document-partitioned distributed index (query broadcast to every peer holding a document shard) | 6 megabytes per query, 6x over the 1-megabyte budget, at 60,000 peers holding a 3-billion-document index at 1 gigabyte each, one 100-byte packet broadcast to every peer (`LI-IPTPS-03`). | Honest, static peer population; the paper evaluates no adversarial peers and no churn (`LI-IPTPS-03`). | Fixed at 6x over budget with no compression sequence applied to it anywhere in the source paper — `LI-IPTPS-03`'s entire optimization chapter (caching, precomputed intersections, Bloom-filter exchange, gap compression, semantic clustering) targets the term-partitioned baseline below, not this one (`registry/conflicts/search.md` section 1). | A shard-assignment mechanism the paper does not specify; every peer to hold a complete local index over its own shard. |
| Term-partitioned distributed index (DHT-routed posting-list intersection) | 530 megabytes per query average, 530x over budget; a specific two-term query costs 4 gigabytes, 4,000x over (`LI-IPTPS-03`). Best combined optimization (caching, precomputed intersection, Bloom-filter exchange, gap compression, adaptive intersection, semantic clustering) reaches a 75x reduction, still about 7x over budget (`LI-IPTPS-03`). | Same honest, static peer population as above. | The paper's own conclusion: even its full, "rather complex," un-implemented-at-scale optimization stack leaves the average query an order of magnitude over budget; closing the remainder needs either dropping term-proximity ranking (the incremental-ranked-intersection compromise) or an unmeasured 10x replication of the full index once per Internet service provider (`LI-IPTPS-03`). | A DHT mapping each term to the peer holding its posting list; a periodic, network-wide-coordinated document-identifier renumbering for gap compression and clustering to work; corpus-wide term-occurrence visibility for the semantic-clustering step, not just a local shard's own statistics. |
| Semantic overlay network (route each query only to the peers whose content a shared classifier or embedding judges similar) | Crespo-Garcia-Molina's Semantic Overlay Networks (SON): 375% fewer messages than query broadcast at 50% recall, 93% average maximum recall, 1,800-node Napster crawl (`CRESPO-AP2PC-04`). Tang, Xu, and Mahalingam's pSearch (peer-to-peer Latent Semantic Indexing): 95% accuracy visiting 0.4%-1.0% of nodes, 528,543-document TREC corpus, 1,000-10,000-node network (`TANG-CCR-03`). Neague et al.'s Semantica (large-language-model-embedding tree): 12.75% two-hop document-retrieval rate versus under 6% for a random-query baseline, even after 10 gossip-based neighbor-expansion rounds, 6,978-user, 187,521-document AOL4PS dataset (`NEAGUE-ARXIV-25`). | Every participating node runs the same classification hierarchy or embedding model, honestly (`CRESPO-AP2PC-04`, `TANG-CCR-03` both state this as a requirement). No entry in this row evaluates an adversarial participant. | Recall is bounded below 100% by construction (93% for SON, `CRESPO-AP2PC-04`); every measured deployment sits 3 to 7 orders of magnitude below web scale (1,800-10,000 nodes, 500,000-2,000,000 documents against a roughly 10-billion-document target, `registry/open-problems/web-scale-search.md`); Semantica's own numbers show the mechanism has not yet reached strong recall even at its own tested, sub-web scale. | Universal, network-wide agreement on one classification hierarchy or embedding function; a separately supplied intra-overlay routing mechanism, which `CRESPO-AP2PC-04` states is out of scope of its own design; corpus-wide document-similarity computation for the clustering step (`TANG-CCR-03`). |
| Probably-approximately-correct (PAC) random-peer-sample search: each node indexes a random subset of the collection, a query samples z random peers, each returns its own top-ranked local matches | 1.0-3.6 megabytes per query at 90% top-1 accuracy, n=1,000,000 nodes, m=10-billion-document collection, 10 million documents held per node (`ASTHANA-ICTIR-11`). At commodity 1-gigabyte-per-node storage (50,000 documents/node), Cox, Fu, and Hansen's own worked example needs 340,000 of 1,000,000 nodes queried for only 63% accuracy at 17 billion documents — three orders of magnitude more nodes, tracing directly to the 200x-lower per-node storage share (`COX-ICTIR-09`; reconciled in `registry/conflicts/search.md` section 2). | A secure, unbiased random-peer-sampling service; malicious peers cannot forge document content that scores well under an honest local ranker (`RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14` states both as assumptions it does not itself supply). | At z=2,000 nodes queried and 10% malicious peers, with no defense, a censorship attack moves a target document's rank from 5 to 582 and a promotion attack moves an unrelated document from rank 20,778 into the top 10 (`RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`). A skewness-based defense holds these attacks off up to about 40% malicious peers, but rests on a random-sampling primitive (Brahms) whose own published tolerance is only 20% malicious — the defense's ceiling exceeds its own foundation's floor (`RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`). | A secure random-peer-sampling service tolerant of the deployment's actual adversarial fraction; a content-signing or provenance check this mechanism assumes but does not supply; every node holding the same fixed document count rho, uniformly at random, or a Sybil-resistance mechanism stopping one high-capacity participant from splitting into many rho-sized virtual nodes (`RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`); sustained per-node crawl-and-storage capacity, measured by Asthana's own thesis at about 58 days per full crawl cycle at 25% of a 2010-era home connection, plus 5-10 gigabytes of local disk (`ASTHANA-PHD-14`). |
| High-capacity volunteer index, results checked by the client | IPFS's Indexer: 173,998,039,712 provider records from 604 major publishers, 2.5 thousand lookups per second, about 10x faster than DHT lookup at the 50th percentile, 65.22% cache hit rate, roughly 100x faster than DHT on a hit (`WEI-NSDI-24`). | The content identifier the client is looking up is self-validating — a hash of the content itself — so a compromised indexer can substitute only false or withheld pointers, never false content; this is the paper's own stated security argument (`WEI-NSDI-24`). | The verification this candidate gives the client covers exact point lookup by hash only. Relevance rank is not a hash-verifiable property of a document, so this candidate supplies no client-side check against a malicious indexer returning a badly ranked, but individually valid, result set — `RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`'s rank-5-to-582 manipulation is exactly this failure, and it survives content-hash verification untouched. | Self-certifying content identifiers from the naming component; continued availability of a fallback index, since some records exist only in the volunteer index and are lost if it fails, a risk `WEI-NSDI-24` states directly; a decentralization accounting that separates provider count, publication volume, query volume, and entry-point traffic, since the one measured deployment shows these four diverge sharply. |
| Learned vector (embedding, approximate-nearest-neighbor) retrieval, trusted-cluster deployment | DistributedANN: 90.8% recall at 5 results / 71.9% recall at 200 results, over 100,000 queries per second, 26-millisecond/35-millisecond 50th/99th-percentile latency, on a 50-billion-vector production Bing web-index slice, across thousands of machines (`ADAMS-ARXIV-25`). Hierarchical Navigable Small World (HNSW), the graph mechanism this builds on, reaches logarithmic query-time scaling on a single trust domain (`MALKOV-TPAMI-20`). | Every storage host runs operator-placed, trusted scoring code. `ADAMS-ARXIV-25` states this directly: "a system without control over storage-host code placement (as in an untrusted peer storage layer) could not deploy the near-data scoring service as described." Its own reliability sweep models host unavailability, never a host returning a wrong answer on purpose. | No defense against a dishonest, as opposed to merely offline, storage host or graph-edge contributor. `MALKOV-TPAMI-20` separately states its fixed top-layer entry point concentrates search traffic and "does not scale well with the number of computer nodes" once partitioned across machines that are not one trust domain. Adversarial insertion: Zhong et al. — 50 crafted passages make 99.4% of held-out queries retrieve at least one in their top 20, against a millions-of-passages corpus (`ZHONG-EMNLP-23`); Li et al. — 98.4% top-1 attack-success rate on TREC DL19 dense retrieval, from text engineered to a 30-fold lower perplexity than Zhong's method specifically to evade detection (`LI-SIGIR-25`); Zou et al. (PoisonedRAG) — 0.97 attack-success rate corrupting what a downstream language model concludes, from 5 crafted texts against a 2,681,468-passage corpus, and all four defenses the same paper tests (embedding-norm clipping, perplexity filtering, duplicate-hash filtering, dilution by returning more results) still fail to fully stop the attack under a single trusted curator (`ZOU-USENIX-25`). No published paper distributes this kind of index across participants outside one operator's administrative control while measuring resistance to any of these attacks (`registry/open-problems/adversarial-ann.md`). | Operator control over where scoring code runs, on every storage host (`ADAMS-ARXIV-25`); a fixed, discoverable top-layer entry point (`MALKOV-TPAMI-20`); a pre-computed embedding function run once over the whole corpus. |
| Learned vector retrieval, single-untrusted-server private search | Tiptoe: 2.7-second end-to-end latency, 364 million web pages, no non-collusion assumption, malicious-tolerant query privacy; MS MARCO mean correct-result rank 7.7 of 100, against 2.3 for a non-private neural retriever and 6.7 for term-frequency-inverse-document-frequency search (`HENZINGER-SOSP-23`). Pacmann: 1.1-3.0-second query latency at 3.2 million-100 million vectors, about 90% of a non-private baseline's recall, semi-honest server only (`ZHOU-EPRINT-24`). | One untrusted server, individually operated, no second non-colluding party required. Pacmann's security proof explicitly does not cover a malicious server, only semi-honest (`ZHOU-EPRINT-24`). | `registry/conflicts/search.md` section 5.1: square-root replication needs the requesting node, after a successful search, to know which item was found, so it can create a new copy of that item (`COHEN-SIGCOMM-02`); Pacmann's own stated design goal is that the server "cannot infer the query's topic from access patterns," removing that signal by construction from every node capable of acting as a forwarder. Pacmann states dynamic database updates are unsupported, an open problem in the paper itself. | A client-side full-database streaming download at setup, repeated on every corpus change — 59.6 gigabytes and 272 seconds at 100 million vectors (`ZHOU-EPRINT-24`); for Tiptoe, a cluster embedding and centroid computation run once over the whole corpus and periodically republished to every client (`HENZINGER-SOSP-23`). |

### Selection

A client-local index of content the node has itself fetched, authored, or been shown answers a query first, at no network cost. A query the local index cannot answer is broadcast under the PAC (probably-approximately-correct) protocol to z peers drawn from a random-peer-sampling service: each queried peer searches its own locally held, uniformly random document subset (`COX-ICTIR-09`, `ASTHANA-ICTIR-11`) and returns its top-ranked local matches, each response carrying the local statistics `RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`'s global-statistics-estimation-plus-skewness-filter technique needs to defend the combined ranking against a minority of adversarial responders. No entry in this corpus establishes that this pipeline runs at web scale against an adversarial peer population; `ASTHANA-ICTIR-11`'s 1.0-3.6-megabyte figure is a closed-form analytical result over stated parameters, not a measurement from a running system, and no experiment in this corpus — analytical, simulated, or deployed — combines web-scale document count, web-scale network size, ranked retrieval, and an adversarial peer population in one result (`registry/open-problems/web-scale-search.md`). This selection is the best-supported partial answer in the corpus, not a demonstrated one; the two sections after this one state exactly what remains unproven and what would have to be true elsewhere in the architecture for the analytical figure to hold in practice.

Against document-partitioned query broadcast (`LI-IPTPS-03`): rejected. Broadcast's 6-megabyte-per-query figure is fixed and directly measured, with no compression sequence available to reduce it — the paper's own optimization chapter never touches this baseline (`registry/conflicts/search.md` section 1). PAC's figure is unproven, but it is the only candidate in this table with a stated, if unconfirmed, path under the 1-megabyte budget at a larger document count than broadcast's own 3-billion-document target. Choosing PAC over broadcast trades a directly measured, fixed, over-budget cost for an analytically projected, under-budget cost carrying three named, currently unsupplied dependencies, stated below rather than hidden.

Against term-partitioned DHT posting-list intersection (`LI-IPTPS-03`): rejected. Even the paper's own full, unimplemented-at-scale optimization stack leaves the average query about 7x over budget, and the paper states the remaining gap closes only by dropping term-proximity ranking or by an unmeasured 10x per-Internet-service-provider replication of the whole index. PAC's figure, unproven as it is, sits inside the budget without either compromise, at a larger document count.

Against semantic overlay networks (`CRESPO-AP2PC-04`, `TANG-CCR-03`, `NEAGUE-ARXIV-25`): rejected on scale evidence, not mechanism quality. Every measured result sits 3 to 7 orders of magnitude below the web-scale target that `ASTHANA-ICTIR-11` and `COX-ICTIR-09` at least analytically project to; no semantic-overlay paper in this corpus offers a comparable web-scale projection. Semantic overlays also demand a stronger cross-network precondition than PAC — universal agreement on one classification hierarchy or embedding model, versus PAC's single shared random-sampling service — and `NEAGUE-ARXIV-25`'s own 12.75% two-hop retrieval rate, after 10 gossip rounds, shows the mechanism unresolved even at its own tested scale.

Against the high-capacity volunteer index with client verification (`WEI-NSDI-24`): rejected as the primary keyword-search mechanism, though it remains the correct choice for exact-identifier lookup, a different sub-problem. `WEI-NSDI-24`'s own security argument for a compromised Indexer or Hydra Booster rests specifically on the content identifier being self-validating, a property a cryptographic hash has and a relevance rank does not. `RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14` demonstrates concretely what a malicious high-capacity indexer can do that hash verification never catches: move a target's rank from 5 to 582 at 10% malicious control, with no defense active. A volunteer-index architecture for ranked search inherits this exposure at whatever scale it runs, with no client-side check available for it in this corpus. PAC's own skewness defense (`RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`) targets this exact failure mode directly, even though its bound (40%) currently exceeds what its own sampling foundation (Brahms, 20%) can deliver.

Against trusted-cluster learned vector retrieval (`ADAMS-ARXIV-25`, `MALKOV-TPAMI-20`): rejected outright for this architecture, not on cost grounds. `ADAMS-ARXIV-25` states its own precondition directly — its measured 90.8%/71.9% recall requires operator-placed scoring code on every storage host, a precondition a network of mutually distrusting, individually operated participants cannot supply. `MALKOV-TPAMI-20` independently states its fixed top-layer entry point does not scale in aggregate throughput once distributed across machines outside one trust domain. Both papers describe a single-operator managed cluster, not a decentralized network; a dedicated search across the corpus and beyond it found no published construction closing this gap as of 2026 (`registry/open-problems/adversarial-ann.md`).

Against single-untrusted-server private vector retrieval (`HENZINGER-SOSP-23`, `ZHOU-EPRINT-24`): not rejected as a mechanism, reserved instead for the tier of this component a person reaches by selecting query privacy over lower latency. Both systems assume one server holding a static snapshot of the whole corpus, not a network of independently publishing content contributors, and `ZHOU-EPRINT-24` states directly that database updates after preprocessing are unsupported — an assumption the default-tier mechanism, where new content is continuously published, cannot make. This selection therefore places two mechanisms across two tiers of one component: local-index-first with PAC network fallback for the tier a person has not opted into strong privacy, and Tiptoe- or Pacmann-style single-server private search for the tier they have, each requiring the composition check elsewhere to state separately what corpus-snapshotting or per-tier server deployment it needs.

Against a purely local index with no network fallback: rejected as incomplete, not wrong. A local-only index answers zero queries for content the node has never seen, and no figure in this corpus states what fraction of a person's real queries a local index alone would answer; `TRAUTWEIN-SIGCOMM-22`'s local-then-DHT pattern for hash-addressed retrieval is the nearest measured analog, and it adds a fixed 1-second timeout on every local miss before the network path even starts — a cost this design inherits and must budget for, not one that disappears by adding a local tier.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- A secure, Byzantine-resilient random-peer-sampling service reachable by every node, whose actual tolerated-malicious-peer fraction must be measured for this deployment and published, because `RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`'s own skewness defense is bounded above by whatever this service tolerates — the cited figures (Brahms at 20%, the skewness defense at 40%) mean the lower of the two binds, and no component selected elsewhere in this corpus's selection set is confirmed to supply a uniform, unbiased random sample as opposed to a trust-weighted or proximity-weighted one; a peer-discovery layer that biases connectivity toward already-trusted or already-nearby peers would violate the uniformity `RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`'s coverage argument depends on, and this is a candidate composition conflict for whichever agent runs the cross-component check.
- A Sybil-resistance or admission mechanism capping how many rho-sized virtual nodes one physical high-capacity participant can register, because `RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`'s accuracy analysis assumes uniform per-node document share and states virtual-node splitting "must be defended against as a Sybil attack using a mechanism outside the scope of this paper."
- A content-signing or provenance check from the identity or naming component, since `RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14` and the PAC framework generally assume malicious peers cannot forge document content that scores well under an honest local ranker; this indexing mechanism supplies no such check itself.
- A per-node local storage commitment, fixed and roughly uniform across nodes, on the order of `COX-ICTIR-09`'s and `RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`'s rho parameter, that the storage-encoding component must guarantee is actually held rather than merely claimed, since accuracy figures assume this uniformity.
- A client-local record, from the application-data-model component, of every piece of content the node has fetched, authored, or displayed, checked before any query leaves the device.
- For the strong-privacy tier: a per-corpus embedding function computed once and periodically republished to every client (`HENZINGER-SOSP-23`), or a full encoded-graph download repeated on every corpus change (`ZHOU-EPRINT-24`, 59.6 gigabytes at 100 million vectors) — a cost the storage or distribution component must carry, and `ZHOU-EPRINT-24`'s own stated limitation (dynamic updates unsupported) means this tier cannot serve continuously published content without a periodic full rebuild whose interval is not measured anywhere in this corpus.
- Per-query observability: the querying node itself must observe, for each of the z sampled peers, whether it responded and what local statistics it returned, and compute the skewness statistic `RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14` defines over those responses before trusting the combined ranking — this is a client-side computation the network layer does not perform on the querying node's behalf.

### What it costs and where it fails

Local-index answers cost nothing beyond local storage lookup, but no figure in the corpus states their hit rate, so the fraction of queries this tier actually resolves without a network round trip is unknown. On a local miss, the network fallback costs 1.0-3.6 megabytes per query at 90% top-1 accuracy under `ASTHANA-ICTIR-11`'s own stated conditions: 1,000,000 nodes, a 10-billion-document collection, 10 million documents held per node. That per-node storage figure is large — `COX-ICTIR-09`'s own worked example at 200 times less storage per node (50,000 documents, the commodity 1-gigabyte case) needs 340,000 of 1,000,000 nodes queried for only 63% accuracy at a comparable 17-billion-document collection. The actual bandwidth this architecture pays therefore depends directly on how much local storage participating nodes are asked to commit, and this document does not derive that figure; it is NOT DERIVED, because it depends on a target document count and a target accuracy this component's own definition does not fix. The mechanism fails outright once the malicious-peer fraction reaching the sampling layer exceeds the lower of the two bounds the corpus reports — 20%, Brahms's own published sampling-tolerance limit — even though the ranking-side skewness defense would otherwise hold to 40%, because sampling bias, not ranking manipulation, becomes the binding failure once the true adversarial fraction crosses 20% (`RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14`). It fails silently under content forgery, since content-signing is assumed by this mechanism, not supplied by it.

### What the corpus does not settle

- No paper combines web-scale document count (billions), web-scale network size (millions of nodes), ranked keyword retrieval, and an adversarial peer population in one measurement, stated directly in `registry/open-problems/web-scale-search.md` after a dedicated search of the corpus and beyond it. This is the single largest gap under this selection.
- No paper measures what fraction of a real person's search queries a client-local index of previously seen content would answer without any network query — the local-index-first half of this selection has no cost or hit-rate figure anywhere in the corpus; `TRAUTWEIN-SIGCOMM-22`'s 1-second Bitswap timeout is the nearest measured analog, and it is for hash-addressed retrieval, not keyword search.
- No paper measures how a PAC-style local ranker (BM25 or a language-model score, computed locally per queried peer) responds to the same crafted-passage attacks that reach 98-99% success against learned dense retrievers (`LI-SIGIR-25`, `ZHONG-EMNLP-23`). The attack is demonstrated against a different ranking mechanism than the one this selection uses; whether it transfers is unmeasured.
- The actual adversarial-peer fraction any specific deployment of this architecture will face is not knowable from the corpus. `RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14` and `ASTHANA-ICTIR-11` both treat it as a swept experimental parameter, not a deployment-specific measurement; the failure threshold stated above (20%, Brahms-bound) can only be checked against a real number once whatever Sybil-resistance mechanism this architecture selects elsewhere states what fraction it actually holds an adversary to.
- No confirmed published follow-up to `LI-IPTPS-03`'s exact bandwidth bound was found; `registry/conflicts/search.md` and `registry/open-problems/web-scale-search.md` both state this directly after a dedicated search. A synthesis that treats `ASTHANA-ICTIR-11` as that follow-up is doing so by topical proximity, not by confirmed authorship or citation continuity.
- The local-storage figure a real deployment should ask each node to commit (`COX-ICTIR-09`'s rho) is a direct, three-orders-of-magnitude-wide trade against `ASTHANA-ICTIR-11`'s low-node-count figure, and the corpus does not resolve it; deriving it needs a target document count and a target accuracy this component's task definition does not fix, so it is left NOT DERIVED here rather than filled with a plausible-sounding number.
- No paper in the corpus measures the interaction between this selection's two tiers — whether a person who switches from the default tier to the strong-privacy tier mid-session, or whose local index holds content indexed under one tier but not the other, experiences any correctness or leakage effect from the switch itself.


---

## Storage encoding: plain Reed-Solomon erasure coding is selected, and the churn regime that decides this is the high-turnover regime, not the low-churn regime most repair-cost-reducing codes were measured in

Storage encoding turns D bytes of unique data a person stores into a larger set of pieces spread across independently operated holders, so the data survives some holders departing permanently, without needing every original holder to remain reachable. Every candidate below is compared on three figures the brief requires for each: the storage-overhead ratio (total bytes stored across all holders, divided by D), the reconstruction threshold (how many holders must respond to recover the data in full), and the count of holders that must be live simultaneously to complete a single-piece repair — a number that is not always equal to the reconstruction threshold, and the gap between the two is where several candidates fail under churn.

Two entries in the corpus reach opposite feasibility conclusions from what looks like the same question, and the gap is a modeled-population difference, not a numeric disagreement: `BLAKE-HOTOS-03` derives a bandwidth bound from an original crawl of ~33,000 Gnutella hosts (April 2003; the availability and permanent-failure rates previously stated here as a=0.38 and 30%/day are NOT this paper's figures — they are `DIMAKIS-TIT-10`'s, for a different Gnutella trace of 1,846 nodes taken May 2001 over a 2.5-day window. `BLAKE-HOTOS-03` reports its own parameters only as swept curves, not as single values) and states storing 1 TB of unique data at high availability is "hopeless" with that population's participation and cable-modem bandwidth. `CHUN-NSDI-06` (Carbonite) measures a 632-host, one-year PlanetLab trace (mean disk lifetime 2.23 years and sustainability ratio θ≈6.85 are this paper's own figures; the a=0.97 previously stated here is NOT — it is `DIMAKIS-TIT-10`'s figure for a separate 303-node PlanetLab trace. `CHUN-NSDI-06` reports a=0.88, and only for a separate 5-year synthetic trace) and reports its algorithm uses only 44% more traffic than an oracle that repairs solely on true disk failure. `CHUN-NSDI-06` itself states the reason for the different conclusion: it analyzes "a relatively stable system membership where data loss is driven by disk failure," not Gnutella's continual membership turnover. This architecture recruits arbitrary, independently operated participants rather than institutionally hosted or staked ones (BRIEF §1); the measured population closest to that description in the corpus is `ANDERSON-CCGRID-06`'s SETI@home/BOINC volunteer pool, mean host lifetime 89.5 days — two orders of magnitude shorter than PlanetLab's 2.23-year mean disk lifetime, and closer in character to Gnutella's turnover than to PlanetLab's stability. The applicable regime is therefore `BLAKE-HOTOS-03`'s regime, not `CHUN-NSDI-06`'s. This resolution matters directly for candidate selection below: the one place a regenerating code was tested against a Gnutella-scale trace (`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`), its measured advantage over a simpler design nearly disappears, and every locally-repairable-code and regenerating-code repair-cost figure in the corpus otherwise comes from a data-center or research-testbed population, never a volunteer-turnover one.

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Whole-copy replication (r full copies) | **All figures in this row are analytical, from a closed-form cost model, not measured on any system** [`WEATHERSPOON-IPTPS-02`]. Fixed matched-MTTF comparison: replication uses 11× the bandwidth, storage, and disk seeks of a matched-durability erasure code (r=22 replicas vs. rate-1/2, n=64) [`WEATHERSPOON-IPTPS-02`]. At fixed storage overhead, replicated block MTTF is 74 years against 10²⁰ years for a matched-overhead code [`WEATHERSPOON-IPTPS-02`]. At a 10⁻¹² durability target under a 1/3-adversary model, full replication needs a 25× overhead versus 4.5× for a two-dimensional Reed-Solomon design [`DANEZIS-WALRUS-25`]. Azure production Markov model: 3-replication MTTF 3.5×10⁹ years vs. 6.1×10¹¹ years for (6,3) Reed-Solomon at the same fault-domain population [`HUANG-ATC-12`] | None beyond returning requested bytes unmodified; no field arithmetic, no decode step — correctness of a returned copy is checked the same way any single object is checked (a hash or signature over the whole object), not by the redundancy scheme itself | All r holders unreachable simultaneously before a replacement copy is made | Storage overhead r (an integer, chosen by the operator); reconstruction threshold 1 of r; holders that must be live for repair: 1 (any single surviving copy suffices to reseed a new holder) |
| Reed-Solomon (n,k) maximum-distance-separable (MDS) erasure coding — any k of n fragments reconstruct the object | At fixed block MTTF and storage overhead, uses 1/28 the repair bandwidth of matched replication [`WEATHERSPOON-IPTPS-02`]. Coding-vs-replication savings range 1×-3×, widening as target availability rises and node availability falls [`RODRIGUES-IPTPS-05`]. Production Facebook cluster, single triggered node loss: RS(10,4) read 486.6 GB total across 369 lost blocks (1.318 GB/block), repair took 26 minutes [`SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`]. Production deployments across ten systems (Google Colossus, Quantcast File System, Hadoop Distributed File System, Baidu Atlas, Facebook f4, Yahoo Cloud Object Store, Windows Azure Storage, Tencent Ultra-Cold Storage, Pelican, Backblaze Vaults) cluster their overhead between 1.18× and 1.50×, with n typically capped at or below 20 "to limit the repair penalty" [`CHENG-TOS25-ECSURVEY`, reporting figures attributed to those systems] | Field arithmetic (Galois-field operations) at every fragment-producing and reconstructing participant; decoding alone does not detect a substituted fragment from a dishonest holder — a separate per-piece hash or commitment is required for that [`WILKINSON-STORJ-18`] | Fewer than k of n fragments simultaneously reachable. On the Overnet (peer-to-peer) trace specifically, even after applying coding the resulting bandwidth needed for four-nines availability is stated by the paper's own authors as unsustainable for home users [`RODRIGUES-IPTPS-05`] | Storage overhead n/k (a chosen design parameter); reconstruction threshold k of n; holders live for a naive single-fragment repair: k (download k fragments, reconstruct, re-derive the missing one) |
| Minimum-storage regenerating (MSR) codes, exact-repair product-matrix construction | Theory only — no implementation, simulation, or deployment benchmark [`RASHMI-TIT-11`]. Proven to meet the cut-set bound with equality for every [n,k,d≥2k-2]; field size ≥n² sufficient (not proven minimal) [`RASHMI-TIT-11`]. The earlier MDS-fragment-size construction in this family (OMMDS) measured a 73% download reduction vs. naive full-file download at k=7,n=14, but that reduction requires connecting to n-1=13 of the remaining fragment-holders, not k [`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`] | Same field-arithmetic requirement as plain RS, at a larger minimum field size (≥n² vs. RS's unconstrained smaller fields), plus every helper node must compute and transmit a linear combination of its stored sub-symbols during repair — a computation step plain fragment-serving does not need [`CHENG-TOS25-ECSURVEY`] | Fewer than d live helpers reachable simultaneously at repair time, where d≥2k-2 for the exact-repair construction — a stricter simultaneous-liveness requirement than plain RS's k, in exchange for the same or lower storage overhead. `RASHMI-TIT-11` proves d≤2k-3 cannot achieve exact repair at all with β=1 | Storage overhead n/k (same as plain MDS at this point); reconstruction threshold k of n; holders live for repair: d≥2k-2 (proven construction) up to n-1 (the OMMDS predecessor at the same storage point) |
| Minimum-bandwidth regenerating (MBR) codes, exact-repair product-matrix construction / RC | Repair bandwidth 84% below naive full-file download and 39% below the MSR-point OMMDS construction, at k=7 [`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`]. On a PlanetLab trace (a=0.97), up to 100× lower unavailability at 58× less bandwidth than a Hybrid (replica+code) design, for a 1 GB file [`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`]. On the corpus's one Gnutella-scale (high-churn, a=0.38) trace, the same construction "can be very slightly worse than Hybrid" — the sole regenerating-code data point in the applicable regime shows the advantage disappearing [`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`]. Read-time overhead (extra bytes to reconstruct the whole object) is 14% at k=7, falling to 3.1% at k=32 [`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`] | Same field-arithmetic and helper-side linear-combination requirement as MSR, with a smaller sufficient field (≥2n) [`RASHMI-TIT-11`] | Fewer than d=k live helpers reachable at once — the same simultaneous-liveness count as plain RS's reconstruction threshold, unlike the MSR point, but at higher per-fragment storage | Storage overhead inflated above n/k by a factor β=k²/(k²-k+1) (≈1.14 at k=7, ≈1.03 at k=32) [`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`]; reconstruction threshold k of n; holders live for repair: d=k |
| Locally repairable codes (LRC) — extra local-parity groups added to an MDS-point base code | Production Windows Azure Storage: at matched 1.5× storage overhead, single-data-fragment reconstruction reads fall from 6 (Reed-Solomon (6,3)) to 3 (LRC (12,4,2), a 50% reduction); at matched 1.33× overhead, reads fall from 12 (Reed-Solomon (12,4)) to 6 (LRC (12,2,2)) [`HUANG-ATC-12`]; small-I/O reconstruction latency 166 ms (LRC) vs. 305 ms (RS) vs. 91 ms direct read, large-I/O 418 ms vs. 893 ms vs. 99 ms, on a production cluster [`HUANG-ATC-12`]. HDFS-Xorbas (production/EC2): 41%-52% of the bytes RS reads for the same repair, 25%-45% faster repair, average blocks read 5.8 vs. 11.5 [`SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`]. Facebook production cluster, one node loss: LRC read 330.8 GB across 563 blocks (0.58 GB/block) in 19 minutes vs. RS's 486.6 GB/369 blocks/26 minutes — but LRC stored 27% more than RS in that same run against an ideal 13%, because of that dataset's small-file skew [`SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`] | Same field arithmetic and separate-hash requirement as plain RS, plus a placement layer that tracks which fragments belong to which local group so repair routes to the right group, and keeps every group member on a distinct fault domain — the reliability figures assume this holds [`HUANG-ATC-12`, `PAPAILIOPOULOS-TIT-14`] | LRC is not fully MDS: not every failure pattern up to the code's nominal fault tolerance is decodable by construction. Azure's own (6,2,2) Markov model reports only 86% of 4-simultaneous-failure patterns decodable [`HUANG-ATC-12`]; losing a global parity fragment falls back to the full k-fragment repair path, the same cost as plain RS [`HUANG-ATC-12`]; every measured repair-cost figure above assumes "more than 98% of failure events are single-chunk," a property measured only in a Facebook data-center population, never in a volunteer-churn one [`CHENG-TOS25-ECSURVEY`] | Storage overhead n/k where n=k+l+g (1.33× for production (12,2,2), 1.67× for the (6,2,2) illustrative point — both above the equal-fault-tolerance MDS overhead); reconstruction threshold: full decode needs the same k as the base code for a decodable pattern; holders live for the common single-fragment-repair case: the other members of the failed fragment's local group — 3 of 4 for (6,2,2), 6 of 7 for (12,2,2) |
| Convertible codes / locally repairable convertible codes (LRCC) — reduces the bandwidth of changing an already-deployed code's (n,k) or (n,k,r) parameters | Theory only across every retrieved construction — no implementation, simulation, or deployment benchmark in any of four papers [`MATURANA-TIT-22`, `MATURANA-ISIT-23`, `KONG-TIT-24`, `SHI-ARXIV-26`]. Each proves a matching lower bound and construction for its own regime (merge, split, or LRC-specific variants) over a field polynomial or linear in n, not exponential [`MATURANA-TIT-22`, `KONG-TIT-24`] | Same field arithmetic as whichever base code (MDS-point or LRC-point) the initial and final codes use, plus a single coordinator that can read every initial-code node and write every final-code node during the conversion — "the read-bandwidth accounting assumes a single coordinator performs the whole download, not a peer-to-peer diffusion" [`SHI-ARXIV-26`] | The initial and final code parameters must be fixed and known to the construction in advance; `MATURANA-TIT-22` states the access-optimal guarantee applies only when "both endpoints... are fixed and known to the code construction in advance," and `KONG-TIT-24` states "the construction is not parameter-agnostic" — a system needing to convert to an arbitrary future rate target, decided only at conversion time, falls outside every retrieved construction | Adds no independent storage-overhead, reconstruction-threshold, or live-holder figure of its own — it inherits whatever the chosen initial/final base code (MDS-point or LRC-point, from the rows above) uses; it requires that future redundancy targets be enumerated and paired with the initial code before deployment, and a coordinator with simultaneous read/write access to the full node set during each conversion |
| Rateless (fountain) codes — LT codes, Raptor codes, RaptorQ | Robust Soliton LT codes: K=k+O(√k·ln²(k/δ)) collected symbols suffice for decode success ≥1-δ, proved asymptotic bound only [`LUBY-FOCS-02`]. RaptorQ (RFC 6330, standardized): decoder fails on average ≤1/100 with exactly the minimum symbol count collected, ≤1/10,000 with one extra, ≤1/1,000,000 with two extra [`LUBY-RFC-11`]. Raptor codes generally: any k(1+ε) collected output symbols suffice for high-probability recovery, for any chosen ε, with encode/decode cost O(k·log(1/ε)) [`SHOKROLLAHI-TIT-06`]. Deployed implementation throughput "several gigabits per second" on a 2.4 GHz Xeon, a vendor deployment claim, not a controlled benchmark [`SHOKROLLAHI-TIT-06`] | Assumes a binary erasure channel — symbols arrive intact or not at all, never corrupted — and supplies no source authentication or corruption detection on its own; a single corrupted repair symbol accepted as legitimate can make the whole decoded object unusable [`LUBY-RFC-11`, `SHOKROLLAHI-TIT-06`] | No fixed total-symbol count n exists by design, so "count of holders that must be live" is not a fixed number the way it is for the row above — decoding needs any sufficiently large collected subset, not a designated k-of-n set, which is why the family targets one-to-many object delivery over a lossy channel rather than fixed-membership distributed storage among specific holders. The one decentralized-storage deployment data point in the corpus is a real system moving away from this family: `DANEZIS-WALRUS-25`'s production codebase "replaced an earlier RaptorQ implementation after a reported vulnerability," with the vulnerability itself not characterized in the retrieved text | Storage overhead and reconstruction threshold are not fixed ratios but functions of how many symbols a receiver chooses to collect (approaching 1× as k grows, per the asymptotic bounds above); requires encoder and decoder to share a fixed degree distribution and carry a per-symbol index (Encoding Symbol ID) alongside every symbol; requires a separate placement/membership model, since the code itself specifies no assignment of which holder stores which symbol or for how long |

### Selection

Plain Reed-Solomon (n,k) MDS erasure coding is selected as the storage-encoding mechanism, over whole-copy replication, over minimum-storage and minimum-bandwidth regenerating codes, over locally repairable codes, over convertible codes, and over rateless codes.

Against whole-copy replication: every fixed-parameter comparison in the corpus that holds durability or storage overhead constant favors plain coding, by a wide and consistent margin — 11× less bandwidth, storage, and disk seeks at matched MTTF [`WEATHERSPOON-IPTPS-02`], 25× versus 4.5× overhead at a matched 10⁻¹² durability target [`DANEZIS-WALRUS-25`], and two orders of magnitude higher MTTF at the same fault-domain population in a production system [`HUANG-ATC-12`]. Replication's only structural advantage — no field arithmetic, verification against a raw byte-for-byte copy — is not decisive: RS decode cost is measured at 13.2 μs per 4 KB fragment, far below fragment-transfer time, so the corpus gives no evidence that replication's simplicity offsets its bandwidth and storage cost [`HUANG-ATC-12`].

Against minimum-storage regenerating (MSR) codes: `RASHMI-TIT-11`'s exact-repair construction needs d≥2k-2 helper nodes simultaneously reachable to realize its bandwidth advantage, a stricter simultaneous-liveness requirement than plain RS's k, and the predecessor OMMDS construction needs n-1 — nearly every remaining holder live at once [`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`]. Under the low-churn PlanetLab-like trace where regenerating codes were measured to help, high simultaneous reachability is cheap (a=0.97); under the applicable high-churn regime this document establishes above, it is exactly the resource that is scarce. No paper in the corpus measures MSR repair bandwidth against any trace resembling that regime — `RASHMI-TIT-11` is theory only, and `DIMAKIS-TIT-10`'s own Gnutella-trace result for the related MBR construction shows the family's advantage evaporating in that regime.

Against minimum-bandwidth regenerating (MBR) codes / RC: this candidate needs only d=k live helpers, matching plain RS's threshold, and its proved bandwidth reduction (84% below naive download, up to 58× less bandwidth at matched unavailability) is real under the traces tested [`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`]. But every trace on which that reduction was measured to hold is PlanetLab-, Microsoft-desktop-, or Skype-superpeer-scale churn; on the paper's own Gnutella-scale trace — the regime this document establishes as applicable — the construction "can be very slightly worse than Hybrid" [`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`]. Selecting MBR regenerating codes for this architecture would mean relying on a measured advantage from the wrong regime and a same-paper result from the right regime showing that advantage gone; plain RS carries no such regime-mismatch risk because its repair-bandwidth cost is not measured as an improvement over some baseline in the first place, only as a fixed multiple of object size.

Against locally repairable codes: every repair-cost reduction measured for this family — 50% fewer reads, 41%-52% of RS's repair bytes, 25%-45% faster repair [`HUANG-ATC-12`, `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`] — comes from Windows Azure Storage, HDFS-Xorbas on Amazon EC2, or Facebook's production Hadoop cluster: institutionally hosted nodes on 1 Gbps links, not volunteer participants. The precondition those figures rest on, "more than 98% of failure events are single-chunk" [`CHENG-TOS25-ECSURVEY`], is itself measured only in that Facebook population; nothing in the corpus establishes whether volunteer-scale churn produces the same single-fragment-dominant failure pattern or a more correlated one, and `CHENG-TOS25-ECSURVEY`'s own literature review — the field's current systematization — cites no peer-to-peer or volunteer-churn repair measurement newer than 2005. Adopting LRC's added complexity (group-membership tracking, fault-domain placement, a code that is not fully MDS and fails 14% of 4-simultaneous-failure patterns even in its measured regime [`HUANG-ATC-12`]) is not supported by evidence in the regime this architecture occupies, whereas plain RS's measured behavior does not depend on that same single-fragment-dominant assumption.

Against convertible codes: this family reduces the bandwidth of changing an already-deployed code's redundancy parameters, not the storage-overhead or reconstruction-threshold of the encoding itself, and every retrieved construction is theory only — no implementation, simulation, or deployment measurement exists for any of the four papers [`MATURANA-TIT-22`, `MATURANA-ISIT-23`, `KONG-TIT-24`, `SHI-ARXIV-26`]. More directly disqualifying for an open network: every construction requires the initial and final code parameters to be fixed and paired in advance — `MATURANA-TIT-22` states this explicitly as a precondition for its own access-optimal guarantee, and `KONG-TIT-24` states its construction "is not parameter-agnostic." A network recruiting arbitrary, unpredictable volunteer participants cannot commit in advance to the specific future redundancy target this family's access-cost optimality depends on, so this is not a candidate for the base encoding at all; it is at best a future add-on if a specific, planned re-parameterization becomes needed.

Against rateless codes: this family was designed for point-to-multipoint object delivery over a lossy channel, where a receiver decides unilaterally how many symbols to collect — not for fixed-membership storage among specific holders whose individual departure has to be repaired. The corpus gives it no built-in defense against a corrupted or substituted symbol [`LUBY-RFC-11`, `SHOKROLLAHI-TIT-06`], the same gap plain RS has, but unlike plain RS it has no fixed reconstruction threshold or holder-liveness count to reason about at all, which makes composing it with a repair-scheduling or placement mechanism that needs to know "how many holders must be live" (the number this document's whole comparison runs on) structurally awkward rather than merely unmeasured. The corpus's one deployment data point for this family in a decentralized-storage role is negative: `DANEZIS-WALRUS-25`'s production system moved away from RaptorQ to Reed-Solomon after a reported vulnerability, the opposite direction from adoption.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- A placement mechanism that assigns n fragment-holders per stripe of an object and can locate at least k of them on demand, at both read time and repair time; the encoding itself specifies no discovery mechanism [`WEATHERSPOON-IPTPS-02` requires fragments "placed on independently, uniformly randomly selected disks" for its durability figures to hold; `DIMAKIS-TIT-10` states the same discovery step is assumed, not supplied].
- A per-fragment integrity mechanism independent of the code: RS decoding does not, by itself, detect a fragment a dishonest holder has substituted. `WILKINSON-STORJ-18` states explicitly that Reed-Solomon decoding alone is insufficient to confirm correctness when only the minimum k fragments are downloaded, and requires a separate per-piece hash validated against the object's metadata pointer. `DANEZIS-WALRUS-25`'s Byzantine-tolerant variant instead verifies each returned symbol against a vector commitment (a Merkle tree in its deployment). Whichever naming or identity component a builder chooses elsewhere for this architecture must supply that per-fragment commitment or hash.
- A repair-trigger mechanism that distinguishes transient unreachability from permanent departure, since the encoding itself carries no notion of a membership timeout. `BLAKE-HOTOS-03`'s own worst-case bandwidth figure improves roughly 30× once transient downtime is distinguished from departure at a well-chosen timeout τ, and `CHUN-NSDI-06`'s reintegration rule (readmitting a returned replica rather than discarding it) is what keeps its measured overhead near an oracle's on its own trace. This decision sits in a repair/maintenance component this document does not select, not in the encoding.
- Field arithmetic (Galois-field operations) at every fragment-producing and reconstructing participant — a lower bar than the regenerating-code candidates, which additionally need every helper node to compute and transmit a linear combination of stored sub-symbols during repair, not merely serve a stored fragment unmodified [`CHENG-TOS25-ECSURVEY`].
- Independent, or at least uncorrelated, failure across the n holders of one stripe for the durability figures above to hold; every cited comparison assumes this (`HUANG-ATC-12`'s fault-domain placement requirement; `WEATHERSPOON-IPTPS-02`'s independent-disk assumption). No entry in the corpus measures how correlated volunteer-participant churn actually is (shared ISP outages, shared software-version bugs, coordinated departure events), so the placement mechanism's own diversity guarantee — not measured here — is a precondition this selection assumes rather than one it establishes.

### What it costs and where it fails

The storage-overhead ratio n/k is a design choice, not a value this corpus derives; production data-center deployments cluster it between 1.18× and 1.50× [`CHENG-TOS25-ECSURVEY`], but that figure reflects independent-disk-failure populations, not volunteer churn, and no entry in the corpus states what ratio a volunteer-churn population needs for a stated durability target. The one real (not simulated) full-repair-event bandwidth figure for plain RS in the corpus — 486.6 GB read to repair 369 lost blocks (1.318 GB/block), in 26 minutes [`SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`] — comes from a Facebook production cluster, not a volunteer population.

The encoding fails whenever fewer than k of a stripe's n holders are simultaneously reachable. `RODRIGUES-IPTPS-05`'s own Overnet-trace result is the closest evidence in the corpus to a direct answer for the applicable regime: even after applying erasure coding and reducing bandwidth substantially relative to replication, the resulting maintenance bandwidth the trace's own paper computes for four-nines availability is stated by the authors themselves as unsustainable for home users. Plain RS narrows the gap `BLAKE-HOTOS-03` identifies; it does not close it. Nothing in the corpus establishes a redundancy level and repair-trigger policy at which four-or-more-nines durability becomes sustainable for a genuinely open, volunteer-recruited population — this is the same gap the corpus's own `volunteer-repair-economics` open-problem entry documents in full.

### What the corpus does not settle

No paper in the corpus measures repair-traffic bytes for any erasure-code family — plain RS, MSR, MBR, or LRC — on a population with volunteer-scale churn. The closest measured churn rate to "volunteer" is Storj's live network, 9.6% average monthly node turnover across a 16-month, 32,881-node crawl spanning 122 countries [`LI-IWQOS-23-STORJ`], but that paper reports no repair-byte count at all, and Storj's own repair-bandwidth table is a 10,000-node Monte Carlo simulation under an assumed constant monthly loss rate, not measured live traffic [`WILKINSON-STORJ-18`]. Closing this gap requires either instrumenting a live plain-RS deployment recruiting genuinely open participants and reporting repair bytes moved, or re-running `WEATHERSPOON-IPTPS-02`'s or `DIMAKIS-TIT-10`'s cost model against a churn trace collected after 2020, neither of which any retrieved source has done.

Given that gap, the specific (n,k) parameters a real volunteer deployment should target — the overhead-versus-repair-frequency tradeoff point — is not derivable from this corpus. A builder choosing a value anyway is making a labelled pick, not reporting a derived or measured figure, and should record it as such.

Whether adding LRC-style local parity groups on top of the selected RS code would help or hurt under volunteer churn is unmeasured, not merely unmeasured-but-probably-fine: the 50% and 41%-52% repair-cost reductions both rest on "more than 98% of failures are single-chunk" [`CHENG-TOS25-ECSURVEY`, citing a Facebook measurement], a property that has never been checked against a population where correlated departure (shared outages, coordinated client-version bugs, a mass-exit event) may be common rather than rare.

The interaction between the selected encoding and a verified-retrievability layer (a proof that a holder actually stores the fragment it claims to, as opposed to merely having once received it) is a separate component this document does not score. `JUELS-CCS-07` and `SHACHAM-ASIACRYPT-08` give only proved asymptotic bounds, no deployment measurement, for the proof-of-retrievability constructions that compose with an erasure-encoded object; what added bandwidth and latency that composition costs at volunteer scale is not established here.

Whether the Byzantine-tolerant verification RS needs against a substituting holder (a per-fragment hash, or a vector commitment as in `DANEZIS-WALRUS-25`) changes the repair-bandwidth or storage-overhead figures reported above for plain RS is not stated by any of the plain-RS-measuring papers in this corpus (`WEATHERSPOON-IPTPS-02`, `RODRIGUES-IPTPS-05`, `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`'s RS baseline) — all of them assume a crash-fault, not Byzantine, failure model for the encoding layer itself, leaving the added cost of Byzantine-tolerant verification on top of plain RS an open figure for this architecture specifically, since it targets a permissionless, uncoordinated participant population where a Byzantine holder cannot be ruled out by assumption.


### Correction, from the claims audit

Three claims in this selection were corrected after every cited figure was traced back to the entry
it cites. Two availability figures stated above were attributed to the wrong papers: `a=0.38` with a
30 percent daily permanent-failure rate, and `a=0.97`, both belong to `DIMAKIS-TIT-10` and describe
different traces than the ones this document attributed them to. The whole-copy-replication row's
figures are analytical rather than measured, and were laid out in the same table as
production-measured rows without that distinction.

**The selection does not change.** The regime argument rests on host lifetime, not on those
availability figures: `ANDERSON-CCGRID-06` measures a volunteer pool at a mean host lifetime of 89.5
days and `CHUN-NSDI-06` measures PlanetLab at a mean disk lifetime of 2.23 years, and both of those
figures check out against their own entries. The two orders of magnitude between them is what places
this architecture in the high-turnover regime, and that is what decides against every
repair-cost-reducing code below.


---

## Repair: local-group exact reconstruction cuts repair traffic to a third or a half of plain Reed-Solomon's, but no source in the corpus tests any coded repair mechanism under both open-participant churn and an adversarial helper

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (conditions) | Security assumption | Failure condition | Requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Full reconstruction from k of n (plain Reed-Solomon / MDS repair) | Facebook production RS(10,4): 11.5 blocks read per lost block on average, live EC2 measurement across 200 files (`SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`). Azure production RS(6,3): repairing one lost data fragment reads all 6 data fragments, "because Reed-Solomon parities are each computed from all data fragments and cannot narrow the read to a subgroup" (`HUANG-ATC-12`). Storj's deployed configuration needs k=29 of n=80 holders reachable per repair, measured from a 16-month live crawl of the production network (`LI-IWQOS-23-STORJ`). | Each helper returns its own already-stored, already content-addressed bytes verbatim, so a pre-existing per-fragment hash catches a substituted response with no added protocol machinery — a structural consequence of the mechanism, not a property any retrieved paper tests against a lying helper. | Repair traffic stays close to a full-object download regardless of n; Storj's own documentation states this as a "key limitation," an inability to heal lost parts without full reconstruction, cited by `DANEZIS-WALRUS-25`. `RODRIGUES-IPTPS-05` and `WEATHERSPOON-IPTPS-02` disagree on how large the resulting bandwidth penalty against replication actually is in practice (see Selection). | A placement mapping from current membership to each fragment's k-of-n holder set (consistent hashing, `RODRIGUES-IPTPS-05`); k holders simultaneously reachable at repair time. |
| Full replica plus coded fragments ("Hybrid") | On the Overnet trace at four-nines target availability, coding cuts the required redundancy factor to under half pure replication's factor of 20, but the resulting ~100 kbps/node average maintenance bandwidth is stated by the authors as unsustainable for home users (`RODRIGUES-IPTPS-05`, 10,000-node/10 TB simulation over three real availability traces). `DIMAKIS-TIT-10` measures a regenerating code beating Hybrid by up to 58x less bandwidth at 100x lower unavailability for a 1 GB file on the PlanetLab trace (k=7), but states RC "can be very slightly worse than Hybrid" on the least-stable Gnutella trace (a=0.38, f=0.30/day). | Ordinary fragment repair needs only the single node holding the complete extra copy, which "can create a replacement fragment directly and push it to the new holder" (`RODRIGUES-IPTPS-05`) — the smallest reachability requirement of any candidate here. | If the full-copy holder is itself lost, repair falls back to downloading m fragments (near the full object size) to reconstitute it — the same cost as row 1's worst case, with no measured frequency for how often this occurs under churn. | Exactly one node per object designated and willing to hold a complete unfragmented copy in addition to the coded fragments (`RODRIGUES-IPTPS-05`); the object must be immutable for the paper's session/lifetime model to hold. |
| Locally Repairable Codes (LRC): local-group exact repair with global fallback decode | At fixed 1.5x storage overhead, Azure LRC(12,4,2) needs 3 fragments to reconstruct a lost data fragment against Reed-Solomon(6,3)'s 6; at fixed reconstruction cost 6, LRC(12,2,2) reaches 1.33x overhead against RS(6,3)'s 1.5x (`HUANG-ATC-12`, production Markov model). Same production deployment, small-I/O latency: direct read 91 ms, LRC(12,2,2) reading 6 fragments 166 ms, RS(12,4) reading 12 fragments 305 ms. Facebook's Xorbas(10,6,5) reads 41-52% of the bytes RS reads and repairs 25-45% faster, live 51-node EC2 Hadoop clusters, 200 files; on Facebook's own 35-node/370 TB test cluster it read 0.58 GB per repaired block against RS's 1.318 GB (`SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`). | Each local-group repair reads its group members' own previously stored, unmodified bytes (Reed-Solomon decode plus XOR, not a value computed fresh for this one repair), so an already-present per-fragment content hash lets the repairing node verify every input before combining it — structural inference; no retrieved paper tests LRC repair against a Byzantine group member. | Not maximum-distance-separable, so it needs strictly more parity than an MDS code at matched fault tolerance (`HUANG-ATC-12`). When a local group loses more members than its local parity covers, repair falls back to the full ("heavy") Reed-Solomon decoder at row 1's cost — `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`'s own Markov model computes this fallback probability, but only under a managed-datacenter annual per-disk failure rate, not volunteer-scale churn. | Fixed, system-known local-group membership, with blocks placed and addressed by group (`PAPAILIOPOULOS-TIT-14`); local-parity coefficients chosen so every repair-time linear system stays invertible, a construction `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13` states is its "main theoretical challenge" and defers to an appendix; each group's members placed in distinct fault domains (`HUANG-ATC-12`). |
| Exact-regenerating codes at the MSR/MBR operating points (product-matrix construction; Clay codes as a deployed realization) | Proven to meet the cutset bound with equality at every feasible [n,k,d] for MBR and every [n,k,d≥2k-2] for MSR — a proof, not a measurement (`RASHMI-TIT-11`). In a real 26-node Ceph-on-EC2 cluster, the Clay-code configuration reaching the paper's headline 2.9x network-traffic reduction and 3.4x disk-read reduction over RS at matched 1.25x overhead is C3=(20,16,19): repair contacts d=19 helpers, every surviving node in the 20-node stripe (`VAJHA-FAST-18`, single injected node failure per trial). | Exact regeneration (bit-identical replacement) requires all d helpers simultaneously reachable and each correctly computing its assigned linear combination of its own stored data (`RASHMI-TIT-11`); `PATRA-TIT-25`'s adversarial extension assumes a helper's stored data can be corrupted but its computation cannot — a strictly weaker adversary than a fully Byzantine helper, and the only adversarial analysis of this code family in the corpus. | On the corpus's least-stable real availability trace, the bandwidth advantage over Hybrid nearly disappears (`DIMAKIS-TIT-10`, Gnutella trace, k=7). The headline traffic reduction is realized at d close to n-1 — more simultaneous holders than any other coded candidate here needs — which is the opposite of what a high-churn population rewards. | Field arithmetic over Fq with q≥2n (MBR) or q≥n² (MSR) at every holder (`RASHMI-TIT-11`); a fixed encoding matrix meeting stated structural conditions, verified at code setup; a coordinator or the newcomer able to select and simultaneously contact exactly d helpers, each with its own assigned share (`VAJHA-FAST-18`). |
| Two-dimensional erasure coding with per-symbol vector commitments (RedStuff / Walrus) | Single-sliver recovery costs O(\|blob\|/n) against O(\|blob\|) for one-dimensional RS recovery — derived from the construction, not bandwidth-measured in isolation (`DANEZIS-WALRUS-25`). On mainnet: epoch 17, 4 shards (~465,000 blobs each) recovered, 3 sequentially in 17-21 hours each; epoch 20, 7 shards (~521,000 blobs each) recovered, 5 concurrently in ~64 hours total, one interrupted and resumed only after its holding node returned online. Committee nodes report median 55 TB capacity, ≥16 CPU cores, 128 GB RAM, 1 Gbps bandwidth — not open-participant hardware. | A per-sliver vector commitment (deployed as Merkle trees) lets a recovering node verify each returned row/column symbol against what the original writer committed, closing the on-the-fly-computed-value gap the regenerating-code row leaves open: "without this, a Byzantine node returning a substituted symbol during recovery would not be detectable" (`DANEZIS-WALRUS-25`). | Requires f+1 of n=3f+1 committee members reachable, and 4.5x storage overhead — higher than any other coded candidate here (LRC ~1.33-1.6x, plain RS 1.25-1.5x at matched targets). The metadata-linear-scaling optimization the design specifies is "not yet deployed," so the measured production system's metadata overhead exceeds the protocol as designed. | An external total-order/Byzantine-fault-tolerant control plane for committee and shard-assignment state (the deployment uses the Sui blockchain); a reliable authenticated channel between every pair of honest nodes within an epoch; the adversary controls at most f of n=3f+1 nodes. |

Two candidates appear in the corpus with no repair-specific measurement and are listed for completeness rather than compared: Filecoin's design paper defines a self-healing repair property in Section 8.3 but states no proof or implementation exists for it (`BENET-FILECOIN-17`); Sia's whitepaper recommends regenerating codes for multi-host placement but reports no measurement of any kind (`VORICK-SIA-14`).

### Selection

Locally Repairable Codes, in the Azure/Xorbas construction — local-group exact reconstruction as the default repair path, with a full Reed-Solomon decode retained as the fallback when a group loses more members than its local parity covers.

Against full reconstruction from k of n: LRC is preferred because it is the only coded candidate with two independent real production deployments confirming the same directional result — Microsoft Azure Storage (`HUANG-ATC-12`) and Facebook's Hadoop cluster (`SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`) — each showing a 2x-or-better cut in fragments read per repair at matched or lower storage overhead than plain Reed-Solomon. Plain reconstruction is not rejected on security grounds; both mechanisms share the same helper-returns-its-own-stored-bytes property, so both get the same free content-hash verification. It is rejected on the traffic and reachability axes the corpus measures directly: `HUANG-ATC-12`'s own (6,2,2) example shows RS(6,3) reading all 6 fragments where LRC(6,2,2) reads 3 for the identical repair, its production (12,2,2) deployment reads 6 fragments where the matched-overhead RS(12,4) comparison reads 12, and Storj's deployed RS configuration needs k=29 holders reachable at once (`LI-IWQOS-23-STORJ`) against LRC's group sizes of 3-6 in the same table.

Against the full-replica hybrid: the hybrid's single-holder repair path is cheaper in the common case than LRC's local group, but `DIMAKIS-TIT-10` measures a regenerating code (a different coded mechanism, cited here only for the comparison point against Hybrid) beating Hybrid by up to 58x less bandwidth at 100x lower unavailability on the PlanetLab trace, and `RODRIGUES-IPTPS-05` — the hybrid's own source paper — states its own resulting bandwidth (~100 kbps/node on the Overnet trace) as unsustainable for home participants. A design placing a full, unfragmented copy of every object on one designated node also reintroduces the single-point-of-concentration problem erasure coding exists to avoid, at a scale (one full object per node) that a storage-constrained participant device cannot always accommodate; no source in the corpus measures this constraint, so it is stated here as a structural consequence of the mechanism's own description, not a further citation.

Against exact-regenerating codes at MSR/MBR: `VAJHA-FAST-18` is the corpus's only real-deployment measurement of this family, and it shows the mechanism's headline traffic reduction (2.9x over RS) realized at d=19 simultaneously reachable helpers, out of 19 surviving nodes in a 20-node stripe — a larger simultaneous-reachability requirement than LRC's 3-6, on the exact axis the brief calls out for this component. `DIMAKIS-TIT-10`'s own Gnutella-trace result shows the family's bandwidth advantage over a plain hybrid nearly vanishing on the corpus's least-stable real trace, which is the trace closest in character to an open volunteer population (`ANDERSON-CCGRID-06`'s BOINC host lifetime, 89.5 days average, sits closer to Gnutella's turnover than to PlanetLab's 2.23-year mean disk lifetime). A second, independent reason favors LRC specifically for this architecture: MSR/MBR repair has each helper transmit a linear combination it computes fresh for that one repair event, not bytes it already stored and hashed, so no existing content-addressing check can verify a helper's response — `RASHMI-TIT-11`, `VAJHA-FAST-18`, and `PATRA-TIT-25` supply no commitment scheme to close that gap, and `PATRA-TIT-25`'s own adversary model stops short of a helper that can falsify its computation, not only its stored data.

Against two-dimensional erasure coding with vector commitments (RedStuff): RedStuff is the only candidate in the corpus that deliberately closes the same on-the-fly-verification gap that exact-regenerating codes leave open, and it is the only candidate measured on a real permissionless-committee deployment moving real repair traffic (`DANEZIS-WALRUS-25`'s epoch 17/20 figures). It is not selected as the default because its measured deployment is not the population this component's economics question is about: Walrus's committee nodes are staked and report median 55 TB capacity, ≥16 CPU cores, 128 GB RAM, and 1 Gbps bandwidth — provisioned infrastructure, not the volunteer or consumer-hardware population `BLAKE-HOTOS-03` and `ANDERSON-CCGRID-06` measure. Its 4.5x storage overhead is also the highest of any candidate here, against LRC's 1.33-1.6x, an overhead `DANEZIS-WALRUS-25` itself states goes specifically toward Byzantine tolerance and reduced recovery bandwidth together, not toward repair traffic alone. Where the architecture needs verified repair against a genuinely adversarial helper population — which an open participant network, unlike Azure's or Facebook's own datacenters, cannot rule out — RedStuff's construction is the corpus's only evidence that the problem is solvable at all; it is treated below, in the open-problem discussion, as the fallback for that case, rather than folded into the primary selection.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- The object encoder must place k data blocks into l local groups of fixed, system-known membership (group size r, plus one local parity per group), and every node performing placement or repair must be able to look up a given block's group members — `PAPAILIOPOULOS-TIT-14`, `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`.
- The object's manifest must carry a per-fragment content hash, established at encode time and itself authenticated (signed by the publisher, or covered by the parent object's own root hash), so a repairing node can verify each local-group member's returned bytes before combining them. This is the mechanism that gives LRC repair Byzantine tolerance without RedStuff's vector-commitment machinery; no retrieved paper implements or measures it, so it is stated here as a requirement this selection needs supplied, not as an evidenced property.
- The system must implement full Reed-Solomon decode as a fallback ("heavy") path for the case a local group loses more members than its local parity covers — `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`'s light/heavy decoder split — meaning this selection does not eliminate row 1's mechanism, it only reduces how often it is invoked.
- Local-group coefficients must be chosen so every linear system a repair could need to solve stays invertible over the working finite field; `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13` defers this construction to an appendix rather than a closed-form rule a builder can apply directly (see "What the corpus does not settle").
- Each group's members must be placed in distinct fault domains, or the equivalent for a peer population (distinct autonomous systems, distinct operators) — `HUANG-ATC-12`'s reliability figures assume this and do not hold without it.
- A liveness-tracking / repair-triggering layer must supply a membership timeout distinguishing transient disconnection from permanent departure: `BLAKE-HOTOS-03` measures up to a 30x maintenance-bandwidth swing from this one parameter alone, independent of which fragment-repair mechanism is chosen. `CHUN-NSDI-06`'s reintegration policy (Carbonite) is the corpus's strongest measured result for this layer — 44% overhead above an oracle that could distinguish permanent from transient failure perfectly, against roughly 2x oracle overhead for a threshold policy that discards and re-replicates on every disconnection (`BHAGWAN-NSDI-04`'s eager repair, and the "Cates" comparison point in `CHUN-NSDI-06`) — and needs no measurement of host availability `a` to achieve it, unlike `BHAGWAN-NSDI-04`'s lazy-repair policy, which requires a continuously measured, deployment-specific availability distribution. This selection is written assuming a Carbonite-style reintegration trigger sits beneath it; a threshold-based trigger instead would multiply this section's repair-traffic figures by roughly the 2x-versus-1.44x ratio `CHUN-NSDI-06` measures between the two policies.
- A loss-detection mechanism must exist to tell a node when to invoke repair at all; the corpus's Proofs of Retrievability / Provable Data Possession literature (`ATENIESE-CCS-07`) supplies one design for this (constant-size challenge-response, independent of file size) but is a separate component from repair itself and is not evaluated here for composition with LRC specifically.

### What it costs and where it fails

Storage overhead: 1.33x-1.6x depending on parameters, against 1.4x-2x for matched-reliability plain Reed-Solomon and 2x-16x for replication at comparable redundancy targets (`HUANG-ATC-12`, `WILKINSON-STORJ-18`, `BHAGWAN-NSDI-04`). Repair traffic: 41-52% of plain Reed-Solomon's bytes moved, measured live on a 51-node EC2 Hadoop cluster (`SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`); reconstruction cost cut from 6 fragments to 3 at matched overhead, measured on Azure production infrastructure (`HUANG-ATC-12`). Reliability, from `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`'s Markov model at N=3000 nodes, 30 PB cluster data, 4-year mean disk lifetime: LRC(10,6,5) reaches a Mean Time to Data Loss the model computes as "2 more zeros" than plain RS(10,4) — this model assumes independent node failures at an annual, managed-datacenter rate, not volunteer-scale churn (see below).

It fails, in the sense of falling back to full-cost repair, whenever a local group loses more members simultaneously than its local parity covers; `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`'s own Markov model computes the resulting light-versus-heavy-decoder invocation probability, but only under that managed-datacenter failure-rate assumption. The assumption behind every measured LRC repair-cost reduction in this table is that a stripe's failures are overwhelmingly single-fragment: "more than 98% of failure events of a stripe are single-chunk failures," a figure `CHENG-TOS25-ECSURVEY`'s survey states is measured on Facebook's own warehouse cluster, not on any peer-to-peer or volunteer population. Azure's own (6,2,2) configuration decodes only 86% of 4-simultaneous-failure patterns (`HUANG-ATC-12`'s Markov-model parameter pd=86%), because LRC is not maximum-distance-separable; under a failure regime with more simultaneous loss than the 98% figure describes, this decodability gap widens in a way plain full reconstruction, which is fully MDS and decodes every pattern up to its distance, does not share. Against this, a lower-availability population makes the reachability argument for LRC stronger, not weaker, on a different axis: with each holder independently reachable with probability a, the chance that all r members of a small local group are simultaneously online falls as a^r, so a smaller r clears that bar far more often than plain reconstruction's larger k does at the same a — at the Gnutella-trace availability BLAKE-HOTOS-03 measures (a≈0.38), a 5-member local group (Xorbas's r=5) has roughly two orders of magnitude higher probability of being fully reachable at once than a k=10 reconstruction set. No retrieved source runs this arithmetic or measures either effect directly against real volunteer-churn data; both are stated here as structural consequences of the two mechanisms' definitions, not as findings from a cited experiment. It fails to defend against a Byzantine group member at all without the content-hash addition stated above as a requirement this selection needs, since Reed-Solomon-decode-plus-XOR is an erasure-correcting operation, not an error-correcting one, and silently produces an incorrect result when combined with corrupted-but-present input rather than a detected absence. It fails to reduce bandwidth if the group-membership placement collapses fault domains, since the reliability model's independence assumption is then violated without the system detecting the violation (`HUANG-ATC-12`).

### What the corpus does not settle

No retrieved source measures repair traffic, in bytes moved, for any coded repair mechanism — LRC included — operating on a population whose churn resembles Storj's measured 9.6% monthly node turnover or Gnutella's higher continuous turnover; this extends the corpus's own existing open-problem finding for regenerating codes specifically (`registry/open-problems/volunteer-repair-economics.md`) to the LRC family this selection picks, since Azure and Facebook's deployments are both managed datacenters, not open participant pools. The Markov reliability models both production LRC papers rely on (`HUANG-ATC-12`, `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`) assume independent failures at an annual per-disk rate; nothing in the corpus recomputes those models' light/heavy-decoder invocation probability under a churn rate two to three orders of magnitude higher.

Whether the content-hash verification this selection requires (stated above) is sufficient against a helper that withholds a correct response rather than substituting a wrong one — a liveness attack, not a correctness attack — is not evaluated by any retrieved source; `LI-IWQOS-23-STORJ`'s reputation-manipulation finding shows the layer that decides which nodes get asked is separately attackable, independent of whether a given response can be verified once received.

The finite-field size and local-parity coefficient-selection procedure LRC repair needs is stated by `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13` to be "the main theoretical challenge" of the construction and deferred to an appendix the retrieved text does not include as extracted content; a builder needs the concrete field size and coefficients, not the paper's claim that a valid choice exists.
