Whether RedStuff's verified-recovery construction, the corpus's only evidence that Byzantine-tolerant coded repair with sub-linear bandwidth is achievable at all, would sustain its measured recovery durations (17-64 hours per multi-shard event, on committee nodes with median 55 TB capacity and 1 Gbps bandwidth) under a churn rate resembling Storj's or Gnutella's is not addressed by `DANEZIS-WALRUS-25` or any other retrieved source. Whether a lighter-weight verification scheme than a full vector commitment — one closing only the gap this selection's local-group repair already avoids by construction, rather than the harder gap regenerating-code and 2D-coding repair face — has been published anywhere is a literature question this pass did not separately search.

This selection disagrees with the storage-encoding component's own selection of plain Reed-Solomon, drawn from an overlapping evidence set (`HUANG-ATC-12`, `SATHIAMOORTHY-VLDB-13`, `DIMAKIS-TIT-10`, `RASHMI-TIT-11`, `CHENG-TOS25-ECSURVEY`, `BLAKE-HOTOS-03`, `ANDERSON-CCGRID-06`, `LI-IWQOS-23-STORJ`), which rejects LRC on the grounds that its 98%-single-chunk precondition is unverified for volunteer churn. Both readings of the same evidence are defensible: `storage-encoding.md` weighs the unverified traffic-reduction precondition as decisive; this document weighs LRC's smaller simultaneous-holder requirement (r versus k, derived above) and the Azure comparison's own weak Pareto-dominance over plain RS at matched reliability targets as decisive instead, and treats LRC's worst-case degradation (falling back to the same heavy-decoder cost plain RS always incurs) as bounding the downside. Neither reading is settled by a measurement on volunteer-scale churn; resolving the disagreement between the two component selections is a composition-check question, not one either component analysis can close alone.


---

## Incentives: storage-retrievability proofs settle payment for capacity; a per-connection reciprocation rule, not a credit ledger, settles bandwidth — and no candidate in the corpus does both

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (conditions) | Security assumption | Failure condition | Requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Payment verified by storage-retrievability proof (blockchain-enforced random challenge over a compact proof-of-retrievability construction — Sia's storage proof built on the Shacham-Waters primitive; Filecoin's PoRep/PoSt is the same family with no measured benchmark) | Query 20 bytes, response 40 bytes at 80-bit security for the publicly-verifiable construction; storage overhead 2x beyond the erasure code's own expansion at one sector per block, falling to (1+1/s)x at a (1+s)x longer response (`SHACHAM-ASIACRYPT-08`). A host storing 50% of a file fails roughly 50% of challenge windows — a probabilistic argument from the random-sampling design, not an independent measurement (`VORICK-SIA-14`). Filecoin's own paper reports no sealing time, proof size, or throughput figure of any kind: Section 8.1 lists benchmarks as future work not yet done (`BENET-FILECOIN-17`). | The challenge-window seed must be unpredictable to the prover in advance; Sia derives it from a block hash the prover cannot bias below a stated threshold. The extraction proof (recovering a rho-fraction of the file from any server passing an epsilon-fraction of challenges) holds only if the erasure code tolerates adversarial, not merely random, erasure (`SHACHAM-ASIACRYPT-08`). | An attacker controlling 50% of the network's block-producing power can bias roughly 50% of challenge outcomes in its own favor (`VORICK-SIA-14`). A proof construction that certifies only an aggregate property, not individual-block possession, lets a server holding no extra data cheat with a nonzero success probability — Shacham and Waters' own attack on a prior scheme (E-PDP) succeeds with near 9% probability under no more storage than honest compliance (`SHACHAM-ASIACRYPT-08`). | A blockchain or equivalent ledger supplying unpredictable per-window randomness and executing the payment transaction automatically on a valid proof (`VORICK-SIA-14`); a content-addressed transfer layer beneath the proof protocol, since Get/Put move bytes outside it (`BENET-FILECOIN-17`); an erasure code chosen jointly with the challenge parameters rho, l, and B so that epsilon-omega stays positive (`SHACHAM-ASIACRYPT-08`); a separate payment-channel primitive for per-part retrieval payment, since a storage proof only certifies continued possession, not willingness to transfer (`BENET-FILECOIN-17`, `VORICK-SIA-14`). |
| Payment or verification tied to a bandwidth-forwarding proof (TorCoin mints currency from a proof that one specific four-party circuit forwarded traffic; Proof of Backhaul measures a node's link capacity via a witnessing group of challengers, without itself settling a payment) | Proof of Backhaul: bandwidth error under 5% with 6 or more challengers and a 100 ms challenge window, using about 3.5 MB of challenge traffic; accurate to under 10% error up to 1,000 Mbps (`SHENG-NDSS-24`). TorCoin: protocol overhead approximately 5% of Tor traffic at a packet-count interval of 10 or more, reported by the authors as "Preliminary Results" from a Python-Twisted message-passing simulation, with no stated relay count, run count, or real-network deployment (`GHOSH-HOTPETS-14`). | Proof of Backhaul is proven for a Byzantine-challenger fraction beta below 1/3 with no verifier timer, or up to 1/2 with a timer (`SHENG-NDSS-24`). TorCoin assumes at most half of a network's temporary keys belong to colluding participants, under which at most 1/16 of assigned circuits are fully colluding and able to mint coins for zero genuine transfer (`GHOSH-HOTPETS-14`). | Proof of Backhaul's own correction factor for a rushing attack (a corrupted challenger colluding with a corrupted prover) lowers the reported "guaranteed" bandwidth by 28% below the true value even when all challengers report honestly, at a measured 20% Byzantine-challenger fraction (`SHENG-NDSS-24`) — accuracy and Byzantine tolerance trade off directly. TorCoin's mining check requires all four onion-forwarded commit/reveal messages per round delivered in order; one lost or reordered message prevents the blob from being validly signed, and a valid coin proves goodput on one past circuit, not an ongoing capacity score (`GHOSH-HOTPETS-14`). | Proof of Backhaul requires a challenge coordinator able to draw a fresh, random challenger subset per measurement and a synchronized-enough clock across challengers for round-trip-time comparison, plus a blockchain in its deployed variant to record challenges and escrow payer tokens (`SHENG-NDSS-24`). TorCoin requires a decentralized circuit-assignment layer (TorPath) built from a verifiable shuffle among assignment servers, and a public consensus log of shuffle matrices for any later coin audit (`GHOSH-HOTPETS-14`). |
| Reciprocal exchange, bandwidth (per-connection tit-for-tat / proportional-share auction over recently observed reciprocation — BitTorrent's choking algorithm and its proportional-share refinement, PropShare) | Piece-announcement overhead under 0.1% of bandwidth in real deployments; tracker overhead about one-thousandth of total bandwidth (`COHEN-IPTPS-03`). A strategic client (BitTyrant) achieves a median download-rate gain of 1.72x over an unmodified reference client — a 1 Mb/s-class client's median gain is 70% — across 114 real-world swarms of 300-800 peers, file sizes under 1 GB, both clients capped at 128 KB/s upload (`PIATEK-NSDI-07`). PropShare finishes faster than plain BitTorrent in 20 of 21 live-swarm downloads and reduces a deviating peer's best-response gain to under 1% of download speed even though that deviation differs from the proportional allocation by roughly 30% in effort (`LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08`). | Each peer needs to observe only how much bandwidth a given neighbor sent it in recent rounds — no observation of a neighbor's total capacity or its bids to other peers is required, unlike the best-response strategy from Feldman et al. (`LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08`). No currency, ledger, or persistent global identity is assumed; a bidirectional TCP connection per peer pair suffices (`COHEN-IPTPS-03`). | The mechanism's own unconditional bootstrap allocation (optimistic unchoke, seeder round robin) admits a client that uploads nothing: BitThief completes all 7 tested torrents with zero upload, running only about 4-5x slower than the reference client in seeder-scarce swarms (`LOCHER-HOTNETS-06`). The large-view exploit (connecting to a swarm view roughly 5x the compliant default) wins 12 of 15 public-torrent trials; at a 60% free-rider population share in a controlled PlanetLab swarm the exploit's own advantage reverses and compliant peers finish faster (`SIRIVIANOS-IPTPS-07`). PropShare's own Sybil-proofness is proven algebraically for identity-splitting but not for collusion below a threshold of non-colluding bidders equal to the victim's unchoke-slot count (`LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08`). | A peer-discovery channel (tracker, DHT, or both) able to return a larger, more frequently refreshed candidate set, since BitTyrant needs hundreds of connections to fully reciprocate capacity that a default 50-100-peer neighborhood cannot supply (`PIATEK-NSDI-07`); a per-piece integrity check gating which held pieces a peer is willing to advertise, since the same signal feeds both reciprocation accounting and rarest-first piece selection (`COHEN-IPTPS-03`); an accepted, unpatched bootstrap-allocation cost, since Locher et al. state that combining tit-for-tat with an efficient bootstrap allowance for new peers "remains an open problem" (`LOCHER-HOTNETS-06`). |
| Symmetric barter without a currency or a retrievability proof (Samsara: a storage consumer must hold an equal-sized claim on behalf of its provider, verified by a keyed hash chain, with probabilistic discard on repeated query failure) | Claim overhead reduced to under 0.02% of global storage at 100% utilization, for both collection-based and distributed-hash-table replication, in a 5,000-node SimPastry simulation at replication factor 4 (`COX-SOSP-03`). At 50% utilization and 16% permanent simultaneous node failure, 0.28% of objects are lost under conservative claim transfer; verification of n objects costs one hash value in bandwidth regardless of n (same source). | A node protects its own stored objects by punishing a peer's failed queries on the claim it holds; the incentive's correctness therefore depends on each node acting in its own retention interest, not on any external auditor (`COX-SOSP-03`). | The authors state explicitly the target is "greedy users, not arbitrarily malicious ones": the scheme "does not stop a node that promises to store data and then immediately discards it," so it certifies a claim's existence, not the original object's continued retrievability (`COX-SOSP-03`). The authors state they "cannot see how to build an equivalent placeholder for a renewable resource such as bandwidth," because monitoring bandwidth's use would itself consume it — a structural limit the mechanism's own authors identify, confining it to non-renewable resources (same source). | Replication across multiple independent sites, since a per-replica probabilistic discard assumes replication is already the availability strategy in use, not an add-on (`COX-SOSP-03`); an underlying placement layer (Pastiche over Pastry in the paper) to choose replica sites, since Samsara performs no lookup or routing of its own (same source). |
| Credit balances (a persistent per-participant scalar balance debited on consumption and credited on contribution — KARMA's distributed bank-set ledger; Dandelion's centralized credit-clearing server; Golle et al.'s micro-payment and point-based mechanisms as the underlying game-theoretic case for both) | KARMA reports one closed-form probability, not a measurement: an attacker controlling 10% of a 1,000,000-node network has under a 5.6×10⁻¹² chance of controlling a majority of a single 64-member bank-set, and under 5.6×10⁻⁶ chance of controlling a majority of at least one bank-set network-wide; its inflation-correction protocol needs O(N²) messages per epoch (`VISHNUMURTHY-P2PECON-03`). Dandelion's centralized credit server plateaus at approximately 1,200 decryption-key responses per second on a single Xeon 3 GHz PlanetLab-class machine at a 4-5 Mb/s access link — a CPU-bound ceiling supporting roughly 1,200 simultaneous 128 KB/s clients; per-operation server cryptography costs about 109 microseconds (`SIRIVIANOS-USENIXATC-07`). Golle et al.'s micro-payment and point mechanisms converge to a stable strategy distribution within roughly 4,000-5,000 simulated periods in a Q-learning simulation, not a deployed system (`GOLLE-EC-01`). | KARMA's bank-set corruption bound holds only conditional on an underlying secure-routing distributed hash table tolerating up to 25% adversarial nodes, a bound imported from Castro et al., not derived within KARMA itself (`VISHNUMURTHY-P2PECON-03`). Dandelion's non-manipulability proof requires every client to hold one authenticated, paid account with the provider, so no client can boost credit by presenting multiple identities (`SIRIVIANOS-USENIXATC-07`). Golle et al.'s micro-payment and point mechanisms require a server, or equivalent trusted component, observing every download and upload transaction and attributing each to a persistent agent identity (`GOLLE-EC-01`). | KARMA states plainly it "permit[s] Sybil attacks but limit[s] the rate at which they can be launched," and its own bank-node compensation fix is presented with the caveat that it risks "a runaway chain reaction of compensatory transactions" unless dampened, and may violate the currency's zero-sum property (`VISHNUMURTHY-P2PECON-03`). Dandelion's proof depends on one server (or a cluster sharing a consistent ledger) being reachable and responsive for every single chunk exchange between any two clients; the authors state explicitly "we trade scalability for the ability to provide robust incentives" (`SIRIVIANOS-USENIXATC-07`). Golle et al.'s quantized micro-payment variant is vulnerable to a coalition attack once one file in a billing block has been downloaded, since the remaining files in that block carry zero marginal cost (`GOLLE-EC-01`). | KARMA requires a secure-routing DHT (built on Pastry) mapping a hashed identifier to its k nearest bank-set members, and persistent public/private keys per participant for signed balance transfers (`VISHNUMURTHY-P2PECON-03`). Dandelion requires a centralized, identity-verifying, paid registration process external to the peer-to-peer protocol, and short-lived per-transaction tickets to bound abandoned-exchange denial of service (`SIRIVIANOS-USENIXATC-07`). Both leave resource pricing to an external, unspecified decision: KARMA states pricing is "left entirely to the application," and Golle et al.'s equilibria hold only for a per-file coefficient alpha satisfying two inequality constraints the paper does not derive a value for (`VISHNUMURTHY-P2PECON-03`, `GOLLE-EC-01`). |
| Voluntary contribution (no enforced reciprocity; the observed baseline before any incentive mechanism is added) | On unmodified Gnutella, 66% of 33,335 observed hosts share zero files, rising to about 69% once NAT-obscured hosts are accounted for; 73% share 10 or fewer files. The top 1% of hosts hold 37% of all shared file volume and the top 20% hold 98%. Among hosts with at least one file to offer, 63% never returned a single query response; the top 1% of responding hosts answered 47% of all query-response traffic and the top 25% answered 98% (24-hour capture, August 2000, `ADAR-FM-00`). | None — the mechanism assumes altruism alone suffices, with no verification, no reciprocity enforcement, and no cost imposed on non-contribution. | Serving capacity concentrates in a small fraction of participants; the paper's own words for the resulting top-1% population are that "these few providers act as a rather centralized server" (`ADAR-FM-00`) — the population an architecture meant to avoid single points of capture would recreate voluntarily. | Nothing — which is the finding itself: no accountability signal exists here for any other component to observe or build on. |

### Selection

Two different mechanisms are selected, one per resource, because the corpus shows no single candidate covering both, and one entry in the corpus states the structural reason directly: Samsara's own authors, having built a placeholder-claim mechanism for storage, write that they "cannot see how to build an equivalent placeholder for a renewable resource such as bandwidth," because monitoring bandwidth's use would itself consume it (`COX-SOSP-03`). Storage is a persistent, auditable state a random challenge can interrogate at any later moment; bandwidth exists only during the transfer that consumes it and must be verified in the moment or not at all.

For storage capacity, the selection is payment verified by a storage-retrievability proof: a blockchain-recorded transaction released automatically when a host answers a randomly timed challenge, built on the Shacham-Waters compact proof-of-retrievability construction and enforced the way Sia enforces it — reading only public data (`VORICK-SIA-14`, `SHACHAM-ASIACRYPT-08`).

For bandwidth, the selection is reciprocal exchange refined toward a proportional-share auction (PropShare) rather than left as classic discretized tit-for-tat: each peer returns upload bandwidth to its neighbors in proportion to what each neighbor sent it in the prior round, rather than splitting capacity equally among a fixed number of top bidders (`LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08`). This distinction matters because classic tit-for-tat is exactly what a strategic client exploits: BitTyrant extracts a median 1.72x download-rate gain from BitTorrent's discretized, equal-split reward, because the top (s-1) bidders at any peer all receive an identical 1/s share regardless of how much more the highest of them contributed, so minimally outbidding the current floor is a free strategy (`PIATEK-NSDI-07`, `LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08`). PropShare closes this by construction — the paper proves algebraically that a fixed total contribution split across any number of Sybil identities returns the identical aggregate bandwidth as one identity would have received, and measures a deviating peer's best-response gain at under 1% of download speed even though that peer's optimal contribution differs from the proportional allocation by roughly 30% (`LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08`).

Against payment verified by a bandwidth-forwarding proof (TorCoin) or a witnessed-capacity proof (Proof of Backhaul) as the bandwidth mechanism: Proof of Backhaul is the corpus's strongest evidence that verifying bandwidth cheaply is possible at all — under 5% error using about 3.5 MB of challenge traffic with 6 or more witnessing challengers over a 100 ms window, accurate to under 10% error up to 1,000 Mbps (`SHENG-NDSS-24`) — but it measures a node's aggregate link capacity, not which specific peer benefited from a specific transfer, so it does not by itself settle a payment between two communicating peers; it needs a witnessing group of otherwise-uninvolved challengers as an added party the reciprocal-exchange candidate does not need. TorCoin attempts exactly the missing connection, minting currency from a proof that one specific circuit forwarded traffic, at a stated approximately-5% overhead (`GHOSH-HOTPETS-14`) — but the authors label that figure "Preliminary Results" from a Python-Twisted message-passing simulation, not a measurement of packets moving between real relays, so it does not clear the bar this corpus sets for treating a number as a measured cost. Reciprocal exchange's own overhead figure — under 0.1% of bandwidth for piece announcement — is drawn from real deployments (`COHEN-IPTPS-03`), which the bandwidth-forwarding-proof evidence does not yet reach.

Against credit balances (KARMA, Dandelion) for either resource: both variants leave resource pricing to a decision the corpus does not supply. KARMA states the resource-pricing mechanism is "left entirely to the application," and its own second-order incentive problem — bank-set members have no built-in reason to perform bank-node work honestly — is presented with a proposed fix the authors flag as risking "a runaway chain reaction of compensatory transactions" (`VISHNUMURTHY-P2PECON-03`); a storage-retrievability proof instead settles price at contract formation and releases payment automatically on a passed challenge, with no discretionary gap (`VORICK-SIA-14`). Dandelion demonstrates the throughput a centralized credit clearinghouse can sustain — about 1,200 operations per second on one commodity server (`SIRIVIANOS-USENIXATC-07`) — but its own non-manipulability proof requires that single server to be a mandatory party to every chunk exchange, which the authors describe as a deliberate trade: "we trade scalability for the ability to provide robust incentives" (same source). Reciprocal exchange needs no such third party in the transfer path (`COHEN-IPTPS-03`), and a storage-retrievability proof's third party is a ledger recording public data, not a continuously available clearinghouse authenticating every individual exchange.

Against symmetric barter without a proof (Samsara) for storage: Samsara enforces that a consumer holds an equal-sized claim but makes no retrievability claim about the original object at all — the authors state the scheme "does not stop a node that promises to store data and then immediately discards it" (`COX-SOSP-03`). A storage-retrievability proof directly answers the question Samsara's authors say they do not address: the extraction guarantee recovers a rho-fraction of the file from any server passing an epsilon-fraction of challenges (`SHACHAM-ASIACRYPT-08`).

Against voluntary contribution for either resource: under no enforced reciprocity, 66-69% of a measured Gnutella population shared zero files and the top 1% of hosts answered 47% of query-response traffic, a concentration the source paper itself describes as producing "a rather centralized server" (`ADAR-FM-00`). Both selected mechanisms replace this by making non-contribution costly rather than merely hoped against: an unmet storage-proof challenge window routes contract funds to an unspendable address with no client action required (`VORICK-SIA-14`), and reciprocal exchange withholds upload bandwidth from a non-reciprocating peer by construction (`COHEN-IPTPS-03`).

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

Storage-proof payment:
- A blockchain or equivalent ledger that supplies per-challenge-window randomness the prover cannot bias in advance, and that executes the payment transaction automatically on a valid proof while redirecting funds on a missed window — no client-side verification action is needed (`VORICK-SIA-14`).
- A content-addressed storage and transfer layer beneath the proof protocol, since Get/Put move the actual bytes outside the proof exchange (`BENET-FILECOIN-17`).
- An erasure code whose rate rho, whose challenge-index count l, and whose coefficient set B are chosen jointly, not independently, so that the extraction bound epsilon-omega stays positive and non-negligible (`SHACHAM-ASIACRYPT-08`).
- A separate payment-channel primitive to release funds per delivered part during retrieval, distinct from the storage-proof settlement, since a storage proof certifies continued possession, not willingness to transfer (`BENET-FILECOIN-17`).
- A multi-host placement or erasure-coding layer above the single-host contract: Sia's own paper states "storing data on a single untrusted host guarantees little in the way of availability, bandwidth, or general quality of service" (`VORICK-SIA-14`).

Reciprocal exchange for bandwidth:
- A peer-discovery channel (tracker, DHT, or both) able to return a candidate set larger and more frequently refreshed than a default 50-100-peer neighborhood, since extracting reciprocation from high-capacity peers needs hundreds of candidate connections (`PIATEK-NSDI-07`).
- A per-piece or per-chunk integrity check that gates whether a peer is willing to advertise a piece as held, since that same held-piece signal feeds both reciprocation accounting and rarest-first piece selection (`COHEN-IPTPS-03`).
- Bidirectional connections between exchanging peers, since choking withholds only the upload direction and leaves the same connection able to resume without renegotiation (`COHEN-IPTPS-03`).
- Each peer's own observation of how much bandwidth a given neighbor sent it in the prior round or rounds — no observation of the neighbor's total capacity or its bids to other peers (`LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08`).
- Acceptance of an unconditional, low-cost bootstrap allocation for newly joined peers with nothing yet to reciprocate; this is the same allocation BitThief and the large-view exploit consume, and the corpus states no closed defense for it (`LOCHER-HOTNETS-06`).

### What it costs and where it fails

Storage-proof payment costs 20 bytes per challenge and 40 bytes per response at 80-bit security for the publicly-verifiable construction, plus a 2x storage overhead beyond the erasure code's own expansion at one sector per block, reducible to (1+1/s)x at the cost of a (1+s)x longer response (`SHACHAM-ASIACRYPT-08`); Filecoin's design target of a 10-100x sealing-time slowdown relative to the challenge-response step is a stated design parameter, not a measured benchmark, since the paper reports no implementation figures at all (`BENET-FILECOIN-17`). It stops working when the challenge randomness can be biased by the party being audited — Sia states an attacker controlling 50% of block-producing power can manipulate roughly 50% of challenges (`VORICK-SIA-14`) — or when the underlying proof construction certifies only an aggregate property across many blocks rather than individual-block possession, which Shacham and Waters' own attack on a prior scheme (E-PDP) exploits to cheat with near-9% success while storing no more than an honest host (`SHACHAM-ASIACRYPT-08`).

Reciprocal exchange costs under 0.1% of bandwidth for piece announcement in real deployments (`COHEN-IPTPS-03`); a strategic client extracts a median 1.72x download-rate gain over an unmodified reference client (a 1 Mb/s-class client's median gain is 70%) across 114 real swarms of 300-800 peers, both clients capped at 128 KB/s upload (`PIATEK-NSDI-07`), and enlarging the candidate-peer set to extract that gain raises the strategic client's own protocol overhead from 0.9% to 1.9% of received data (same source). PropShare reduces the corresponding best-response gain to under 1% of download speed (`LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08`). It stops working, in the sense of admitting a zero-contribution client, whenever a client targets the mechanism's unconditional bootstrap path instead of its reciprocation logic: BitThief completes every one of 7 tested torrents while uploading zero data, at roughly 4-5x the reference client's completion time in seeder-scarce swarms (`LOCHER-HOTNETS-06`), and the large-view exploit wins 12 of 15 public-torrent trials by holding a swarm view roughly 5x the compliant default (`SIRIVIANOS-IPTPS-07`). This failure is bounded, not unbounded: at a 60% free-rider population share in a controlled PlanetLab swarm, the large-view exploit's own advantage reverses and compliant peers finish faster than free-riders (`SIRIVIANOS-IPTPS-07`), and a community that verifies its own sharing ratio (TorrentLeech, contrasted against the open tracker Mininova in the same study) shows measurably more balanced per-peer contribution under the identical exploit (`LOCHER-HOTNETS-06`).

### What the corpus does not settle

No entry reports a measured, not simulated or design-stated, cost or throughput for a storage-retrievability proof running against a live, adversarial storage-provider population at production scale: Filecoin's paper reports zero implementation benchmarks (`BENET-FILECOIN-17`), and Sia's paper reports no deployment measurement of its storage-proof mechanism at all (`VORICK-SIA-14`).

No entry connects a witnessed bandwidth-capacity proof (Proof of Backhaul) to a per-transfer payment settlement measured on live traffic between two specific peers. TorCoin is the corpus's only attempt at that connection and its overhead figures are simulated, not measured (`GHOSH-HOTPETS-14`); whether a witnessed-challenge bandwidth proof can be composed with reciprocal exchange or with per-transfer payment at the low overhead Proof of Backhaul demonstrates for aggregate capacity measurement alone is not addressed by any retrieved source.

No entry proposes and measures a countermeasure to the large-view exploit against reciprocal exchange. Sirivianos et al.'s own synchronized-peer-assignment proposal — the countermeasure their paper describes — is stated as unimplemented, with its governing parameters (the interval duration, the post-interval connection delay, and the per-client allowed connection count) explicitly left as future work (`SIRIVIANOS-IPTPS-07`); a system relying on reciprocal exchange for bandwidth has no measured defense against this exploit to select from this corpus, only a bounded-failure observation (the exploit's advantage reverses once free-riders are a majority of the swarm).

No entry states how to jointly select a storage proof's erasure-code rate and its challenge-window frequency against a stated participant-churn rate for an open, volunteer-scale population. Storj's own erasure and audit parameters are stated as values that "may change in the future," chosen for that project's own measured churn (`LI-IWQOS-23-STORJ`), not derived from a general formula independent of a specific deployment's measured turnover.

No entry measures what fraction of a real population would supply nonzero storage or bandwidth once the specific free-riding paths this selection is exposed to (the unconditional bootstrap allocation, the large-view exploit) are closed by whatever defense is eventually built; the free-riding measurements in this corpus (`ADAR-FM-00`, `LOCHER-HOTNETS-06`, `SIRIVIANOS-IPTPS-07`) measure exploit success against the mechanism as currently specified, not the residual contributing population after a fix.

No entry measures Dandelion-style or KARMA-style credit balances at a scale or under an adversarial condition comparable to the two selected mechanisms' own evidence, so the comparison above rests on KARMA's closed-form probability and Dandelion's single-machine throughput ceiling, not on a deployed credit-balance system operating at the population scale Walrus, Storj, or real BitTorrent swarms are measured at.


---

## Group Encryption: A decentralized ratchet tree over causal broadcast (BeeKEM) reaches the same logarithmic per-update cost as a centralized ratchet tree, without a total-order delivery service, at the price of an unproved causal-broadcast substrate and a conjectured, not proved, tension between partition recovery and forward secrecy

Continuous group key agreement (CGKA) is the protocol family that lets a set of members holding one
shared symmetric group key add members, remove members, and periodically refresh that key, so that
the key stays useless to anyone who is not a current member (forward secrecy, FS: past keys stay
secret after a later compromise) and recovers its usefulness once a compromised member takes a
further protocol step (post-compromise security, PCS). Every candidate below is a way to run CGKA;
they differ in what ordering guarantee they demand from the layer that delivers protocol messages
between members, and in what that demand costs in bandwidth and proof strength.

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost, as a function of group size n | Security assumption | Failure condition | Requires from other components | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| TreeKEM / MLS ratchet tree, sequential operation | O(log n) hashes and public-key operations, sender and recipient, per Update/Add/Remove | PRG + IND-CPA public-key encryption (PKE); proof holds only against a non-adaptive adversary, one that commits to every corruption before the protocol runs | Adaptive-adversary security is only sketched; the reduction needs block-cipher keys of order n·log(n) bits to be meaningful, which the paper's own authors call "practically speaking, unrealistic" | A delivery mechanism supplying one consistent per-session total order of protocol messages to every member; an external PKI supplying initial member keys, with peer long-term-key authentication left as an unmodeled out-of-band step | BHARGAVAN-HAL-18, ALWEN-CRYPTO-20 |
| Same construction, under real concurrent proposal/commit load | Expected commit cost Ω(n·log₂(1+P/(P+C))), P proposals and C commits per round, derived over a randomized-operation model — already "over √n" at one proposal per commit, reaching an exponent of 0.99 (near-linear) at 50 proposals per commit; separately, Ω(n) worst case for any sequence of adds followed by refresh rounds among the remaining active members, proved for any CGKA construction that uses PKE only through its encrypt/decrypt interface | Same as row above, plus the black-box-PKE restriction the worst-case proof needs | The worst-case bound is proved to hold for "every currently known practical CGKA protocol, including every TreeKEM variant" that treats PKE as a sealed component | Same as row above | AUERBACH-CRYPTO-25, BIENSTOCK-TCC-22 |
| CoCoA (batched-round variant) | ⌈log n⌉+1 rounds to heal every compromised member, proved regardless of how the server breaks concurrent-update ties; cumulative per-user communication O(log²n) when the compromised set is known, O(n·log²n) total when it is not | IND-CPA encryption + UF-CMA signatures; "partially active" adversary — can leak any member's state adaptively but cannot forge protocol messages, because it never obtains signing keys through corruption | A fully active adversary (one that can forge as a corrupted member) is explicitly open, not proved; distributing n fresh signature-verification keys after any update costs Ω(n) per recipient regardless of round count, an unavoidable floor the paper itself proves | A server that computes an individualized packet per recipient, not a passive relay — cannot be swapped for a dumb broadcast channel without losing the bandwidth result; still needs synchronized rounds and one consistent batch order | ALWEN-EUROCRYPT-22 |
| MLS-Cutoff (bounded-concurrency fix to plain TreeKEM) | Expected commit/proposal size O(log n) at a constant proposal rate P; still Ω(n) once proposal rate scales with group size, "matching plain MLS" | Same as the MLS insider-security proof it follows: IND-CCA2 PKE, SEUF-CMA signatures, random-oracle-modeled hash/KDF/MAC | Falls back to the same Ω(n) worst case as plain TreeKEM once concurrency scales with group size; evaluated only under a uniform-random proposer/committer model, explicitly not a claim about real deployment traffic | Same total-order delivery server and tree-hash/parent-hash consistency checks as plain MLS — the fix changes only how proposals are absorbed into the tree | AUERBACH-CRYPTO-25 |
| ART (Asynchronous Ratchet Trees) | O(log n) exponentiations and bandwidth, sender and recipient, per Update/Add/Remove; O(n)/O(log n) setup exponentiations (sender/other) | Diffie-Hellman-based PRF-ODH hardness; Tamarin-verified secrecy of the initial group key, but only under the assumption that no member the agent believes present has been compromised — sender authentication and malicious-insider-subset security are both out of scope by design | Executability under concurrent conflicting updates is an unsolved open problem; the paper's own two mitigations (a grace window accepting stale-keyed messages, or transport-layer rejection of out-of-order updates) are informal and unproved | Reliable broadcast of updated public keys to the whole group after every update, plus one of the two informal concurrency mitigations — an ordering dependency structurally like TreeKEM's, but never formalized | COHNGORDON-CCS-18 |
| DeCAF (blockchain-total-order variant) | O(n·log(n)·log(t)) cumulative sender+recipient communication to heal t corrupted members in ⌊log t⌋+1 epochs, independent of which members are corrupted; in the decentralized instantiation, per-user download communication rises to O(n·log²n), losing CoCoA's O(log²n) recipient-bandwidth advantage entirely, by the paper's own comparison | IND-CPA secretly-key-updatable PKE (skuPKE) + random-oracle-modeled hashing; formally proved, concrete bound (O(εEnc·2(nQ²)²), t, Q)-secure | Forward secrecy requires deleting superseded keys timed to blockchain finality, and the paper gives no confirmation-depth value for when that deletion is safe on a forkable (longest-chain) blockchain; recording only a message hash on-chain to control chain cost loses the paper's own robustness properties unless data availability is separately solved, which it is not | An append-only, globally agreed-upon transcript — instantiated as a blockchain providing consensus (a strictly total order), not merely causal delivery — plus that chain's own liveness guarantee | ALWEN-SCN-24 |
| Weidner, Kleppmann, Hugenroth, Beresford DCGKA ("WKHB") | Highest measured CPU time of three systems compared, sender and recipient, at every tested group size 8 to 512, measured by an independent Java reimplementation built by the BeeKEM authors for their own benchmark; asymptotically O(n) sender / O(1) recipient for Update/Remove, O(n²+h_D) storage, where h_D is the size of WKHB's own membership history — figure independently re-derived, matching, by two further papers | Not assessable from this corpus: the original paper (Weidner, Kleppmann, Hugenroth, Beresford, CCS 2021) was not retrieved as a primary source; every figure here traces to other papers' reimplementation or re-derivation of it | No proof-strength comparison possible without the primary source | Decentralized: a reliable broadcast/gossip layer among members, no total-order delivery service, per every citing paper's characterization | YEN-EPRINT-26 (measured); ALWEN-EUROCRYPT-22, ALWEN-SCN-24 (independent derivations, secondary) |
| BeeKEM (selected) | O(log n) sender and recipient cost for Update/Remove in the no-concurrency case, measured as the lowest CPU time of three systems compared at every tested group size 8–512; under a network partition of 64 members into 4 groups of 16 with U members updating during the partition, total post-partition recovery CPU time and network traffic rise linearly with U then plateau once every member has updated at least once | Post-compromise security and forward secrecy reduced, by a proved game-hop argument, to the security of a non-interactive key exchange (NIKE) under the paper's own HKR-CKS notion plus IND-CPA symmetric encryption; strong convergence, remove-liveness, add-liveness-under-remove-wins, and no-secret-after-merge are proved properties; the tension between partition recovery (Correctness Under Concurrency) and forward secrecy is explicitly conjectured, not proved | Achieves only κ-parameterized forward secrecy: a member who has not updated recently exposes every group secret since her last update if compromised; deleting old secrets promptly to strengthen personal forward secrecy forfeits recovery of group secrets defined on a different partition branch once that partition heals; threat model is honest-but-partition-prone, not Byzantine | Authenticated causal broadcast (ACB): causally-ordered, eventually-reliable, sender-authenticated delivery, no group-wide total order; the shipped implementation builds ACB from a strictly weaker reliable broadcast protocol (RBP, e.g. a gossip network) via a hash-linked DAG plus signatures, a construction cited to prior work rather than proved in this paper; an external PKI for initial member keys; per-device persistent storage of the operation graph, since a new member's join cost is dominated by O(h_B) replay of it | YEN-EPRINT-26 |
| Fork-Resilient CGKA (FREEK / O-FREEK) | No implementation and no benchmark of either construction exists in the corpus; "practical" (FREEK) versus "only theoretical efficiency" (O-FREEK) is the authors' own characterization; O-FREEK's punctured-key size structurally scales with fork-branch depth — how long a fork persists, not group size | FREEK achieves a security predicate weaker than optimal, because an init secret and a MAC key are shared across all members rather than personalized, so exposing any one member's state helps compromise more than that member's own secrets should allow; O-FREEK achieves the paper's optimal predicate via hierarchical-identity-based (binary-tree) encryption and signatures | dMLS, Matrix's own competing fork-tolerant CGKA, is stated by this paper to reach the same any-causal-order processing property only by storing (not deleting) old secrets, "seriously weakening forward secrecy" — the one head-to-head comparison in this row shows causal-order tolerance and forward secrecy trading directly against each other unless FREEK's specific puncturing construction is used | Built on SAIK, a server-aided CGKA — still needs a delivery/mailboxing service for availability, though the paper states confidentiality and authenticity do not depend on that service behaving correctly; needs causality-respecting delivery per sender-receiver pair, weaker than a global total order but a real ordering requirement, distinct from BeeKEM's arbitrary-order requirement across the whole group | ALWEN-CRYPTO-23 |
| Sender Keys (base protocol; WhatsApp, Signal, up to 1,024 members) | O(1) ongoing bandwidth and symmetric encryptions per message (one signed, hash-chained ciphertext per sender); O(n) Add, O(n²) Remove communication complexity, derived not implemented | Proved (Theorem 1) to meet only a weak security notion | A malicious server can mount a censorship attack and add arbitrary members undetected, because control messages carry no per-message authentication of their own; two independent papers disagree on the base protocol's PCS: one finds it restores a weak PCS after an on-demand or removal-triggered update, the other, describing the base protocol with no update operation modeled, finds it exposes all future sender keys immediately on any compromise via deterministic hash-chaining | Authenticated pairwise two-party channels between every member pair, n(n−1)/2 of them, independently maintained, for key distribution; a central server providing total ordering of control messages | BALBAS-ASIACRYPT-23, BIENSTOCK-TCC-20 |
| Sender Keys+ (PCS-bearing update mechanism) | Update/PCS-refresh complexity O(n), down from a naive Signal-style O(n²), derived not implemented | Proved (Theorem 2) to meet a stronger, but still bounded, security notion | Requires total ordering of control messages specifically to avoid overlapping updates — the same delivery-order dependency the ratchet-tree family carries; even a triggered update does not restore PCS over a given pairwise channel unless that specific channel has separately "healed" through its own round-trip exchange since the exposure, which the paper states does not hold "by default" for member pairs that do not exchange private messages regularly | Same pairwise-channel and total-order requirements as the base protocol | BALBAS-ASIACRYPT-23 |
| Pairwise Double Ratchet fan-out, no shared group key (e.g. Apple iMessage, Signal multi-device) | O(n) sender exponentiations and bandwidth, O(1) recipient, on every single message sent — not only on membership-change operations, since there is no shared group key at all | Diffie-Hellman-based PRF-ODH hardness per pairwise channel, formally verified for the two-party case | Cost grows without bound in group size on every message, not merely on rarer key-update operations | n(n−1) independent pairwise channels, each individually maintained; no group-wide ordering or consensus primitive required at all, since every channel is independent | COHNGORDON-CCS-18, COHNGORDON-EUROSP-17 |

### Selection

BeeKEM is selected: a decentralized CGKA protocol whose members exchange protocol messages over
authenticated causal broadcast (no group-wide total order) and whose key-refresh, member-add, and
member-remove operations cost O(log n) in the common, no-concurrency case, degrading gracefully
rather than catastrophically as concurrent operations increase.

Against the TreeKEM/MLS baseline and its total-order-preserving descendants (CoCoA, MLS-Cutoff):
every one of them requires a delivery mechanism that supplies one consistent order of protocol
messages to every group member in a session (ALWEN-CRYPTO-20), a requirement the same paper's own
Section 8.3 attack shows is not merely inconvenient but load-bearing — a leaked state from one
sibling under concurrent, unordered delivery recovers a group key no honest party should be able to
compute, and the authors state this generalizes to every TreeKEM variant they are aware of. That
total order is exactly the coordination point a decentralized architecture removes elsewhere;
building it back in for group encryption alone reintroduces a central or consensus-backed
dependency the rest of the system does not otherwise need. Even granting a working central server,
TreeKEM's real cost under uniformly random concurrent proposals reaches near-linear (exponent 0.99
of n) at moderate concurrency (AUERBACH-CRYPTO-25), and any construction using PKE as a sealed
component is proved to hit Ω(n) worst case regardless of concurrency, on ordinary add-then-refresh
sequences (BIENSTOCK-TCC-22) — a bound that governs TreeKEM, CoCoA, and MLS-Cutoff alike, since none
exploits PKE's internal algebraic structure to escape it. BeeKEM's measured cost, by contrast, rises
with the number of members who updated during a partition and then plateaus, rather than continuing
to grow with group size (YEN-EPRINT-26 Fig. 5) — a different, and for a partition-tolerant
architecture more favorable, degradation shape than TreeKEM's.

Against ART, the earlier Diffie-Hellman-tree construction that produced the pairwise/Sender-Keys
comparison table cited throughout this document: ART's O(log n) cost figures match TreeKEM's, but
its concurrency handling is not formalized at all — the paper offers two informal mitigations for
concurrent conflicting updates and states a complete solution is an open research question
(COHNGORDON-CCS-18) — whereas BeeKEM proves strong convergence, remove-liveness,
add-liveness-under-remove-wins, and no-secret-after-merge as theorems, not informal mitigations
(YEN-EPRINT-26).

Against DeCAF, the closest attempt at decentralizing the same batched-round family: DeCAF replaces
a single company's server with a blockchain, but a blockchain supplies consensus — a strictly
stronger and more expensive coordination primitive than the causal broadcast BeeKEM needs — and the
DeCAF paper's own comparison shows the decentralized instantiation losing CoCoA's recipient-bandwidth
advantage entirely, rising from O(log²n) to O(n·log²n) per user (ALWEN-SCN-24). DeCAF also leaves
the confirmation depth needed for safe key deletion on a forkable chain unspecified, an unresolved
parameter BeeKEM's causal-broadcast substrate does not need, since it assumes no chain fork to wait
out.

Against Weidner et al.'s DCGKA ("WKHB"), the direct predecessor in the same decentralized,
causal-order family BeeKEM belongs to: WKHB matches BeeKEM's ordering requirement exactly, but in
the one head-to-head measurement available — BeeKEM's own authors' reimplementation of both
protocols in the same benchmark harness — WKHB has the highest measured CPU time of the three
systems compared, for both sender and recipient roles, at every tested group size from 8 to 512
(YEN-EPRINT-26 Fig. 4), consistent with its O(n) sender-side asymptotic cost against BeeKEM's
O(log n). This document cannot compare the two constructions' proof strength, because the original
WKHB paper was not retrieved as a primary source into this corpus; the comparison rests on the
measured cost gap alone, which is sufficient to reject WKHB given no evidence entry supplies an
offsetting proof property BeeKEM lacks.

Against Fork-Resilient CGKA (FREEK/O-FREEK): FREEK is architecturally still server-based, built on
SAIK, a server-aided CGKA — it relaxes what a member may do with out-of-order messages once
received, not the underlying delivery infrastructure's role, and no implementation or benchmark of
either FREEK or O-FREEK exists anywhere in the corpus, so no cost comparison against BeeKEM's
measured figures is possible (ALWEN-CRYPTO-23). Where the corpus does offer one comparable data
point — Matrix's own dMLS, which reaches similar causal-order tolerance — that construction is
stated to reach it only by weakening forward secrecy, the exact trade-off BeeKEM's κ parameter is
designed to make explicit and adjustable rather than silently accept.

Against Sender Keys and Sender Keys+: the base protocol's O(1) per-message cost is real, but it
achieves this by giving up post-compromise security almost entirely — one source finds it exposes
every future sender key immediately on any compromise via deterministic hash-chaining
(BIENSTOCK-TCC-20), a stronger negative claim than a second source's finding that a later triggered
update restores a weak form of PCS (BALBAS-ASIACRYPT-23); the two papers examine different versions
of the protocol (one with no update operation modeled, one that formalizes the update Sender Keys
does support), and BALBAS-ASIACRYPT-23's is the more applicable figure for a deployment that
performs updates, since that is the version actually shipped. Even under that more favorable
reading, restoring PCS with Sender Keys+ costs O(n) and requires total ordering of control messages
specifically to avoid overlapping updates (BALBAS-ASIACRYPT-23) — the same centralization dependency
being rejected above, arrived at from a different direction. Post-compromise security and forward
secrecy are part of this component's defining function, stated in the task that produced this
document; a candidate that gives up PCS to hold cost at O(1), or gives it back only by reimporting a
total-order server, does not meet that function.

Against pure pairwise Double Ratchet fan-out: this candidate achieves real, formally verified PCS
with no group-wide ordering dependency of any kind (COHNGORDON-CCS-18, COHNGORDON-EUROSP-17), which
is the strongest partition-tolerance property in this table. Its cost, however, is O(n) sender
bandwidth and computation on every single message sent, because there is no shared group key at
all — every recipient decrypts an independently ratcheted copy. BeeKEM holds one shared symmetric
group key, so its O(log n) figures apply only to membership-change and key-refresh operations;
ordinary message encryption under that shared key is O(1) regardless of group size. Pairwise fan-out
pays its O(n) cost on every message a group ever sends, not only when membership changes — a
materially worse steady-state cost for any group that exchanges more messages than it changes
membership.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- The transport/broadcast component selected elsewhere must supply Authenticated Causal Broadcast
  (ACB) to every BeeKEM group: causally-ordered, eventually-reliable, sender-authenticated delivery,
  with no total order required across the group (YEN-EPRINT-26). BeeKEM's own implementation
  achieves this by extending a strictly weaker Reliable Broadcast Protocol (RBP) — the paper's own
  example is a gossip network all group members participate in — into ACB via a hash-linked
  directed acyclic graph of operations plus digital signatures, a construction the paper cites to
  prior work (Kleppmann and Howard's Byzantine-eventual-consistency result; Kleppmann's
  CRDT-Byzantine-fault-tolerance construction) rather than proving inside this paper. Any transport
  chosen for the wider architecture must either already meet ACB, or must be extended by the same
  cited construction before BeeKEM's security proofs apply to it as stated.
- The identity component must supply a public key infrastructure (PKI), used by BeeKEM as an
  external black box, that lets a member obtain another member's initial public key before adding
  them to a group. BeeKEM's own Add operation performs no identity check on the added public key
  beyond what that external PKI already vouches for, so any Sybil resistance, key-transparency, or
  key-recovery mechanism selected for identity applies before a key reaches BeeKEM, not inside it.
- Every group member's client must persist a durable local copy of both the current tree state and
  the full operation graph, or enough of it to replay from a known-valid starting point — a new
  member's join cost is dominated by O(h_B) replay of that history (h_B being the size of the
  operation graph), and a device that discards its history cannot independently verify or merge
  concurrent branches it did not witness firsthand.
- The cryptographic primitives selected for asymmetric key exchange must supply a non-interactive
  key exchange (NIKE) meeting the paper's own HKR-CKS security notion, not merely generic NIKE
  correctness, paired with a symmetric authenticated-encryption scheme whose key space is
  compatible; the paper's own implementation and benchmark instantiate this as elliptic-curve
  Diffie-Hellman plus ChaCha20-Poly1305, fixed and not varied in the evaluation.
- A system-wide or per-deployment policy must set the retention parameter κ — how many of a
  member's past personal secrets stay retained. This value is left unset by the paper and directly
  trades cross-branch partition recovery against forward secrecy; any component reasoning about
  forward secrecy elsewhere in the architecture (a privacy tier, a data-retention policy) needs to
  know which κ is in force for the groups it covers.
- Any component that composes with BeeKEM must not assume it defends against an actively malicious
  group member forging protocol messages: BeeKEM's threat model is honest-but-partition-prone
  (crash and network-partition faults only), and its proofs do not cover a Byzantine participant.

### What it costs and where it fails

Best case (no concurrent operations): the lowest measured CPU time of three systems compared, for
both sender and recipient roles, across group sizes 8 to 512, on a 16-core AMD CPU with 128 GB RAM
simulating all users and network on one machine, 5 runs per condition, median reported
(YEN-EPRINT-26 Fig. 4, Section 6.1). Asymptotically: O(log n) sender and recipient cost for Update
and Remove; O(log n) sender cost for Add, with O(1) cost to an existing recipient and O(h_B) cost to
a newly joining member who must replay the operation history. Each Update grows the welcome message
by 2.5 kB and adds 40 microseconds to a new member's processing time, in the sequential benchmark
setting.

Under partition: a group of 64 members split into 4 equal partitions of 16, with U members drawn
without replacement issuing an Update during the partition, U swept from 0 to 128 — total
post-partition recovery CPU time and cumulative network traffic rise linearly with U, then plateau
once every member has updated at least once; the cost of the very first post-partition Update grows
more slowly than the cumulative recovery cost as U increases (YEN-EPRINT-26 Fig. 5).

Where it fails: forward secrecy is only κ-parameterized, not full. A member who has not updated
recently must, by the protocol's own correctness requirement, retain access to every group secret
established since her last update — compromising that member exposes all of them, a limitation
BeeKEM inherits from centralized TreeKEM's own Forward-Secrecy-with-Updates weakness
(ALWEN-CRYPTO-20). Specific to the decentralized setting: a member who deletes an old personal
secret promptly, to strengthen her own forward secrecy, loses the ability to decrypt group secrets
defined on a different partition branch once that partition heals, so recovering secrets after a
partition heals is in direct tension with deleting old secrets promptly. The paper's own word is
that this tradeoff "may be inherent in decentralized settings" — a conjecture, not a proof.
Separately, the paper's security theorem is stated over ACB, while the shipped implementation only
guarantees the weaker RBP, with the RBP-to-ACB upgrade cited to other authors' work rather than
proved in this paper — a gap between what is proved and what is deployed that no entry in this
corpus closes.

### What the corpus does not settle

- No wall-clock or millisecond figures for BeeKEM's best-case comparison are extractable from the
  paper's text; YEN-EPRINT-26 records only the qualitative ranking (lowest of three) and the
  Big-O classification read from Fig. 4, a plotted figure, not a table of numeric values. A reader
  who needs concrete latency numbers has to consult the figure directly.
- No default or derived value for the retention parameter κ exists anywhere in the corpus.
  Deployers choose it themselves, trading cross-branch partition recovery against forward secrecy,
  with no measurement of that trade-off's effect at any specific κ.
- No proof of BeeKEM's security against an actively malicious (Byzantine) group member exists in
  this corpus. The construction needed to reach that setting — extending ACB to tolerate forged
  messages, not merely crash faults — is cited to other authors' work, not proved inside the BeeKEM
  paper itself.
- Whether Bienstock, Dodis, Garg, Grogan, Hajiabadi, and Rösler's Ω(n) worst-case black-box-PKE
  impossibility result (BIENSTOCK-TCC-22) applies to BeeKEM's NIKE-tree construction is not checked
  by any entry in this corpus. A 2026 construction that does state its relationship to that same
  impossibility result explicitly (Bartusek, Bitansky, Dodis, Garg, Wu, achieving worst-case
  polylogarithmic CGKA cost) states plainly that it "does not claim to remove" the impossibility
  result, and instead avoids its scope by using the internal structure of a lattice-based encryption
  scheme rather than treating public-key encryption as a sealed component (BARTUSEK-EPRINT-26) — the
  recent logarithmic-cost result operates outside the impossibility result's model rather than
  refuting it. BeeKEM's own NIKE-based construction plausibly escapes the same bound by the same
  general mechanism — direct use of Diffie-Hellman algebraic structure rather than opaque PKE
  encrypt/decrypt calls — but this is a reasoned inference from the structural similarity between
  BeeKEM's and BARTUSEK-EPRINT-26's escape routes, not a claim any entry in this corpus states or
  checks directly.
- No proof-strength comparison between BeeKEM and its direct predecessor, Weidner et al.'s DCGKA,
  is possible from this corpus, because the original DCGKA paper (Weidner, Kleppmann, Hugenroth,
  Beresford, CCS 2021) was never retrieved as a primary source. Every cost figure attributed to it
  here comes from other papers' reimplementation or asymptotic re-derivation.
- The largest group size measured for BeeKEM in this corpus is 512 members, and the largest
  partition experiment used 4 partitions of 16 members each. Whether the observed plateau in
  partition-recovery cost (rather than continued growth) holds at larger group sizes or higher
  partition counts is unmeasured by any entry in this corpus.
- What group size the wider client application actually needs to support in practice is not a
  number any entry in this corpus measures. BeeKEM's own motivating scenario, cited from a study of
  mesh-messaging tools used in large-scale protests, argues that groups of "thousands of members"
  make WKHB's O(n) update cost prohibitive (Albrecht, Blasco, Bjerg Jensen, Mareková, cited within
  YEN-EPRINT-26's bibliography), but this corpus contains no independent measurement of what group
  sizes the specific client being designed here will actually need to support.


---

## Transport: WebRTC data channels carry every connection touching a browser tab, QUIC carries every connection between two socket-capable processes, and onion-routed multi-hop encapsulation is a selectable tier layered on top of either, not a default

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (conditions) | Security assumption | Failure condition | Requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| QUIC (direct, socket-capable peers) | 8.0%/3.6% mean search-latency reduction, 18.0%/15.3% mean rebuffer-rate reduction, desktop/mobile, against TLS/TCP, over 1 billion samples [LANGLEY-SIGCOMM-17]. 88% of desktop connections and 65-68% of mobile connections reach 0-RTT handshake [LANGLEY-SIGCOMM-17]. Server CPU cost roughly 2x TLS/TCP after optimization [LANGLEY-SIGCOMM-17]. On a shared 5 Mbps/36ms-RTT bottleneck, one QUIC flow takes ~2x one competing Cubic-TCP flow's throughput and stays over half the link against 4 competing TCP flows [KAKHKI-IMC-17]. Mobile handsets spend 58% of connection time "Application Limited" (userspace packet processing bottleneck) versus 7% on desktop [KAKHKI-IMC-17]. Connection migration succeeds for only 52% of IPv4 / 78% of IPv6 QUIC servers that already completed a handshake with SNI supplied, 94% of the IPv6 figure concentrated in one hosting organization [BUCHET-CCR-25]. | TLS 1.3-integrated handshake; encrypted packet header leaves only flags and Connection ID unencrypted, so no other component may re-derive session identity from the IP/port 4-tuple [LANGLEY-SIGCOMM-17]. | No browser exposes a raw UDP socket API to page script; [SINGH-ARXIV-26]'s own implementation states directly that "web apps cannot create listening sockets in JavaScript," and the corpus's only browser-reachable network mechanisms are WebRTC and WebTransport (rows below) — QUIC by itself is unusable from an unprivileged browser tab. Throughput and 0-RTT advantages measurably shrink or vanish on mobile handsets [KAKHKI-IMC-17]. | A path-discovery mechanism from the NAT traversal component, since QUIC itself performs no rendezvous. The receiving side must key connection state by Connection ID, not the 4-tuple, for migration tolerance to hold [LANGLEY-SIGCOMM-17]; a fallback reconnection path is needed for the roughly half of servers that do not support active migration [BUCHET-CCR-25]. |
| TCP (direct, socket-capable peers, over TLS) | Hole-punch time ~56ms at 20ms RTT, close to QUIC's ~55ms [LIANG-ARXIV-24]. TCP hole-punch compatibility 64% versus UDP's 82% across a 68-vendor/8-OS NAT sample [FORD-USENIX-05]. Retransmission timeout floored at 1000ms (RFC 6298) versus QUIC's fixed 200ms bound — roughly 5x slower loss recovery under 1% injected loss [LIANG-ARXIV-24]. Outperforms QUIC specifically under packet reordering (9% measured on live Verizon 3G), because QUIC's fixed NACK threshold of 3 false-triggers loss detection where TCP's adaptive DSACK does not [KAKHKI-IMC-17]. | TLS layered on top; no encryption intrinsic to TCP itself. | Same browser-tab restriction as QUIC (no raw TCP socket API in page script). Lower hole-punch compatibility than UDP-based QUIC [FORD-USENIX-05]. Requires `SO_REUSEADDR`/`SO_REUSEPORT` socket options for one local port to both listen and originate outbound attempts [FORD-USENIX-05]. | Same rendezvous dependency as QUIC, from the NAT traversal component. |
| WebRTC data channels (SCTP over DTLS, negotiated via ICE) | Reachable from an unprivileged browser tab; capped at 500 concurrent `RTCPeerConnection` objects per browsing context [CHROMIUM-BLINK-SRC]. +15% CPU / +10% memory per additional simultaneous peer in a production peer-assisted-delivery deployment [TANG-ARXIV-22]. Real IP address and port of every matched peer disclosed in plaintext STUN Binding-success responses, regardless of mutual trust [TANG-ARXIV-22]. Corroborating (non-browser) connectivity data for the same class of UDP hole-punching mechanism ICE performs: ~21% of peers directly connectable, up to 64% more reachable via hole punching at >80% pairwise success (85% for the least-restricted NAT class) [HALKES-NETWORKING-11]. | DTLS handshake for data-channel encryption. STUN Binding responses are unauthenticated by protocol design [PETIT-HUGUENIN-RFC-18]; a reported candidate address must be corroborated by an actual successful connectivity check, not trusted outright. | Structurally cannot accept an unsolicited inbound connection — a browser tab needs an external channel to receive the first offer [SINGH-ARXIV-26]. Capped at 500 simultaneous peer connections per tab [CHROMIUM-BLINK-SRC]. Direct connection discloses each peer's real IP to every peer it is matched with unless relayed [TANG-ARXIV-22]; relay throughput then collapses to the relay path's own bottleneck under contention — measured falling from 88.49 Mbps direct to 9.28 Mbps relayed in a bandwidth-constrained scenario [DUARTE-ABAKOS-20]. | An out-of-band signaling channel (push notification, an already-open channel, or a human-relayed message) for the first SDP offer/answer exchange [SINGH-ARXIV-26]. STUN/TURN server infrastructure from the NAT traversal component. A long-term credential from the identity component for TURN relay authentication [REDDY-RFC-20]. |
| WebTransport | No evidence entry in this corpus. QUIC measurements ([LANGLEY-SIGCOMM-17], [KAKHKI-IMC-17], [BUCHET-CCR-25]) describe the underlying transport WebTransport carries, not the browser API itself — no entry measures WebTransport's own connection-establishment latency or success rate. | Same TLS 1.3-integrated QUIC handshake as the QUIC row, but the connection is client-to-named-server (the browser validates a server certificate), not peer-to-peer — a structural property of the API, not a measured figure in this corpus. | Unmeasured. Structurally does not perform ICE-style multi-candidate address discovery between two browser tabs, so it cannot supply arbitrary tab-to-tab connectivity without introducing a server role for every pair. | Unmeasured. |
| Onion-routed transport (Tor-style multi-hop) | Median page-fetch latency 2.8s (90th percentile 5.3s) versus 0.3s direct, on a live 32-relay network fetching a 55KB page [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]. 60MB file download averaged ~300s versus ~210s direct [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]. Fixed 512-byte cells (498 usable payload bytes); minimum 3-hop circuits; per-circuit congestion-control windows of 1000 cells [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]. | Defends against an adversary that does not simultaneously observe both circuit entry and exit; the authors state directly the design does not protect against a global passive adversary correlating traffic timing [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]. | Fails outright against a global passive observer [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]. The authors' own stated expectation for the measured clique-topology, full-visibility-directory design is that it needs restructuring before "a few hundred relays and maybe 10,000 users" — an expectation, not a measured ceiling [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]. | A signed, current relay directory reachable by every client before building a circuit [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]. Introduction points established in advance for a listener wanting unlinkable inbound reachability [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]. |

### Selection

The selection is composed by participant class, because no single mechanism is reachable from every participant this architecture must connect. WebRTC data channels (SCTP over DTLS, negotiated by ICE with STUN and TURN fallback) carry every connection where at least one side is an unprivileged browser tab. QUIC carries every connection between two socket-capable processes — a native mobile app, a desktop client, a relay or seed node — falling back to TCP exactly where QUIC's own discovery procedure already falls back to it: when the QUIC handshake is blocked or exceeds the path MTU. Onion-routed multi-hop encapsulation is a separate, user-selectable privacy tier layered on top of whichever of the above carries the actual bytes, not a default path for every connection.

**Against TCP as the general-purpose default (not merely QUIC's fallback):** [KAKHKI-IMC-17] found QUIC outperforms TCP on page load time in nearly every desktop scenario tested across a 5-100 Mbps / 0-100ms-RTT / 0-1%-loss matrix, and [LANGLEY-SIGCOMM-17] measured an 8.0%/3.6% mean search-latency reduction and an 18.0%/15.3% rebuffer-rate reduction (desktop/mobile) for QUIC against TLS/TCP at production scale (over 1 billion samples). QUIC's connection migration — surviving an IP-address change without a new handshake [LANGLEY-SIGCOMM-17] — matches a mobile participant switching from Wi-Fi to cellular without losing an open connection, a property TCP structurally lacks. TCP is retained only as a fallback, because [KAKHKI-IMC-17] also found TCP measurably outperforms QUIC under packet reordering (9% measured on live Verizon 3G) specifically because QUIC's fixed NACK threshold of 3 false-triggers loss detection where TCP's adaptive DSACK-based threshold does not — a condition a mobile participant on cellular data will encounter, so eliminating TCP entirely would remove the path that measurably performs better under that specific, common condition.

**Against WebTransport as the browser-facing candidate (instead of WebRTC data channels):** no entry in this corpus measures WebTransport's connection-establishment success rate, latency, or peer reachability — it is listed with no evidence. Independent of that gap, WebTransport's connection shape (a client validating a named server's certificate) does not perform the multi-candidate address exchange between two browser tabs that WebRTC's ICE machinery performs. A design whose client "reproduces the functional patterns of the most-visited websites" and has "no functional reason to return to a centralized service" needs arbitrary browser-tab-to-browser-tab connectivity; a mechanism shaped for client-to-known-server connectivity cannot supply that without introducing a server role for every pair, which is a structural mismatch this corpus does not quantify but which follows directly from the two mechanisms' differing connection models.

**Against onion-routed transport as the default path (rather than a tier):** [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]'s own measurement shows a median page-fetch latency of 2.8s through a 32-relay network against 0.3s direct — roughly a 9x latency multiple — and the same paper's authors state their own threat model excludes a global passive adversary, the exact class of adversary a user who chooses this tier is defending against. Running every connection through this multiple by default is incompatible with reproducing the functional patterns of the most-visited websites; a user who does not face a global passive adversary pays the 2.8s median unnecessarily if it is the default rather than a choice, which is why [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]'s own stated non-goal (no protection against a global passive adversary) is the deciding fact, not merely the latency number.

**Against QUIC or TCP as the sole default (excluding WebRTC entirely):** [SINGH-ARXIV-26]'s own implementation states directly that "web apps cannot create listening sockets in JavaScript," and the corpus contains no browser API exposing a raw UDP or TCP socket to page script — the only browser-reachable network mechanisms found in this corpus are WebRTC (`RTCPeerConnection`/`RTCDataChannel`) and WebTransport (rejected above). A default limited to QUIC or TCP would be unreachable from any participant running as an ordinary browser tab, which this architecture requires as its primary client shape.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- The out-of-band signaling channel for a browser-tab participant's first WebRTC offer/answer exchange, since a tab cannot accept an unsolicited inbound connection [SINGH-ARXIV-26], must be supplied by whichever component handles peer discovery or introduction: a push-notification path, a rendezvous relay, or an already-open data channel from a prior connection.
- STUN and TURN server infrastructure reachable by every WebRTC participant must be provisioned by the NAT traversal component. [PETIT-HUGUENIN-RFC-18] states STUN Binding responses are unauthenticated by protocol design, so it is the NAT traversal component's connectivity-check step, not this transport selection, that a receiving side must trust before accepting a candidate address as valid.
- The identity/credential component must issue a long-term credential that a TURN relay authenticates against before granting a relay allocation [REDDY-RFC-20].
- Continuous per-connection keepalive traffic must be budgeted for the full lifetime of every open path, not only at setup, because NAT and firewall UDP bindings expire. [HALKES-NETWORKING-11]'s measured field data (a real peer population) recommends an interval no longer than 55 seconds — tighter than [KERANEN-RFC-18]'s specified-but-unmeasured 15-second minimum default — so the measured figure, not the protocol default, should set the budget.
- For a QUIC connection between two socket-capable participants, the receiving side must key connection state by Connection ID rather than the network 4-tuple for passive migration tolerance to hold [LANGLEY-SIGCOMM-17], and any component depending on active migration for continuity needs a full-reconnection fallback, since [BUCHET-CCR-25] measured migration succeeding for only 52% of IPv4 and 78% of IPv6 QUIC servers that had already completed a handshake with SNI supplied.
- The storage and content-delivery components built on top of this transport cannot assume uniform throughput across all reachable pairs: [HALKES-NETWORKING-11] measured roughly 15% of a real peer population unreachable even after hole punching, and [DUARTE-ABAKOS-20] measured relay-path throughput collapsing to the relay's own bottleneck (9.28 Mbps against an 88.49 Mbps direct/reversal path in a bandwidth-constrained scenario) for the population that falls back to relay.
- The naming/registry component must distribute a signed relay directory reachable by every client before a client can build an onion-routed circuit for the privacy tier [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]; the transport layer does not supply this itself.

### What it costs and where it fails

The browser-facing half of this selection succeeds end-to-end for roughly the population [HALKES-NETWORKING-11] measured for UDP hole punching generally: about 21% of peers directly connectable plus up to 64% more reachable via hole punching at an 80%-or-higher pairwise success rate, leaving a minority on the order of 15% needing relay, at the throughput cost [DUARTE-ABAKOS-20] measured for a saturated relay path. This figure is corroborating, not WebRTC-specific evidence — [HALKES-NETWORKING-11]'s trial ran over a BitTorrent client's own UDP hole-punching implementation, not a browser's ICE stack, so it is directionally applicable to WebRTC (which performs the same class of mechanism) without being a direct measurement of it.

The socket-capable half of this selection (QUIC) fails to deliver its measured advantage on mobile handsets: [KAKHKI-IMC-17] measured QUIC spending 58% of connection time in an "Application Limited" congestion-control state on Nexus 6 and MotoG devices versus 7% on desktop, because QUIC's userspace packet processing cannot keep pace with TCP's kernel-resident processing — a mobile participant should not expect QUIC's desktop-measured latency and throughput gains to hold. QUIC's server-side cost also remains roughly double TLS/TCP's even after the cryptography, packet-reception, and state-handling optimizations [LANGLEY-SIGCOMM-17] describes, a cost every relay or seed node running this design pays per connection.

The onion-routing privacy tier's measured cost is the 2.8s median / 5.3s 90th-percentile page-fetch latency against 0.3s direct [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04], measured on a network roughly four orders of magnitude smaller (32 relays, a stated ~10,000-user design ceiling) than this architecture's likely user base. No entry in this corpus measures onion-routing latency at a larger relay population, so this figure should be treated as a floor for this design's actual privacy-tier latency, not a prediction of it.

### What the corpus does not settle

- No entry measures ICE/STUN/TURN connectivity success rates through an actual browser's WebRTC stack on a present-day (2020s) NAT and carrier-grade-NAT population at Internet scale. [HALKES-NETWORKING-11] (2011) and [FORD-USENIX-05] (2005) measure the same class of UDP hole-punching mechanism through a BitTorrent client, not a browser, and predate widespread carrier-grade NAT deployment; [RICHTER-IMC-16] (2016) measures CGN prevalence and NAT restrictiveness but not end-to-end WebRTC connection success.
- No entry measures the WebTransport browser API directly: its connection-establishment latency, its success rate, or a controlled comparison against WebRTC data channels for the same connectivity task is absent from this corpus.
- No entry measures onion routing at a relay count and user population matching this architecture's likely scale. [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]'s own numbers come from a 32-relay network the authors designed for roughly 10,000 users; how the fixed 512-byte cell size and per-circuit congestion windows behave at a much larger relay population and user count is not measured here.
- [LIANG-ARXIV-24]'s QUIC-hole-punching timing figures (~55ms) were measured against a NAT ruleset the same paper's authors forced to Endpoint-Independent Mapping via `iptables`, which the paper states explicitly differs from the Address-and-Port-Dependent Mapping behavior they found on real NAT devices during preliminary testing — the measured figure applies to a NAT behavior the authors themselves say is not what they found in the field.
- [ENGELBART-ANRW-26]'s proposal to multiplex WebRTC's RTP and data-channel traffic over one QUIC connection (potentially resolving the bandwidth-unfairness problem between the two) carries no usable numeric result in this corpus — the retrieved source is an OCR transcript of presentation slides whose bandwidth/latency comparison exists only as unlabeled plots. It is a proposed evolution of the WebRTC data-channel candidate, not a measured or deployed one, and is not selected on that basis.
- No entry in this corpus gives a current, post-2020 measurement of the fraction of this architecture's actual target population reachable end-to-end via WebRTC/ICE. The best available connectivity figures ([HALKES-NETWORKING-11], [FORD-USENIX-05]) come from a self-selected file-sharing-client population 15-20 years old; [RICHTER-IMC-16]'s CGN-prevalence figures bound the problem (11% of non-cellular CGN autonomous systems and roughly 40% of cellular CGN autonomous systems show symmetric NAT mapping, the type hole punching cannot traverse) but do not themselves measure end-to-end connection success.


---

## NAT traversal and reachability: a relay-introduced, timing-synchronized simultaneous connection attempt, with the relay kept as the session's fallback path, is the only mechanism in this corpus measured at internet scale

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (conditions) | Security assumption | Failure condition | Requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **A.
