Bare rendezvous hole punching, no persisted fallback** — a rendezvous server introduces two NATed (Network-Address-Translated) peers, who each send a packet toward the other's reported address; the server plays no further role once the path opens | 82% of tested devices support UDP (User Datagram Protocol) hole punching, 64% support TCP (Transmission Control Protocol) — 380 volunteer-submitted data points, 68 vendors [FORD-USENIX-05]. Among a deployed peer population classified compatible, first-attempt success is only ≈41% for an asymmetric pair and 85% even for the most favorable pair type — field trial of 646-2,000 peers [HALKES-NETWORKING-11]. A punched mapping needs a keepalive at intervals ≤ ≈55 s to stay open [HALKES-NETWORKING-11] | None of its own; the calling application must authenticate every inbound packet, since a stray host sharing the peer's private-address pool can otherwise be mistaken for the intended peer [FORD-USENIX-05] | Fails permanently, with nothing to fall back to, whenever a NAT is Endpoint-Dependent-Mapping (symmetric) — 11% to over 40% of a NATed population depending on cellular versus fixed access [RICHTER-IMC-16] — or when a multi-level NAT stack lacks hairpin translation, measured present in only 24% (UDP) / 13% (TCP) of tested devices [FORD-USENIX-05] | A rendezvous server reachable by both peers before the session starts; nothing afterward [FORD-USENIX-05; DUARTE-ABAKOS-20] |
| **B. Relay-introduced, timing-synchronized hole punching, relay retained as fallback path (the DCUtR / Circuit-v2 pattern)** — both peers first hold a reservation on a resource-capped relay; one peer dials the other directly if it looks publicly reachable, and otherwise both peers time a simultaneous outbound attempt using the relay-measured round-trip time; if the attempt fails, the session stays on the relay | Conditional hole-punch success 70% ± 7.1% — 6.25 million attempts, December 2022-January 2023 [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26]. Conditional on the relay-reservation-and-address-discovery step already succeeding, which itself fails ≈29% of attempts [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26]. Coordination overhead under 500 bytes per direction over two relay round trips; 97.6% of successes complete on the first of up to three attempts [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26]. TCP and QUIC (Quick UDP Internet Connections) succeed at statistically indistinguishable ≈70% rates once timing-synchronized [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26]. A controlled testbed forcing Endpoint-Independent-Mapping NATs only: punching completes in ≈55 ms (QUIC) / ≈56 ms (TCP) at 20 ms round-trip time, rising to ≈416 ms / ≈505 ms at 200 ms round-trip time [LIANG-ARXIV-24] | The relay honors whatever duration/data limit it advertises — the specification's own default is no limit unless the operator sets one [VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23]. Reservation vouchers are advisory, not enforced [VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23]. Both peers disclose their public address to the initiator and to the relay during setup, which can observe and correlate both parties' addresses and timing [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26] | Degrades to a durable relayed session, not total failure, whenever the reservation/discovery precondition fails (≈29%) [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26], the NAT on one or both sides is symmetric, or the two peers' one-way delays are significantly asymmetric, which shrinks the synchronization margin [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26] | A relay [VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23] reachable by both peers and willing to hold a bounded reservation before punching begins; a self-reachability discovery step; a transport supporting near-simultaneous outbound attempts (TCP simultaneous open or QUIC) |
| **C. Relay-only, mandatory** — every packet transits a third party for the life of the session; no punching is attempted | Setup ≈15 ms mean, 1,000 trials [DUARTE-ABAKOS-20]. Data-plane throughput collapses to the relay path's own bottleneck: 9.28 Mbps measured against 88.49 Mbps for a direct/reversal path under a 10 Mbps-vs-100 Mbps bottleneck asymmetry — an 89% reduction [DUARTE-ABAKOS-20]. An economically incentivized variant adds ≈$0.40-$0.60 per client-side session and ≈$0.10-$0.20 per relay-side session in transaction charges, plus a one-time ≈$1.34-$2.68 deployment charge — April 2020 Ethereum gas prices, a private test blockchain, not the public network [KEIZER-MOBIHOC-20] | Relay trusted not to correlate or tamper with traffic; TURN's (Traversal Using Relays around NAT) Send/Data messages carry no authentication [REDDY-RFC-20]. The incentivized variant instead enforces honesty economically, verified by an independent second node comparing delivered hashes and timestamps, and assumes a profit-motivated rather than purely destructive adversary [KEIZER-MOBIHOC-20] | Session throughput is capped by the relay operator's own available bandwidth regardless of either endpoint's capacity [DUARTE-ABAKOS-20]; resource limits are unenforced by default unless the operator explicitly configures them [VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23] | A continuously reachable population of relay-capable nodes with spare bandwidth; the incentivized variant additionally needs an append-only public ledger and, per session, simultaneous independent contact with two separate relay-capable nodes [KEIZER-MOBIHOC-20] |
| **D. Port prediction / birthday-paradox brute force against a symmetric NAT** — guess or exhaustively probe the external port a symmetric NAT will assign, instead of learning it from a rendezvous exchange | A field-validated technique against one specific NAT subtype (a linear, time-dependent port-increment rate, then a ±15-port probe window) reached 80% success within 12 s — 1,000 trials — but that subtype was found in only 8 of 72 measured cellular carriers; two unmodified commercial peer-to-peer applications failed outright in the same environment [WANG-SIGCOMM-11]. The fully general symmetric case is derived, not measured: ≈54,000 packets for 50% collision probability, ≈170,000 for 99.9%, and a pure two-sided brute force at ≈21 hours, all computed from one assumed national median connection speed [KANARIS-ARXIV-23] | None stated; the mechanism is itself a high-volume probing burst against the target NAT's own connection-state table | Fully random port allocation defeats prediction outright — measured in 19 of 72 cellular carriers (26.4%), a behavior the source paper states "cannot be handled by most existing NAT traversal schemes" [WANG-SIGCOMM-11]. The probe burst can exhaust a router's connection-table capacity before a collision occurs; some routers cap active connections near 64,000, close to the ≈54,000-packet threshold the birthday approach needs [KANARIS-ARXIV-23] | Prior per-carrier NAT behavioral characterization to select the correct prediction model [WANG-SIGCOMM-11]; tolerance, on the probed network, for a short high-volume packet burst |
| **E. IPv6 (Internet Protocol version 6) direct addressing** — when both participants hold a routable IPv6 address, connect without any translation step | No translation overhead when it works, but availability is partial and volatile: only 12.6% of the Tranco top-100,000 sites are fully IPv6-reachable end to end as of July 2025, a figure that rose only 0.6 percentage points over the preceding nine months [VALAPU-ARXIV-25]. A single residence's external IPv6 byte-fraction carries a daily standard deviation exceeding 15% [VALAPU-ARXIV-25]. Where an IPv6-only client must reach an IPv4-only destination, the NAT64 bridge needed is present on only ≈4% of tested paths and adds a measured 23.13% more hops and 17.47% higher round-trip time than native IPv4 when present [BOSWELL-ARXIV-24] | None beyond ordinary IPv6 routing; an unconditionally reachable IPv6 host has a different exposure profile than a NATed host, a consequence this corpus does not separately measure for residential router defaults | Either participant is IPv4-only, or sits on a network without IPv6 deployment — the large majority of the population measured as sitting behind Carrier-Grade NAT [RICHTER-IMC-16; LIVADARIU-INFOCOM-18], a condition those papers tie to IPv4 address scarcity, independent of that same network's own IPv6 status | Nothing, when IPv6 is present on both sides; a NAT64/DNS64 translator, or one of the other rows here, when it is absent on either side |
| **F. Structural avoidance in the routing/storage layer** — exclude unreachable peers from the set that stores and forwards for others; a NATed peer publishes and searches but is never asked to serve | No traversal attempted for the excluded population. In one large deployed Kademlia-derived distributed hash table, ≈40-50% of the peer population (1.5-2 million of 3-4.3 million found per crawl) sits behind NAT or firewall and is held out of the serving role for exactly this reason [STEINER-IMC-07] | None of its own; a policy about which peers are asked to route or store, not a traversal mechanism | Supplies no reachability for a session that specifically needs two NATed peers to reach each other directly — delivering a chunk held by exactly one NATed peer, or a live call — because it removes traversal from the storage/routing path only [STEINER-IMC-07] | Still needs row B, A, or D for any function requiring a session with one particular NATed peer; needs a self-reachability test to sort peers into the two roles, a step not separately measured in STEINER-IMC-07 |
| **G. Structural avoidance at the application layer** — route every interaction through voluntarily public storage/relay nodes; an ordinary participant only ever makes outbound connections and never accepts one | In one large deployed network built this way, each post replicates to a mean of 34.6 relays and 12.4 Autonomous Systems [WEI-PACMNET-25]. A client following 3.5 million follower edges wastes a measured 98.2% of its download traffic (144 TiB) absent a relay-selection step, because it fetches every followed user's posts from every relay it shares with that user [WEI-PACMNET-25]. Post availability stays above 90% after removing the top 50 relays by user count, and above 80% after removing the top 10 hosting Autonomous Systems [WEI-PACMNET-25]. Most free relays serving more than ≈10 users fail to cover an estimated six-month operating cost from admission fees and micropayments alone [WEI-PACMNET-25] | Relay operators are assumed to publish honestly and to remain reachable; no operator is compensated by protocol design for continued operation [WEI-PACMNET-25] | Supplies no mechanism for two participants to hold a live, direct session — every interaction is publish-then-poll through a relay, so the pattern does not by itself support a call, a synchronous exchange, or delivery of an object held by only one specific currently-online participant [WEI-PACMNET-25] | A discovery layer telling a client which relays hold a given participant's data, absent which the measured 98.2% waste occurs by default [WEI-PACMNET-25]; an externally supplied revenue source to keep free relays solvent past ≈10 users [WEI-PACMNET-25]; a party willing to run a publicly reachable server, a different requirement from "any participant on any residential connection can serve" |
| **H. Explicit port mapping (UPnP Internet Gateway Device, NAT-PMP, or PCP — asking the local router to open and forward a port)** | **No evidence entry in this corpus measures this mechanism directly.** One indirect data point exists: clients that self-report an active port mapping show noticeably higher Connection Reversal (dial-back without punching) effectiveness than clients that do not [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26] — but this is a side observation drawn from self-reported, unverified client configuration, not a controlled measurement of the mapping protocols' own success rate, latency, or availability behind a Carrier-Grade NAT (which typically does not expose these protocols to the customer at all) | — | — | — |

### Selection

Select row B: a relay introduces two peers, each learns its own externally observed address, one peer attempts a direct dial if the other looks publicly reachable, and otherwise both peers time a simultaneous outbound connection attempt from the relay-measured round-trip time; if that attempt fails, the session continues over the relay rather than terminating. This is the mechanism libp2p ships as DCUtR (Direct Connection Upgrade through Relay), built on the Circuit-v2 relay specification, and it is the only mechanism in this corpus with a success rate measured on a real, geographically distributed peer population rather than in a laboratory testbed or as an arithmetic projection [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26; VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23].

**Against A (bare hole punching, no fallback).** Row A and row B are close to the same mechanism at the moment of punching — FORD-USENIX-05's own connection-reversal-and-punch procedure is what DUARTE-ABAKOS-20 measures as comparably low-overhead, and TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26's ≈70% figure is not obviously higher than HALKES-NETWORKING-11's own ≈80% figure for eligible pairs. The difference is what happens on the ≈30% of attempts that fail. Row A has nothing left to try: HALKES-NETWORKING-11 measures ≈79% of the peer population as not directly connectable without traversal, and a punch that does not land there ends the session. Row B's relay never stops carrying the session, so a punch failure in TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26's data is a latency and bandwidth cost, not a connectivity failure [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26 vs. FORD-USENIX-05; HALKES-NETWORKING-11].

**Against C (relay-only, mandatory).** Row C guarantees connectivity independent of NAT type, which row B does not fully match — TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26's own 29% prerequisite-failure figure is a case where row B needs C's relay path anyway. But DUARTE-ABAKOS-20 measures exactly what row C loses when it is the only path: a bandwidth-constrained relay collapses session throughput to 9.28 Mbps against an 88.49 Mbps direct/reversal alternative, an ≈89% reduction, and that reduction applies to every session, not only the ≈30% a punching attempt would otherwise resolve. Row B pays row C's cost only on the minority of sessions punching does not resolve; row C pays it on all of them. KEIZER-MOBIHOC-20's incentive layer addresses who funds row C's relay bandwidth, not this throughput ceiling, so it does not change the comparison [DUARTE-ABAKOS-20 vs. VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23, TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26].

**Against D (port prediction).** WANG-SIGCOMM-11's own field-validated port-prediction technique reaches 80% success in 12 seconds against one specific NAT subtype (a measured, linear, time-dependent port increment), found in only 8 of 72 carriers studied — a narrower population than row B's 70% figure, which TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26 measured across 859 client networks in 39 countries without restricting to one NAT subtype. Against the fully general symmetric case, KANARIS-ARXIV-23 has no field measurement at all, only a derived packet count, and that same paper notes the probe burst risks exhausting a router's own connection table before succeeding — a cost row B's timing-based synchronization does not carry, since TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26 measures a coordination exchange under 500 bytes per direction rather than tens of thousands of probe packets [WANG-SIGCOMM-11; KANARIS-ARXIV-23 vs. TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26].

**Against E (IPv6 direct addressing).** Row E, where available, removes the reachability problem at zero ongoing cost, and nothing in row B contradicts adding an IPv6 host candidate to the address-discovery step B already performs. The comparison is availability, not mechanism: VALAPU-ARXIV-25 measures only 12.6% of popular web destinations as fully IPv6-reachable end to end nine years after CZYZ-SIGCOMM-14 first measured single-digit IPv6 reachability, and BOSWELL-ARXIV-24 measures the IPv4-bridging fallback (NAT64) as both rare (≈4% of tested paths) and materially slower when present. A design that relies on E alone has no basis in this corpus for treating it as sufficient for the near term; row B is what a session falls back to when E is unavailable, which the corpus shows is the common case [VALAPU-ARXIV-25; BOSWELL-ARXIV-24 vs. TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26].

**Against F (structural avoidance in routing/storage).** Row F and row B answer different questions and are not substitutes. STEINER-IMC-07 measures that excluding ≈40-50% of a deployed peer population from the DHT's (distributed hash table's) own serving role works for that role specifically — a lookup can always be satisfied by a reachable holder of the same key. It supplies nothing when the object needed, or the session needed, belongs specifically to one currently-online NATed peer, which is exactly the case row B is measured against. A system can and should combine both: F reduces how often a session needs row B by preferring reachable holders when several exist, but does not remove the need for B when only one, NATed, holder exists [STEINER-IMC-07 vs. TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26].

**Against G (structural avoidance at the application layer).** Row G is measured, in WEI-PACMNET-25's Nostr dataset, to work well for what it is built for: publish-and-poll social content stays 80-90% available after removing a large fraction of relays or hosting networks. It is not built for, and WEI-PACMNET-25 does not measure it performing, a live session between two specific participants — every interaction in that architecture is asynchronous, mediated by a relay's storage. It also carries a directly measured cost the architecture under design here would inherit for every content type it applies to: 34.6x replication per post and 98.2% wasted download traffic absent a relay-selection mechanism [WEI-PACMNET-25]. Row G is a legitimate design for content publication and discovery; it is not a candidate that answers "how does a NATed participant become reachable for a session," because it declines to open sessions [WEI-PACMNET-25 vs. TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26].

**Against H (UPnP/NAT-PMP/PCP).** No comparison is possible on measured grounds; row H has no dedicated evidence entry in this corpus. The one available data point — TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26's finding that self-reported port-mapping correlates with higher Connection Reversal effectiveness — is consistent with including a port-mapping attempt as a cheap first step ahead of row B's punching sequence, since a successful mapping request removes the need for punching entirely, but this corpus supplies no success-rate, latency, or Carrier-Grade-NAT-availability figure for the mapping protocols themselves, so this remains an unevaluated addition rather than a selected alternative.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- **Ordering.** The system must sequence, not parallelize arbitrarily: (1) obtain a relay reservation and confirm the relay holds it, (2) run a self-reachability/address-discovery step (an Identify/AutoNAT-equivalent) so each peer knows its own externally observed address, (3) attempt a direct dial if the target peer's discovered address looks publicly reachable, (4) failing that, exchange candidate addresses and a relay-measured round-trip time over the existing relayed connection and time a simultaneous outbound attempt from that measurement, (5) on failure of steps 3-4, keep the relayed connection as the session's path rather than terminating it [VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23; TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26].
- **Participant honesty assumed.** The relay is assumed to enforce whatever duration/data limit it advertises rather than relaying unboundedly — the specification's default is no limit unless the operator configures one, so a production deployment must not leave this field unset [VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23]. The relay is assumed not to deliberately delay or corrupt the coordination exchange, since the timing synchronization degrades under path asymmetry that a congested or adversarial relay can induce, and TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26's residual ≈30% failure rate is partly attributed to exactly this [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26].
- **What another component must supply.** (a) A discovery mechanism publishing which relay or relays a given peer currently holds a reservation with — the relay specification states this is out of scope and names a distributed hash table or a rendezvous point only as examples [VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23]. (b) An admission or Sybil-resistance layer for relay operators, since an unauthenticated relay population is exactly the address-and-timing-correlation exposure TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26 and VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23 both state as a property of this mechanism, not a flaw to be fixed within it. (c) An application-layer authentication step for the first packets on a freshly punched path, since punching alone does not distinguish the intended peer from a stray host sharing the same private-address pool [FORD-USENIX-05].
- **What must be observable.** Each peer must be able to read its own externally observed address (Identify or STUN, Session Traversal Utilities for NAT) and the relay-measured round-trip time, since the synchronization wait time is computed directly from that measurement [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26]. The calling system must be able to observe punch outcome (success or failure) per attempt, to drive the retry policy and to trigger the fallback-to-relay path on exhaustion [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26].

### What it costs and where it fails

Conditional hole-punch success measures at 70% ± 7.1% across 6.25 million attempts spanning 859 client networks in 39 countries, December 2022-January 2023 [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26]. That figure is conditional on the relay-reservation-and-address-discovery step already succeeding, which itself fails on ≈29% of attempts, so the unconditional success rate from a cold start is lower than 70% — though those failures degrade to a relayed session rather than to no session at all [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26]. Coordination overhead is under 500 bytes per direction across two relay round trips; 97.6% of successful punches complete on the first of up to three attempts [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26]. Once a direct path opens, its round-trip time falls to 70% or less of the pre-punch relayed round-trip time for half of measured peers [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26].

The mechanism stops resolving to a direct path — falling back permanently to the relayed session — under three measured conditions: the NAT on one or both sides is symmetric (Endpoint-Dependent-Mapping), since TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26's outcome-only measurement cannot isolate this cause but RICHTER-IMC-16 measures symmetric mapping present in 11% of non-cellular Carrier-Grade-NAT Autonomous Systems and ≈40% of cellular ones; the relay-reservation or address-discovery precondition fails outright (≈29% of attempts) [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26; VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23]; or the two peers' one-way path delays are significantly asymmetric, shrinking the synchronization margin the timing calculation depends on [TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26]. In every one of these cases the session continues on the relay, at the relay's own bandwidth ceiling — measured elsewhere in this corpus at up to an 89% throughput reduction under a constrained relay path [DUARTE-ABAKOS-20] — rather than failing outright.

### What the corpus does not settle

- **No entry measures the composite including an IPv6 host-candidate check.** Adding IPv6 direct addressing as a first-tried candidate ahead of relay-mediated punching is a natural extension of the address-discovery step row B already performs (analogous to the host-candidate step already defined in ICE, Interactive Connectivity Establishment [KERANEN-RFC-18]), but no entry in this corpus measures that specific combined pipeline's success rate or latency; VALAPU-ARXIV-25 and TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26 measure their respective mechanisms separately, never composed.
- **No entry reports what duration/data caps production relay operators actually set.** VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23 states the specification carries no default; TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26's 212 volunteer clients used relays whose configured limits are not reported in that paper. A builder needs an operational survey or a chosen, explicitly labeled default — not a figure this corpus supplies.
- **No entry separates the residual ≈30% failure rate by cause.** TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26 states directly that its outcome-only measurement cannot distinguish a genuinely unresolvable symmetric-NAT pair from a synchronization-margin failure caused by path asymmetry, nor can it distinguish a single NAT layer from an added Carrier-Grade NAT layer. A builder cannot derive, from this corpus, what fraction of failures a better synchronization formula (such as the refined, undeployed formula TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26's own §7.2 proposes) would actually recover.
- **No entry measures relay-operator sustainability for the unpaid, volunteer-run Circuit-v2 model this selection depends on.** VYZOVITIS-SPECS-23 states running a relay "proved to be an expensive proposition requiring dedicated hosts" but supplies no cost figure; KEIZER-MOBIHOC-20 measures an alternative, compensated relay design's transaction charges, not this one's operating cost, and its own evaluation runs on a private test blockchain, not a production deployment at any measured scale.
- **No entry measures residential or mobile router default behavior toward an unsolicited inbound IPv6 connection specifically.** Row E's "zero cost when available" claim assumes a dual-stack peer is reachable once an IPv6 address is confirmed; whether typical customer-premises equipment blocks unsolicited inbound IPv6 by default, the way NAT incidentally does for IPv4, is not measured by any entry in this corpus — a builder needs this figure before treating E's low measured translation overhead as equivalent to low measured reachability.


---

## Capacity-heterogeneous participation: A capacity-selected minority carries all relay traffic while everyone else is excluded from relay duty by structural role, and no mechanism in this corpus defends the capacity claim that selection runs on

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (conditions) | Security assumption | Failure condition | Requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Uniform assignment with per-participant budgets (every peer treated as an interchangeable relay, e.g. flat Gnutella query broadcast) | Bandwidth, latency, availability, and shared-content volume vary "between three and five orders of magnitude" across a 509,538-peer Napster and 1,239,487-peer Gnutella crawl (May 2001) [SAROIU-MMCN-02]. Storing 1 TB of unique data at high availability is derived as effectively infeasible under Gnutella-like participation and cable-modem-like bandwidth, from a bandwidth-maintenance model fit to a 33,000-host Gnutella crawl (April 2003) [BLAKE-HOTOS-03]. 66% of 33,335 measured Gnutella peers (24-hour capture, August 2000) share zero files; the top 1% hold 37% of shared file volume and answer 47% of query responses [ADAR-FM-00]. | Every participant performs its assigned share of relay or serving work regardless of actual capacity; no differential treatment by capacity exists. | Population capacity variance beyond roughly one order of magnitude (measured here at three to five) concentrates load onto whichever small subset happens to stay reachable and willing, and a peer given equal transit share regardless of true capacity either drops the excess or forces every path through it down to its own rate. | None — this is the absence of a capacity-observation mechanism, not a candidate that consumes one. |
| Two-tier clustering: a minority of nodes (super-peers) answer queries and relay for a cluster of client leaves that connect to exactly one super-peer each and never route for anyone else | Redesigning a 20,000-node, cluster-size-1 (uniform), average-outdegree-3.1 topology into cluster size 10 with average outdegree 28 reaches the same 3,000-node query result with over 79% reduction across incoming bandwidth, outgoing bandwidth, and processing power, and cuts individual load for the lowest 90% of nodes (the clients) by one to two orders of magnitude — from a cost model built on Gnutella wire-protocol costs and Pentium III 930 MHz processing measurements [YANG-ICDE-03]. Corroborated at live scale: a 100,000-node, ~20-million-file Gnutella crawl found real ultrapeers supporting 30 or 75 leaf nodes each, with ultrapeer connections averaging 93 minutes versus leaf connections averaging 58 minutes [LOO-IPTPS-04]. | "Limited altruism" — every super-peer voluntarily accepts load up to a self-set limit; outdegree increases must be adopted uniformly, since a single super-peer raising its own outdegree from 4 to 9 alone raises its own load 303%, while the same change made network-wide instead lowers that peer's load 14% [YANG-ICDE-03]. | A super-peer's failure disconnects every one of its clients until a replacement is located; the paper's own text states its "limited altruism" assumption "does not address the possibility of freeloading or malicious non-participation," citing separate research on detecting such nodes as outside its own scope [YANG-ICDE-03]. Bootstrapping — how a joining node finds its assigned super-peer — is stated as "an open problem in pure and super-peer networks, with many potential solutions," unsolved by the paper [YANG-ICDE-03]. | Continuous client-to-super-peer metadata synchronization at join, update, and leave [YANG-ICDE-03]. k-redundancy, if adopted to reduce the single-point-of-failure property, multiplies super-peer-to-super-peer open connections by k² [YANG-ICDE-03]. A routing protocol for the super-peer-to-super-peer layer, which the paper states explicitly is "orthogonal" and pluggable [YANG-ICDE-03]. |
| Bandwidth-ordered self-stabilizing routing (HSkip+, an asynchronous variant of the SKIP+ skip graph that orders nodes by declared bandwidth) | **No full-text evidence entry exists in this corpus under any key for Feldotto, Scheideler, Graffi, P2P 2014.** The figures commonly attributed to it — O(log²n) messages against SKIP+'s O(log⁴n) total work, a routing property that "never transits a node with less bandwidth than min of endpoints," survival of 60% random and 35% adversarial churn, O(log n) dilation and congestion — are recorded only in this project's own already-verified reading list, not in an independently checkable primary-source entry this synthesis can inspect. A 2021 systematic survey confirms only that the design exists and states it "reduces the stabilization time in practice and needs less work for single join or leave events" than SKIP+, without stating a numeric bound for that improvement, and finds no self-stabilizing overlay design published through the survey's own 2020 window that revisits HSkip+'s bandwidth-ordering property [FELDMANN-CSUR-21]. The unordered predecessor SKIP+ (no capacity term) is fully evidenced: full construction from any weakly connected graph in O(log²n) rounds with high probability, and a single join or leave in O(log n) rounds / O(log⁴n) total work, both under a synchronous round model [JACOB-JACM-14]. | Node identifiers, and the capacity/bandwidth value used to order them, are assumed correct and uncorrupted at the start of the protocol and are only compared, stored, and transmitted, never independently checked [FELDMANN-CSUR-21]. | The Finite Departure Problem (a node leaving permanently) "has to rely on an oracle" — no purely local self-stabilizing protocol in this literature can guarantee a departed node's references are fully purged [FELDMANN-CSUR-21, Theorem 7.2]. Convergence "may not be reached if the node set changes during convergence," and the survey states churn-tolerance research and self-stabilization research "has been largely independent" of each other — no bound in this literature covers concurrent join/leave rate during convergence [FELDMANN-CSUR-21]. | An external failure detector or identifier-assignment scheme to guarantee node-identifier correctness before the protocol begins [FELDMANN-CSUR-21]. An oracle supplying permanent-departure detection [FELDMANN-CSUR-21]. A transport exposing full neighbor-state inspection on demand, since the algorithm's rules are Boolean guards "over the state of the executing node and its neighbors" [JACOB-JACM-14]. |
| Redirecting message flow toward higher-capacity peers without a formal supernode tier (Lv, Ratnasamy, Shenker, "Can Heterogeneity Make Gnutella Scalable?", IPTPS 2002) | **No evidence entry exists in this corpus.** Known only secondhand, through YANG-ICDE-03's own bibliography, as "a pure-peer-to-peer alternative in which nodes redirect message flow away from themselves toward higher-capacity nodes, addressing the same capability-heterogeneity problem without introducing super-peers" [YANG-ICDE-03]. | Unknown — not retrieved. | Unknown — not retrieved. | Unknown — not retrieved. |
| Local proportional-response allocation among already-connected peers (PropShare: a peer returns bandwidth to each neighbor in proportion to what that neighbor sent it the prior round) | PropShare finishes faster than BitTorrent's original choking rule in 20 of 21 live public-swarm downloads (University of Maryland network, upload capped at 100 KB/s) [LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08]. Proven, not measured: splitting a fixed total contribution C across any number of identities returns the same aggregate bandwidth B_v·C/(C+y_v) regardless of split count — Sybil-proof by algebraic construction [LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08, §6.3]. Best-response deviation from PropShare improves a deviating peer's own download speed by under 1% in every tested scenario (0.1x to 40x average bandwidth, 30 runs per point) [LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08]. | Each peer observes, per round, how much bandwidth every connected neighbor sent it in the prior round; needs no knowledge of a neighbor's total capacity or its bids elsewhere [LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08]. | Decides only how much bandwidth flows on a link a peer is already connected to over content it is already interested in; supplies no admission or topology decision. The unconditional bootstrap path this allocation rule (and BitTorrent generally) still requires is exactly what a client that uploads nothing and simply connects to far more peers than the reciprocity rule assumes defeats: one such client completed every one of 7 real torrents while uploading zero bytes [LOCHER-HOTNETS-06], and a large-view free-rider outperformed compliant peers below a 40% free-rider population share on a 300-leecher PlanetLab swarm [SIRIVIANOS-IPTPS-07]. | A swarm/interest-group membership mechanism supplying the candidate peer set (tracker or DHT) [LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08]; requires the piece-integrity and piece-availability signal (have-messages) the choking layer's traffic accounting itself consumes [COHEN-IPTPS-03]. |
| Peer-measured capacity verification against self-report (PeerFlow: the largest fraction µ of relays by capacity, per circuit position, keep application-layer byte counts of every relay they interact with, aggregated and trimmed by a small quorum) | Against Tor's deployed self-report-plus-centralized-probe baseline (TorFlow), one malicious relay combining a false self-report with selective traffic-dropping raised its median consensus-weight share from 7% to 11% while median goodput fell from 22.5 to 0.2 MiB/s — a demonstrated 177x inflation-per-unit-bandwidth factor (498-relay, 7,500-client Shadow simulation) [JOHNSON-POPETS-17]. PeerFlow's own peer-measurement design bounds this analytically to a factor γ=4.52 at its worked parameters (trim threshold λ=0.256, measuring-relay fraction µ=0.75, per-period increase cap 0.25) [JOHNSON-POPETS-17]. | An adversarial coalition's aggregate voting weight stays below the trim threshold λ=0.256; the paper states the bound holds "even without any trusted relays" only under that condition, and that "some other component" must supply it [JOHNSON-POPETS-17]. | An adversary sending traffic in only one direction, or concentrating exchange on a minority-weight subset of measuring relays, can approach the γ bound; the paper calls this "highly observable" but does not itself block it [JOHNSON-POPETS-17]. | A functioning Directory-Authority-equivalent quorum that collects, trims, and votes on submitted peer measurements [JOHNSON-POPETS-17]. Differential-privacy noise injection (δnoise=1 MiB, εnoise=0.1) before publishing aggregated peer-traffic statistics, since raw measurements leak which relay pairs exchanged how much traffic [JOHNSON-POPETS-17]. A Sybil-resistance mechanism controlling how much aggregate weight one identity can acquire, to keep any coalition under λ [JOHNSON-POPETS-17]. |
| Active load-testing capacity verification (FlashFlow: a coordinated measurement team forces a target relay to carry traffic approaching its claimed capacity, cross-checking a sample of responses byte-for-byte) | Bounds inflation to at most 1.33x true capacity (traffic-ratio cap, recommended r=0.25), against PeerFlow's 4.52x and TorFlow's demonstrated 177x [TRAUDT-ICDCS-21]. Measured relay capacity within 11% of ground truth in 95% of real-Internet trials (5 sites, 7 runs each over 24 hours) [TRAUDT-ICDCS-21]. Measures the full ~6,400-relay simulated Tor network in a median 5 hours using one 3-Gbit/s measurement team [TRAUDT-ICDCS-21]. | A majority of Bandwidth Authorities is honest; the target cannot change its true capacity faster than the measurement schedule (drawn from a shared secret random seed each 24-hour period) detects [TRAUDT-ICDCS-21]. | A relay controlling several IP addresses on one physical machine can be measured separately for each alias and obtain the shared machine's full capacity for each — an unmitigated Sybil-style gap the authors state explicitly [TRAUDT-ICDCS-21]. | Measurer bandwidth exceeding the highest-capacity target relay by a factor f=2.84 at recommended parameters, provisioned as an ongoing operational allowance separate from relay bandwidth (3 Gbit/s per team at deployed scale) [TRAUDT-ICDCS-21]. Synchronized clocks and a shared secure-randomness source across Bandwidth Authorities for the measurement schedule [TRAUDT-ICDCS-21]. |
| Trustfree crowd-challenger link measurement (Proof of Backhaul: a randomly drawn group of ordinary-bandwidth peers jointly saturate a target's link and report a median round-trip time, tolerating a Byzantine minority of challengers) | Under 5% measured error at 250 Mbit/s with 6 or more challengers and a 100 ms challenge [SHENG-NDSS-24]. Real deployment (~25-30 active challengers across US/Europe/Asia): 500/700/1000 Mbit/s backhauls measured with 4.2%/4.1%/9.9% average error [SHENG-NDSS-24]. A correction factor α=(n−2f)/(n−f) curtails inflation from a colluding challenger-and-target pair, at the stated price of lowering the reported "guaranteed bandwidth" 28% below the true value even when every challenger is honest, at the tested β=0.2 setting [SHENG-NDSS-24]. | Proven correct for a corrupted-challenger fraction β<1/3 with no verifier timer, up to β<1/2 with one [SHENG-NDSS-24]. | The challenge coordinator itself is not Byzantine-fault-tolerant for liveness; a poorly behaving coordinator can stall a measurement even though it cannot corrupt its result [SHENG-NDSS-24]. | The ability to select "a fresh, randomly drawn subset of challengers per measurement from a larger active pool," so a corrupted target cannot predict who will test it — a Sybil-resistance precondition the mechanism consumes rather than supplies [SHENG-NDSS-24]. Synchronized-enough clocks across challengers for round-trip-time computation [SHENG-NDSS-24]. |
| Multi-party per-circuit proof of transfer (TorPath/TorCoin: the four participants of one relay circuit jointly produce a cryptographic proof, obtainable only if all four actually forwarded traffic, backing a mined coin) | Roughly 5% protocol overhead at packet-interval m≥10, from a Python-Twisted message-passing simulation the authors describe as "Preliminary Results," with no relay count, run count, or real-network deployment stated [GHOSH-HOTPETS-14]. | A majority of a decentralized "assignment server" quorum is honest, the rest honest-but-curious; at most half of network identities collude, under which at most 1/16 of assigned circuits are fully colluding and able to mint coins for zero genuine transfer [GHOSH-HOTPETS-14]. | Requires reliable, in-order delivery of every onion-forwarded commit/reveal message per round, or the round fails to mint [GHOSH-HOTPETS-14]. A minted coin proves goodput on one specific circuit at mint time only — the paper states explicitly it is "not a general, ongoing measure of a relay's capacity," so it cannot itself supply the sortable capacity value a two-tier or bandwidth-ordered layer needs without a further, unmeasured aggregation step [GHOSH-HOTPETS-14]. | An already-functioning, majority-honest assignment-server quorum running a verifiable shuffle (Neff shuffle) to assign circuits in the first place [GHOSH-HOTPETS-14]. A distributed ledger for double-mint prevention [GHOSH-HOTPETS-14]. Incompatible as specified with the deployed Tor network, since it requires route assignment and access control ordinary relays do not implement [GHOSH-HOTPETS-14]. |

### Selection

The selected mechanism excludes low-capacity peers from relay duty by structural role rather than by throttling their share of it: a minority of peers, selected by claimed capacity, forms a transit-carrying tier that every other peer connects to but never routes through — the two-tier design measured in [YANG-ICDE-03] and corroborated at real-network scale in [LOO-IPTPS-04]. Within that transit-carrying tier, routing is recommended to be ordered by capacity so that no transit-tier member ever sits on a path between two higher-capacity members, which [YANG-ICDE-03] states directly is an orthogonal, pluggable choice at that layer — "the use of super-peers and the choice of routing protocol are orthogonal issues." The specific mechanism recommended for that layer is the bandwidth-ordered self-stabilizing overlay commonly attributed to Feldotto, Scheideler, and Graffi (HSkip+, P2P 2014). **This half of the selection is a labeled pick, not a corpus-backed selection**: no full-text entry for that paper exists anywhere in this corpus's evidence file, so its specific bounds cannot be independently checked here. What can be checked is that a systematic 2021 survey of the entire self-stabilizing-overlay literature confirms the design exists and remains, as of that survey's own forward-mining check, the only design in its coverage window that orders routing by node capacity at all [FELDMANN-CSUR-21]. Anyone building this needs to retrieve Feldotto, Scheideler, and Graffi directly and re-verify the specific bounds before relying on them.

**Against uniform assignment with per-participant budgets:** [SAROIU-MMCN-02] measures bandwidth, latency, availability, and shared-content volume varying "between three and five orders of magnitude" across a combined 1.75-million-peer Napster and Gnutella crawl, and states directly, as a requirement any uniform-treatment system inherits, that such a system "requires a mechanism to detect and differentially weight peer capability rather than treating peers as interchangeable." [BLAKE-HOTOS-03] derives, from an independent 33,000-host Gnutella crawl, that storing 1 TB of unique data at high availability is effectively infeasible under Gnutella-like uniform participation and cable-modem-like bandwidth, and separately measures that restricting membership to the top 5% of hosts by availability cuts required maintenance bandwidth roughly 1,000-fold relative to the least favorable uniform-participation point. [YANG-ICDE-03]'s own measured comparison is the direct rebuttal: moving from cluster size 1 (uniform) to a capacity-selected cluster size of 10 at matched query reach reduces bandwidth and processing load over 79%. Uniform assignment is rejected on this evidence, not merely disfavored.

**Against local proportional-response allocation (PropShare) as a standalone answer:** [LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08] proves PropShare's allocation rule Sybil-proof against splitting a fixed contribution across identities (§6.3), the strongest defense of this specific kind anywhere in this corpus — but the rule only decides how much bandwidth flows on a link two already-connected, already-interested peers share. It answers nothing about whether a low-capacity peer sits on a path between two other peers in the first place, which is the property this component needs. Its own family demonstrates the gap directly: a client requesting from far more peers than the reciprocity rule assumes, and uploading nothing, still completed every one of 7 real BitTorrent downloads [LOCHER-HOTNETS-06], and a large-view free-rider outperformed compliant peers below a 40% free-rider population share [SIRIVIANOS-IPTPS-07] — both exploit the unconditional admission path every allocation-only mechanism in this family still needs for bootstrapping, not the proportional-split formula itself. PropShare is retained, not rejected outright, as the allocation rule for bandwidth already flowing between peers once the topology-level selection above has already excluded low-capacity peers from transit duty — see the requirements below.

**Against redirecting flow toward high-capacity peers without a formal tier (Lv, Ratnasamy, Shenker):** no evidence entry for this paper exists in this corpus. It is recorded here, per this project's own rule that a candidate with no evidence entry is listed and marked as having none, rather than silently omitted. No comparison against the selection can be made on evidence; a future pass should retrieve it directly, since [YANG-ICDE-03]'s own bibliography note suggests it addresses the identical problem this component exists to solve, without introducing the single-point-of-failure-per-client property the two-tier design carries.

**Against adopting one of the misreport-defense mechanisms (PeerFlow, FlashFlow, Proof of Backhaul, TorCoin) as the primary selection, rather than the two-tier structure plus capacity-ordered routing:** each of these four answers a different question — how much to trust a claimed number — not the question this component is about, which is who is asked to relay at all and in what order. [JOHNSON-POPETS-17] states PeerFlow "requires a functioning Directory Authority infrastructure"; [TRAUDT-ICDCS-21] states FlashFlow "requires the same Directory Authority (DirAuth) and Bandwidth Authority (BW Auth) infrastructure Tor already runs"; [SHENG-NDSS-24] requires a coordinator able to draw "a fresh, randomly drawn subset of challengers... from a larger active pool" whose own freedom from Sybil control the mechanism does not itself supply; [GHOSH-HOTPETS-14] requires an already-functioning, majority-honest assignment-server quorum and, even then, produces only a per-circuit proof at mint time, not a general per-node capacity value a topology or ordering rule could sort by. None of the four supplies a role-assignment or routing-order mechanism; each is a verification layer that could feed a truer capacity number into the selected structure, at the mechanical cost detailed below, not a replacement for it.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- A bootstrap/discovery mechanism that assigns a joining node to a transit-tier position or to a specific transit-tier neighbor set, without funneling every join through one centralized rendezvous point — [YANG-ICDE-03] states this is unsolved by its own design ("an open problem in pure and super-peer networks, with many potential solutions") and no other entry in this corpus closes it.
- A capacity value to sort or select by, at minimum a self-declared one; and — because nothing in this selection defends that value against a false claim — either a Sybil-resistance mechanism from the identity component keeping any single coalition's claimed aggregate capacity under whatever coalition-weight threshold a verification layer requires (PeerFlow's bound holds only "even without any trusted relays" if a coalition stays under trim threshold λ=0.256 [JOHNSON-POPETS-17]), or an explicit acceptance that admission to the transit tier and rank within it can be gamed, recorded as an open risk rather than assumed away.
- Continuous per-client metadata synchronization to the assigned transit-tier node at join, update, and leave — the transit node's ability to answer on a client's behalf is only as current as this channel [YANG-ICDE-03].
- If k-redundancy is adopted to reduce the single-point-of-failure property of a client's one assigned transit-tier connection, every neighboring transit-tier node must open k connections instead of one, an O(k²) growth in transit-tier connection count the underlying transport/NAT-traversal component must provision for [YANG-ICDE-03].
- If the bandwidth-ordered self-stabilizing routing layer is adopted among transit-tier members, it requires an external failure detector or identifier-assignment scheme guaranteeing node-identifier correctness before the protocol begins, an oracle for permanent-departure detection (no purely local rule can supply one — [FELDMANN-CSUR-21], Theorem 7.2), and a transport exposing full neighbor-state inspection on demand [JACOB-JACM-14].
- Once transit-tier admission has already excluded low-capacity peers, the local proportional-response rule (PropShare) is required as the within-tier allocation mechanism deciding how much bandwidth flows on each already-established transit-tier link, consuming an observable per-round received-bandwidth signal per neighbor [LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08] and a swarm/interest membership source supplying the candidate peer set [LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08].
- If a PeerFlow-style peer-measurement layer is added to bound self-report inflation, it requires per-peer, per-position application-layer byte accounting at each measuring node, a small aggregation quorum running trim/probation/increase-cap logic, and differential-privacy noise injection before publishing aggregated statistics, since raw peer-to-peer byte counts otherwise leak which pairs exchanged how much traffic [JOHNSON-POPETS-17].
- If a FlashFlow-style active load-test layer is added instead, it requires a dedicated measurer-bandwidth allowance exceeding the highest-capacity target by a factor of roughly 2.84 at recommended parameters, provisioned on an ongoing basis separately from relay bandwidth, plus synchronized clocks and a shared secure-randomness source across whatever quorum runs the measurement schedule [TRAUDT-ICDCS-21].

### What it costs and where it fails

At the redesign scale [YANG-ICDE-03] models (a 20,000-node network, cluster size 10, matched to a 3,000-node query reach), the two-tier structure reduces incoming bandwidth, outgoing bandwidth, and processing power by over 79% against uniform assignment, and cuts individual load for the lowest 90% of nodes by one to two orders of magnitude. Cluster size carries an unresolved numerical tension the source paper states directly rather than closes: individual load favors small clusters, aggregate load and per-cluster efficiency favor large ones, and reliability (fewer single points of failure per client) favors small ones, with the paper stating only that "the answer to this question is specific to the application and situation" [YANG-ICDE-03]. A single transit-tier node's failure disconnects every one of its clients until a replacement is located; k=2 redundancy lowers a partner's individual load about 48% at cluster size 100 at the cost of roughly 2.5% higher aggregate bandwidth and 17% higher aggregate processing load, and does not eliminate the disconnection window, only shorten it [YANG-ICDE-03]. A single transit-tier node that unilaterally raises its own capacity (equivalently, its own declared or acted-on outdegree) sees its own load rise 303%, while the identical change adopted network-wide instead lowers that node's load 14% [YANG-ICDE-03] — a first-mover penalty against acting on a truthfully higher capacity that exists independent of any adversary, and one that points in the same direction as a measured real-world finding: peers in a large deployed system under-report their own capacity in a direction consistent with avoiding attracting requests, with up to 30% of self-declared-low-bandwidth users measured to hold significantly higher true bandwidth [SAROIU-MMCN-02]. Real-deployment churn data show the transit tier is only moderately more stable than the leaf population it protects, not dramatically so: measured average connection lifetime was 93 minutes for ultrapeers against 58 minutes for leaves, a 1.5x difference, not an order of magnitude [LOO-IPTPS-04].

The recommended bandwidth-ordered routing layer among transit-tier members fails under concurrent churn during convergence, per the general statement in [FELDMANN-CSUR-21] that termination "may not be reached if the node set changes during convergence" and that no bound in this literature covers concurrent join/leave rate — a statement in evident tension with this project's own unsourced claim that HSkip+ specifically survives 60% random and 35% adversarial churn, a tension this corpus supplies no way to resolve (see below). Neither half of this selection defends against a misreported capacity value at the point that value determines admission to the transit tier or rank within it: [YANG-ICDE-03] states its own "limited altruism" assumption explicitly does not address freeloading or malicious refusal, and no entry attributed to HSkip+ in this corpus addresses misreport at all. Layering in a verification mechanism narrows, but does not close, that gap, at a measured price: PeerFlow's aggregation quorum adds 119.6 bytes/second average per-authority reporting overhead and up to 2.38 KiB/s network-wide dummy-traffic overhead under its highest-trim setting, and takes a median 27.7 hours (75th percentile 70.7 hours, worst case 316.4 hours) to establish a given relay's measured capacity [JOHNSON-POPETS-17]; FlashFlow's dedicated measurement team requires standing bandwidth provisioned at roughly 2.84x the target's own claimed capacity, an ongoing operational allowance rather than a one-time charge, and both mechanisms depend on a small semi-trusted quorum this design's own goal of resisting single-organization capture argues against reintroducing at scale [TRAUDT-ICDCS-21].

### What the corpus does not settle

- HSkip+ itself (Feldotto, Scheideler, Graffi, P2P 2014) has no full-text entry under `registry/evidence` in this corpus. Every specific figure attributed to it in this document — the O(log²n)/O(log⁴n) message-count comparison, the bandwidth-ordering routing property, the 60%/35% churn-survival claim, the O(log n) dilation and congestion bounds — is sourced only to this project's own already-verified reading list, not to an independently retrievable primary source this synthesis, or a future one, can check. Retrieving the paper directly and entering it as a full evidence-file record is a precondition for treating any of those figures as settled.
- The apparent tension between [FELDMANN-CSUR-21]'s general statement that this literature's self-stabilization convergence proofs assume a static node set during convergence, and the specific claim that HSkip+ survives 60% random and 35% adversarial churn, is not adjudicated by anything in this corpus. The survey does not cite a churn bound for HSkip+ itself; whether HSkip+'s own paper proves a churn-tolerance result outside the base model the survey describes is unknown here.
- No entry in this corpus measures or proves a defense against a false capacity claim made specifically to gain a structural position — admission to a transit tier, or rank within a capacity-ordered routing structure — in an open-membership overlay with no privileged administrative quorum. `registry/open-problems/honest-capacity-reporting.md` records this as its own verdict of "open" after an independent search, and states directly that the nearest published defenses (PeerFlow, FlashFlow) bound a *selection probability* over a flat, unordered relay set, a different quantity from the *position* a capacity-ordered structure assigns, and that neither was designed, tested, or discussed by its own authors as an ordering input. That same open-problem entry also reports two papers checked in full but not entered as their own evidence-file records in this corpus — `PATEL-ARXIV-25` (a September 2025 survey of secure peer-to-peer networks) and `IHLE-CSUR-23` (a 2023 systematic review of peer-to-peer incentive mechanisms) — so their findings are relayed here at one remove, through that open-problem document, rather than independently verified by this selection.
- No entry composes the local proportional-response rule (PropShare) with a two-tier or bandwidth-ordered admission layer running above it. [LEVIN-SIGCOMM-08] evaluates PropShare only within an already-formed BitTorrent swarm of directly interested peers; how it behaves layered under a supernode cluster or a capacity-ordered routing structure is untested anywhere in this corpus.
- No entry quantifies what fraction of a population matching [SAROIU-MMCN-02]'s measured heterogeneity, filtered specifically by cellular or metered-connection status rather than merely low peak bandwidth, would fall below any candidate's actual admission threshold for the transit tier. [DOAN-NETWORKING-20] measures a mobile client's own retrieval performance over cellular versus WiFi (startup delay, path length, round-trip time to a content gateway), not a mobile device's suitability or exclusion as a transit node for other peers' traffic, so it does not supply this number either.
- Bootstrapping — how a joining node is assigned to the transit tier or to a specific transit-tier neighbor set without a centralized directory — is stated as an open problem by [YANG-ICDE-03] itself and is not closed by any other entry in this corpus.
- Lv, Ratnasamy, and Shenker's alternative ("Can Heterogeneity Make Gnutella Scalable?", IPTPS 2002), which [YANG-ICDE-03]'s own bibliography describes as redirecting flow toward high-capacity nodes without a formal supernode tier, has no full-text entry anywhere in this corpus and cannot be compared against the selection on evidence.


---

## Application data model: one data shape needs one mechanism, not one mechanism for all data — hash-identified operation-based CRDTs for editable state, single-writer signed logs for feeds, content-addressed immutable objects for media, and Operational Transformation excluded entirely

The application data model states how a person's edit to a shared object, a person's append to their own feed, and a person's upload of a photo or video each reach every other replica and converge to one agreed value, without any replica waiting for a server or a quorum vote before accepting the local edit. Three shapes of data appear in every candidate application (a post being corrected is editable state with more than one possible writer over time; a user's timeline is a feed only that user ever appends to; an uploaded photo is written once and never edited again), and the corpus's evidence separates cleanly along exactly that boundary: no single mechanism among the four candidates handles all three shapes at its measured or proven cost, and each of the three shapes has one candidate whose requirements match it and two whose requirements do not.

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| State-based Conflict-free Replicated Data Type (CvRDT): full or partial local state transmitted, merged by a least-upper-bound (LUB) operation over a join semilattice | No implementation or benchmark in the founding papers; proved only formally [`SHAPIRO-SSS-11`, `SHAPIRO-EATCS-11`]. Merkle Search Trees, a large-collection instance of this style, measure 66% lower bandwidth and 34% lower dissemination-entropy than vector-clock gossip at 1000 nodes, 0.1 events/round, base B=16 [`AUVOLAT-SRDS-19`] — but at 1 event/round the same construction loses on entropy and delivery delay to the vector-clock baseline, and a competing hash-prefix-tree variant fails to terminate at 2000 nodes under that load [`AUVOLAT-SRDS-19`]. | Delivered state values form a join semilattice and every update is an inflation (never decreasing under the semilattice order) [`SHAPIRO-SSS-11`]. Merge is idempotent, commutative, and associative, so message loss, duplication, and reordering are all tolerated [`SHAPIRO-EATCS-11`]. | Metadata (a replica-indexed map, e.g.) grows linearly with the number of replicas that have ever mutated the object [`ALMEIDA-CSUR-25`]; garbage-collecting that metadata needs the full replica set known and no undetected permanent crash [`SHAPIRO-EATCS-11`]. | Only best-effort, eventually-complete delivery — no ordering, no exactly-once guarantee [`SHAPIRO-SSS-11`]. |
| Operation-based Commutative Replicated Data Type (CmRDT): each update broadcasts only its operation, applied at every replica once a precondition holds | RGA (Replicated Growable Array), the list instance, has proved metadata overhead O(D log k) for D deletions and k operations, against a proved Ω(D) lower bound for any protocol in its class [`ATTIYA-PODC-16`]. The tree-move instance measures 5,700 operations/sec sustained throughput and ~1–2 µs median local-apply latency on an optimized single-writer-equivalent Scala implementation, versus 22,000 ops/sec for leader-based state machine replication on identical EC2 hardware — but with up to 100,000× lower latency than the leader-based alternative for a remote replica issuing one write [`KLEPPMANN-TPDS-22`]. | Every pair of concurrent operations commutes (produces equivalent results regardless of order and preserves each other's enabledness) [`SHAPIRO-SSS-11`]. | Requires causal-order delivery: an update that happened-before another must be delivered first, everywhere [`SHAPIRO-EATCS-11`]. Unbounded memory growth under a long partition, because every undelivered operation must be retained by every replica that generated it [`ALMEIDA-CSUR-25`]. | A causal-broadcast transport (vector clocks or an equivalent) [`SHAPIRO-EATCS-11`]. |
| Hash-identified, Byzantine-tolerant operation-based CRDT: operations identified by the cryptographic hash of their own encoding, forming a directed acyclic graph (DAG) of hash-linked predecessors, reconciled by comparing head hashes | 96.7% of pairwise reconciliations complete in one round trip, 3.2% in two, using a Bloom-filter-assisted head comparison; near-constant ~1 kB network overhead per reconciliation regardless of accumulated backlog, versus more than double the theoretical optimum for the naive (non-Bloom) variant; 4-replica, in-memory, single-process prototype, 600 reconciliations averaged per data point [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]. The complete delivered-update set for up to three concurrent heads and 256-bit hashes summarizes in under 100 bytes regardless of total update count [`KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22`]. | Collision resistance of the hash function (SHA-256, both papers) [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`, `KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22`]. Every replica signs its own messages and discards any message with an invalid signature [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]. | Correct replicas must form one connected component in the network graph — a faulty replica on every path between two correct replicas can block their exchange indefinitely, and the paper states ensuring full connectivity "an open question" [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]. Provably cannot preserve any invariant that is not invariant-confluent (I-confluent) under arbitrary Byzantine faults — proved concretely for a non-negative account balance under concurrent debits [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]. Extending the construction's convergence guarantee to a specific CRDT's own validity conditions (RGA, WOOT-style list types) is stated as an unproved conjecture [`KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22`]. | Every node stores the complete DAG, not a summary [`KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22`]; each replica durably persists, per remote peer, the head set from the last successful reconciliation [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]. |
| Operational Transformation (OT): each site applies an edit immediately, then transforms every subsequently arriving concurrent edit against its own local log before applying it | Reduces to 3 transformation calls versus 4 on one 3-operation worked example (GOTO vs. GOT); no benchmark with node counts, message counts, or wall-clock timing exists in the retrieved corpus for any surveyed OT algorithm [`SUN-CSCW-98`, `ELLIS-SIGMOD-89`]. | The chosen transformation function satisfies TP1 (pairwise commutation of independent operations) for two-way (client-server) topologies, and both TP1 and TP2 (path-independence across differing chains of concurrent operations) for arbitrary peer-to-peer topologies [`SUN-CSCW-98`]. | "No method exists to verify whether a given application-specific transformation function" satisfies TP1/TP2, and "some seemingly correct transformation functions do not" [`SUN-CSCW-98`]. A naive priority scheme (site identifier alone) is shown, by the founding paper's own three-site worked example, to diverge to two different final strings [`ELLIS-SIGMOD-89`]. Detecting quiescence to bound unbounded log growth "is equivalent to the well known distributed consensus problem" [`ELLIS-SIGMOD-89`]. TP1-only simplification (Jupiter) is available solely under a single central relay server; a fully decentralized topology needs both TP1 and TP2 proved per transformation function [`SUN-CSCW-98`]. | A hand-specified transformation function per operator pair, proved correct independently for every new data model [`ELLIS-SIGMOD-89`]. Exactly-once, error-free message delivery "taken as given," not supplied by OT itself [`ELLIS-SIGMOD-89`]. A full causally-ordered log or history buffer retained at every site [`SUN-CSCW-98`]. |
| Signed append-only log: one owner per log, each entry signed and hash-linked to the log's own immediately preceding entry, reconciled by comparing per-log frontier indices | No controlled measurement; the corpus's only entry for this mechanism explicitly states it reports no benchmark methodology, sample size, or conditions, and flags its own figures as anecdotal [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`]. The paper's structural claim, not a measured one: frontier-based reconciliation needs no tunable buffer size, retransmission policy, or gossip fan-out, because logs persist in full and reconciliation exchanges exactly the missing suffix [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`]. | Only the log owner's private key can extend that log — enforced by per-entry signature verification tied to the log's own public key [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`]. | A single log has no cross-writer conflict to resolve by construction, but nothing in the mechanism merges two different logs' concurrent claims about one shared mutable object — it has no merge operation at all [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`]. Open (unrestricted) replication grows per-store memory proportional to the total activity of every participant ever recorded and cannot resist unwanted content, since a removed log is copied back by the next peer that still holds it [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`]. | Durable, unbounded per-store persistence with no eviction, because frontier-difference computation is undefined once events are evicted [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`]. Reliable, ordered delivery between two connected stores, but not causal-broadcast delivery across the whole network [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`]. |
| Content-addressed immutable object: every object located and verified by the cryptographic hash of its own bytes, linked into a Merkle directed acyclic graph (DAG) | No experiment, benchmark, or deployment measurement in the design paper itself; every performance figure it states is either a fixed design parameter (a 1 KB direct-storage threshold in the routing layer) or a number the paper attributes to a separately cited system, not measured here [`BENET-ARXIV-14`]. | Collision resistance of the addressing hash function; tampering with any linked object changes its hash and is therefore detected by whichever object refers to it, without a quorum or a trusted verifier [`BENET-ARXIV-14`]. | Objects are immutable by construction — there is no operation that edits an object in place, only publication of a new object under a new hash [`BENET-ARXIV-14`]. An object with no participant choosing to retain ("pin") it beyond transient caching has no guaranteed persistence: "all blocks available in IPFS are in some node's local storage," and there is no other persistence layer [`BENET-ARXIV-14`]. Mutability (a stable name resolving to a changing object) is layered on separately via a signed pointer record, itself a single-entry signed log, published through the routing/DHT layer [`BENET-ARXIV-14`]. | Every participating node supplies local storage for retrieved blocks [`BENET-ARXIV-14`]; a routing/discovery layer that keeps answering which peers currently hold and are willing to serve a given hash [`BENET-ARXIV-14`]. |

### Selection

Three mechanisms compose, one per data shape, and Operational Transformation is excluded in every topology this design permits. Editable, potentially multi-writer state (a post's text, a profile field, a folder tree) is represented as a hash-identified, Byzantine-tolerant operation-based CRDT, following the construction of `KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20` and `KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22`. A feed — any sequence only one identity ever appends to (a user's post history, an activity stream) — is represented as a signed append-only log, following `KERMARREC-DICG-20`'s Secure Scuttlebutt (SSB) model. Immutable media (an uploaded photo, video, or document, never edited after upload) is represented as a content-addressed immutable object in a Merkle DAG, following `BENET-ARXIV-14`'s IPFS model. These three are not substitutes for each other and the composition between them is mechanical, not architectural: the mutable "name" that lets a stable identifier resolve to the current head of a CRDT's operation DAG, or to the newest object in an evolving media collection, is itself one signed log entry per publish — the same primitive selected for feeds, reused as the pointer mechanism between the other two.

Against plain state-based CRDTs for editable state (rejecting them as the general case, while retaining Merkle Search Trees as a conditional secondary choice, addressed below): state-based and operation-based CRDTs are proved equally expressive — either can emulate the other [`SHAPIRO-SSS-11`, Theorems 3–4] — so the choice is a transport-cost tradeoff, not an expressiveness one. Operation-based transmission carries only the delta an edit produced, not the whole object; for the tree-move instance this is measured directly against a leader-based alternative on identical hardware, giving up to 100,000× lower write latency for a geographically remote replica in exchange for 4×–23× lower peak throughput than the leader [`KLEPPMANN-TPDS-22`] — a bound the state-based style's own founding papers report no comparable measurement for [`SHAPIRO-SSS-11`, `SHAPIRO-EATCS-11`]. Merkle Search Trees, the corpus's one measured state-based instance at scale, only wins on bandwidth and dissemination at light update rates (0.1 events/round); at 1 event/round it loses to plain vector-clock gossip on entropy and delivery delay, and a related hash-prefix variant fails to terminate outright at 2000 nodes [`AUVOLAT-SRDS-19`] — so state-based propagation is selected only for large, low-mutation-rate collections (an index over an already-settled dataset), not for the general editable-object case.

Against Operational Transformation: OT is excluded from the general (peer-to-peer, no permanent relay) case because its only simplification to one provable condition, TP1, is available exclusively under a single central server relaying every transformed operation [`SUN-CSCW-98`] — a permanent centralized relay is precisely the capture point this architecture is built to avoid (§1 of the brief). Outside that topology, OT requires both TP1 and TP2 proved for every operator pair in the application's data model, and the founding survey states plainly that "no method exists to verify" this and that "some seemingly correct transformation functions do not" satisfy it [`SUN-CSCW-98`] — a per-data-type correctness burden with no general proof technique, where the selected CRDT construction's corresponding burden (proving operations commute, or proving a merge is an inflation) has already been discharged once for the counter, last-writer-wins register, multi-value register, and observed-remove set, each with an implementation proved to match its own worst-case metadata-overhead lower bound [`BURCKHARDT-POPL-14`], for maps, sets, registers, and nested JSON documents generally [`KLEPPMANN-TPDS-17`], for ordered lists and text via RGA [`ATTIYA-PODC-16`, proved to satisfy the strong list specification], and for hierarchical trees via the move operation [`KLEPPMANN-TPDS-22`, mechanically checked in Isabelle/HOL with no unproved assumption]. The metadata-cost argument sometimes made for OT over CRDTs does not survive the corpus's own lower bound: `ATTIYA-PODC-16` proves any protocol in the "push-based" class — which the paper states explicitly includes deployed eventually-consistent write-propagating protocols generally, OT included — carries Ω(D) metadata overhead in the number of deletions, the identical floor RGA's proved O(D log k) nearly meets. OT supplies no advantage CRDTs lack and adds an unresolved, per-data-type proof obligation CRDTs do not.

Against plain (non-Byzantine) operation-based CRDTs, for the specific choice of identifier scheme: sequence-number or vector-clock identifiers let one faulty replica equivocate — emit two distinct updates carrying the identical declared identifier to two different correct replicas — which is demonstrated concretely (Figure 1) to defeat version-vector-based reconciliation [`KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22`]. Deriving each operation's identifier from its own content hash removes this failure because a faulty replica cannot alter the identifier without changing the hash, at a measured cost — 96.7% one-round-trip reconciliation, ~1 kB overhead — that the plain vector-clock construction's own founding papers report no comparable figure for [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`; `SHAPIRO-SSS-11`, `SHAPIRO-EATCS-11`]. The one recorded cost of this substitution is storage: hash-based identifiers are larger than short sequence numbers, a cost the paper states can be reduced by recomputing hashes on demand rather than storing them all, without measuring that reduction [`KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22`].

Against using a signed append-only log as the mechanism for editable multi-writer state directly (rather than only for feeds): a log by itself supplies no merge operation — SSB's reconciliation resolves exactly which entries one store is missing from another, not what a shared mutable field's value should become when two owners have each proposed a different one [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`]. Using a signed log to carry a CRDT's own operations is not a competing choice at all; it is the operation-based CRDT's own transport requirement (reliable, hash-linked delivery of signed operations) under another name, so the two candidates are complementary rather than substitutable for this shape of data.

Against using content-addressed immutable objects for editable state directly: every "edit" under this mechanism is an entirely new object under a new hash, since objects are immutable by construction, and the mechanism supplies no algorithm at all for reconciling two concurrently-proposed new roots for the same logical document [`BENET-ARXIV-14`] — precisely the missing piece a CRDT's proved commutativity or inflation property supplies. Its own mutable-pointer layer (IPNS) is constructed as a signed record published to a routing table, the identical single-writer signed-pointer pattern selected for feeds [`BENET-ARXIV-14`], not a distinct mechanism.

Against using signed logs or content-addressed objects for each other's shape: media benefits specifically from content addressing's automatic cross-user deduplication — two participants uploading byte-identical media collapse to one stored object under this scheme, a property no per-writer signed log gives, since every entry in an SSB-style log is scoped to one owner's key regardless of content [`BENET-ARXIV-14` vs. `KERMARREC-DICG-20`]. Conversely, a feed gains nothing from content-addressed immutability specifically, because a feed's ordering and single-writer-authorship guarantees are exactly what a hash-chained signed log already supplies at lower mechanism cost — a plain per-entry hash link plus one signature check, not a whole Merkle-DAG object model with a separately layered naming scheme [`KERMARREC-DICG-20` vs. `BENET-ARXIV-14`].

### Byzantine tolerance

Classical Byzantine fault-tolerant (BFT) state machine replication bounds the fraction of faulty replicas a protocol tolerates — typically requiring 3f+1 replicas to tolerate f faulty ones, reducible to 2f+1 under synchrony and digital signatures [figures cited in `KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20` from the prior literature it surveys]. That bound presupposes a fixed, known replica count an adversary cannot inflate. An open peer-to-peer network with no admission authority cannot supply that precondition: `DOUCEUR-IPTPS-02` proves that without a logically centralized identity issuer, a single faulty participant commanding resources ρ times a minimally-capable participant's resources presents ρ distinct identities to any validator demanding proof of resources, and, absent synchronized validation rounds, presents an unbounded number of identities regardless of resource ratio (Lemma 2) — so a bounded-adversary-fraction assumption has no basis to hold, and `KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22` cites exactly this result as its own stated reason for rejecting quorum-based BFT protocols for this setting.

The selected hash-identified CRDT construction removes the bounded-fraction requirement rather than tightening it: `KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`'s central theorem states a fault-tolerant Byzantine Eventually Consistent (BEC) algorithm exists for a set of operations if and only if those operations are invariant-confluent (I-confluent) — concurrent executions, each individually preserving an invariant, still preserve it when merged in either order — a condition that holds independent of how many replicas are faulty. Every commuting-operation or inflation-based CRDT selected above for editable state is, by the corresponding SEC (Strong Eventual Consistency) proof, exactly this kind of I-confluent construction [`SHAPIRO-SSS-11`], so the Byzantine variant tolerates an unbounded fraction of faulty replicas for the same operations the non-Byzantine variant already handles, measured at 96.7% one-round-trip reconciliation cost in the 4-replica prototype [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]. The limit is exact, not approximate: the same theorem proves no fault-tolerant BEC algorithm exists for an invariant that is not I-confluent under arbitrary Byzantine faults, demonstrated concretely on a cryptocurrency balance, where two concurrent debits from one account can jointly violate a non-negative-balance invariant even though each alone would not [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`] — so any operation this design needs to enforce a cross-replica invariant outside pure commuting/inflating updates (a global uniqueness constraint on a handle, an exactly-once counter) needs a separate, explicitly bounded-adversary consensus sub-mechanism for that specific operation, which this selection does not supply and no entry in this corpus's Domain D measures for an unbounded-Byzantine setting.

For feeds and media, Byzantine tolerance is a structural consequence of the mechanism rather than a separately proved property.
