A signed log's single-writer-per-key construction means a faulty writer can corrupt only its own log, never another's, and other participants simply choose whether to continue following it [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`]; a content-addressed object is self-verifying by its own hash, so a faulty peer serving the wrong bytes is detected on receipt, not merely outvoted by a quorum [`BENET-ARXIV-14`]. Neither of these properties is separately measured in the corpus; both follow directly from the stated construction.

### Secondary indexes and range queries over content-addressed data

No entry in this corpus measures a secondary index (for example, "every post by principal X" or "every entry with tag Y") or a range query built directly over a CRDT-object store or a content-addressed immutable-object store at multi-writer, adversarial, internet scale. What exists is three unconnected pieces.

First, content-addressed ordered trees that support range access with a real measured cost: prolly trees, a content-addressed key-value structure supporting both Merkle verification and B-tree-style ordered range access, measure 24%–45% faster tree creation and roughly 3× faster large-scale insertion than two real deployed prolly-tree implementations (Dolthub, Canvas/okra), at 10,000,000–17,000,000 entries, on one benchmarked machine [`RAWAT-DLT-24`]. Second, range-based set reconciliation protocols that assume a range-queryable local index already exists and reconcile over ranges of it, rather than building that index: Willow's three-dimensional reconciliation splits a mismatched range recursively until fingerprints agree, in a logarithmic number of rounds by construction, but reports no measured round count, message count, or bandwidth figure on any concrete topology, and states outright that the requisite local index over (subspace, path, timestamp) triples supporting efficient range splitting by entry count is a precondition the specification assumes, not one it supplies [`WILLOW-SPEC-23`]. A concrete backend for exactly this kind of reconciliation is measured: an aggregate-augmented B+-tree realization is 4.69×–13.98× faster to reconcile than a naive auxiliary-tree baseline across six synthetic workload families, on one machine, single-threaded, with concurrent-transaction and crash-recovery behavior explicitly out of scope [`AMPARORE-ARXIV-26`]. Third, the correctness property both range-reconciliation approaches require of their backing structure — that clamping an arbitrary subrange of two structurally different, same-content trees always yields an identical clamped subtree ("clamping-invariant") — is proved in this corpus only for treaps and is proved to fail for prolly trees specifically: "Prolly-trees are not clamping-invariant," because chunk boundaries are set by a rolling hash over a window of consecutive items, and clamping changes which items fall inside that window [`MEYER-TR-24`]. Merkle Search Trees are listed among the structures the same paper asserts, but does not prove, are clamping-invariant [`MEYER-TR-24`], and Merkle Search Trees' own founding paper independently states that using them as a secondary index over data distributed via a distributed hash table is proposed but explicitly unevaluated: "the feasibility of such an approach when many updates occur at many nodes has not yet been evaluated" [`AUVOLAT-SRDS-19`].

The corpus therefore records this incompatibility, not merely an absence of measurement: the one content-addressed range-supporting structure with real deployed-scale performance numbers (prolly trees) is proved unusable, as-is, with the one reconciliation protocol built specifically for three-dimensional range queries over content-addressed entries (Willow/RBSR), because the former is proved not to have the property the latter's own correctness proof requires [`MEYER-TR-24` vs. `RAWAT-DLT-24`]. Building a secondary index over the CRDT and content-addressed stores selected above therefore needs one of: a clamping-invariant backing structure (a treap, proved; or one of the other twelve structures `MEYER-TR-24` lists but does not prove) accepting whatever performance cost that substitution carries relative to prolly trees' measured numbers, an adaptation of prolly-tree chunking that restores clamping-invariance, or an index built and reconciled by a mechanism outside the range-based family entirely — none of which is proved, measured, or ruled out by any entry in this corpus. This is also where a further, uncosted consequence attaches, belonging to the privacy-tiers component rather than to this selection: once a range query returns which stored entries matched, that return channel alone — independent of any encryption on the matched content — is sufficient for a statistical-learning attack to reconstruct the approximate order and value of the underlying records, demonstrated at 500 range queries recovering record order to within 2% of a 10^6-value domain, and separately at 100 range queries predicting a majority of a real 248,000-record salary dataset to within 10,000 currency units [`GRUBBS-SP-19`]. Any secondary index built on the structures above needs the privacy-tiers component to account for this leakage explicitly; this selection does not resolve it.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- The transport component supplies, for the hash-identified CRDT construction, pairwise reconciliation of head-hash sets between any two replicas, and durable per-remote-peer storage of the head set from the last successful reconciliation [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]; it need not supply causal-broadcast ordering, since the DAG's own predecessor hashes carry causal order.
- The identity component supplies, per replica, a distinct signing key pair, with every other replica's public key discoverable, since the Byzantine-tolerant construction discards any message with an invalid signature [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]; per feed writer, one persistent signing key whose corresponding public key serves as the log's identifier [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`].
- The network/overlay component keeps correct replicas connected as one component in the network graph; the selected Byzantine-tolerant construction's own guarantee is void for any pair of correct replicas an adversary can fully separate, and the source paper states ensuring this "an open question" it does not resolve [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`].
- Whichever component enforces cross-replica invariants outside plain commuting or inflating operations (uniqueness, exact counts) supplies a separately bounded-adversary consensus mechanism for exactly those operations; the selected construction proves it cannot supply this for non-I-confluent invariants under unbounded Byzantine faults [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`].
- The storage/retention component supplies an explicit pinning or retention decision for every content-addressed media object; the selected mechanism supplies no default persistence policy of its own, and an unpinned object surviving only in transient caches has no durability guarantee [`BENET-ARXIV-14`].
- The content-location/routing component keeps answering, for both content-addressed objects and the mutable name pointing at the current CRDT-DAG head or feed frontier, which peers currently hold and will serve the requested hash; both the object-retrieval path and the name-resolution path degrade together with routing, even though the underlying immutable objects remain independently held [`BENET-ARXIV-14`].
- Every CRDT type this design introduces beyond the four the corpus has verified generally (maps/sets/registers/JSON documents, ordered lists/text, hierarchical trees, plus the graph type sketched but not resolved [`SHAPIRO-SSS-11`]) needs its own commutativity or inflation proof before deployment; the hash-identified construction's `before(u)`-restricted validity rule is conjectured, not proved, to extend to arbitrary CRDT validity conditions beyond bare identifier uniqueness [`KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22`].

### What it costs and where it fails

The hash-identified Byzantine-tolerant CRDT construction costs 96.7% one-round-trip reconciliation and ~1 kB network overhead per reconciliation in a 4-replica, single-process, in-memory prototype with no actually-faulty replicas present [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`] — CPU and memory use were not measured, and the paper's own statement that behavior would not fundamentally change with faulty replicas present is an unmeasured belief the paper itself flags as such, not a reported result [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]. It fails outright — no fault-tolerant algorithm exists, proved, not merely unmeasured — for any invariant that is not invariant-confluent under arbitrary Byzantine faults [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]. It fails to make progress between two correct replicas an adversary has fully separated on the network graph [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]. Storage grows without bound: every node retains the complete update DAG, and no garbage-collection mechanism compatible with an unbounded, unknown effective replica set is proved or measured anywhere in this corpus's Domain D — the one stability-based GC scheme this corpus records requires a known, bounded replica set with no undetected permanent crash [`SHAPIRO-EATCS-11`], a precondition the selected construction's whole point is to avoid needing.

For feeds, the signed-log mechanism's central limitation is scale under the open (unrestricted) replication policy: per-store memory grows proportional to the total activity of every participant ever recorded, and the paper's own stated fix — restricting replication to a transitively-followed interest graph — leaks the follower graph to anyone observing the update exchange, which the source paper states makes the mechanism unsuitable for a participant whose safety depends on hiding whom they follow [`KERMARREC-DICG-20`].

For media, the content-addressed mechanism's central limitation is that persistence is opt-in and unenforced: an object nobody chooses to pin survives only as long as some node's transient cache happens to retain it, with "no other persistence layer" supplying a default [`BENET-ARXIV-14`].

For secondary indexing, the corpus's own measured range-reconciliation speedup (4.69×–13.98×, `AMPARORE-ARXIV-26`) and the corpus's own measured content-addressed range-index speedup (24%–45% and ~3×, `RAWAT-DLT-24`) cannot currently be composed, because the structure carrying the second measurement is proved to lack the property the first measurement's protocol requires [`MEYER-TR-24`], and returning any range-query match set at all — independent of this composition question — is proved sufficient, on its own, to reconstruct approximate record order and value from encrypted content [`GRUBBS-SP-19`].

### What the corpus does not settle

The hash-identified Byzantine-tolerant CRDT construction's own convergence proof for specific data types beyond bare identifier uniqueness (RGA-style lists needing deletion-reference validity, WOOT/YATA-style insertion-predecessor validity) is stated by its own author as an open conjecture: "I leave a detailed proof of this conjecture... for future work" [`KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22`]. No entry in this corpus supplies that proof for any specific type.

No entry measures Byzantine causal broadcast performance beyond a 4-replica, single-process, non-adversarial laboratory prototype [`KLEPPMANN-ARXIV-20`]; how reconciliation cost, DAG storage growth, or head-comparison round count behaves with millions of writers and a genuinely adversarial, churning replica population is unmeasured anywhere in the corpus.

No entry measures long-run storage growth of retained operation history at the multi-year timescale a real social application would run at. The one field report in this corpus states plainly that a CRDT's complete change history "created measured performance and memory/disk problems in real use" in one internally-built prototype, but gives no figure — no byte count, no growth rate, no time-to-problem [`KLEPPMANN-ONWARD-19`]. Whether the selected hash-identified construction's ~100-byte-per-frontier summary [`KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-22`] postpones or merely relocates this problem is not addressed by any entry.

No entry benchmarks a secondary index or range query built directly over any of the three selected mechanisms — a hash-identified CRDT store, a signed-log store, or a content-addressed immutable-object store — at any scale; the closest available pieces (prolly trees, Willow, the RSOS/AELMDB backend) neither target this combination directly nor compose with each other without an unproved or explicitly absent adaptation, as detailed above [`RAWAT-DLT-24`, `WILLOW-SPEC-23`, `AMPARORE-ARXIV-26`, `MEYER-TR-24`].

Garbage collection compatible with an unbounded, adversarial, unknown-membership replica set — as opposed to the known-bounded-membership stability model this corpus's non-Byzantine CRDT literature assumes [`SHAPIRO-EATCS-11`] — is not proposed, proved, or measured by any entry in this corpus. Unbounded DAG and log growth under the selected Byzantine-tolerant construction is therefore an open cost, not a bounded and merely unmeasured one: no candidate mechanism for bounding it exists in the retrieved evidence.

Whether the corpus's one proved metadata-overhead lower bound for push-based protocols, which rules out any OT-family metadata advantage over CRDTs for lists and text [`ATTIYA-PODC-16`], generalizes to the map, set, and tree data types this selection also needs is unaddressed; no entry proves or measures an equivalent Ω(D)-class lower bound for those types under a push-based protocol.

RGA, the list/text instance this selection relies on for `ATTIYA-PODC-16`'s strong-list-specification proof, is separately shown by the same research group to still permit a lesser interleaving anomaly: a third party's concurrent insertion can land between two runs of one user's own earlier insertions when that user typed non-sequentially, and the proposed per-operation fix (a set of sibling timestamps carried on every insertion) is stated as an unproved conjecture — "a formal proof of this conjecture is left for future work" [`KLEPPMANN-PAPOC-19`, corroborated in `KLEPPMANN-AFP-18`]. No entry in this corpus proves the fix, measures its bandwidth or storage cost, or establishes how often the triggering non-sequential-insertion pattern occurs in real editing.


---

## Ranking: Independently operated feed generators a client subscribes to compute every ranking, but the only deployed measurement of default-switching shows fewer than 3 in 100 users ever pick an alternative — so whatever mechanism computes the default, not the marketplace around it, is what nearly everyone experiences, and no candidate in this corpus has been deployed as that default while also being measured for content-ranking quality

Ranking orders a set of candidate items — posts, videos, search results — for one viewer, without any party holding a global index of the whole collection. The question this component answers is not only which computation produces the order, but who is allowed to choose that computation. Four candidates answer that second question differently: a marketplace of independently operated ranking services a client opts into (`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`, `BALDUF-IMC-24`, `QUELLE-PLOSONE-25`), a value each viewer's own device computes from its private position in a linkage graph with no company or committee involved (`NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`, `ZIEGLER-ISF-05`), a single designated aggregation point that produces one ranking every viewer downstream of it sees identically (`KAMVAR-WWW-03`, `GYONGYI-VLDB-04`, `POLINSKI-CCR-24`), and a ranking that emerges from raw behavioral signals peers exchange directly with each other by gossip, with no marketplace, no committee, and no company (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`, `HEGEDUS-ECMLPKDD-19`, `GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`). Only the first candidate has been deployed to real users at a scale anyone has measured, and that measurement (`QUELLE-PLOSONE-25`: 139,033 of 5,000,000 Bluesky users, 2.8%, ever liked even one alternative feed) is the only entry in this corpus that speaks to the decisive question stated in this component's own task definition — whether a person changes a default. For every other candidate, whether a person would change away from a default computed that way is not measured anywhere in this corpus; stating so is a guess, not a citation-backed claim, and is labeled as one below.

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Client-selected feed generator marketplace, network-wide (AT Protocol Feed Generators) | Approximately 30 gigabytes/day of Firehose bandwidth per full-network-observing generator operator; roughly 40,000-43,000 generators created by 18,000-18,352 distinct accounts by 2024 (`BALDUF-IMC-24`, `QUELLE-PLOSONE-25`). Only 2.8% of 5,000,000 users ever liked an alternative feed (`QUELLE-PLOSONE-25`). | Every generator computes its own ranking honestly from a public Firehose stream it independently verifies; the platform places no cryptographic check on a generator's output (`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`). | New-author reciprocal-rank penalty of -0.061 (SE 0.020, p=0.003), robust across all 250 independently tested feeds, independent of follower count (`WANG-ARXIV-26`). Hosting re-concentrates: top 3 hosting platforms serve 95.8% of all generators, one platform (Skyfeed) alone serving 85.86% (`BALDUF-IMC-24`). | A Relay/Firehose component delivering every signed repository update to any generator operator (`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`); stable, network-wide content and account identifiers a generator can reference (`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`); a hosting-diversity mechanism this candidate does not itself supply. |
| Client-side source-merge, no separate generator network (braids.social priority-slider over existing Mastodon API feeds) | Qualitative only: 9 of 10 lab participants set the unranked "Following Content" source to High priority; 8 of 11 part-one interviewees stated a preference for Mastodon's chronological order over an algorithmic one (`LIU-PACMHCI-25`). No throughput, bandwidth, or scale figure reported; n=10-11. | The home server's existing API endpoints (Home Timeline, Public, Trending Status) return correct, complete results; the client trusts the server-computed "Trending" source it merges in, which is itself one server-side ranking (`LIU-PACMHCI-25`). | Small sample (n=10-11) drawn from a self-selected Mastodon user base the authors state is atypically privacy- and transparency-conscious; no scale, attack, or production-adoption data (`LIU-PACMHCI-25`). | No protocol change; requires the home server to already expose a Home Timeline, a Public/local timeline, and a Trending Status endpoint (`LIU-PACMHCI-25`); requires OAuth 2.0 read-only account access. |
| Per-viewer graph metric, personalized propagation over a subjective feedback graph (Personalized PageRank / Personalized Hitting Time / MaxFlow) | No content-ranking figure in this corpus. Measured only for reputation/reward allocation: on a 2,057-node, 35,853-edge governance-forum graph, Personalized Hitting Time (PHT) reaches the lowest attacker's gain of the three algorithms tested, combined transitivity+connectivity decay bounds gain to 1.5 at 50 injected Sybil nodes per epoch (`NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`). | Each viewer's subjective graph view converges as gossip propagates; a fixed seed node whose own trustworthiness is established outside the mechanism (`NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`). | Repeated per-epoch Sybil injection still produces higher attacker's gain than a single attack for all three algorithms; epoch decay is stated to fail against repeated attacks; direct neighbors of the seed gain up to 7x their baseline value under maximum transitivity decay (`NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`). | A gossip protocol propagating feedback-graph edges (`NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`); an externally established seed node; a consuming component that accepts a per-viewer, not a globally agreed, score. |
| Per-viewer graph metric, personalized spreading activation (Appleseed) | Converges in 38-45 iterations on a 384-node social-trust graph regardless of a 4x change in injected trust (`ZIEGLER-ISF-05`). Measured for trust assessment, not content ranking. | A per-node trust graph is retrievable on demand by any assessing party (`ZIEGLER-ISF-05`). | "Eager truster" penalty: a node that issues many statements has each one diluted (`ZIEGLER-ISF-05`), a distortion that would apply identically to a node that engages with many items if reused for content ranking. | A publishing convention or lookup service for per-node graph edges; a chosen spreading factor and convergence threshold not derived by the source paper for an arbitrary topology (`ZIEGLER-ISF-05`). |
| Consensus-favouring aggregation, network-wide global eigenvector (EigenTrust) | Converges in fewer than 10 power-iteration rounds on a simulated 1,000-peer network; with the mechanism active, inauthentic downloads stay near 10% across 0-70% simulated malicious population, versus up to 87% with no mechanism at 40% malicious (`KAMVAR-WWW-03`). Originally deployed to rank which peer to download a file from — a content-ranking task. | A small, externally "designated" pre-trusted peer set whose own trustworthiness is assumed, with "no verification procedure" stated in the source (`KAMVAR-WWW-03`). | A malicious collective exceeding 40% of the network "can influence global trust value assignment during computation" (`KAMVAR-WWW-03`). | A DHT to locate each peer's score-computation manager; an entry-cost mechanism from elsewhere to bound identity creation, stated by the source as "outside EigenTrust itself" (`KAMVAR-WWW-03`). |
| Consensus-favouring aggregation, human-labeled-seed energy propagation (TrustRank) | 178-site seed set, drawn from 1,250 manually checked candidates, demotes spam sites by 7 PageRank buckets on average; pairwise orderedness approximately 0.95 at 500 sampled sites, on a 31,003,946-site graph (`GYONGYI-VLDB-04`). | A human oracle correctly labels the seed set by hand; "good" nodes point predominantly to other good nodes (`GYONGYI-VLDB-04`). | Cannot separate low-scored good sites from bad sites for any node with no inlinks (13,197,046 of 31,003,946 sites in the source's own graph); the seed-selection heuristic is itself gameable by anyone able to mimic a listed directory's link structure (`GYONGYI-VLDB-04`). | A directed graph over the items ranked; a fixed, human-vetted seed set re-labeled by hand whenever it must change (`GYONGYI-VLDB-04`). |
| Consensus-favouring aggregation, single designated per-item authority (PeerTube origin-instance view/like count) | Over 92% of videos held with no inter-instance redundancy at all — one authoritative copy (`POLINSKI-CCR-24`). The instance an item was uploaded to is stated to retain sole authoritative control over that item's view count and comment set even after other instances mirror the file (`POLINSKI-CCR-24`). | Every instance computing a "most-viewed" or "most-actively-trending" local ranking queries the one origin instance for that item's authoritative count (`POLINSKI-CCR-24`). | The listing count (how many instances list an item) diverges sharply from the redundancy count (how many instances actually hold a copy): 80% of items are listed on 3 or fewer instances, and listing does not imply a local copy exists (`POLINSKI-CCR-24`). | An ActivityPub-style follow/list relation reported back to the origin instance; per-instance administrator opt-in for any redundancy at all, since none occurs by default (`POLINSKI-CCR-24`). |
| Gossip-based collaborative filtering, unsupervised click-log ranking (G-Rank) | Per-peer distance to the globally optimal ranking approaches near-zero under no attack, on a 100-peer, 256-item simulated network (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`). Gossip message size rarely exceeds 600 kilobytes; each clicklog row is approximately 600 bytes (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`). | Peer discovery and ranking evidence share one gossip message type; nodes "cannot refuse update requests" (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`). | At 75 of 100 peers adversarial (a network supermajority), ranking quality degrades "faster and further than any other tested condition," and the source states the network "converges towards a single set of rankings that it appears unable to escape from" (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`). | A bootstrap peer supplying a first clicklog sample to any joining node; a transport for one-to-one gossip requests between already-address-aware peers; a Sybil-resistance mechanism bounding how many adversarial node IDs one honest peer's known-peer list can absorb (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`). |
| Gossip-based collaborative filtering, gossiped matrix factorization vs. a federated-learning baseline | On the largest tested collection (MovieLens 10M, 69,878 users), gossip learning reaches nearly identical error to federated learning by the end of a run and is stated to outperform it "by a significant margin" specifically at that scale; federated's early-round lead disappears within a few simulated hours (`HEGEDUS-ECMLPKDD-19`). Full-model message sizes: 0.6/1.5/4.1 megabits at the 100K/1M/10M scales (`HEGEDUS-ECMLPKDD-19`). | A membership/peer-sampling service already exists, supplying live peer addresses; the source states this is assumed, not supplied or evaluated, by the mechanism itself (`HEGEDUS-ECMLPKDD-19`). | Under a real smartphone-churn trace, convergence quality depends directly on the transfer-time-to-churn-rate ratio: a 10x bandwidth increase (172.8 s vs. 1,728 s per full-model transfer) produces markedly better outcomes purely because convergence then "beats" churn (`HEGEDUS-ECMLPKDD-19`). | An external membership/peer-sampling service (centralized, decentralized, or a static NAT-capable overlay — the source names all three without evaluating any); a shared item-feature matrix treated as safe to transmit unencrypted (`HEGEDUS-ECMLPKDD-19`). |
| Gossip-based collaborative filtering, peer-shared click logs training a local learn-to-rank model with a data-valuation poisoning defense (SwarmSearch / DART) | Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR) rises from 0.38 (local-only training) to 0.67 (fusing 10 gossiped neighbors' click logs) (`GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`). With a Data-Shapley marginal-contribution filter active, MRR holds flat against up to 50% label-flipping-poisoned neighbors; without the filter, unfiltered sharing still exceeds the local-only baseline up to 60% poisoned share (`GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`). | Each receiving node already holds enough of its own click history (at least 3 clicklogs in the tested configuration) to value an incoming dataset's marginal contribution against its own ground truth (`GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`). | With the Shapley defense active, MRR drops below the local-only 0.38 baseline once poisoned share of the 10 sampled neighbors reaches 90%, attributed to Monte Carlo approximation error in the defense itself (`GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`). | A local ground-truth click history per node before the poisoning defense can run at all; numerically comparable, per-agent-normalized scores for any merge step upstream of ranking (`GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`). |

### Selection

Client-selected feed generators are selected as the governing pattern deciding who may compute a ranking, combined with gossip-based collaborative filtering specifically selected — not merely retained — as the mechanism computing whichever feed a client is shown by default, because the only measurement in this corpus that bears on the decisive question named in this component's task (whether a person changes a default) shows that almost nobody does: 2.8% of 5,000,000 measured users ever liked an alternative feed at all (`QUELLE-PLOSONE-25`), and among a smaller lab sample of people actively given a feed-composition tool, 9 of 10 still weighted the unranked default source highest (`LIU-PACMHCI-25`). Because the marketplace's value is realized by a small, engaged minority while the default determines what nearly everyone else experiences, whatever computes the default carries the architecture's stated goal — that ranking cannot be captured by any company — directly, and no company-operated or single-authority mechanism can be selected to fill that role without contradicting the goal for the majority of users this measurement describes. This pairing itself — a gossip-computed default inside a feed-generator marketplace — is not measured anywhere in this corpus; no entry deploys gossip-based collaborative filtering as a majority-facing default and measures the resulting retention behavior. That composition is a guess, stated as one, not a selection with a citable basis of its own; the basis cited above supports each half of the pairing separately, not the pairing itself.

Against the client-side source-merge pattern with no separate generator network (`LIU-PACMHCI-25`): not rejected as a mechanism, reserved instead as the simplest instance of the selected governing pattern — a client merging existing, unranked server feeds is a valid, minimal feed generator, and its own measured preference data (8 of 11 favoring the unranked chronological order) is the strongest reason in this corpus to make the default itself low-opacity rather than an elaborate learned ranking. It is not selected as the sole answer because its own sample (n=10-11, one platform) is too small to state what a production-scale default-switching rate would be, unlike `QUELLE-PLOSONE-25`'s 5-million-user figure for the marketplace pattern generally.

Against per-viewer graph metrics, both the propagation family (`NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`) and spreading activation (`ZIEGLER-ISF-05`): rejected as the mechanism for the default, though retained as a compatible option any individual generator inside the marketplace could choose to implement. Both are measured only for reputation or trust assessment, never for ranking content by relevance, and neither paper's own text or numbers speak to whether a person would keep or leave a ranking computed this way — selecting either as the default would be exactly the unlabeled guess this synthesis's own rule against unsupported selections prohibits. `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`'s own largest tested graph (2,057 nodes) is three to four orders of magnitude below `QUELLE-PLOSONE-25`'s 5-million-user deployed scale, so even setting the content-ranking gap aside, no measurement in this corpus establishes the mechanism functions at the scale the marketplace pattern is already measured to reach.

Against consensus-favouring aggregation over groups, in each of its three measured instances: rejected, on the same structural ground reputation.md's own selection already rejects the network-wide-eigenvector instance of this family for the reputation component — `registry/selections/reputation.md` reaches the identical conclusion independently, and the reasoning transfers directly because the mechanism, not the task, produces the exposure. EigenTrust requires a pre-trusted peer set "designated" out-of-band with "no verification procedure" (`KAMVAR-WWW-03`); TrustRank requires a human oracle to hand-label a seed set, sourced in the paper's own deployment from one evaluator manually checking 1,250 candidates (`GYONGYI-VLDB-04`); the PeerTube pattern requires every instance to defer to one origin instance's authoritative count for any item (`POLINSKI-CCR-24`). Every one of these three measured instances places one designated party — a committee, a human labeler, or a single server — in a position to determine or veto the ranking every downstream viewer sees identically, which is the exact capture point the architecture's stated goal rules out. This is not a cost trade-off weighed against the family's benefits; it is a disqualifying structural property present in all three measured instances of the family in this corpus.

Against gossip-based collaborative filtering as the sole governing pattern, replacing the marketplace entirely: rejected, because no entry in this corpus deploys it to real, identifiable end users and measures whether they would keep using it, or would leave it for something else — the same decisive-question gap that applies to per-viewer graph metrics and, unlike the client-selected-generator marketplace, this family has never been measured outside a simulation (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`: 100 simulated peers; `HEGEDUS-ECMLPKDD-19`: PeerSim simulation; `GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`: 1,000 simulated users). It is selected for the narrower role of computing the default specifically because, among the three candidates with no deployed-adoption data, it is the only one with any measured content-ranking-quality figure at all (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`'s rank-distance-to-optimal convergence; `GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`'s MRR 0.38 to 0.67), and the only one with measured behavior under an adversarial population reaching the item-ranking task directly rather than a reputation or trust-scoring proxy task.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- A Relay- or Firehose-equivalent component delivering every signed content update to any party wishing to operate a feed generator, at a bandwidth this corpus measures at approximately 30 gigabytes/day per full-network-observing subscriber (`BALDUF-IMC-24`); a generator that cannot sustain this either serves a partial view or depends on someone else's full-network component.
- Stable, network-wide content and account identifiers a generator can reference across the whole population, from the naming/identity component (`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`).
- A hosting-diversity mechanism this component's own selection does not supply: measured hosting concentration (85.86% of generators through one platform, `BALDUF-IMC-24`) shows the marketplace pattern alone does not prevent re-concentration at the infrastructure layer beneath it — this is a composition gap for whichever component governs service hosting or admission, not resolved here.
- For the gossip-computed default: a peer-sampling or membership service supplying live peer addresses, stated by two separate entries as assumed rather than supplied by the ranking mechanism itself (`HEGEDUS-ECMLPKDD-19`, `GOLD-ARXIV-23`'s bootstrap-peer dependency).
- A Sybil-resistance or admission mechanism bounding how many adversarial identities can enter one honest peer's gossip neighborhood; `GOLD-ARXIV-23`'s own measured degradation scales directly with adversarial peer count (10 of 100 versus 75 of 100), with no defense in the source preventing eventual convergence to a shared degraded ranking once a supermajority is reached.
- A per-node local click-log or interaction-history retention commitment, measured at approximately 600 bytes per row and gossip messages rarely exceeding 600 kilobytes (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`) — a cost the local-storage component must carry for every participating node, not only generator operators.
- Content-signing or provenance from the identity/naming component, since `GOLD-ARXIV-23`'s own defense against fabricated evidence — preferring metadata matches over click-history matches — is stated by its authors only to blunt, not stop, an attack manufacturing spurious click-log entries.
- For the Shapley-value poisoning defense specifically: every participating node must already hold a minimum local click history (3 clicklogs in the tested configuration) before it can value incoming peer data at all (`GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`); a newly joined node has no such history and is exposed to unfiltered poisoning until it accumulates one, a cold-start gap this candidate does not itself close.

### What it costs and where it fails

The marketplace layer costs approximately 30 gigabytes/day of bandwidth per full-network-observing generator operator (`BALDUF-IMC-24`) and produces measured exposure inequality independent of the specific generator: in 33% of naturally occurring byte-identical duplicate-content groups, one copy is returned while its pair is not; a first-time author on a given feed carries a robust -0.061 reciprocal-rank penalty across all 250 individually tested feeds, independent of follower count (`WANG-ARXIV-26`). Despite architectural pluralism, generator hosting itself re-concentrates: the top 3 hosting platforms serve 95.8% of all generators, one platform alone serving 85.86% (`BALDUF-IMC-24`). The default's own mechanism, once selected as gossip-based collaborative filtering, fails specifically once an adversarial supermajority is reached: at 75 of 100 simulated peers adversarial, the source states the ranking "converges towards a single set of rankings that it appears unable to escape from" (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`) — the sharpest documented risk for this exact selection, because the decisive-question evidence above shows a default, once poisoned this way, would not be escaped by switching away either. The Data-Shapley variant of this same family holds Mean Reciprocal Rank flat against up to 50% poisoned peer data and only falls below its own local-only floor (0.38) at 90% poisoned share (`GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`), a materially higher measured tolerance than G-Rank's own tested conditions, though the two papers evaluate different tasks (unsupervised click ranking versus a learned ranking model with an explicit data-valuation filter) and neither is confirmed in this corpus to reach the other's tolerance under the other's attack model.

### What the corpus does not settle

- Whether a gossip-computed default would retain users at anything near the 97.2% no-alternative-adoption rate `QUELLE-PLOSONE-25` measures for Bluesky's actual, company-operated default is not measured anywhere in this corpus. Stating that it would is a guess; no entry evidences it, and this is the single largest gap under this selection.
- No entry measures gossip-based collaborative-filtering ranking at production content scale. The largest tested populations in this family are 1,000 simulated users (`GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25`), 69,878 simulated users (`HEGEDUS-ECMLPKDD-19`), and 100 simulated peers over a 256-item catalog (`GOLD-ARXIV-23`) — three to five orders of magnitude below `QUELLE-PLOSONE-25`'s 5-million-user deployed measurement of the marketplace pattern itself.
- `QUELLE-PLOSONE-25`'s 2.8% figure measures adoption (a like on an alternative feed), not a stated cause; it cannot distinguish satisfaction with the default, unawareness that alternatives exist, or a switching cost, from an actual preference for the default's own ranking. `LIU-PACMHCI-25`'s interview data is the only causal or attitudinal figure in this corpus bearing on the decisive question, and it is an n=11 sample on one platform, not a production behavioral measurement.
- No entry attributes `WANG-ARXIV-26`'s measured new-author exposure penalty to any specific ranking mechanism; the study observes the outcome across black-box, operator-chosen algorithms without access to their internal computation, so whether the penalty is a general property of using per-author history as a ranking input, specific to certain mechanisms among the four candidates above, or specific to none of them, is unmeasured.
- `GOLD-ARXIV-23` tests only two fixed adversarial populations (10 of 100, 75 of 100 peers), not a swept range with a measured collapse threshold comparable to the 20%/40% figures the indexing component's own selection cites for a different random-sampling mechanism (`registry/selections/indexing.md`); the adversarial fraction at which this candidate's ranking quality actually collapses, as opposed to merely degrading, is not derived anywhere in this corpus.
- No entry measures what happens when a person who has adopted an alternative feed generator, under the selected marketplace pattern, subsequently experiences a change to that generator's underlying computation — for instance, an operator switching from a locally computed ranking to a gossip-degraded one following the failure condition described above. Whether such a change would be visible to that person, or would itself change their measured retention behavior, is unaddressed by any source in this corpus.


---

## Moderation: Client-subscribed labeling services are selected for unwanted-but-lawful content, because every mechanism acting at server or list granularity removes content for every viewer of that server regardless of individual preference; illegal content instead routes through an identifier deny list honored at retrieval, and the one live measurement of that mechanism in this corpus shows voluntary, unevenly enforced adoption defeated by re-encoding the same bytes under an alternate identifier

A content identifier in this architecture is a fixed value derived from a piece of content's own bytes, so no protocol-level action can strike a record from existence: changing the bytes changes the identifier, and every node already holding a copy of the old identifier still answers requests for it unless that node itself chooses to refuse. Moderation is therefore never deletion at the identifier layer; it is a decision, made somewhere, to withhold a response or a display for a request that would otherwise succeed. Two distinct decisions get called moderation, and the evidence below shows they need different mechanisms because they answer different questions. Unwanted content is content a specific viewer, or a specific server operator, does not want shown, for a reason particular to that viewer or operator — nudity, harassment, a topic, a language — and the correct action is filtering: a decision that legitimately differs from one viewer to the next. Illegal content is content whose possession or distribution a jurisdiction criminalizes outright, the clearest case in the measured evidence being child sexual abuse material; the correct action is refusal to serve it to anyone, not a per-viewer preference.

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Independently operated labeling services, client-subscribed (AT Protocol Labelers) — any account publishes machine-readable labels on specific posts, accounts, or media, and each viewer's own client chooses which labeler accounts to subscribe to and how to react to a given label [`BALDUF-IMC-24`, `KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`] | 62 labeler accounts identified as of May 2024, 46 with a working endpoint, 36 having issued at least one label; 3,160,851 unique objects labeled cumulatively, 1,082,207 of them (88.7% of that month's volume) issued by community-run labelers in the first full month after public labeler registration opened [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. Consuming the full underlying content-plus-identity-plus-label stream directly (the only measured route to full network visibility) costs approximately 30 GB/day per subscribed client [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. | Each label carries the issuing labeler's own repository signature, checked by the client; no agreement across labelers or viewers is assumed or needed, since each client applies only the labels of the labelers it chose [`KLEPPMANN-CONEXT-24`]. | Only 3.2% of labeled objects carry labels from more than one labeler; labelers cover almost entirely disjoint content [`BALDUF-IMC-24`], so a viewer subscribed to the wrong (or no) labeler sees no label at all. One label value, `!takedown`, is honored only when issued by the single official Bluesky-operated labeler, reintroducing one privileged party for that specific action [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. | An aggregation point that subscribes to every known labeler and stores and serves their labels — in the one measured deployment this is the centralized AppView, which "subscribes to all known Labelers and needs to store all labels" [`BALDUF-IMC-24`] — or, absent that, each client independently bearing the full-stream bandwidth cost above. Requires a stable, network-wide object identifier from the naming/identity component for a labeler to attach a label to. |
| Instance-level blocking (Mastodon `domain_blocks`) — a server administrator unilaterally severs federation with a remote domain, removing that domain's content for every locally hosted user [`BONO-WEBSCI-24`, `ANAOBI-WWW-23`] | Average in-degree (bans received) 3-4 per instance, July-November 2023 crawl [`BONO-WEBSCI-24`]. Across a 5,413-instance interface, an average of ≈4.5×10^4 block actions issued per day over one year [`ARREGUI-GARCIA-ARXIV-26`]. Average delay from first federation to first policy action against a newly federated instance: 82.3 days, ranging from 19 days (gab.com) to 98.4 days (neckbeard.xyz) for specific controversial targets [`ANAOBI-WWW-23`]. | Trust in one administrator's unilateral judgment per blocking instance; no cross-instance verification of a block decision exists [`BONO-WEBSCI-24`]. | Public transparency: only 364 of 1,807 instances (20.1%) with ≥10 active users publicly share their blocklist at all, and only 169 of those 364 (46.4%) state a reason [`ZHANG-ARXIV-25`]. Granularity: the block acts on the whole remote domain, not on individual content or individual viewers, so it removes content from every user of the blocking instance regardless of that user's own preference [`BONO-WEBSCI-24`]. Measured network effect: the inter-instance ban graph splits into at least two connected components from August 2023 onward [`BONO-WEBSCI-24`]; treating blocked edges as severed shows information from the minority of moderating instances reaching the majority of moderated instances more efficiently than the reverse [`ARREGUI-GARCIA-ARXIV-26`]. | A per-instance administrator (or, in 3.5% of measured Pleroma instances, a dedicated moderator distinct from the administrator) who observes that instance's own federated-peer traffic and decides when to act [`ANAOBI-WWW-23`]. Interview evidence: administrators report the work as manual, tedious, stressful, and in at least one case directly traumatic — one participant described exposure to child-abuse material while moderating [`ZHANG-PACMHCI-24`]. |
| Shared policy lists — a maintainer curates one list of domains (or, in a collaborative-classifier variant, one trained model) and distributes it for independent instances to apply as their own local policy, either as a synchronized feed (FediCheck, FediBlockHole) or a manually applied file [`ZHANG-ARXIV-25`], or as periodically exchanged classifier parameters between instances with no raw content leaving either instance (FedMod) [`ZIA-ARXIV-25`, `AGARWAL-ICWSM-24`] | Five widely referenced domain lists identified (Seirdy Tier-0, FediNuke, Garden Fence, CARIAD, IFTAS-DNI); none discloses its full underlying evidence per blocked domain, and one (Garden Fence) incorporates further external lists that are themselves undisclosed [`ZHANG-ARXIV-25`]. FedMod's classifier-sharing variant raises a small instance's harmful-content macro-F1 by 12.69% over training locally (128 labeled posts, k=2 peers, hashsim peer selection, p<0.001) [`ZIA-ARXIV-25`]; a separate conversation-model-sharing variant raises small-instance macro-F1 from 0.60 (global training, local inference) to 0.8826 (model sharing) [`AGARWAL-ICWSM-24`]. | Every subscribing administrator trusts the list maintainer's (or peer instance's) undisclosed inclusion criteria wholesale, since none of the five measured lists publishes per-domain evidence [`ZHANG-ARXIV-25`]; the classifier variant additionally assumes peers are not adversarially poisoning shared parameters [`ZIA-ARXIV-25`]. | No entry measures adoption count for any one of the five listed sources, nor the fraction of the federation graph its adoption would disconnect [`ZHANG-ARXIV-25`]. Each list's own stated bias, by its own maintainers: Garden Fence toward English-language sources, CARIAD toward Global North perspectives, IFTAS-DNI with an undisclosed decision process, Seirdy Tier-0 requiring continuous manual labor [`ZHANG-ARXIV-25`]. The classifier variant degrades as peer policies diverge: at 50% of peers holding an inverted harmful/non-harmful label definition, FedMod's advantage over training locally disappears entirely [`ZIA-ARXIV-25`]. | The same whole-domain granularity as instance-level blocking, plus a distribution channel (sync tool, manually applied file, or peer-to-peer parameter exchange) and an administrator-level trust decision at each subscribing instance [`ZHANG-ARXIV-25`]. The classifier variant needs a communication channel able to carry trained model weights (172M-parameter mBERT, 714 MB) between instances, separate from ordinary content federation [`ZIA-ARXIV-25`]. |
| Identifier deny lists honored at retrieval (IPFS `badbits`) — a maintaining organization publishes hashes of content identifiers subject to a takedown request; any independently operated retrieval gateway voluntarily checks each incoming request's identifier against the list before serving [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`] | 417,912 combined denylist entries recovered (411,522 badbits plus 16,128 from Web2 anti-phishing feeds) [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. Reporting-to-listing latency ≈24 hours; flagged content persists on the network "for years" before it is reported at all [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. Content-type breakdown of the 368,762 downloaded and classified entries: copyright content ≈87.97%, phishing ≈5.81%, terrorist content ≈0.06%, potential CSAM <0.01% [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. | Every independent gateway operator voluntarily fetches and enforces the identical list; the list itself exposes only content-identifier hashes, not the identifiers, so recovering what it refers to requires independent traffic observation [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. | Enforcement is measured highly uneven: the maintaining organization's own gateways block ≈100% of listed identifiers, CDN-operated gateways ≈18%, other public gateways similarly low [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. Re-uploading the same bytes under one of the underlying protocol's other 71 supported hash functions evaded 56 of 57 gateways that blocked anything at all, succeeding for 97.4% of usually-blocked identifiers; wrapping blocked content in an unblocked parent directory evaded one tested gateway on 67 of 68 (98.5%) of its blocked identifiers [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. Individual peers were found hosting 19%-63% of the entire list's content, but replication elsewhere means removing that peer does not remove the content network-wide [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. | A retrieval-serving component that performs the check at the moment it would otherwise answer a request, since the underlying store cannot delete an identifier's content. A passive traffic-observation capability spanning a long window and a large request volume (the source paper used Bitswap and DHT logs spanning mid-2021 to January 2024, ≈300 billion and ≈1.3 billion requests respectively) to map hash-only list entries back to actual identifiers [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. A canonicalization step reducing every valid identifier encoding of one underlying byte sequence to one comparable check target, or an independent content-based detection layer — the measured deployment supplied neither, which is why the hash-substitution evasion above worked [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. |

### Selection

For unwanted-but-lawful content, independently operated labeling services, each client subscribed individually, are selected over instance-level blocking and shared policy lists. For illegal content, an identifier deny list honored at retrieval is selected as the only mechanism this corpus evidences in live deployment — selected not because it works, but because it is the sole candidate measured, and its own measurement shows it failing along two specific, stated dimensions rather than succeeding.

Against instance-level blocking: the labeler mechanism's filtering unit is one object and one viewer's subscription list; a labeler's decision reaches only the viewers who chose to subscribe to that labeler, and 3.2% cross-labeler overlap shows labelers operate largely independently of one another rather than converging on one shared judgment [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. Instance-level blocking's filtering unit is one remote domain and every locally hosted user simultaneously: a block removes a domain's content for every viewer on the blocking instance whether or not that particular viewer objects to that content [`BONO-WEBSCI-24`], and the measured 82.3-day average response delay [`ANAOBI-WWW-23`] means the decision, once made, is both coarse and slow. The interview evidence adds a cost the labeler mechanism does not carry in the same form: instance-level blocking is performed by a human administrator who reports the work as tedious, stressful, and in one documented case exposed them directly to child-abuse material while triaging what to block [`ZHANG-PACMHCI-24`] — the labeler mechanism does not eliminate human judgment, since a labeler is still typically an operated service, but it does not concentrate that judgment and its exposure onto one person per server who must decide for every user of that server at once.

Against shared policy lists: this candidate inherits instance-level blocking's whole-domain granularity and adds a further, less transparent trust hop — none of the five measured lists discloses its per-domain evidence, and one incorporates further undisclosed lists on top of its own [`ZHANG-ARXIV-25`]. The labeler mechanism already supplies the function a shared list provides — delegating a filtering judgment to a party a viewer trusts — through per-viewer subscription, without imposing that delegated judgment on every other user of the same server. The collaborative-classifier variant of this candidate is the more capable of the two: FedMod raises a small instance's macro-F1 by 12.69% over training alone [`ZIA-ARXIV-25`], and the conversation-model variant raises small-instance macro-F1 from 0.60 to 0.8826 [`AGARWAL-ICWSM-24`]. Both remain, however, an instance-wide classification applied uniformly to every user of the receiving instance, not a per-viewer choice, and both degrade as participating instances' own policies diverge — FedMod's benefit disappears entirely once half of peers hold an inverted label definition [`ZIA-ARXIV-25`]. Evidence that individual viewers' own "unwanted" judgment is itself heterogeneous, not one shared signal a classifier or a list could converge on, comes from a fully user-controlled setting: on memo.cash, the strongest predictor of which user gets personally muted is that user's own platform-activity count, not any hatefulness or toxicity score, which ranks 26th among 406 candidate features [`ZUO-ICWSM-24`]. A mechanism whose filtering unit is one viewer's own subscription list accommodates that heterogeneity directly; a mechanism whose filtering unit is one shared list or one shared classifier, applied identically to every subscribing instance's users, does not.

Against reserving the labeler mechanism for illegal content too, instead of a separate deny list: none of the three per-viewer or per-server mechanisms above stops a person who never subscribed to any relevant labeler, and is not a user of any blocking instance, from retrieving illegal content directly — filtering is opt-in by construction, and illegal content specifically requires refusal that does not depend on any viewer's or administrator's opt-in choice. The identifier deny list is the only candidate in this corpus built to act at the point every retrieval passes through, independent of which labelers or blocks any particular requester has chosen. Its own measured failure — enforcement voluntary and uneven (≈100% at the maintaining organization's own gateways versus ≈18% at CDN-operated ones), and near-total evasion by re-encoding the same bytes under an alternate valid identifier (97.4% of usually-blocked identifiers) [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`] — is a documented limitation of the only measured candidate, not evidence that a labeler or blocking mechanism would do better at this specific job; those mechanisms were never measured operating at this job's required granularity (every request, independent of viewer choice) at all.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- The naming or identity component supplies one stable, network-wide identifier per moderable object (post, account, media item) that a labeler can attach a label to and that a deny-list check can be run against; the labeler mechanism's own measured deployment attaches labels to identifiers drawn from exactly this kind of per-object identity scheme [`BALDUF-IMC-24`].
- Either a content-location or indexing component supplies a service equivalent to the measured deployment's centralized AppView — subscribing to every known labeler, storing every label, and answering a client's "does object X carry a label from any labeler I subscribe to" query — or every client bears the ≈30 GB/day full-stream bandwidth cost of consuming labelers' streams directly [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]; no entry in this corpus measures a bandwidth or storage cost for the aggregation-service alternative, so which of these two paths is viable is unsettled (see below).
- The retrieval-serving component — whatever node or gateway answers a request for a given content identifier — performs the deny-list check at the moment of serving, before returning bytes, since the underlying content-addressed store supplies no deletion primitive at the identifier layer [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`].
- The storage-encoding or naming component supplies a canonicalization step mapping every valid identifier encoding of one underlying byte sequence to one comparable check target before the deny-list check runs; absent this, a re-encoding under any of the other supported identifier schemes evades the check, as measured [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`].
- Some network-observation capability — sustained passive monitoring of retrieval and lookup traffic over a long window and large request volume — is needed to translate a hash-only deny-list entry back into an actual, checkable content identifier, mirroring the passive Bitswap and DHT observation the source measurement itself relied on [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`].
- Sybil resistance or an equivalent registration control is explicitly not assumed for labeler registration: any account can register as a labeler in the one measured deployment [`BALDUF-IMC-24`], so the choice of which labelers are trustworthy is left entirely to each client, and no mechanism in this corpus is evidenced to help a client make that choice at the labeler-discovery problem's scale (see below).
- The illegal-content deny-list check and the unwanted-content labeling mechanism are independent and both mandatory where applicable: a client's labeler subscriptions must not be treated as satisfying, replacing, or gating the deny-list check, since the deny-list check's entire value is that it does not depend on any viewer's subscription choice.

### What it costs and where it fails

The labeler mechanism's clearest measured cost is the bandwidth of the only alternative to a centralizing aggregator: ≈30 GB/day per client for full network visibility, the condition under which the one live deployment's own authors state it "remains to be seen" whether an independently operated (non-platform-operated) equivalent of that aggregator is economically viable [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. It fails to supply coverage when no relevant labeler yet exists for a given kind of content or community: for eleven months after this deployment's launch, only one labeler (the platform operator's own) existed at all, and the 88.7% share of monthly label volume now issued by independently operated labelers accumulated only after public labeler registration opened [`BALDUF-IMC-24`] — a client relying on this mechanism from a network's earliest days has thin coverage until an independently operated population of labelers has time to form.

The identifier deny list's measured cost is the ≈24-hour reporting-to-listing latency once a takedown request is received, against content persisting "for years" before that report happens at all [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. It fails in two independently measured, structurally different ways. First, adoption is voluntary and uneven: enforcement ranges from ≈100% (the maintaining organization's own gateways) to ≈18% (CDN-operated gateways), so the mechanism's actual network-wide coverage is set by the least-compliant class of gateway a requester can reach, not by the existence of the list itself [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. Second, even a fully compliant gateway is evaded by an adversary who re-encodes the same bytes under a different valid identifier: 97.4% of usually-blocked identifiers became retrievable this way [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. The content actually removed by the one measured deployment of this mechanism skews overwhelmingly toward copyright and phishing content (≈87.97% and ≈5.81% respectively) rather than the CSAM or terrorist-content categories (<0.01% and ≈0.06%) the mechanism is most often justified by [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`] — a fact about what this specific deployment actually does, not a claim about what the mechanism could do differently configured.

### What the corpus does not settle

1. No entry isolates the bandwidth or storage cost of running a label-aggregation service alone (the measured 30 GB/day figure is for the full content-plus-identity-plus-label stream, not labels alone) [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. A builder choosing between a centralizing aggregator and full-stream client-side consumption has no measured cost for the aggregator side of that choice.
2. No entry measures which labelers users actually subscribe to, or how many labelers a typical user subscribes to; the source paper's own authors state they deliberately did not attempt to infer this, for ethical reasons [`BALDUF-IMC-24`]. Whether the labeler mechanism achieves adequate coverage for an ordinary user in practice, rather than only in principle, is unmeasured.
3. No entry proposes or measures a labeler-discovery mechanism — how a new client, with no prior relationships, learns which of many independently operated labelers exist and are worth subscribing to. This is a cold-start problem structurally similar to the naming component's own first-contact bootstrap problem, and no paper in this corpus addresses it for labelers specifically.
4. No entry measures a canonicalization step or a content-based (non-hash) detection layer actually deployed and resisting the alternate-identifier evasion the deny-list measurement documents; the source paper's own proposed improvement (tighter anti-phishing-service integration, measured as a 227% increase in detected phishing content and a 43% reduction in reporting latency) was evaluated only by the authors' own methodology and explicitly "not deployed on the live network," and it addresses reporting speed for one content category, not the hash-substitution evasion itself [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. The identifier-canonicalization problem this selection depends on is an open problem in this corpus, not a solved one — stated here as a guess-labeled gap, because no entry in the corpus measures a working solution to it.
5. No entry measures whether making deny-list enforcement structurally binding — rather than each gateway operator's voluntary choice — is achievable at all in a design where retrieval nodes are independently operated; the only measured deployment is voluntary, and its own authors describe uneven enforcement as the direct cause of the mechanism "facilitat[ing] [the] spread" of the content it targets [`SOKOTO-USENIXSEC-24`]. What a structurally binding version would require is not stated or tested by any paper in this corpus.
6. No entry measures legal-liability allocation for a retrieval node that does, or does not, enforce a deny list under a specific jurisdiction's law; interview evidence records that administrators themselves report exactly this uncertainty about what they are legally permitted to do about illegal content, with no institutional or protocol-level support described for resolving it [`ZHANG-PACMHCI-24`], but no paper in this corpus measures or resolves the underlying legal question.


---

## Reputation: A participant assessing a stranger computes a bounded network-flow value from itself, as source, to that stranger, over a trust-linkage graph whose edges cost something real to form

A participant deciding how much to trust a stranger — whether to accept a message from them, complete a transaction with them, or count their vote — needs a number computed without a central operator holding every participant's history, and without waiting for the whole network to agree on one shared score. Every candidate below answers this from a different starting point: a value computed from the assessing participant's own position in a graph of linkages (`LEVIEN-USS-98`, `TRAN-NSDI-09`, `POST-NSDI-11`, `MISLOVE-NSDI-08`, `VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`, `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`), a value diffused outward from a labeled seed set along the same kind of graph (`GYONGYI-VLDB-04`, `FURUTANI-TIFS-23`, `ZIEGLER-ISF-05`), a single value every participant in the network converges to agreeing on (`KAMVAR-WWW-03`), or a value built by summing what other participants directly report about their own past transactions with the stranger (`ISMAIL-BLED-02`, `XIONG-TKDE-04`, `SRIVATSA-WWW-05`).

### Candidates

| Mechanism | Measured cost (experimental conditions) | Security assumption required | Condition under which it fails | What it requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Global eigenvector — EigenTrust | Converges in fewer than 10 power-iteration rounds on a simulated 1,000-peer network; with the mechanism active, inauthentic downloads stay near 10% from 0% to 70% simulated malicious population, versus up to 87% with no reputation system at 40% malicious `KAMVAR-WWW-03`. | A small, out-of-band-designated "pre-trusted peer" set whose own trustworthiness is assumed, not derived `KAMVAR-WWW-03`. | The paper's own text: a malicious collective exceeding 40% of the network can influence global trust value assignment during computation under the majority-vote secure scheme `KAMVAR-WWW-03`. Self-rating via unbounded new identities is stated as countered only by an entry-cost mechanism external to EigenTrust `KAMVAR-WWW-03`. | A functioning DHT (CAN or Chord named) to locate each peer's score managers; an entry-cost mechanism from elsewhere to bound identity creation; every peer must record its own per-transaction satisfaction outcomes `KAMVAR-WWW-03`. |
| Energy propagation from a labeled seed set — TrustRank | 178-site seed set (from 1,250 manually checked candidates) demotes spam sites in PageRank bucket 2 by 7 buckets on average; pairwise orderedness ≈0.95 at 500 sample sites, on a 31,003,946-site AltaVista-derived graph `GYONGYI-VLDB-04`. | A human oracle correctly labels the seed set, and "good" nodes point predominantly to other good nodes ("approximate isolation") `GYONGYI-VLDB-04`. | TrustRank "is unable to effectively separate low-scored good sites from bad ones" for any node with no inlinks (13,197,046 of 31,003,946 sites here); the paper's own seed-selection heuristic is vulnerable to being gamed by any actor that can mimic a listed directory's link structure `GYONGYI-VLDB-04`. | A directed graph over the items ranked; a fixed, human-vetted seed set re-labeled by hand whenever it must change `GYONGYI-VLDB-04`. |
| Energy propagation, unified low-pass-filter family — SybilRank / CIA / SybilWalk / SybilSCAR / SybilBelief / SybilHeat | Detection AUC on real graphs ranges 0.75–1.0 with zero label noise, falling to 0.52–0.96 at 20% label-flip noise, across four benchmark graphs (34 to 1,224 nodes) `FURUTANI-TIFS-23`. | Pre-labeled benign nodes (and, for four of the six methods, pre-labeled Sybil nodes too) already exist and are correct; the caller's graph sits above a stated community-detectability threshold `FURUTANI-TIFS-23`. | Below the detectability threshold, SybilBelief's accuracy collapses sharply `FURUTANI-TIFS-23`; the two production deployments of this family in this corpus (Íntegro at Tuenti, `BOSHMAF-NDSS-15`; SybilRank-style detection at Tuenti, `CAO-NSDI-12`) both required the operator to hold the full graph plus human-verified seeds. | Full graph materialized wherever the computation runs (SybilHeat's Chebyshev approximation still needs the whole edge set and the graph's average degree); pre-labeled ground truth from elsewhere `FURUTANI-TIFS-23`. |
| Energy propagation, personalized (spreading activation) — Appleseed | On a 384-node Advogato sub-network (avg. out-degree 6), converges in 38–45 iterations regardless of a 4× change in injected trust `ZIEGLER-ISF-05`. | A per-agent trust graph is retrievable on demand (the paper's own implementation fetches it "just in time" from a personal homepage) `ZIEGLER-ISF-05`. | Shares the "eager trusters" penalty with EigenTrust and PageRank: an agent who issues many trust statements has each one diluted, a limitation the same source states Advogato's capacity-based design does not have `ZIEGLER-ISF-05`. | A publishing convention or lookup service for per-agent trust assertions; a chosen spreading factor d and convergence threshold, not derived by the paper for arbitrary topologies `ZIEGLER-ISF-05`. |
| Energy propagation, personalized — MeritRank Personalized PageRank (PPR) | On a 2,057-node/35,853-edge MakerDAO feedback graph, attacker's gain higher than the same paper's Personalized Hitting Time variant under an identical repeated-Sybil-attack model `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`. | A gossip-propagated, per-peer subjective graph view; a fixed seed node trusted externally `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`. | Vulnerable to cycle attacks via random-walk restarts, per the paper's own comparison `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`. | A gossip protocol propagating feedback-graph updates; an externally established seed node `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`. |
| Energy propagation, personalized — MeritRank Personalized Hitting Time (PHT) | Same graph and attack model: lowest attacker's gain of the three algorithms MeritRank tests; combined with transitivity+connectivity decay (both coefficients 0.3), attacker's gain limited to 1.5 at 50 Sybil nodes injected per epoch `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`. | Same as PPR above. | Repeated-attack gain still exceeds single-attack gain; epoch (time) decay "fails to address repeated Sybil attacks effectively" and can be counterproductive; decay itself inflates direct seed-neighbors' reputation up to 7× `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`. | Same as PPR above. |
| Bilateral outcome counting, unweighted — Beta reputation system | No experiments; closed-form E(p) = (r+1)/(r+s+2) from accumulated positive/negative counts r, s `ISMAIL-BLED-02`. | Feedback is bound to a persistent identity by an external authentication mechanism `ISMAIL-BLED-02`. | The paper's own eBay example: two colluding parties "had good Feedback Forum ratings, developed through rating each other favourably" before a fraudulent sale; simple addition provides no mechanism against this, since it treats every unit of feedback as equally trustworthy once received `ISMAIL-BLED-02`. States directly it does not address identity change `ISMAIL-BLED-02`. | An external identity/authentication layer; a feedback-delivery channel; a way to detect and exclude duplicate counting of one transaction `ISMAIL-BLED-02`. |
| Bilateral outcome counting, credibility-weighted (recursive/eigenvector-style) — PeerTrust TVM, TrustGuard | RMS trust-computation error stays low below 50% malicious peers (non-collusive), but reaches 100% error above that threshold; "extremely sensitive" to collusion even at small malicious fractions `XIONG-TKDE-04`, `SRIVATSA-WWW-05`. Cost: O(N) DHT hops per computation (dynamic variant) `XIONG-TKDE-04`. | An underlying DHT (O(log N) hops) storing feedback records; a feedback source's own recursively-computed trust value used as its credibility weight `XIONG-TKDE-04`. | 100% misclassification once malicious peers exceed 50% of the community, N=128 simulated `XIONG-TKDE-04`. Does not address identity whitewashing, citing it as future work `XIONG-TKDE-04`. | A DHT (P-Grid in the prototype); per-peer public/private keys, unspecified issuance `XIONG-TKDE-04`. |
| Bilateral outcome counting, credibility-weighted (similarity-based) — PeerTrust PSM | PSM "remains effective even with both large fraction of malicious nodes and collusion," same 1024-node simulator `SRIVATSA-WWW-05`. | Assessor and each feedback source must already share a nonempty set of peers both have independently transacted with `XIONG-TKDE-04`. | If two peers' transaction histories never overlap, PSM has no basis to compute a similarity-based credibility for one from the other's ratings — the exact condition of a first encounter with a stranger `XIONG-TKDE-04`. | Same DHT and identity dependencies as PeerTrust TVM above, plus an overlap-detection procedure across transaction histories `XIONG-TKDE-04`. |
| Flow-based, own position, boolean threshold — Advogato (Levien max-flow) | Proven bound, not measured on any implemented system: an attacker needs d keys for a node attack, β·d/2 for an edge attack, matching the paper's own proved best-case limit for any trust metric of this class `LEVIEN-USS-98`. | Every widely-accepted key is certified by ≥d others; the flow source is the assessor's own already-trusted key `LEVIEN-USS-98`. | On the real PGP web-of-trust graph, ~35% of keys have exactly one predecessor (d=1), where the authors state a trust metric of any kind is "not particularly helpful" `LEVIEN-USS-98`. | A directed graph of signed certificates reachable from the assessor's own key; an external certificate-distribution and revocation system `LEVIEN-USS-98`. |
| Flow-based, own position, capacity-bounded continuous value — SumUp / Bazaar / Ostra, accelerated by Canal | SumUp: ≈1 vote per attack edge vs. SybilLimit's ≈30 under identical conditions, same O(log n) asymptotic bound `TRAN-NSDI-09`. Bazaar: fraud strictly bounded by cut value; ≤5% (1.11–4.68%) of real non-fraudulent transactions wrongly flagged, on an 8,874,521-item eBay crawl `POST-NSDI-11`. Ostra: 0.22 unwanted messages/week/user at 1% attacker population on a 446,181-node YouTube-derived network `MISLOVE-NSDI-08`. Canal: exact max-flow costs 0.352s–220s per query (33K–234M links); its landmark approximation answers in <1ms (median, 5,000 queries) at 94.2%–99%+ agreement with exact, and a 785×–2,329× speedup integrated into Bazaar and Ostra `VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`. | Trust-graph edges cost something real to form and maintain; capacity along a used path is reserved at authorization and resolved by a feedback/timeout signal `POST-NSDI-11`, `MISLOVE-NSDI-08`. | Ostra's own evaluation substituted YouTube subscriptions, which "does not meet Ostra's requirements, because there is no significant cost" to form a link `MISLOVE-NSDI-08`. A compromised long-history account can still be drained up to its own bound `POST-NSDI-11`. Canal trades a bounded false-negative rate (wrongly denying a legitimate interaction) for speed, never a false positive `VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`. | A trust-linkage layer with real edge-formation cost; an atomic hold/release protocol at the interaction point; a new-participant bootstrap (a real vouching link or an escrow deposit) `POST-NSDI-11`; Canal additionally needs a link-level lock and enough memory to hold the full graph plus cached landmark universes (≈20GB at 234M links) `VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`. |
| Flow-based, own position, additive-across-paths — MeritRank MaxFlow (MFW) | Same MakerDAO graph and repeated-attack model as PPR/PHT above: highest attacker's gain of the three algorithms tested, and the most computationally expensive `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`. | Same subjective-graph and seed assumptions as MeritRank PPR/PHT. | Gain is consistently higher than single-attack gain, most pronounced for MFW, under repeated per-epoch Sybil injection across 153 epochs in a compounding reward setting `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`. | Same gossip and seed dependencies as MeritRank PPR/PHT. |

### Selection

The selected mechanism is the capacity-bounded, own-position network flow family: SumUp's ticket-distributed max-flow approximation (`TRAN-NSDI-09`), Bazaar's max-flow risk network (`POST-NSDI-11`), and Ostra's credit-network variant (`MISLOVE-NSDI-08`), made tractable at scale by Canal's landmark-routing approximation (`VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`), resting on Levien's proof that a max-flow construction of this kind is the provably tightest achievable trust metric in its class (`LEVIEN-USS-98`). A participant assessing a stranger computes the maximum flow, or an approximation of it, from itself as source to that stranger as sink, over a graph whose edges represent linkages that cost something real to form; the resulting value bounds how much the participant should risk on, or accept from, that stranger.

Against the four attacks this component must address: discarding a poorly rated identity gains nothing structurally, because Bazaar starts every new or reborn identity with zero flow to every existing participant until a real vouching link or an escrowed deposit creates one (`POST-NSDI-11`), which is exactly the only equilibrium Friedman and Resnick prove cannot be beaten once identity change is free and any positive error rate exists — a strategy that extends trust only to linked history and treats every newcomer, indistinguishable from a repeat offender, at the same distrustful floor (`FRIEDMAN-JEMS-01`: no equilibrium exceeds V(PYD) = 1 − α/(2−α) − O(ε) − O(1/M) under those conditions). Coordinated mutual rating gains nothing structurally either, because Ostra's credit-conservation invariant holds the sum of every link balance in the system at exactly 0 after every operation (`MISLOVE-NSDI-08`: "the sum of all credit balances is 0"), so a set of colluding identities rating each other well only redistributes credit among themselves and cannot manufacture capacity toward a stranger outside the group — Bazaar's cut-capacity bound carries the identical property: colluders "can only defraud together for the total of what they could defraud separately" (`POST-NSDI-11`). Self-rating through controlled identities is bounded by the same cut-capacity argument: SumUp measures the resulting attack capacity at ≈1 fraudulent vote per attack edge, independent of how many Sybil identities sit behind that edge, against SybilLimit's ≈30 accepted Sybils per attack edge on the identical graph and edge count despite both carrying the same asymptotic O(log n) bound (`TRAN-NSDI-09`); Levien's proof establishes this is not an accident of one construction — no trust metric in this class can be forced below d nodes for a node-compromise attack or β·d/2 for an edge-only attack, the proven-optimal bound for the class (`LEVIEN-USS-98`). Rating a competitor falsely low is bounded, not eliminated: a false negative report reduces only the reporting participant's own link to the target, so damaging a competitor requires the attacker to already hold real prior transaction volume with that specific competitor — Bazaar's own stated reasoning is that this raises the attack's cost relative to unbounded feedback-based systems, since the attacker must first accumulate non-fraudulent transaction history before it can inflict any damage (`POST-NSDI-11`), bounding a slanderer's damage to its own accumulated legitimate link weight against the one victim, without preventing a single false report entirely.

Against EigenTrust (global eigenvector): rejected.
