EigenTrust requires a pre-existing, out-of-band-designated set of pre-trusted peers whose trustworthiness is assumed rather than derived, and the paper states network designers or founders fill this role but gives no verification procedure for it (`KAMVAR-WWW-03`) — a small committee whose membership decides the whole network's convergence is a durable control point, at odds with a design whose stated goal is that no company can capture the network. Measured degradation is by network fraction, not by attack edge: a malicious collective exceeding 40% can influence global trust value assignment during computation under the majority-vote secure scheme (`KAMVAR-WWW-03`), which has no counterpart bound in the selected mechanism, whose bound scales with the attacker's own cut capacity regardless of what fraction of the network it represents. EigenTrust's own text states self-rating via unbounded identities is countered only by an entry-cost mechanism external to EigenTrust itself (`KAMVAR-WWW-03`); the selected mechanism converts whatever entry cost exists elsewhere into the sharpest measured bound in this corpus (`TRAN-NSDI-09`). EigenTrust also converges to one value every peer agrees on, not a value computed from the assessing participant's own position, the opposite of what this component's task definition requires.

Against TrustRank and the unified SybilRank / CIA / SybilWalk / SybilSCAR / SybilBelief / SybilHeat family (energy propagation from a labeled seed set): rejected for assessing one stranger, though this family remains a documented option for bulk classification of an entire population against a labeled seed set — a different task. Every member requires the caller to already hold a pre-labeled trustworthy set, and most require a pre-labeled untrustworthy set too, materialized over the full graph before any query runs (`FURUTANI-TIFS-23`); TrustRank's own seed set came from one human evaluator manually checking 1,250 candidate sites (`GYONGYI-VLDB-04`), and the two production deployments of this family measured in this corpus both required the operator to hold the complete social graph plus human-verified seeds distributed by analysts (`CAO-NSDI-12`, `BOSHMAF-NDSS-15`) — the same operator-capture point this design rules out. The selected mechanism needs no labeled seed set at all: it needs only a graph of costly linkages and the assessor's own position in it.

Against Appleseed (personalized spreading-activation, energy propagation from the assessor's own seed): rejected on a directly documented distortion the selected mechanism does not carry. Appleseed inherits the "eager trusters" penalty from unweighted spreading activation — a participant who legitimately vouches for many honest strangers has each individual vouch diluted — a limitation the same source states Appleseed shares with EigenTrust and PageRank, and states Advogato's capacity-based design does not exhibit (`ZIEGLER-ISF-05`, Table 5.1 feature F.2). The selected mechanism's guarantee depends on cut capacity between assessor and target, not on how many statements a node has issued.

Against MeritRank's Personalized PageRank and Personalized Hitting Time (energy propagation, personalized): this is the closest measured competitor, and it is also the entry in this corpus that measures the selected family's own MaxFlow variant losing. On MeritRank's own feedback graph and repeated-Sybil-attack model, Personalized Hitting Time reached the lowest attacker's gain of the three algorithms tested, and MeritRank's MaxFlow variant reached the highest, while costing more to compute (`NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`). That measurement targets a different quantity than the one this selection is built for: MeritRank's "attacker's gain" is a ratio applied to a continuously compounding, per-epoch reward score across 153 repeated attack rounds in a tokenomics setting, not a single admit-or-refuse decision about one stranger. Bazaar's and SumUp's measured guarantee is a bound on total extractable value or vote count per interaction, evaluated as a threshold decision (`POST-NSDI-11`, `TRAN-NSDI-09`), and Advogato's guarantee is a proven bound on attacker resources for exactly a one-shot accept/reject decision (`LEVIEN-USS-98`). No entry in this corpus runs SumUp, Bazaar, or Advogato's construction under MeritRank's repeated-epoch, compounding-reward attack model, or runs MeritRank's PHT algorithm under Bazaar's or SumUp's threshold-decision model, so which family costs less for the task this component performs — deciding how much to trust one stranger, once, at the moment of interaction — is not settled by a shared experiment. The selection is made for that threshold-decision task, the one the component's own definition states, and MeritRank's result is the strongest evidence against extending this same selection to a compounding-reward use elsewhere in the architecture.

Against the Beta reputation system (bilateral outcome counting, unweighted): rejected because the mechanism, by its own worked example, provides no mechanism against collusion — its combination operator treats every unit of feedback as equally trustworthy once received (`ISMAIL-BLED-02`), so two colluding identities that transact with each other repeatedly accumulate the same weight as two strangers with a genuine history; the source's own eBay example states "two of the fraudsters actually had good Feedback Forum ratings, developed through rating each other favourably." The paper states directly it has "not tried to address the problem of immunity against agents changing identities" (`ISMAIL-BLED-02`). The selected mechanism's credit-conservation property (`MISLOVE-NSDI-08`) is a structural answer to exactly the collusion case Beta states it cannot handle.

Against PeerTrust and TrustGuard (bilateral outcome counting, credibility-weighted): the recursive, trust-value-weighted form (TVM) reaches 100% RMS trust-computation error — "complete misclassification of good vs. malicious peers" — once malicious peers exceed 50%, and is "extremely sensitive" to collusion even at small malicious fractions (`XIONG-TKDE-04`, `SRIVATSA-WWW-05`). The similarity-weighted form (PSM) survives the same collusion condition (`SRIVATSA-WWW-05`), but requires the assessor to already share a nonempty set of prior transactions with each feedback source to compute that similarity — if two peers' transaction histories never overlap, PSM has no basis to compute a similarity-based credibility for one from the other (`XIONG-TKDE-04`) — which is precisely the direct-experience requirement a stranger has not yet supplied. Neither paper measures or claims a defense against self-rating through controlled identities beyond citing whitewashing countermeasures as future work (`XIONG-TKDE-04`); the selected mechanism's bound on self-rating (`TRAN-NSDI-09`, `LEVIEN-USS-98`) has no counterpart in this family for a first encounter with a stranger.

Against Advogato's own construction, unaccelerated and boolean-only, as a stopping point within the selected family: not rejected as a mechanism, but not adopted as the complete selection. Advogato's proof gives the tightest known bound (`LEVIEN-USS-98`) but returns only a boolean accept/reject for a fixed flow target of 1, not a graded value a participant can weight a decision by, and the paper itself ends without validating the metric on any deployed or prototype certificate infrastructure, stating "only practical experience with a prototype system can determine whether this tradeoff is worthwhile" (`LEVIEN-USS-98`). Bazaar, SumUp, and Ostra extend the identical construction to a continuous, per-interaction capacity value with measured production-scale performance (an 8,874,521-item eBay feedback crawl, a 446,181-node YouTube trust network: `POST-NSDI-11`, `MISLOVE-NSDI-08`) and add feedback-driven link elimination and credit decay that Advogato's static construction does not specify; Canal supplies the tractable-cost result needed at that scale (`VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`). Advogato's proof is cited above as the reason the extended family's bound is not an artifact of any one paper's construction, not as a competing selection.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- A trust-linkage layer (an identity or social-graph component elsewhere in this architecture) supplying edges that cost something real — time, money, social capital, or another scarce resource — to create and maintain; the bound collapses to nothing if this layer allows free edge creation, exactly the gap Ostra's own authors flag for the YouTube graph they had to substitute (`MISLOVE-NSDI-08`).
- Either a party able to compute over the full linkage graph on the assessor's behalf (Bazaar's design, `POST-NSDI-11`), a decentralized route-discovery layer (Ostra §7's Bloom-filter-plus-landmark sketch, `MISLOVE-NSDI-08`), or a Canal-style precomputed landmark index rebuilt continuously in the background (`VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`) — some component must expose enough of the graph for a flow or approximate-flow computation to run from the assessor's own position.
- An atomic hold/release protocol at the point of interaction: capacity along the flow path is reserved before the interaction proceeds and resolved — released or permanently debited — once an outcome signal or a bounded timeout arrives; the component consuming this reputation value (messaging, marketplace, or voting) must supply that outcome classification (`POST-NSDI-11`, `MISLOVE-NSDI-08`, `TRAN-NSDI-09`).
- A new-participant bootstrap: an existing participant extending a starter link at their own real risk, or an escrow/deposit mechanism creating a link of that deposited value; without one of these paths from the identity/onboarding component, every new participant starts at zero flow to everyone (`POST-NSDI-11`).
- Rational-actor incentive alignment for truthful feedback: the reasoning that a participant reports outcomes honestly depends on that participant's own links being at stake (`POST-NSDI-11`), which composes with the edge-cost requirement above — a participant with no remaining stake in its own linkage position has no measured incentive to report truthfully.
- No privacy tier offered elsewhere in this architecture may hide a participant's linkage graph from whichever party executes the flow computation; the evidence record for Bazaar notes explicitly that this guarantee composes poorly with any anonymity or partial-visibility tier that hides participants' linkage graphs from a shared computation point, since the flow computation cannot run over information the computing party does not hold (`POST-NSDI-11`) — this is a composition conflict to resolve explicitly against whatever privacy tiers this synthesis selects, not an open question to leave unresolved.
- If Canal's landmark acceleration is used: a link-level locking mechanism so concurrent path-stitching does not double-consume one link's capacity, and enough memory on whichever machine builds landmark universes to hold the full graph plus every cached universe — measured at almost 20GB for a 234-million-link graph on a single machine, with a distributed, cluster-based version left unevaluated by its own authors (`VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`).

### What it costs and where it fails

Exact max-flow (push-relabel) costs 0.352 seconds on a 1.4-million-link graph and rises to 220 seconds on a 234-million-link graph, per single query on one machine (`VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`) — too slow for an online, per-interaction decision at the upper end of that range. Bazaar's own multi-graph optimization, unaccelerated, brings average per-category query time to 1.18–6.29 seconds and needs a minimum 6-core server to keep pace with its trace's measured peak daily auction volume in the busiest category (`POST-NSDI-11`). Canal's landmark approximation reduces this to a median under 1 millisecond for a 1-credit query across five graphs up to 234 million links, and to 0.1–0.2 milliseconds (a 785×–2,329× speedup) once integrated into Bazaar's own eBay trace, at 94.2%–99%+ agreement with the exact computation (`VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`) — the residual disagreement is entirely false negatives (wrongly refusing a stranger the exact computation would have accepted), never a false positive, so the approximation trades a small, bounded rate of wrongly-refused strangers for tractable cost, never trades away the underlying fraud or Sybil bound.

The bound is only as strong as the minimum-cut weight between assessor and stranger: a legitimate stranger connected to the honest region through one thin link is starved of trust regardless of the honest population's total connectivity elsewhere, a risk Ostra's own min-cut analysis found falls as a participant's degree rises but does not eliminate for low-degree participants (`MISLOVE-NSDI-08`). A compromised long-history account can still be drained of value up to its own accumulated bound — bounded, not prevented (`POST-NSDI-11`). And, as established in the Selection comparisons above, this bound is measured to be the worst-performing option among three personalized alternatives, in a setting where the same underlying reputation value must compound continuously across many repeated attack rounds rather than support one threshold decision (`NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`) — a condition that does not hold for the single-stranger assessment task this component is built for, but is a documented failure mode for any future component that reuses this same mechanism for a compounding score.

### What the corpus does not settle

1. No entry measures this family's fully peer-executed, decentralized variant end-to-end. Ostra's own decentralized design (Bloom-filter and landmark route discovery, each node holding only local per-link state) is validated only by a preliminary Bloom-filter-size and landmark-coverage measurement, not an end-to-end deployment (`MISLOVE-NSDI-08`). Canal's landmark-index acceleration is itself a single-machine, centrally-computed background process (`VISWANATH-EUROSYS-12`); no entry measures a Canal-style landmark index built and queried without a party that holds the whole graph.
2. No entry measures the cut-capacity bound this selection relies on against an adversary who selectively targets which honest participants to attach Sybil links to, the way `VISWANATH-SIGCOMM-10` measured for SumUp's use as a Sybil classifier (where targeted attachment drove classification accuracy below random). Bazaar's guarantee is a different quantity — a bounded monetary or vote amount, not a binary label — so whether targeted attachment degrades the cut-capacity bound the same way it degrades classification accuracy is unmeasured in this corpus.
3. No entry measures how the slander defense (damage bounded by the attacker's own accumulated real transaction volume with the specific victim) behaves when many separate attackers pool their individually-accumulated links against one small-degree target; Bazaar's own text flags this attack qualitatively without a swept experiment (`POST-NSDI-11`).
4. Every tunable parameter cited above (Ostra's L, U, and decay rate d; Bazaar's multi-graph base k and feedback timeout T; Canal's landmark-universe count and level) is evaluated only at whatever value each paper's own trace happened to need, not derived from first principles. A builder needs to re-measure these against the target deployment's own linkage-formation rate and interaction volume rather than reuse the values cited here.
5. No entry directly compares Bazaar/SumUp/Ostra-style max-flow against MeritRank's Personalized Hitting Time on the same graph and the same attack model. The two measured comparisons available (SumUp vs. SybilLimit, `TRAN-NSDI-09`; MeritRank's three algorithms against each other, `NASRULIN-BRAINS-22`) use different graphs, different attack models (static attack-edge injection vs. repeated per-epoch Sybil injection), and different outcome metrics (bogus-vote count vs. a continuously compounding reputation-gain ratio), so a single shared experiment settling which family costs less for a threshold accept/reject decision specifically does not exist in this corpus.
6. No entry measures whether the fast-mixing or bounded-modularity assumption that degrades classification-style Sybil defenses (`VISWANATH-SIGCOMM-10`: accuracy falls toward random, and below random under targeted attachment, as network modularity rises) has any analogous effect on a pure cut-capacity bound of the kind Bazaar and Ostra compute, since that bound is an algebraic property of max-flow rather than a classifier's accuracy against ground truth, and no paper in this corpus tests it against the modularity sweep `VISWANATH-SIGCOMM-10` ran for classification-style methods.


---

## Privacy tiers: three independently cost-ordered ladders, one per action — fetch, query, message — and two named candidate families withheld from the ladders because their own measured cost is not reliable enough to offer a person as an informed choice

### Candidates

**Table 1 — fetching one object from one peer: defends the connection itself against a network-position or on-path observer**

| Mechanism | Measured cost (conditions) | Security assumption | Failure condition | Requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| No anonymity layer (direct connection) | Zero, by construction — every other row's overhead below is stated against this reference point. | None. | Fails against any adversary that can observe the connection at all: an on-path router, the serving peer, or a passive metadata collector. | The transport component's own confidentiality (TLS/QUIC-equivalent) protects payload content; this tier supplies no protection for which peer was contacted or when. |
| 3-hop onion circuit, no padding (Tor-class) | Median page-fetch latency 2.8s (0.4s fastest, 5.3s 90th percentile) versus 0.3s direct; 60MB file download averaged ~300s versus ~210s direct — both on the live 32-relay 2004 Tor deployment, roughly four orders of magnitude smaller than a present-day relay population [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]. Against a fingerprinting classifier specifically (not raw fetch latency): 77.5% ideal-attacker accuracy at a stated 25% overhead, a pair [CAI-CCS-14] states it imports from a separate 2012 measurement not independently retrieved into this corpus. | Entry and exit relay independently operated and not colluding; the adversary does not observe both circuit ends at once. | The authors state directly that a global passive adversary correlating traffic timing at both ends "will immediately and automatically defeat" the design [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]. A local eavesdropper running any website-fingerprinting classifier succeeds at high accuracy absent an added padding defense [CAI-CCS-14]. | A signed, current relay directory reachable before circuit construction [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]; a role-diverse, independently operated relay population for the non-collusion assumption to mean anything. |
| Lightweight zero-delay padding: WTF-PAD / FRONT | WTF-PAD: 0% latency, <60% bandwidth, cuts a k-NN classifier from 91% to 20% accuracy on a live Tor-Browser-crawl dataset [JUAREZ-ESORICS-16]; independently reproduced at 54.0% bandwidth [HOLLAND-POPETS-22], 61% [SHEN-SP-24], and 32.71% under a different histogram configuration [GONG-USENIXSEC-20] — the identical named defense measured roughly 2x apart in bandwidth overhead, all four figures from simulation replaying captured Tor traffic, none from a live deployment. FRONT: 0% latency, 33.01%-48.80% bandwidth, on the authors' own live-Tor-Browser-crawl dataset [GONG-USENIXSEC-20]. | Passive, single-point eavesdropper (client-to-guard link only); no active delay, drop, or modification. | Both are defeated by deep-learning classifiers: WTF-PAD leaves Deep Fingerprinting (DF) at 92.4% accuracy against a 98.4% undefended baseline, and Tik-Tok at 94.2% [HOLLAND-POPETS-22]; the RF attack (built specifically against zero-delay defenses) holds above 90% against FRONT [SHEN-SP-24]. | A cooperating relay running matching padding-endpoint logic — the guard/bridge for WTF-PAD, the circuit's middle node for FRONT [JUAREZ-ESORICS-16; GONG-USENIXSEC-20]. |
| Constant-rate provable padding: Tamaraw | Simulated ideal-attacker bound: 199% bandwidth / ~320% latency for 3.4% adversarial accuracy, or 687% bandwidth for 0.4% accuracy, against an information-theoretic upper-bound attacker on an 800-site closed-world dataset, not a trained classifier [CAI-CCS-14]. On a live-Tor pluggable-transport deployment with parameters recalculated to Tor's actual 514-byte cell (the original paper assumed a 750-byte payload): four trained deep-learning classifiers held at or below roughly 15% accuracy, at 135% bandwidth / 78% time overhead [SHEN-SP-24]. | Proves a bound over a defined attacker class (ambiguity-set partitioning); the authors state the connection between Tamaraw's fixed-bucket construction and the theory's dataset-optimized partitioning is only partial [CAI-CCS-14]. | Does not hide total transmitted size against a generator that deliberately varies it (the paper's own G1/G2 attacker classes) [CAI-CCS-14]. Requires one system-wide fixed padding parameter L; a per-client choice fragments the anonymity set the proof needs [CAI-CCS-14]. | A padding endpoint that knows, out of band, when a page's real transfer ended — the paper's own simulation assumes an oracle for this rather than solving it [CAI-CCS-14]. |
| Deep-learning-tuned shape regularization: RegulaTor / Surakav / Palette | Three independent measurements of the identical RegulaTor defense disagree by up to 100 percentage points on latency overhead: closed-world offline simulation, DF-CW dataset — 6.6% latency / 79.7% bandwidth [HOLLAND-POPETS-22]; the same paper's own live pluggable-transport deployment — 13.9% latency / 78.2% bandwidth [HOLLAND-POPETS-22]; an independent live-Tor reproduction by a separate paper — 112% time overhead, "versus 5% in simulation" [SHEN-SP-24]. Surakav (live Tor, open-world headline): 55% data / 16% time overhead for a 57% reduction in attacker true-positive rate [GONG-SP-22]. | Reduces modern deep-learning classifiers to a floor accuracy, not to a proven bound — a materially weaker guarantee than Tamaraw's. | [SHEN-SP-24] states its own real-world measurements "diverge from simulation for every defense tested, most severely for RegulaTor," attributed to packet dependencies between incoming and outgoing traffic that offline simulation cannot represent. | A cooperating entry-side proxy running the matching scheduler in lockstep with the client [HOLLAND-POPETS-22]; advance, periodically refreshed knowledge of the target site population's typical traffic volume, since unadjusted parameters measurably degrade against a different-volume population [HOLLAND-POPETS-22]. |

**Table 2 — querying a shared index: defends which record is requested, hidden even from the single peer answering, for an index that peer legitimately holds**

| Mechanism | Measured cost (conditions) | Security assumption | Failure condition | Requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Multi-server information-theoretic PIR (two-database XOR) | No experimental implementation. The base two-database construction sends n bits total for an n-bit database — no better than downloading it whole — with sub-linear compression proved only for larger, unbenchmarked server counts [CHOR-JACM-98]. | Unconditional (information-theoretic), given at least two non-communicating, non-colluding database copies. | Collapses to zero privacy if the client's two chosen servers collude or are the same operator under different names — the protocol supplies no way to detect this [CHOR-JACM-98]. | Two independently operated database copies the client can address separately. |
| Single-server computational PIR, known index (SimplePIR/DoublePIR, YPIR, Piano, FrodoPIR, OnionPIR, Spiral) | Piano, 1GB/2^27 entries: 3.0ms online query latency, 32KB online communication, after a 629s/111s (1/8-thread) client-side preprocessing pass [ZHOU-SP-24]. Piano, 100GB/1.68B entries: 11.9ms online, 100KB communication, versus SimplePIR's 10.9s extrapolated at the same scale [ZHOU-SP-24]. FrodoPIR, 2^20 1KB-element database: 3.556KB response, 825ms single-threaded server response time [DAVIDSON-POPETS-23]. OnionPIR, 2^20 30KB entries: 128KB response versus 3,200KB for SealPIR at the same size [MUGHEES-CCS-21]. | Computational — Learning-With-Errors (LWE) or Ring-LWE hardness. A single server, allowed fully malicious behavior in Piano's and Tiptoe's definitions [ZHOU-SP-24; HENZINGER-SOSP-23]. | SimplePIR/DoublePIR's precomputed hint (121MB or a database-size-independent 16MB) and YPIR's packing key must be re-downloaded whenever the database changes enough to invalidate them; neither paper bounds this against a continuously updating database [HENZINGER-USENIXSEC-23; MENON-USENIXSEC-24]. Piano's own hint set must be refreshed roughly every sqrt(n)·ln(n) queries [ZHOU-SP-24]. | The client must already know the numeric index of the record it wants; none of these schemes locates a record by content, only by a position the client supplies [stated as the same precondition in ANGEL-SP-18]. |
| Full private search, unknown index (Tiptoe, Pacmann, Panther) | Tiptoe: 364M-page web crawl, 45-machine cluster, simulated 100Mbps/50ms client link — 2.7s end-to-end latency, 56.9MiB communication (74% completing before the query is entered), average rank of the correct result 7.7/100 (a non-private neural retriever reaches 2.3, classical tf-idf reaches 6.7) [HENZINGER-SOSP-23]. Pacmann: single-thread server, 50ms round-trip — 3.0s at 100M vectors, 1.1s at 3.2M documents, ~90% of a non-private baseline's recall, after a 59.6GB/272s client preprocessing download [ZHOU-EPRINT-24]. Panther: 64-vCPU/256GB instance — 3.89s local-area / 18.3s wide-area at 10M points, 284MB communication [LI-CCS-25-PANTHER]. | Tiptoe: a single server allowed to deviate arbitrarily from the protocol (malicious), no second party [HENZINGER-SOSP-23]. Pacmann and Panther: semi-honest (protocol-following) server only — a strictly weaker guarantee [ZHOU-EPRINT-24; LI-CCS-25-PANTHER]. | Tiptoe's own authors state the method performs worst on exact-string queries such as phone numbers or addresses [HENZINGER-SOSP-23]. Pacmann's preprocessing must repeat in full on any database change; dynamic updates are stated as unsupported, left open [ZHOU-EPRINT-24]. Panther needs a local-area-class round trip to approach 2 seconds; its own wide-area figure at the same scale is 8.71-18.3s [LI-CCS-25-PANTHER]. | An embedding function run once over the whole corpus at indexing time and periodically re-run for updates, with cluster assignments published to every client [HENZINGER-SOSP-23]. Query privacy alone, absent a connection-unlinkability tier from Table 1, still discloses the requester's network identity to the server [stated as the same composition gap by ASI-ARXIV-24-WALLY for its own design]. |
| Differential-privacy batched search with a non-colluding anonymizer (Wally) | Up to 66,000 queries/second at 1M entries / 500,000 concurrent users, but a client's own query is not answered until its epoch closes: 10 seconds (1M entries/100,000 users) to 6 minutes (100M entries/500,000 users) [ASI-ARXIV-24-WALLY]. | (epsilon, delta)-differential privacy over the whole batch (tested at epsilon=0.1, delta=2^-26), not cryptographic per-query indistinguishability, plus a second, non-colluding anonymization service the paper does not build or measure, naming Tor or a mix network as an assumed-adequate substitute [ASI-ARXIV-24-WALLY]. | The paper states the batch-noise design needs "tens to hundreds of thousands" of simultaneous, non-coordinating queriers to keep each client's own fake-query volume low [ASI-ARXIV-24-WALLY]; a narrow-topic or early-stage index lacks this population. The authors state plainly this system targets "high throughput... while low latency can be relaxed," not an interactive query [ASI-ARXIV-24-WALLY]. | The same clustering index structure Tiptoe uses, so switching index mechanisms invalidates Wally's derived noise parameters [ASI-ARXIV-24-WALLY]; an already-deployed, already-trusted anonymizing relay layer — precisely Table 1's connection-unlinkability tier, which Wally assumes rather than supplies. |

**Table 3 — direct messaging: defends who talks to whom against an adversary controlling the network and some servers**

| Mechanism | Measured cost (conditions) | Security assumption | Failure condition | Requires from other components |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Small-clique DC-net broadcast (Herbivore, Dissent, Riposte) | Herbivore, live PlanetLab (~100 nodes): median anonymous bandwidth ~250Kb/s at clique size 10 / 1 active sender, falling to 50-100Kb/s at clique size 40 / 4 active senders [GOEL-CORNELLTR-03]. Riposte (3-server variant, DeterLab testbed, 20ms round-trip): 751.5 writes/second at a 64-row table, falling to 32.8 writes/second at 65,536 rows [CORRIGANGIBBS-SP-15]. | Information-theoretic — no cryptographic hardness assumption for the core anonymity property. Herbivore requires all k-1 other clique members to collude before deanonymizing a sender [GOEL-CORNELLTR-03]; Riposte's 3-server variant needs only that no two of the three servers collude [CORRIGANGIBBS-SP-15]. | Throughput collapses against clique/table size. Vuvuzela's own related-work section states Herbivore and Dissent scale to at most about 5,000-user broadcast groups because of superlinear (quadratic) cost — a claim this document attributes to Vuvuzela's authors, not independently reconfirmed here [as reported within VANDENHOOFF-SOSP-15]. | A fixed, small clique membership per round. Riposte's audit protocol requires the audit server not to collude with either database server [CORRIGANGIBBS-SP-15]. |
| Cover-traffic mix network under a proven differential-privacy bound (Vuvuzela, Loopix, Karaoke, Stadium, Yodel) | Vuvuzela: 68,000 messages/second at 37s end-to-end latency, 1M users, 3 relay servers plus 1 entry server [VANDENHOOFF-SOSP-15]. Loopix: 0.6ms/packet processing, Gamma-distributed end-to-end latency mean 1.93s (std 0.87s) at 500 users, 6 mix nodes / 3 layers, per-mix saturation near 225 messages/second [PIOTROWSKA-USENIXSEC-17]. Karaoke: 6-8s at 2-8M users on 100 servers, 5-10x lower latency than Vuvuzela/Stadium at matched user count [LAZAR-OSDI-18]. Stadium: 43.6s at 6.3M users/100 servers, rising to 876s at 46M users/chain-length 9 [TYAGI-SOSP-17]. Yodel (voice-specific circuit reuse): under 1s at 5M users/100 servers with the LPCNet codec [LAZAR-SOSP-19]. | A proven (epsilon, delta)-differential-privacy bound over the observed traffic pattern, against a global passive network adversary, provided at least one server on the message's path is honest and actually performs its assigned mixing and noise-generation duties [VANDENHOOFF-SOSP-15, Theorem 1/2]. | No enforcement mechanism exists for the one-honest-server assumption against a fully open, permissionless server population: a single faulty entity can present an unbounded number of distinct identities absent a synchronized, challenged admission process [DOUCEUR-IPTPS-02, Lemma 1/2]. Loopix/Nym's cover-traffic efficiency additionally requires a stratified topology with intersecting routes across layers; measured directly against a flat P2P mesh under the identical simulator, the flat topology's anonymity stays low and nearly flat from 10^2 to 10^5 users and needs cover-to-real traffic ratios up to 10:1 to improve it, still below the stratified design's anonymity at far less cover traffic [PIOTROWSKA-WPES-21]. Yodel's own trust-assumption sweep ties path length directly to latency: 2 hops (~100ms) at 100%-honest, 15 hops at the paper's 80%-honest baseline [LAZAR-SOSP-19, §6.3]. | Every user, active or not, must generate cover traffic every round — a bandwidth floor independent of whether any message is actually sent [VANDENHOOFF-SOSP-15]. Loopix requires an external presence mechanism (the paper names DP5/MP3 as candidates, integrates neither) for a sender to learn a receiver's provider address [PIOTROWSKA-USENIXSEC-17]. Loopix's sender-anonymity guarantee does not strip an application-layer sender identifier the application itself writes into a message body. |

**Excluded outright: searchable encryption over the client's own stored index, as a query-content-privacy candidate.** [CASH-CCS-15]'s count attack recovers a client's queried keywords from access-pattern leakage alone — the least-revealing published leakage profile (L1) — using only public per-keyword document-count statistics, no known queries required; on the Enron corpus (30,109 documents, vocabulary swept 500-6,500 keywords) the worst tested trial failed to reconstruct only 5 of 650 keywords. [GRUBBS-SP-19] shows the same class of attack (order and value reconstruction from range-query access patterns) has a query count independent of the number of records or possible plaintext values — the attack does not get harder as the deployment scales. No tested operating point of searchable encryption in this corpus survives passive observation by the server it is stored on, so it is not offered as a tier: every published construction fails the requirement that a tier's stated adversary-defeated claim hold under the adversary the tier claims to defeat.

### Selection

Three ladders, ordered by measured cost within each, one per action a person takes. **Fetch** (retrieving one object from one peer): direct connection, then a 3-hop onion circuit with no padding, then the circuit plus WTF-PAD, then the circuit plus Tamaraw at its live-deployment operating point. **Query** (searching or reading a shared index without disclosing which record matched): direct query, then single-server computational PIR for a known index (Piano as the default instantiation), then Tiptoe for an unknown index needing ranked search. **Message** (point-to-point communication where the pattern of who-talks-to-whom is itself sensitive): direct message, then a mix network under a proven differential-privacy bound (Karaoke as the default instantiation for text, Yodel for real-time voice). Multi-server PIR, Stadium, and small-clique DC-net broadcast are offered as opt-in alternates within the Query and Message ladders, not as defaults, for the specific reasons given below. Wally is offered only as a background, non-interactive prefetch mode, never as an interactive Query tier. Searchable encryption is not offered at all.

**Against the deep-learning-tuned regularization family (RegulaTor, Surakav, Palette) as a Fetch tier.** [HOLLAND-POPETS-22]'s own live pluggable-transport deployment measured RegulaTor-Heavy at 13.9% latency / 78.2% bandwidth; [SHEN-SP-24]'s independent live-Tor reproduction of the same named defense measured 112% time overhead, described in that paper's own text as "versus 5% in simulation." Two independently produced live-network figures for one defense disagree by roughly 100 percentage points, and a third, simulated figure disagrees with both by a comparable margin. This document's own admission requirement — "a tier whose cost is not measured cannot be offered, because a person cannot make an informed choice about an unquantified cost" — is violated by any single number drawn from this family, since no single number represents what a live deployment of it will actually cost. WTF-PAD and FRONT remain offered instead because two to four independent measurements of each agree to within roughly 2x of each other, all under live-Tor-crawl conditions [JUAREZ-ESORICS-16; HOLLAND-POPETS-22; SHEN-SP-24; GONG-USENIXSEC-20], and Tamaraw remains offered at its own live-deployment figure specifically [SHEN-SP-24], not its simulated one, for the same reason.

**Against Tamaraw's simulated figure as the offered Fetch-tier cost, in favor of its live-deployment figure.** [CAI-CCS-14]'s own simulated headline (199%-687% bandwidth) assumed a 750-byte payload the authors chose to cover most outgoing packets in their own dataset, not Tor's actual 514-byte cell. [SHEN-SP-24] recalculated Tamaraw's rate parameters to the real cell size and measured 135% bandwidth / 78% time overhead on the live network against four trained deep-learning classifiers, each held near or below 15% accuracy. Citing the simulated figure as "Tamaraw's cost" would import a padding parameter fixed to one paper's own assumed transport frame size rather than the transport this architecture actually uses; the transferable form of the finding is that a padding defense's rate and size parameters must be recalculated to whatever frame size the deployment's transport component actually uses, and only the recalculated figure is offered here.

**Against multi-server information-theoretic PIR as the default Query tier, in favor of single-server computational PIR.** [CHOR-JACM-98]'s base two-database construction sends n bits total for an n-bit database, with no benchmarked implementation of any compressed variant, against Piano's measured 3.0ms/32KB per query at 1GB after amortized preprocessing [ZHOU-SP-24]. The multi-server scheme's unconditional privacy guarantee is real, but it requires the client to address two independently operated, non-colluding database copies — a trust decision the client must make in advance and cannot verify cryptographically — while the single-server schemes need only a computational hardness assumption (LWE or Ring-LWE) already relied upon elsewhere in this architecture's cryptography, and no second party to select or trust. Multi-server PIR is retained as an opt-in for a deployment where two specific, already-trusted, independently operated index-holding peers exist, not as the default.

**Against Pacmann and Panther as the default unknown-index Query tier, in favor of Tiptoe.** [ZHOU-EPRINT-24] reports Pacmann reaching 1.1-3.0s latency, comparable to or faster than Tiptoe's 2.7s [HENZINGER-SOSP-23], but Pacmann's security proof explicitly does not cover a malicious server, only a semi-honest one that follows the protocol — a strictly weaker guarantee than Tiptoe's, which tolerates a server that deviates arbitrarily. A single storage/index peer in a permissionless network cannot be assumed to follow its own protocol honestly merely because it is not actively colluding with a second party; Tiptoe's guarantee matches that threat model and Pacmann's does not. [LI-CCS-25-PANTHER]'s Panther adds the further property of hiding the corpus itself from the client, at the cost of both a semi-honest-only guarantee and materially worse wide-area latency (8.71-18.3s against Tiptoe's 2.7s at comparable scale) — a property this architecture does not need, because the content being searched is itself meant to be publicly indexed, not secret from the querier who is a legitimate participant in the same network.

**Against Wally as an interactive Query tier.** [ASI-ARXIV-24-WALLY]'s own throughput figures assume 100,000 to 500,000 simultaneous, non-coordinating queriers per epoch and a client's answer arriving only when that epoch closes — 10 seconds at the smallest tested configuration, 6 minutes at the largest. Tiptoe answers a single query in 2.7 seconds with no dependency on how many other people are querying at the same moment [HENZINGER-SOSP-23]. Wally's own authors state their target workload directly: "high throughput is a must, while low latency can be relaxed," naming background retrieval of photo context for on-device models, not a query a person is waiting on. A person fetching a search result is waiting on it; Wally is offered here only for a background prefetch action, never for an interactive one.

**Against Vuvuzela and Stadium as the default Message tier, in favor of Karaoke.** [LAZAR-OSDI-18] measures Karaoke reaching 6-8 seconds of latency at 2-8 million users on 100 servers, 5 to 10 times lower than Vuvuzela [VANDENHOOFF-SOSP-15] and Stadium [TYAGI-SOSP-17] at matched user count, because only a fraction of Karaoke's servers process each additional user's messages rather than every server processing every message. Stadium is retained as an opt-in alternate, not rejected outright: its own contribution is spreading trust across many independently operated mixchains rather than concentrating the one-honest-server requirement in as few parties as Vuvuzela's four-server design, a genuinely different security property a user distrusting Karaoke's smaller server set may prefer — at the measured cost of 43.6 seconds to 876 seconds depending on chain length and scale [TYAGI-SOSP-17].

**Against small-clique DC-net broadcast (Herbivore, Dissent, Riposte) as the default Message tier.** [GOEL-CORNELLTR-03] measures Herbivore's anonymous bandwidth falling to 50-100Kb/s once a clique reaches 40 members with 4 active senders, and [CORRIGANGIBBS-SP-15] measures Riposte's write throughput falling to 32.8 requests/second at a 65,536-row table — both far below Karaoke's 6-8 second latency at millions of users [LAZAR-OSDI-18]. The DC-net family's information-theoretic guarantee needs no cryptographic hardness assumption at all, a strictly stronger property than the mix network's proven-but-computational differential-privacy bound; that property is retained here as an opt-in for a small, bounded, mutually acquainted group that wants an assumption-free guarantee and does not need this architecture's general messaging scale.

### What this selection requires from the rest of the system

- The Query ladder's single-server PIR tier requires the client to already hold the numeric index of the object it wants, which composes directly with content addressing: a client that already has an object's content hash can supply that as the PIR index, but a client that does not yet know which object it wants needs the Tiptoe tier instead, which requires an embedding pipeline run over the whole corpus at indexing time [HENZINGER-SOSP-23] — a component this selection consumes, not one it supplies.
- The Query ladder's single-server PIR tier destroys the feedback signal square-root replication needs: a forwarding or storage node running Piano, SimplePIR, or Tiptoe never learns which item a query touched by design [ZHOU-SP-24; HENZINGER-SOSP-23], so content replicated through search-driven feedback (documented elsewhere in this corpus as a composition conflict between square-root replication and PIR-protected search) must be replicated by some other trigger for any content queried through this tier.
- The Message ladder's mix-network tier requires at least one server on every message's path to be honest and to actually perform its assigned mixing and noise-generation duties, with no cryptographic mechanism to enforce this against a fully open peer population [VANDENHOOFF-SOSP-15; DOUCEUR-IPTPS-02]. An identity or admission component that can vet or bond a small server-role subset is required before this tier can be offered with its proven guarantee intact; absent that, the tier's stated adversary-defeated claim (a global passive network adversary) should be disclosed to the user as conditional on trusting the specific server operators selected, not unconditional.
- The Message ladder's mix-network tier requires every user, active or idle, to generate cover traffic every round [VANDENHOOFF-SOSP-15; PIOTROWSKA-USENIXSEC-17]; any component that suspends a client's network activity to save power or bandwidth — including a mobile background-execution policy this architecture's transport/reachability component must contend with separately — collapses this tier's guarantee for that user during the suspended interval, since the guarantee depends on continuous, indistinguishable traffic volume, not on any single message.
- The Message ladder's mix-network tier hides network-level message origin only; it does not strip an application-layer sender identifier the application itself writes into a message body [PIOTROWSKA-USENIXSEC-17]. Any component that writes a peer's stable identifier into application data carried over this tier (a ranking or gossip mechanism that logs a querying peer's node ID, for instance) defeats the tier's guarantee at a layer this tier does not reach; the application-data component must be told explicitly which fields this tier does not protect.
- The Fetch ladder's onion-circuit tiers require a signed, current relay directory reachable by every client before circuit construction [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04], supplied by this architecture's naming/discovery component, not by this selection.
- Every tier in every ladder that names a specific padding parameter, chunk size, or cell size (Tamaraw's L/rho_out/rho_in, PIR's database-block size) must have that parameter recalculated against the transport component's actual frame size, not imported from the source paper's own assumed value [SHEN-SP-24 demonstrates the consequence of skipping this step directly, for Tamaraw].

### What it costs and where it fails

No ladder position anywhere in this document escapes a proven three-way bound. Das, Meiser, Mohammadi, and Kate ("Anonymity Trilemma: Strong Anonymity, Low Bandwidth Overhead, Low Latency — Choose Two," IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy 2018) prove that a communication protocol defended against a global passive network-level adversary achieves at most two of three properties at once — anonymity up to a negligible probability of deanonymization, low bandwidth overhead, and low latency overhead — deriving, for a stated count of passively compromised protocol nodes, a required relationship between bandwidth overhead and latency overhead that a design violating it cannot achieve strong anonymity within. The same authors' follow-up ("Comprehensive Anonymity Trilemma: User Coordination is not Enough," Proceedings on Privacy Enhancing Technologies 2020) closes the one route around the 2018 bound that had been proposed — letting users coordinate their sending behavior out of band — by proving a second, strictly stronger impossibility result that covers coordinating users as well. Neither paper carries a full-text entry with its own key in this evidence corpus: this document states both results from this repository's own retained prior-pass record, which the project brief lists as already read in full before this pass began, and states them only in the qualitative form that record preserves — a required relationship between the two overhead quantities, not the closed-form bound itself. No specific numeric constant from either paper is asserted here; that constant is unverified in this corpus.

The measured tiers in this document trace the same shape directly, without needing the abstract bound. [LAZAR-SOSP-19]'s own trust-assumption sweep (§6.3) holds latency to roughly 100ms only by assuming every server honest, and needs 15 relay hops, with correspondingly higher latency, once the honesty assumption drops to 80%. [PIOTROWSKA-WPES-21]'s comparison of Elixxir, HOPR, and Nym under one simulator shows the same trade from the opposite direction: Elixxir's batch-and-reorder design bounds anonymity to its fixed batch size regardless of user count while latency grows without bound as users scale; HOPR's flat peer-to-peer topology keeps latency low but needs cover-to-real traffic ratios up to 10:1 to raise anonymity at all, and still falls short of Nym's stratified design at far less cover traffic. Every row in Table 3 sits somewhere on this surface — cheaper in bandwidth or latency at the price of weaker anonymity, a stronger honesty assumption, or a smaller usable scale — and no combination of parameters published in this corpus escapes it.

Fetch-ladder failure is per-tier and already stated in Table 1: no padding fails against any website-fingerprinting classifier; lightweight padding fails against deep-learning classifiers specifically; Tamaraw fails against an attacker that observes total transmitted size directly. Query-ladder failure is compositional: every tier in Table 2 protects only the query, not the requester's network identity, so a Query tier used without an underlying Fetch tier discloses the requester by IP address even while hiding which record was requested [stated directly by ASI-ARXIV-24-WALLY for its own design]. Message-ladder failure is assumption-dependent: the mix-network tier's proven bound holds only while at least one server per path stays honest, a condition this corpus supplies no cryptographic enforcement for in a fully open peer population [DOUCEUR-IPTPS-02].

### What the corpus does not settle

- The exact closed-form relationship Das, Meiser, Mohammadi, and Kate derive between bandwidth overhead, latency overhead, and compromised-node count is not present in this corpus with a citable full-text entry; a builder needing the precise numeric trade-off, rather than its qualitative shape, needs to retrieve IEEE S&P 2018 (ePrint 2017/954) and PoPETs 2020 directly.
- No entry measures onion-routing latency at a relay count and user population matching this architecture's likely scale; [DINGLEDINE-USENIXSEC-04]'s own figures come from a 32-relay network designed for roughly 10,000 users, and no later entry in this corpus re-measures Tor's own baseline circuit latency at a modern relay population.
- No entry measures a padding defense's cost on a transport this network actually uses (a peer-to-peer overlay circuit rather than Tor specifically); every website-fingerprinting figure in Table 1 is measured on live or simulated Tor traffic, and the transferable finding — recalculate padding parameters to the deployment's real frame size — has no measured worked example outside Tor's own 514-byte cell.
- No entry measures the mix-network family's client-side battery, background-execution, or intermittent-connectivity cost; every Table 3 figure comes from a server-side or cloud-VM deployment where clients are simulated as continuously connected, and this architecture's own mobile participants are not.
- No entry proposes or measures a mechanism enforcing the mix-network tier's one-honest-server (or non-collusion) assumption in a genuinely open, permissionless peer population; this is recorded directly, not merely implied, as an unresolved requirement above.
- No entry in this corpus measures a mix design reaching Loopix/Nym-class anonymity-per-cover-traffic-byte in a flat, undifferentiated peer-to-peer topology; [PIOTROWSKA-WPES-21] measures only that the flat topology (HOPR) falls short, not what would close the gap.
- A fourth action class — a person reading their own previously stored, private data back from a peer that hosts it, distinct from either fetching public content or querying a shared index — has a candidate mechanism in this corpus (Path Oblivious RAM, hiding which of a client's own records is accessed on each read [STEFANOV-CCS-13]) with only a proved asymptotic bandwidth bound (O(log N) to O(log² N) blocks moved per access, depending on block size) and a simulated stash-size result, no live wall-clock latency or network-deployment measurement. This document does not offer it as a fourth ladder for that reason; a builder needing a measured cost for private access to one's own remotely stored data has no entry in this corpus to draw one from.
- No entry measures whether the single-server PIR tier's precomputed-hint or streaming-preprocessing cost (SimplePIR/DoublePIR's hint, Piano's linear pass, Tiptoe's embedding index) can be kept current against this architecture's own actual object-publication rate; every figure in Table 2 is measured against a static or slowly-changing snapshot, and the corpus's own open-problems record states this gap directly for a continuously updating index without resolving it.


---

# Part 3 — Where one selection removes a precondition another needs

One selection removes a precondition another selection needs, in 21 places, across 28 component pairs checked. Eleven of the 21 have no published resolution, and are open problems. Ten degrade a stated property, and the report states the degradation. None is left unclassified. The section below is the composition check verbatim from `architecture.md`.
## Composition conflicts

### 1. A blockchain or equivalent network-wide consensus ledger supplying unbiased per-challenge-window randomness and automatic payment execution, for the storage-capacity payment mechanism.

**Required by** incentives.md (storage-retrievability-proof payment, Sia/Shacham-Waters construction) · **removed by** No selected component supplies this ledger; naming.md, key-transparency.md, group-encryption.md, and ranking.md each independently reject a blockchain/consensus dependency for their own resource, on the identical stated ground that it reintroduces a capture point or a coordination primitive stronger than the architecture otherwise needs. · **no-published-resolution-open-problem**

incentives.md's own selection text requires the ledger outright with no other component in the registry providing one; the absence is not silent but pointed, since four other selections explicitly reasoned their way out of the same dependency for their own resource (naming rejects Namecoin's mining-majority consensus; key-transparency rejects on-chain root posting and full on-chain binding; group-encryption rejects DeCAF's blockchain-total-order variant as a stronger primitive than the causal broadcast it needs; ranking rejects every consensus-favouring aggregation candidate as the exact capture point the architecture's stated goal rules out).

**Resolution.** Three options recorded in registry/composition/ordering.md: (1) change the selection to Samsara's ledger-free symmetric barter (accepting its stated degradation that it does not stop a node from discarding data it promised to store), or keep the retrievability proof as a verification signal settled through the already-selected reciprocal-exchange mechanism instead of automatic ledger payment; (2) accept a degraded property by substituting per-verifier-trusted randomness and off-protocol settlement, explicitly giving up the 'prover cannot bias the challenge' guarantee with its magnitude unmeasured; (3) record as an open problem, noting the four-component pattern as evidence the tension is architectural.

### 2. The retrieval-serving node must read the requested content identifier at the moment of serving, to test it against the illegal-content deny list, independent of any viewer's choices.

**Required by** moderation.md (identifier deny list honored at retrieval) · **removed by** privacy-tiers.md (single-server computational PIR, the default Query tier) · **no-published-resolution-open-problem**

PIR's security property is precisely that the answering peer cannot determine which index (content identifier) the client queried; privacy-tiers.md states this tier applies to retrieving an already-known object by content hash, not only to keyword search, so a server serving under this tier structurally cannot read the identifier moderation.md's deny-list check needs.

**Resolution.** No published construction in the corpus lets a server test a PIR-protected query against a blocklist without learning the query, so this is recorded as an open problem alongside registry/open-problems/illegal-content-removal.md. Operationally, the corpus supports disclosing to the user that illegal-content interdiction does not cover a fetch made under the Query privacy tier (an accepted degradation), the same pattern registry/conflicts/composition.md finding 3 already uses for a different pairing. Changing a selection instead would mean dropping single-server PIR as the default Query tier or excluding some content class from that tier, neither of which any entry in the corpus evaluates.

### 3. A capacity value used to admit peers to the transit tier and to order routing within it (HSkip+) must be verified, not merely self-declared, or a coalition can claim capacity it does not have to gain a structural relay position.

**Required by** capacity-ordering.md (two-tier structure + HSkip+ bandwidth-ordered routing) · **removed by** forgery-resistance.md (selected: computational work priced per write) · **no-published-resolution-open-problem**

forgery-resistance.md's selected mechanism prices writes only and states directly that 'no identity, key-transparency, or admission component is consulted by this mechanism at all'; joining the transit tier or being ranked within it is not a write, so nothing prices a false capacity claim. incentives.md's PropShare (the only other candidate honesty mechanism) governs bandwidth already flowing on an already-established transit-tier link, after admission — capacity-ordering.md's own text confirms this. Real-world evidence already shows the incentive runs toward misreporting: up to 30% of self-declared-low-bandwidth users hold significantly higher true bandwidth (SAROIU-MMCN-02), and a peer that honestly raises its claimed capacity sees its own load rise 303% while the same change adopted network-wide lowers it 14% (YANG-ICDE-03).

**Resolution.** capacity-ordering.md itself names the fork but does not resolve it: either make one of its own optional verification layers (PeerFlow, FlashFlow) a mandatory precondition for transit-tier admission (selection changes, with PeerFlow's ~27.7-hour measurement latency and per-authority overhead accepted), or explicitly disclose the resulting degradation to users (admission and rank can be gamed up to a verification layer's own trim threshold, e.g. PeerFlow's λ=0.256), or record it as an open problem since no entry measures real-deployment misreport rates.

### 4. PAC random-peer-sample search's accuracy proof assumes every node truthfully holds the same fixed document count (rho), uniformly at random; a high-capacity node splitting into many rho-sized virtual identities must be excluded by

**Required by** indexing.md (local-index-first + PAC network fallback) · **removed by** forgery-resistance.md (selected: computational work priced per write) · **no-published-resolution-open-problem**

Registering as an index-holding peer and answering queries against a claimed document share is not a write with a canonical target string forgery-resistance.md's mechanism binds to, so its per-write price is never consulted at admission time (same scope limit as finding 1). incentives.md prices storage payment and bandwidth between already-transacting peers, not the count of index-holding identities one entity registers. registry/conflicts/search.md section 5.3 already names this precondition and explicitly defers the question to 'whichever agent covers Sybil resistance' without answering it; no selection in this registry supplies that mechanism. The consequence is measured directly: at 10% malicious peers and z=2,000 queried nodes with no defense, RICHARDSON-SIGIR-14 measures a censorship attack moving a target's rank from 5 to 582 and a promotion attack moving an unrelated document into the top 10.

**Resolution.** Either adopt a mandatory per-identity admission cost scoped to index-registration specifically (no candidate in this corpus supplies one without a ledger, a pattern already declined elsewhere per registry/composition/ordering.md finding 2), or accept the degraded, corpus-stated bound: indexing.md's own skewness defense holds only to the lower of its own tolerance (40%) and its sampling primitive's published tolerance (Brahms, 20%), so 20% is the actual claimable bound absent a Sybil layer, or record the real adversarial fraction as unmeasured and therefore this choice as unmade.

### 5. The selected relay-assisted NAT-traversal mechanism assumes the relay enforces its own advertised duration/data limits and does not deliberately delay, correlate, or corrupt the coordination exchange.

**Required by** nat-traversal.md (relay-introduced, timing-synchronized hole punching, DCUtR/Circuit-v2) · **removed by** forgery-resistance.md (selected: computational work priced per write) / incentives.md · **no-published-resolution-open-problem**

Relaying a NAT-punch coordination exchange for another peer is a standing service, not a write with a canonical target string, so forgery-resistance.md's price is not consulted (same scope limit as findings 1-3). incentives.md prices bandwidth and storage between two already-transacting peers, not a relay forwarding a third party's setup traffic. nat-traversal.md's own text states the missing defense directly: 'an admission or Sybil-resistance layer for relay operators' is required and not supplied, and attributes part of the mechanism's own measured ~30% residual coordination-failure rate to exactly this exposure (TRAUTWEIN-ARXIV-26). The one candidate that verifies relay conduct economically (KEIZER-MOBIHOC-20, an independent second node comparing delivered hashes and timestamps) belongs to the rejected row C and itself requires 'an append-only public ledger' — the infrastructure four other selections in this registry decline to build for the same reason (registry/composition/ordering.md finding 2).

**Resolution.** Either adopt KEIZER-MOBIHOC-20's economically-verified relay design for the relay role specifically (accepting its ledger dependency and its measured ~$0.40-$0.60 client-side / ~$0.10-$0.20 relay-side per-session charge), or accept the degradation nat-traversal.md's own numbers already state — part of the measured ~30% coordination-failure rate is attributable to unverified relay conduct, so the mechanism's success rate is conditional on the currently reachable relay population's honesty, not purely a function of NAT topology — or record relay-operator misbehavior rates as unmeasured, matching nat-traversal.md's own stated gap for relay sustainability.

### 6. No single operator or company can capture the mechanism a client relies on to detect a substituted or maliciously issued key

**Required by** the architecture's stated design target (BRIEF §1: identity, indexing, and storage cannot be captured by any company) · **removed by** key-transparency.md (Parakeet witness-committee selection) · **no-published-resolution-open-problem**

The selection needs a fixed 3f+1-witness committee whose non-equivocation guarantee holds only while 2f+1 members are honest; key-transparency.md's own text states no witness-selection or rotation process resisting single-operator capture exists anywhere in the corpus, so whoever controls a majority of seats controls the mechanism undetected.

**Resolution.** Recorded as an open problem: key-transparency.md's own 'what the corpus does not settle' section states this directly, and no other selection in this corpus is evidenced as a witness-selection mechanism that could close the gap.

### 7. No party needs to hold the whole network's trust-linkage graph to answer one participant's question about a stranger

**Required by** the architecture's stated design target (BRIEF §1) · **removed by** reputation.md's selected flow-based mechanism (Bazaar/SumUp/Ostra, accelerated by Canal) · **no-published-resolution-open-problem**

The selection's own text states the computation needs a party able to compute over the full linkage graph on the assessor's behalf, or a Canal-style landmark index rebuilt continuously in the background; every measured cost figure this selection cites comes from exactly that whole-graph-holding configuration, while the one decentralized alternative it also lists (Ostra's route-discovery sketch) is validated only by a preliminary measurement, not end-to-end.

**Resolution.** Recorded as an open problem, matching reputation.md's own stated gap: no entry in this corpus measures this family's fully peer-executed, decentralized variant end-to-end.

### 8. A feed's identifier, and the mutable pointer reused for a CRDT's current head and for the newest object in an evolving media collection, must remain the same persistent signing key's public key for as long as followers and other o

**Required by** application-data.md (signed append-only log / feed / reused pointer mechanism, KERMARREC-DICG-20) · **removed by** identity.md + key-recovery.md (FROST device-set DKG on membership change; guardian-authorized non-reconstructive rotation to a new key PK′) · **no-published-resolution-open-problem**

identity.md states a device-set change implies 'a fresh distributed key generation round,' with no established technique for preserving the same public key across it; key-recovery.md's flow states explicitly 'the original private key is never regenerated at any point in this flow' — PK′ has no algebraic relation to the key it replaces, so the feed/pointer identifier changes with no stated migration step.

**Resolution.** No published resolution in the corpus. Change-a-selection direction: bind the feed identifier to the DID with an explicit update chain, the way identity.md's own rejected did:plc baseline already does ('each later document version is valid only if signed by the key authorized in the immediately preceding version') — untested for a signed log in this corpus. Degraded-property direction: every rotation orphans prior feeds/pointers unless old key material is retained indefinitely, defeating the purpose of loss recovery — magnitude unmeasured. Recorded here as an open problem since no entry proposes or measures either fix.

### 9. A GNS zone's entire published record set must remain reachable at the DHT position determined by that zone's own private key, for as long as those records and every other zone's delegation into them must resolve.

**Required by** naming.md (GNS per-zone delegation, WACHS-CANS-14) · **removed by** identity.md + key-recovery.md (device-set DKG; guardian-authorized rotation to PK′) · **no-published-resolution-open-problem**

naming.md states GNS's DHT lookup key is 'a scalar multiple of the zone's private key by a representation of the label' — an algebraic dependency, not merely a signature check — so a rotated key relocates every record to an unreachable DHT position with no republication step stated anywhere in naming.md or identity.md.

**Resolution.** No published resolution. Change-a-selection direction: indirect GNS's DHT position through the identity's DID (via an update chain analogous to did:plc) rather than deriving it directly from the raw private key — untested in this corpus. Degraded-property direction: accept that identity rotation silently orphans a zone's entire prior record set and every delegation into it, with the resulting exposure unmeasured. Recorded as an open problem, since neither naming.md's nor identity.md's own gap lists name this consequence.

### 10. A node's network identifier must remain the same, already-verified value for content-location.md's S/Kademlia (to preserve accumulated routing-table bucket position and the puzzle-derived Sybil cost) and for capacity-ordering.md's

**Required by** content-location.md (S/Kademlia) and capacity-ordering.md (HSkip+, FELDMANN-CSUR-21) · **removed by** identity.md (the public key each node's identifier is stated to derive from, subject to device-set DKG rotation) + key-recovery.md · **no-published-resolution-open-problem**

Both selections derive the node identifier from an identity-supplied public key; a rotation event (ordinary device-set change or key-recovery.md's PK′ flow) presents a new identifier to every peer holding the old one in a routing table, discarding S/Kademlia's puzzle cost and bucket longevity and directly violating HSkip+'s stated never-re-checked precondition.

**Resolution.** No published resolution; reported at lower confidence than the other two instances because neither file states whether the DHT/routing 'node identifier' is scoped to the whole multi-device identity or to one participating device — that scoping question is itself unresolved in the corpus, and the finding's severity depends on which reading applies. Recorded as an open problem pending that clarification.

### 11. An atomic hold/release protocol per interaction plus a link-level locking mechanism, so that a shared link's bounded capacity in the reputation flow-network is never reserved twice at once; the security bound (fraud strictly bound

**Required by** reputation.md (Bazaar/SumUp/Ostra + Canal, capacity-bounded network-flow family) · **removed by** application-data.md (selected hash-identified Byzantine CRDT for editable multi-writer state) · **property-degrades-and-is-stated**

The selected CRDT's own convergence theorem proves no fault-tolerant algorithm exists for an invariant that is not invariant-confluent under Byzantine faults; a non-negative/never-double-spent balance is its own cited counterexample (two concurrent debits from one account can jointly violate the invariant even though each alone would not). A reputation link balance is multi-writer mutable state with no other supplying mechanism in this registry, so by default it has nowhere to live except this CRDT, which explicitly states it cannot enforce this class of invariant without a separate bounded-adversary consensus sub-mechanism it does not supply.

**Resolution.** Three options recorded in registry/composition/ordering.md: (1) change the design so link balances are bilateral two-party signed state (payment-channel-like) rather than routed through the general multi-writer CRDT; (2) accept that Bazaar/SumUp's exactness bounds hold only between reconciliations, with an unmeasured transient over-commit possible during a partition; (3) record as an open problem alongside composition.md finding 2's open list of resource classes needing exactly-once semantics.

### 12. The flow-computing party (a central service, a route-discovery layer, or a precomputed landmark index) must observe the full trust-linkage graph — who has transacted with or vouched for whom — to compute a bounded flow value betwe

**Required by** reputation.md (capacity-bounded network-flow trust metric: SumUp, Bazaar, Ostra, accelerated by Canal) · **removed by** privacy-tiers.md (cover-traffic mix network, the default Message tier) · **property-degrades-and-is-stated**

The Message tier's proven differential-privacy bound holds against any server on a message's path, including a server occupying the flow-computing role; when two participants form a real linkage (a vouch, a transaction) through this tier, the mix network hides the who-talked-to-whom fact from any observing server by design, so the flow-computing party cannot learn the edge exists.

**Resolution.** reputation.md's own selection text already names this exact requirement as a conflict to resolve rather than an open question, but does not resolve it against a specific privacy tier; this document completes that resolution as an accepted degradation: a linkage formed through a Message-tier-protected interaction contributes nothing to either participant's flow-based reputation value, so computed reputation undercounts real linkages in proportion to Message-tier adoption. The alternative of changing a selection — forcing every linkage-forming interaction to disclose its two endpoints to the flow-computing party regardless of chosen Message tier — is not specified by any mechanism in either selection file.

### 13. Both peers exchanging bandwidth must observe how much bandwidth a specific, stable neighbor identity sent in the prior round or rounds, so each can proportionally reciprocate to that same counterparty in the following round.

**Required by** incentives.md and capacity-ordering.md (proportional-share reciprocation rule, PropShare) · **removed by** privacy-tiers.md (3-hop onion circuit, the default Fetch tier) · **property-degrades-and-is-stated**

privacy-tiers.md lists the serving peer itself among the adversaries the onion-circuit tier defeats, hiding the requester's identity from the peer serving content; under this tier a requester's directly observed counterparty is an entry relay, not the peer that actually served the object, so neither side can attribute received bandwidth to a stable, recognizable counterparty across rounds.

**Resolution.** Recorded as an accepted degradation, since it is the reading both selection files' text supports without inventing an unstated mechanism: per-neighbor bandwidth reciprocation functions only for direct connections or for Fetch-tier padding defenses that still expose a stable serving-peer identity (WTF-PAD, Tamaraw), and a transfer routed through the onion-circuit tier contributes nothing to either side's reciprocation ledger, which should be disclosed to the user as outside the incentive mechanism's coverage. Changing a selection instead — settling bandwidth for anonymized transfers through the storage component's ledger-based payment rather than peer-observed reciprocity — is not evaluated by any entry in the corpus.

### 14. The Message privacy tier's proven differential-privacy bound holds only if at least one server on a message's path is honestly performing its mixing and noise-generation duties; an open, permissionless server population needs an a

**Required by** privacy-tiers.md (Message ladder: cover-traffic mix network, Karaoke default) · **removed by** forgery-resistance.md (selected: computational work priced per write) / incentives.md (storage and bandwidth pricing between transacting peers) · **property-degrades-and-is-stated**

Acting as a mix server is a standing service role, not a write with a content address the per-write price binds to, so forgery-resistance.md's mechanism is not consulted by a peer registering for that role (same scope limit as findings 1 and 2). incentives.md prices resources exchanged between two transacting peers, not a third party's conduct while mixing traffic on behalf of others who did not individually select it. privacy-tiers.md's own text states directly: 'no enforcement mechanism exists for the one-honest-server assumption against a fully open, permissionless server population... an identity or admission component that can vet or bond a small server-role subset is required before this tier can be offered with its proven guarantee intact' — and no such component is selected anywhere in this registry.

**Resolution.** privacy-tiers.md's own text already states the accepted degradation: disclose to a person selecting the Message tier that its proven bound is conditional on trusting the currently selected server-role operators, not unconditional against a fully open population. Changing the selection would mean layering a bonding requirement onto the mix-server role specifically, using forgery-resistance.md's rejected staked-deposit candidate as the nearest analog, which reintroduces the ledger dependency registry/composition/ordering.md finding 2 already documents as declined elsewhere in this architecture.

### 15. Gossip-based collaborative filtering, selected as the mechanism computing the default feed nearly every user experiences, requires that a peer's reported click/interaction log actually reflects real interactions; a validly signed 

**Required by** ranking.md (gossip-based collaborative filtering as the default-computing mechanism) · **removed by** forgery-resistance.md (selected: computational work priced per write) · **property-degrades-and-is-stated**

Reporting an interaction into a gossip stream is not a write forgery-resistance.md's per-write price was built to bind to (same scope limit as findings 1-4), and content-signing from the identity/naming component — which ranking.md does require and which is supplied elsewhere in this architecture — proves who sent a record, not that the interaction it reports occurred. ranking.md's own text states the corpus's nearest defense (GOLD-ARXIV-23's preference for metadata matches over click-history matches) is 'stated by its authors only to blunt, not stop' fabrication. The one measured stronger defense, a Data-Shapley marginal-contribution filter (GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25), is not committed as ranking.md's default instantiation and carries its own stated cold-start gap: a newly joined node has no local click history to value incoming data against and is exposed to unfiltered poisoning until it accumulates one.

**Resolution.** Either commit to the Shapley-filtered instantiation as the mandatory default (accepting its cold-start exposure for every newly joined node), or accept the degradation ranking.md's own numbers already state: unfiltered, ranking 'converges towards a single set of rankings that it appears unable to escape from' once adversarial peers reach 75 of 100 (GOLD-ARXIV-23); filtered, Mean Reciprocal Rank holds at or above its local-only floor of 0.38 only below a 90% poisoned-neighbor share (GREGORIADIS-ARXIV-25) — or record as open, since no entry deploys this mechanism to real users and measures whether a poisoned default would be detected and abandoned, which ranking.md's own text already flags as an unanswered question for the pairing it selects.

### 16. No party needs to ingest the whole network's content stream to provide indexing, ranking, or moderation coverage

**Required by** the architecture's stated design target (BRIEF §1) · **removed by** moderation.md's labeler-aggregation service and ranking.md's feed-generator marketplace · **property-degrades-and-is-stated**

Both selections depend on subscribing to the full signed content-and-identity stream at roughly 30 gigabytes per day per subscriber; moderation.md states the only alternative to a centralizing aggregator is that same per-client cost and calls independent viability unsettled, while ranking.md requires the identical stream for any feed-generator operator and separately measures the hosting role concentrating at 85.86% on one platform.

**Resolution.** Accept a degraded property, stated precisely: full moderation-label coverage and full feed-generator comprehensiveness are, in practice, delivered by a small number of well-resourced aggregator or hosting operators rather than by every peer independently; a client can still bear the full per-client stream cost itself to avoid depending on any one of them, so the no-capture property is priced rather than lost.

### 17. No company can capture the mechanism that decides which content the network serves

**Required by** the architecture's stated design target (BRIEF §1) · **removed by** moderation.md's illegal-content mechanism (identifier deny list) · **property-degrades-and-is-stated**

The selection requires one maintaining organization to publish the denylist that each independently operated gateway voluntarily honors; its own measured deployment shows enforcement at roughly 100% on the maintaining organization's own gateways against roughly 18% elsewhere, evidence that the mechanism's actual reach is set by trust in that one organization's list, with no corpus-evidenced way to distribute or cross-check that curation authority.

**Resolution.** Accept a degraded property, stated precisely: illegal-content moderation depends on trust in whichever party or parties maintain the denylist, a distinct, legally-motivated exception to the no-capture goal — moderation.md itself already frames the mechanism as selected 'not because it works, but because it is the sole candidate measured.'

### 18. BeeKEM's security for a given group member depends on that member's continued good behavior after Add, with no live re-check against the identity layer; key-recovery.md's revocation is meant to take away a lost device's authority 

**Required by** group-encryption.md (BeeKEM) · **removed by** key-recovery.md (non-reconstructive rotation to PK′, designed for the case where a lost device's holder may still exist and act) · **property-degrades-and-is-stated**
